Mixing times for Glauber dynamics of lozenge tilings of the hexagon
Amol Aggarwal, Fabio Toninelli

Abstract
. We prove that the continuous-time, single-flip Glauber dynamics for lozenge tilings of the size- N N hexagon mix in time N 2 + o ⁡ ( 1 ) N^{2+o(1)} . This was predicted to hold on fairly general domains of diameter N N (on the basis of the “Lifshitz law” heuristic) but had previously only been established in domains such that the associated limit shape has no frozen facets. To access the hexagon, we introduce a multi-scale comparison argument between the height function of the random tiling evolving under the Glauber dynamics and the limit shape of a volume-tilted tiling (whose tilting parameter varies suitably in time).



1.
Introduction

1.1.
The lozenge tiling Glauber dynamic on the hexagon

Random lozenge tilings, or equivalently the dimer model on the
honeycomb lattice, are a classical subject of study in statistical
mechanics, probability and combinatorics; see e.g., the monograph

[
23
]
. To introduce the problem, let
\mathbb{T}_{N},N\in\mathbb{N}
,
be the infinite triangular grid with equilateral triangular faces of
side-length
1/N
, and let
D
be a bounded domain of the plane that can be
tiled by lozenge-shaped tiles, obtained as the union of two adjacent
triangular faces. Call
\Omega^{N}
the set of all
lozenge tilings of
D
and
\mathbb{P}^{N}
the uniform probability
measure on
\Omega^{N}
.

In addition to being of intrinsic interest, random tilings are archetypes of strongly correlated systems that are very sensitive to boundary conditions. They may be viewed as models of random surfaces, which are known
[
17
,
16
]
to converge to a
limit shape
as
N
tends to
\infty
. A salient feature of these limit shapes is that they are highly inhomogeneous. They admit
frozen regions
, where the surface is almost deterministically flat, and
liquid regions
, where it appears more rough and random; the curve separating these phases is called an
arctic boundary

[
26
]
. Across these different regions, the tiling admits considerably different asymptotic behaviors, which can be computed exactly due to the underlying determinantal structure of the model
[
29
,
31
]
that becomes explicit on specific polygonal domains, such as the hexagon
[
28
]
. For example, when
D
is a regular hexagon, in the liquid region the tiling has Gaussian free field fluctuations
[
45
]
; around the arctic boundary it has Kardar–Parisi–Zhang (KPZ) fluctuations
[
41
,
19
,
6
,
44
]
; and near points where the arctic boundary meets the hexagon, called tangency locations, the tiling has Gaussian unitary ensemble (GUE) fluctuations
[
27
,
42
]
.

While the measure
\mathbb{P}^{N}
is by now well studied, local Markovian dynamics on
\Omega^{N}
(admitting
\mathbb{P}^{N}
as stationary measure) and their mixing properties are still poorly understood. In this work, we consider the most natural such dynamic, that is
the
single-flip Glauber dynamic
on
\Omega^{N}
, which is the
continuous-time Markov chain on
\Omega^{N}
whose updates are the
elementary rotations depicted in Figure
1
(right), both
having transition rate
1
. This dynamic is routinely used
1
1

1

a freely available code implementing the Glauber dynamic can be found at https://lpetrov.cc/lozenge-draw/
, to numerically sample uniformly chosen random lozenge tilings of the domain
D
(and can be implemented within the coupling-from-the-past framework; see
[
46
]
and
[
36
, Chapter 25]
).
We mention also that the single-flip Glauber dynamic is the zero-temperature limit of the Glauber dynamic of the three-dimensional Ising model with Dobrushin boundary conditions
[
11
,
13
]
.

The uniform measure
\mathbb{P}^{N}
on
\Omega^{N}
is reversible for the Glauber dynamic and is
its unique stationary measure. Since the state space is
finite, the total variation mixing time

t_{\mix}^{N}
is also finite. The question of quantifying
t_{\mix}^{N}
as a function of

N
has an interesting history, and an incomplete status. It was first proven in
[
38
]
that a
non-local version
of the Glauber
dynamic, called the “tower-move dynamic,” mixes in polynomial (in
N
) time. Via standard spectral
gap comparison tools, this implies polynomial time mixing for the single-flip
dynamic, as well. Subsequently, when
D
is the regular hexagon
\mathfrak{X}
of side-length
1
with sides parallel to the
axis of the triangular grid (that can be tiled by exactly
3N^{2}
tiles),
the mixing time of the tower-move
dynamic was determined in
[
49
]
to be of
order
N^{2}\log N
(with upper and lower bounds differing by a constant
prefactor). This result implies (via the above spectral gap comparison)
that
t^{N}_{\mix}\lesssim N^{6+o(1)}

[
47
]
. A more refined comparison argument (that combines the analysis of the tower-dynamic mixing time with the censoring inequality
[
43
]
) allows to prove
t^{N}_{\mix}\lesssim N^{4+o(1)}

[
11
]
; this was the best existing upper bound,
for the mixing time of the Glauber dynamic in the hexagon,
before the present work.

However, on the basis of a general “mean curvature motion heuristic” (see also
Section

1.2
for more discussion), it was predicted that
t^{N}_{\mix}\approx N^{2}
also for
the single-flip dynamic, possibly with logarithmic sub-leading
corrections. This conjecture turned out to be rather elusive, for
several reasons. First, in contrast with the tower-move dynamic,
coupling arguments fail to provide any contraction for the Glauber
dynamic. Second, the dynamic has no perturbative parameter to tune
(such as the temperature for Ising models, or the number of colors and
graph degree for random colorings). Third, equilibrium correlations
for the system are not summable, so one cannot exploit strong spatial
mixing properties (in the spirit of
[
40
]
) to upper bound the mixing time. Moreover, due
to the hard constraints intrinsic in the tiling model, configurations
can be blocked (unable to perform any local move) in arbitrarily large
regions of the graph.

Still, a first step towards the conjecture was done in

[
13
]
, where the bounds

(1.1)

N^{2}\lesssim t^{N}_{\mix}\lesssim N^{2+o(1)}

were proven for the single-flip dynamic in a tilable domain
D
of the plane (of diameter of order
1
), provided that the limit shape on
D
is affine. Later, (
1.1
) was generalized
[
33
,
35
]
to the case where the limit shape is
C^{\infty}
. These partial results point out that the geometry of the domain
D
, and
in particular the regularity properties of the associated limit shape, play a subtle role in the convergence to equilibrium.

Arguably, the most natural and classical geometric domain where
lozenge tilings can be studied is the hexagon
\mathfrak{X}
, and neither of the cited results
[
13
,
33
,
35
]

applies there. Indeed, the limit shape on
\mathfrak{X}

[
17
]
(as well as on other polygonal domains
[
30
,
5
]
) admits an arctic boundary, along which the limit shape is not smooth.

The main result of the present work is a proof of the asymptotic

t_{\mix}^{N}\approx N^{2}
for the Glauber dynamic on
\mathfrak{X}
, up to
sub-leading multiplicative corrections:

Theorem 1.1
.

For any
\delta>0
, there exist constants
\kappa>0
and
C=C(\delta)>0
such that the mixing time of the single-flip Glauber dynamic on tilings of the hexagon
\mathfrak{X}
satisfies

(1.2)

\displaystyle\kappa N^{2}\leq t^{N}_{\rm mix}\leq CN^{2+\delta}.

With minor modifications, one can also prove the same
statement for a hexagon of fixed positive side-lengths
a,b,c
(in which case the constants
\kappa,C
would depend on
a,b,c
). However, we will not pursue this here, in order to avoid making the notation heavier.

The effort in proving
1.1
is the upper bound: the argument for
t^{N}_{\rm mix}\geq\kappa N^{2}
, already given in

[
13
, Section 5.2]
for the dynamic in a domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{T}_{N}
where the
limit shape is affine, works identically for the dynamic in

\mathfrak{X}
and we will not repeat it.

1.2.
Proof ideas

The conjecture
t_{\mix}=N^{2+o(1)}
is based on the “Lifshitz law” heuristic
[
37
]
stating that, upon rescaling time by
N^{2}
and in the limit
N\to\infty
, the limiting evolution of the tiling height function
\varphi
under the Glauber dynamics should follow an anisotropic variant of mean curvature motion, of the form

(1.3)

\displaystyle \partial_{t}\varphi=\mu(\nabla\varphi)\cdot\mathcal{L}\varphi.

In (
1.3
),
\mathcal{L}
is an explicit semi-elliptic, non-linear operator (related to the surface tension functional of random tilings) and
\mu(\nabla\varphi)
is a certain slope-dependent “mobility coefficient” governing the speed of the evolution; see
[
48
]
for further commentary. The limit (
1.3
) has only been proven in the case of the tower-move dynamics
[
34
]
(assuming the solution to (
1.3
) is
C^{2}
), where the associated mobility coefficient
\mu
is known explicitly. For the Glauber dynamics, there is no predicted expression for
\mu
. Moreover, under hexagonal boundary conditions, the solution
\varphi
to (
1.3
) should not be
C^{2}
, so even the well-posedness of (
1.3
) is unclear.

At least the first issue was circumvented in
[
13
,
33
,
35
]
, by instead proving that the evolution
H_{t}
of the tiling height function under the Glauber dynamics likely remains between two deterministic “barrier” functions
\varphi^{-}_{t}
and
\varphi^{+}_{t}
. The latter might be viewed as “proxies” to solutions of (
1.3
) and were obtained by perturbing
\mathcal{H}
by functions
\psi^{\pm}_{t}
whose Hessian dominates that of
\mathcal{H}
. The latter in particular necessitates that
\mathcal{H}
is
C^{2}
, which does not hold if
\mathcal{H}
is the limit shape for a random tiling of the hexagon.

To address this, we instead introduce different barriers, given by the limit shapes
\mathcal{H}_{q}
of a random tiling of the hexagon, weighted by
e^{q\vol}
, where
\vol
denotes the volume of the stepped surface associated with the tiling. Such “
q
-deformed” tiling models have been studied quite extensively over the past two decades
[
14
,
41
,
30
,
8
,
22
]
. Here, we use their limit shapes to study the Glauber dynamics of the original (
q=0
) tiling model. In particular, we prove (Proposition
5.14
) by induction on
t
that

(1.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{-q(t)}\leq H_{t}\leq\mathcal{H}_{q(t)},

holds with high probability, where
q(t)>0
depends suitably on
t
(and
N
), decreasing to
0
as
t
becomes large. We will eventually take
q(t)
as small as
N^{-1}
for

t=N^{2+o(1)}
. This implies at such times that the height
H_{t}
is so
close to the limit shape
\mathcal{H}
associated to the uniform measure

\mathbb{P}^{N}
, that after another time-lag of
N^{2+o(1)}
equilibrium is
reached.

These
\mathcal{H}_{q}
limit shapes are both fully explicit
[
8
,
22
]
and more closely model the behavior of the Glauber dynamics on the hexagon than the barrier functions
\varphi^{\pm}_{t}
used in earlier works.
2
2

2

In fact, if one stops these Glauber dynamics before they mix, the associated height function looks numerically (see, e.g., https://lpetrov.cc/lozenge-draw/) qualitatively quite similar to (though is not predicted to exactly coincide with) some
\mathcal{H}_{q}
.
For instance, they also admit arctic boundaries separating liquid from frozen phases. We will see that the mechanism under which the Glauber dynamics locally mixes will vary in mesoscopic regions between the bulk of the liquid phase and the arctic boundary, and that this must be taken into account when establishing (
1.4
).

To implement this idea, we begin by showing a “base case” for (
1.4
), verifying it (Theorem
4.3
) when
t=N^{2+o(1)}
and
q(t)=q_{0}>0
is a constant that is arbitrarily small but independent of
N
. First, we perturb the boundary conditions of
\mathcal{H}
to make the associated limit shape smooth (Proposition
4.6
), enabling us to apply the prior results
[
33
,
35
]
to show that the Glauber dynamics under these perturbed boundary conditions mix in time
N^{2+o(1)}
. By comparing these to the original Glauber dynamics, we obtain (Proposition
4.5
) that
|H_{t}-\mathcal{H}|\lesssim q_{0}
for
t=N^{2+o(1)}
and fixed
q_{0}>0
. This is in the direction of (
1.4
) but does not quite verify it where
\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}
is frozen, as there we have
\mathcal{H}_{-q_{0}}=\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}
. These regions are addressed by induction on
N
, involving “embedding” smaller hexagons (whose frozen regions contain those of
\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}
) into the original one; see Section
4.2.2
for further details.

Next, we must show (
1.4
) for
q(t)<q_{0}
, which is
the main task in this work. A primary heuristic here is that,
throughout the Glauber dynamics, one should expect all level
lines of
H_{t}
to move at a speed of order
N^{-2}
. However,
unlike on domains admitting
C^{2}
limit shapes, different
level lines of
H_{t}
on the hexagon should exhibit different
fluctuations. Thus, it should take different amounts of time
to “detect” whether the level line has moved; in this way,
local mixing occurs at different rates across the hexagon. For
example, in the bulk of the liquid region, the level lines
should have
N^{o(1)-1}
fluctuations, so their motion is
detected after time
N^{1+o(1)}
; generically along the arctic boundary, they
should have
N^{o(1)-2/3}
fluctuations, so their motion is
detected after time
N^{4/3+o(1)}
; and at the tangency locations, they should have
N^{o(1)-1/2}
fluctuations, so their motion is detected only after time
N^{3/2+o(1)}
.

To resolve this, we decompose the hexagon
\mathfrak{X}
into “annuli” (Definition
5.1
), in which these level line fluctuations (and thus the rate of local mixing) are of the same order. We then establish the following two types of statements; in what follows, we focus on decreasing the upper bound on
H_{t}
(increasing the lower bound is entirely analogous). The first, which we may refer to as “liquid phase mixing,” states that if the height function
H_{t}
is upper bounded in an annulus not too close to the arctic boundary, then the upper bound on
H_{t}
improves (that is, it decreases further) on inner annuli, after running the Glauber dynamics for a certain time (Lemma
5.6
). The second, which we may refer to as “edge phase mixing,” states that if
H_{t}
is upper bounded in an annulus reasonably close to the arctic boundary, then this bound extends to the outer annuli (up to a negligible error) after some time (Lemmas
5.7
and
5.8
). By combining these two statements, we then prove (
1.4
) by induction on
t
.

To show these annulus mixing results, we first reduce them to their “local versions” (Propositions
6.4
,
7.6
, and
7.7
) that restrict to mesoscopic cells contained within the annuli. The benefit to doing this is that the limit shapes
\mathcal{H}_{q}
are more regular in these mesoscopic cells. For example, although
\mathcal{H}_{q}
is not
C^{2}
near the arctic boundary, it becomes smooth upon an explicit rescaling
3
3

3

This is sometimes called a “blow-up” procedure in the literature on obstacle problems
[
9
]
.
dependent on the cell’s location (Proposition
3.27
). Given this, the proof of local bulk mixing (Proposition
6.4
) uses an idea introduced in
[
33
]
. Specifically, we compare (the “regular rescaling” of) the limit shape
\mathcal{H}_{q(t)}
to that
\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
of an unweighted (that is, uniform) tiling on a certain
a\times b\times c
hexagon, so that their gradients coincide at the cell’s center but
\mathcal{H}_{q}
is “more concave” than
\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
(Proposition
3.28
). Using the added concavity of
\mathcal{H}_{q}
, with concentration bounds for the unweighted tiling around
\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
, we show that
H_{t^{\prime}}
must “deflate” to a smaller
\mathcal{H}_{q(t^{\prime})}
after some time
t^{\prime}-t
; see Section
6.3
for further details.

To show the local edge mixing results, we separate into cases, depending on whether the cell is away from or at the arctic boundary (Propositions
7.6
or
7.7
), and whether it is away from or at the tangency location (Sections
8.1
or
8.2
, and Sections
9.1
or
9.2
). The proofs in all of these cases are quite similar to each other, but they differ from the bulk case, since the limit shape
\mathcal{H}_{q}
will not behave regularly in our mesoscopic cells close to the arctic boundary. However, the level lines of
\mathcal{H}_{q}
will be more regular (Proposition
3.26
). So, to show the local edge mixing results, we develop an analog of the local bulk mixing reasoning that compares the convexity properties between the level lines of the tiling height functions (which are equivalent to non-intersecting random walks) approximating
\mathcal{H}_{q}
and
\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
. See the beginning of Section
8.1.2
for a more detailed exposition.

In order to implement the above strategy, we need to prove, among others, several analytic statements concerning the volume-tilted limit shape
\mathcal{H}_{q}
, that make precise the intuition that
\mathcal{H}_{q}
becomes more concave as
q
grows, and that the curvature of its level lines changes in a specific way as a function of
q
. These statements are given in
Section

3
and proved (by direct, though tedious, Taylor expansions of the exact expressions for these limit shapes) in Appendices
B
and
C
.

1.3.
Relation with existing literature

The Lifshitz law heuristic mentioned above applies
to any reasonable reversible interface dynamics in
(d+1)
dimensions, where
d
refers to the space dimension and
1
to time, corresponding in the case of lozenge tilings to
d=2
. This heuristic
leads to the conjecture that the inverse spectral gap is of order

N^{2}
and that the mixing time is of order
N^{2}
times a logarithmic
(in
N
) factor, the latter originating from the fact that once the
interface is macroscopically close to the equilibrium one (which
happens after a time of the order of the inverse spectral gap), it
still takes some time before its law is close to the equilibrium one
in total variation distance.

The prior rigorous proofs of the

t_{\rm mix}\asymp N^{2}\log N
asymptotic for interface dynamics had been been mostly limited to
(1+1)
-dimensional
random interface models
[
10
,
39
,
32
]
(see also

[
20
]
and references therein).
A simplifying feature of one-dimensional
interface models is that their stationary measure, when viewed in
terms of the interface gradients, has an i.i.d. structure. In
contrast, for random tilings, equilibrium correlations have a slow
decay in space, of the order of the squared inverse distance.
Another case where such results were proven
is the Glauber dynamic of the
(d+1)
-dimensional Gaussian lattice free field
[
21
]
(where the spectral gap can be computed exactly). In a different but related direction,
[
7
]
proved sharp results for the inverse spectral gap of Glauber dynamics of interface models on hierarchical lattices; the inverse gap has the same order as that of the corresponding Gaussian free field dynamic.
We point out that
[
10
,
21
,
32
]

further prove that these dynamics exhibit the cutoff (in total variation) phenomenon.

Finally, let us mention the earlier works
[
12
,
24
]
on mixing of Glauber dynamics of random lozenge tilings, that take a point of view quite different from that of the present paper. These works study a biased version of the Glauber dynamic, where the two updates in
Figure

1
have different transition rates. In this case, the phenomenology (both dynamic and equilibrium) is very different. First, the stationary measure of the biased dynamic is not uniform on the set of all tilings, but is instead the volume-tilted measure
e^{q\vol}
, where
q=-\gamma N
is of order
N
, and the parameter
\gamma\neq 0
is related to the ratio of the two transition rates. Such a measure concentrates on configurations whose volume is of order
1/N
, while typical configurations sampled from
\mathbb{P}^{N}
have volume of order
N^{2}
. Also, the Lifshitz law heuristic does not apply to the biased dynamic, because the drift induced by the bias dominates the curvature effect; in fact, the mixing time and inverse spectral gap turn out to be of order
N
and
O(1)
respectively, in contrast with
N^{2+o(1)}
and
N^{2}
for the symmetric dynamic we study here.

1.3.1.
Notation

We say that an event
\mathscr{E}
(that may depend on
N
) holds with
overwhelming probability (w.o.p.)
(with respect to some sequence of probability measures
\mathbb{P}^{N},N\in\mathbb{N}
) if the following holds. For any integer
L\geq 1
, there exists a constant
C>1
independent of
N
(but possibly dependent on
L
and other parameters involved) such that
\mathbb{P}^{N}[\mathscr{E}^{\complement}]\leq CN^{-L}
for all
N\geq 1
.

We use also the following standard notation:
f_{N}\lesssim g_{N}
and
g_{N}\gtrsim f_{N}
if there exists a positive constant
C>0
(independent of
N
) such that
f_{N}\leq Cg_{N}
(we may write
f_{N}\lesssim_{Q}g_{N}
if
C
depends on some parameter
Q
);
f_{N}\ll g_{N}
and
g_{N}\gg f_{N}
if
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}f_{N}/g_{N}=0
; and
f_{N}\asymp g_{N}
if
f_{N}\lesssim g_{N}
and
g_{N}\lesssim f_{N}
. Given
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we let
{\rm diam}(D)
denote its diameter.

Finally, given
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
, we will let
\partial_{\gamma}
denote the differential operator
\partial^{\gamma_{1}}_{x}\partial^{\gamma_{2}}_{y}
, that will be applied to smooth functions of
z=(x,y)
, while
D^{2}f
denotes the Hessian of a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
.

2.
Some preliminaries

2.1.
Height function and domains

Given
a,b,c>0
, let
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
denote the
a\times b\times c
hexagon with vertices
(0,0)
,
(a,0)
,
(a+c,c)
,
(a+c,b+c)
,
(c,b+c)
, and
(0,b)
. We call these six corners the SW (southwest), S (south), SE (southeast), NE (northeast), N (north), and NW (northwest) corners of this hexagon, respectively. The sides of the hexagon are labeled W, SE, SW, E, NE, NW, with the obvious convention. Throughout, we assume implicitly that
aN,bN,cN
are integers, so that the vertices of the hexagon are also vertices of
\mathbb{T}_{N}
.
Whenever
(a,b,c)=(1,1,1)
, we drop them from the notation.

We adopt the conventions in Figure
1
for the coordinates
(x,y)
in the hexagon. Since the
x
-axis is tilted in our figures, we sometimes refer to
(1,0)
as the SE direction, and to
(1,1)
as the NE direction.

Definition 2.1

(
\Omega^{N}_{a,b,c}
and
\mathbb{P}^{N}_{a,b,c}
)
.

The set of lozenge tilings of
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
is denoted by
\Omega^{N}_{a,b,c}
and the uniform measure on
\Omega^{N}_{a,b,c}
is denoted by
\mathbb{P}^{N}_{a,b,c}
.
More generally, given a finite tilable domain
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
we let
\Omega_{D}
denote the set of lozenge tilings of
D
.

To each
\eta\in\Omega_{D}
we can associate a height function
H=H_{\eta}
(indexed by the vertices of
\mathbb{T}_{N}
contained in
D
), that takes values in
N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
.
More precisely:

Definition 2.2

(Discrete height function)
.

Given a simply-connected, tilable domain
D
, a tiling
\eta\in\Omega_{D}
, and a point
z_{0}\in D\cap\mathbb{T}_{N}
, we define
H_{\eta}:D\cap\mathbb{T}_{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
as follows. First set
H_{\eta}(z_{0})=0
; then,
H_{\eta}
is determined by the relations

(2.1)

\displaystyle H_{\eta}(z^{\prime})-H_{\eta}(z)=N^{-1}\cdot\begin{cases}-1+{\bf 1}_{(z,z^{\prime})\text{ is the edge of a lozenge in $\eta$}}&\text{if }z^{\prime}-z=(N^{-1},0)\\
{\bf 1}_{(z,z^{\prime})\text{ is the edge of a lozenge in $\eta$}}&\text{if }z^{\prime}-z=(0,N^{-1})\end{cases}.

We further extend
H_{\eta}
to
D
by linearity. In the specific case
D=\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
we always choose
z_{0}=(0,0)
.

Remark 2.3
.

The correspondence between
\eta
and
H_{\eta}
is a bijection. Therefore, we will implicitly identify a lozenge tiling with its height function. We will also often drop the index
\eta
in the height and write just
H
.

Note also that
H_{\eta}
changes by either
-1/N
,
0
, or
1/N
between adjacent vertices of
\mathbb{T}_{N}
. Lastly, note that when
D=\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
,
H_{\eta}
is zero on the SW, S, SE corners of the hexagon, it equals
b
at the NE, N, NW corners and it is affine on each side of
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
. See Figure
1
.

The configuration space
\Omega_{D}
has a natural partial order:

Definition 2.4

(Partial order on
\Omega_{D}
)
.

Given a simply-connected, tilable domain
D
and
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\Omega_{D}
, we write

\eta\leq\eta^{\prime}
if and only if
H_{\eta}(z)\leq H_{\eta^{\prime}}(z)
for all
z\in D
. We let
\eta^{\wedge}\in\Omega_{D}
(resp.
\eta^{\vee}\in\Omega_{D}
) denote the maximal (resp. minimal) configuration, that is the unique tiling such that
\eta\leq\eta^{\wedge}
(resp.
\eta\geq\eta^{\vee}
) for every
\eta\in\Omega_{D}
.

The existence and uniqueness of
\eta^{\wedge},\eta^{\vee}
follows from the fact that, if
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\Omega_{D}
, then
z\in D\mapsto\max(H_{\eta}(z),H_{\eta^{\prime}}(z))
and
z\in D\mapsto\min(H_{\eta}(z),H_{\eta^{\prime}}(z))
are height functions of tilings of
D
.

Definition 2.5
.

Given a simply-connected, tilable domain
D
and two probability measures
\mu,\nu
on
\Omega_{D}
, we say that
\mu
is stochastically dominated by
\nu
(in formulas,
\mu\preceq\nu
) if there exists a coupling
(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
with
\eta\sim\mu,\eta^{\prime}\sim\nu
such that
H_{\eta}\leq H_{\eta^{\prime}}
almost surely.

Figure 1.
(a) The hexagon
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
. The height values are associated to lozenge vertices and height is measured with respect to the horizontal plane, if one interprets the tiling as a monotone stack of cubes of side-length
1/N
. The side-lengths
a,b,c
are assumed to be multiples of
1/N
. (b) The elementary move
\eta\leftrightarrow\eta^{(z)}
of the Glauber dynamic, both with rate
1
.
z
is the common point of the three lozenges with different orientations.

Lozenge tilings of
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
are also in bijection with a collection of
bN
non-intersecting simple-random walk paths. To set up this correspondence, we first define level lines of the height function. First of all, given
\eta\in\Omega^{N}_{a,b,c}
, we extend its height function to a function
H_{\eta}:\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}\mapsto[0,b]
by extending
H_{\eta}
of
Definition

2.2
linearly in each lozenge tile. Then, level lines are defined as follows:

Definition 2.6

(Level lines of the discrete height)
.

Given
a,b,c>0
and the height function
H
of a tiling
\eta
of
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
we define the level lines
\mathtt{U}_{a,b,c}^{h}:[0,a+c]\cap(1/N)\mathbb{Z}\mapsto[0,b+c]\cap(1/N)(\mathbb{Z}+1/2)
with
h\in[0,b]\cap(1/N)(\mathbb{Z}+1/2)
as

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mathtt{U}^{h}_{a,b,c}(x)=y\in[0,b+c]\cap(1/N)(\mathbb{Z}+1/2):H(x,y)=h.

Note from Figure
2
that level lines are disjoint zig-zag paths whose endpoints are spaced by
1/N
.
Later, we will need to consider collections of non-intersecting paths with different geometries. Therefore, we introduce the following:

Definition 2.7

(Bernoulli paths and Bernoulli path ensembles)
.

Given
p=(x_{p},y_{p}),q=(x_{q},y_{q})\in\mathbb{T}_{N}+(0,1/(2N))
such that
x_{p}<x_{q}
and
0\leq y_{q}-y_{p}\leq x_{q}-x_{p}
, we call
Bernoulli path
with endpoints
p,q
any path
S
that starts at
p
, ends at
q
and
such that
each step of
S
is either
(1/N,0)
or
(1/N,1/N)
(that is, steps are either in the SE or in the NE direction).

Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
and distinct points

p_{1},\dots,p_{n},q_{1},\dots,q_{n}
of
\mathbb{T}_{N}+(0,1/(2N))
such that all the
p_{i}
have
the same
x-
coordinate
x_{p}
(and the
q_{i}
also have all the same
x
-coordinate
x_{q}>x_{p}
) and ordered so that the
y-
coordinate of
p_{i}
is larger than that of
p_{j}
(resp. the
y
-coordinate of
q_{i}

is larger than that of
q_{j}
) for
i>j
, we let the
Bernoulli path ensemble
with endpoints

p_{1},\dots,p_{n},q_{1},\dots,q_{n}
denote the uniform measure on

n-
ples
(S_{1},\dots,S_{n})
of non-intersecting (i.e., disjoint) Bernoulli paths, where
S_{i}
has endpoints
(p_{i},q_{i})
. (We assume
that the points
p_{i},q_{i},i\leq n
are such that the configuration
space is nonempty).

Note that the lines
\mathtt{U}_{a,b,c}^{h}
are Bernoulli paths in the sense of
Definition

2.7
.

Figure 2.
A lozenge tiling of
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
with
a=b=c=1,N=4
and the corresponding height level lines, that form a collection of non-intersecting Bernoulli paths. Note that level lines are shifted vertically by
1/(2N)
with respect to the vertices of the triangular graph.

2.2.
The Glauber dynamics on lozenge tilings

2.2.1.
Definition of the dynamics and general facts

In this section we define the Glauber dynamic on lozenge tilings of a finite, simply-connected, tilable domain
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
. First we introduce some notation.
Given
\eta\in\Omega_{D}
, we let
M_{\eta}
be the set of points
z\in D\cap\mathbb{T}_{N}
such that
z
belongs to three tiles in
\eta
of the three possible orientations (see Figure
1
(b)). Given
z\in M_{\eta}
, we let
\eta^{(z)}
be the tiling obtained by rotating the three tiles that contain
z
by an angle
\pi
around
z
(see Figure
1
(b)).

Definition 2.8

(Glauber dynamics)
.

The lozenge tiling Glauber dynamic on
D

is the continuous-time Markov process with state space
\Omega_{D}

defined by the properties that the only allowed transitions from a configuration
\eta\in\Omega_{D}
are the configurations
\{\eta^{(z)}\}_{z\in M_{\eta}}
and that each such transition occurs according to an exponential clock of rate
1
.

Since the transitions
\eta\to\eta^{(z)}
and
\eta^{(z)}\to\eta
have the same rate, the uniform measure on
\Omega_{D}
is stationary and reversible.
It is quickly verified that, when
D=\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
, the Glauber dynamic is irreducible.

We let
\eta_{t}
denote the configuration at time
t
, and we write
H_{t}:=H_{\eta_{t}}
for the associated height function.

Definition 2.9

(
\mu_{t}^{\eta_{0}},\mathbf{P}_{\eta_{0}},\mathbf{P}_{\nu}
)
.

Given
\eta_{0}\in\Omega_{D}
and
t\geq 0
, we let
\mathbf{P}_{\eta_{0}}
denote the law of Glauber dynamic with initial condition
\eta_{0}
, and
\mu_{t}^{\eta_{0}}
the law of
\eta_{t}
.
If
\eta_{0}
is random and sampled according to a distribution
\nu
on
\Omega_{D}
, then we write
\mathbf{P}_{\nu}
for the law of the Glauber dynamic.

We recall the definition of mixing time: for an irreducible Markov chain on a finite state space
\Omega
and stationary measure
\pi
, one defines for
\epsilon\in(0,1)

(2.3)

\displaystyle t_{\mix}(\epsilon)=\inf\{t\geq 0:\max_{\eta\in\Omega}\|\mu_{t}^{\eta}-\pi\|\leq\epsilon\}

where
\|\cdot\|
denotes the total variation distance. The following holds
[
36
, Section 4.5]
for
0<\epsilon^{\prime}\leq\epsilon<1/2
:

(2.4)

\displaystyle t_{\mix}(\epsilon^{\prime})\leq t_{\mix}(\epsilon)\left\lceil\frac{|\log\epsilon^{\prime}|}{|\log 2\epsilon|}\right\rceil.

When we do not specify the value of
\epsilon
, it is meant that
t_{\rm mix}:=t_{\rm mix}(1/4)
.

We will use repeatedly a
constrained version of the dynamic.

Definition 2.10

(Constrained dynamic)
.

Fix
H_{-},H_{+}:D\mapsto\mathbb{R}
with
H_{-}\leq H_{+}
pointwise,
let
\Omega_{H_{\pm}}
be the set of tiling configurations in
\Omega_{D}
such that
{H_{-}}\leq H_{\eta}\leq{H_{+}}
(we assume
\Omega_{H_{\pm}}\neq\emptyset
) and fix an initial
condition
\eta_{0}\in\Omega_{H_{\pm}}
. The Glauber dynamic
with
floor constraint

H_{-}
and
ceiling constraint

H_{+}
is the Markov chain on
\Omega_{H_{\pm}}
that is defined just like the original Glauber dynamic, except that any update that would violate the constraint
H_{-}\leq H_{\eta_{t}}\leq H_{+}
is discarded.

It is immediate to check that the uniform measure
\pi_{H_{\pm}}
on
\Omega_{H_{\pm}}
is stationary and reversible for the dynamic constrained between
H_{\pm}
.
The following stochastic ordering result holds:

Proposition 2.11
.

Given two
floors
H_{-},H^{\prime}_{-}:D\mapsto\mathbb{R}
with
H_{-}\leq H^{\prime}_{-}
, two ceilings
H_{+},H^{\prime}_{+}:D\mapsto\mathbb{R}
with
H_{+}\leq H^{\prime}_{+}
and two
initial conditions
\eta_{0},\eta^{\prime}_{0}\in\Omega_{D}
satisfying
\eta_{0}\leq\eta^{\prime}_{0}
,
{\eta_{0}}\in\Omega_{H_{\pm}},{\eta^{\prime}_{0}}\in\Omega_{H^{\prime}_{\pm}}
, the Glauber dynamic
\eta_{t}
in
D
constrained between
H_{\pm}
and the dynamic
\eta^{\prime}_{t}
constrained between
H^{\prime}_{\pm}

can be coupled in such a way that
\eta_{t}\leq\eta_{t}^{\prime}
for all times
t
, with probability
1
.
In particular, it holds that
\pi_{H_{\pm}}\preceq\pi_{H^{\prime}_{\pm}}
.

Proof.

Assign an independent Poisson clock of rate
2
to each
z\in D\cap\mathbb{T}_{N}
. If the clock at
z
rings at some time
t
, then flip an independent fair coin and:

•

if
z\not\in M_{\eta_{t}}
then do nothing.

•

if
z\in M_{\eta_{t}}
, then call
\eta_{t}^{(z,+)}
(resp.
\eta_{t}^{(z,-)}
) the configuration among
\{\eta_{t},\eta_{t}^{(z)}\}
whose height function is maximal (minimal) at
z
. If the coin gives head (resp. tail), then replace
\eta_{t}
with
\eta^{(z,+)}
(resp.
\eta^{(z,-)}
) provided that the new configuration satisfies the floor and ceiling constraint, otherwise do nothing.

If we use the same Poisson clocks and coin flips for all processes with initial conditions
\eta_{0}\in\Omega_{D}
(compatible with their respective floor/ceiling), it is immediately checked that ordering is preserved.

The statement
\pi_{H_{\pm}}\preceq\pi_{H^{\prime}_{\pm}}
follows in the limit
t\to\infty
, when the laws of
\eta_{t}
and
\eta^{\prime}_{t}
approach their stationary distributions.
∎

In fact, the coupling described above provides a global monotone coupling of the evolutions started from any
\eta\in\Omega_{D}

in such a way that if

\eta_{0}\leq\eta_{0}^{\prime}
, then the corresponding evolutions satisfy

\eta_{t}\leq\eta^{\prime}_{t}
for every
t
, almost surely.

The following stochastic domination statement similar to
2.11
holds also for Bernoulli path ensembles (
Definition

2.7
); we omit its proof (as it follows from a very similar argument as used in that of
2.11
; for the case without constraints
V^{\pm},W^{\pm}
; see also
[
15
, Lemma 18]
).

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
S=(S_{1},\dots,S_{n})
and
S^{\prime}=(S^{\prime}_{1},\dots,S^{\prime}_{n})
be two
Bernoulli path ensembles with endpoints
(p_{1}=(x_{p},y_{p_{1}}),\dots,p_{n}=(x_{p},y_{p_{n}})),(q_{1}=(x_{q},y_{q_{1}}),\dots,q_{n}=(x_{q},y_{q_{n}}))
for
S
, and
(p^{\prime}_{1}=(x_{p},y^{\prime}_{p^{\prime}_{1}}),\dots,p^{\prime}_{n}=(x_{p},y^{\prime}_{p^{\prime}_{n}})),(q^{\prime}_{1}=(x_{q},y^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}_{1}}),\dots,q^{\prime}_{n}=(x_{q},y^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}_{n}}))
for
S^{\prime}
. Assume that
y_{p_{i}}\geq y^{\prime}_{p^{\prime}_{i}}
and
y_{q_{i}}\geq y^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}_{i}}

for every
i=1,\dots,n
. Then, we can couple
S,S^{\prime}
in such a way that, almost surely, the
S_{i}
is weakly above
S^{\prime}_{i}
for every
i=1,\dots,n
.

More generally, fix functions
V^{\pm}_{i},W^{\pm}_{i}:(x_{p},x_{q})\mapsto\mathbb{R}
such that
V_{i}^{\pm}\geq W_{i}^{\pm}
. Condition
S
to the event that
S_{i}
is above
V^{-}_{i}
and below
V^{+}_{i}
for every
i=1,\dots,n
, and condition
S^{\prime}
to the event that
S^{\prime}_{i}
is above
W^{-}_{i}
and below
W^{+}_{i}
for every
i=1,\dots,n
(we assume that
(V_{i}^{\pm},W_{i}^{\pm})_{i=1,\dots,n}
are such that these events are nonempty). Then, we can couple
S,S^{\prime}
in such a way that, almost surely, the
S_{i}
is weakly above
S^{\prime}_{i}
for every
i=1,\dots,n
.

Proposition 2.13

(
[
13
, Theorem 4.3]
)
.

Let
D\subset\mathbb{T}_{N}
be a finite, simply-connected tileable domain. For the Glauber dynamic in
D
constrained between
H_{-}
and
H_{+}
, one has

(2.5)

\displaystyle t_{\rm mix}\leq C(N{\rm diam}(D))^{2}\times(NH_{\max})^{2}(\log N)^{2},\quad H_{\max}:=\max_{z\in D}(H_{+}(z)-H_{-}(z))

for some universal constant
C
.

Note that
N{\rm diam}(D)
and
NH_{\rm max}
are the diameter of the domain and the maximal height distance
between floor and ceiling (before the rescaling by
1/N
), respectively.
As a corollary, the mixing time of the unconstrained dynamic satisfies

(2.6)

\displaystyle t_{\rm mix}^{N}(\epsilon)\lesssim CN^{4}(\log N)^{2}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}.

We need a last general result, that uses the notation in
Definition

2.9
and
Definition

2.10
:

Proposition 2.14

(
[
35
, Lemma 3.1]
)
.

Let
D
be a tilable domain with
|D|
denoting the cardinality of
D\cap\mathbb{T}_{N}
, let
H_{\pm}:D\mapsto\mathbb{R}
be as in
Definition

2.10
and

A\subset\Omega_{H_{\pm}}
. For any
fixed
T\geq 1
, the following holds for the dynamic in
D
constrained between
H_{-}
and
H_{+}
:

(2.7)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{\pi_{H_{\pm}}}(\exists t\leq T:\eta_{t}\in A)\leq 8|D|T\,\pi_{H_{\pm}}(A).

We will use this to deduce
that for the stationary process, if
\pi_{H_{\pm}}(A)
is smaller than any inverse power of
N
, then the probability that there exists a time before
N^{C}
(with
C
any fixed constant) when
A
occurs is still smaller than any inverse power of
N
.

2.2.2.
The Peres-Winkler censoring inequality

The Peres-Winkler censoring inequality
[
43
]
states that, for certain monotone Markov dynamics under certain initial data, censoring (that is, ignoring) updates slows down convergence to equilibrium.
We give in
2.17
the precise statement we need in the specific case of the lozenge tiling dynamic.

Definition 2.15

(Restricted dynamic)
.

Given the Glauber dynamic in a tilable domain
D
and a (not necessarily tilable) subset
D^{\prime}\subset D
, the Glauber dynamic
restricted to
D^{\prime}
is the one obtained by ignoring the Poisson clocks in
D\setminus D^{\prime}
.

Definition 2.16
.

Given
H_{+},H_{-}:\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, with
{H_{-}}\leq{H_{+}}
pointwise,
let
\mathcal{P}^{+}[H_{-},H_{+}]
denote the collection of probability distributions
\mu
on
\Omega_{H_{\pm}}
that are increasing with respect to
\pi_{H_{\pm}}
:

(2.8)

\displaystyle\eta\geq\eta^{\prime}\Rightarrow{\mu(\eta)}\geq\mu(\eta^{\prime}).

When
H_{+}
and
H_{-}
are the height functions of the maximal and minimal configurations in
\Omega^{N}_{a,b,c}
, respectively, we write
\mathcal{P}^{+}
instead of
\mathcal{P}^{+}[H_{-},H_{+}]
.

Examples of
\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{+}[H_{-},H_{+}]
are the uniform measure on
\Omega_{H_{\pm}}
and the Dirac measure on the maximal configuration in
\Omega_{H_{\pm}}
.

In the following proposition,
H_{\pm}
and
\mathcal{P}^{+}[H_{-},H_{+}]
are as in
Definition

2.16
.

Proposition 2.17

(
[
43
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{+}[H_{-},H_{+}],n\in\mathbb{N}
and
t_{0}=0<t_{1}<\dots<t_{n}=T
. For any integer
i\in[1,n]
, let
\Lambda_{i}\subseteq\mathfrak{X}
be a subset and
H^{\pm}_{i}:\mathfrak{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
satisfy
H^{-}_{i}\leq H^{+}_{i}
. Let
(\tilde{\eta}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be the censored Glauber dynamics restricted to
\Lambda_{i}
in the time interval
[t_{i-1},t_{i})
(as in Definition
2.15
); with ceiling constraint
\min(H_{i}^{+},H_{+})
and floor constraint
\max(H_{i}^{-},H_{-})
(as in
Definition

2.10
); and with initial condition distributed according to
\mu
. Let
\tilde{\mu}_{t}
be the law of
\tilde{\eta}_{t}
, and
\mu_{t}
be the law of the unrestricted and uncensored Glauber dynamics at time
t
, with the same initial condition
\mu
. Then, for any
t\leq T
:

(2.9)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}_{t}\in\mathcal{P}^{+}[H_{-},H_{+}];\qquad\mu_{t}\preceq\tilde{\mu}_{t};\qquad\|\mu_{t}-\pi_{H_{\pm}}\|\leq\|\tilde{\mu}_{t}-\pi_{H_{\pm}}\|.

3.
Volume-tilted measure and its limit shape

3.1.
Volume-tilted measure and limit shape

Even though the Glauber dynamic is reversible with respect to the uniform measure
\mathbb{P}^{N}_{a,b,c}
, we will frequently make use of a tilted version of this measure.
Fixing real numbers

a,b,c>0
and
q\in\mathbb{R}
, we
define the volume-tilted measure
\mathbb{P}^{N,q}_{a,b,c}
with parameter
q
in
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
as

(3.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{N,q}_{a,b,c}(\eta)\propto e^{q\sum_{z\in\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}\cap\mathbb{T}_{N}}H_{\eta}(z)},\quad\eta\in\Omega^{N}_{a,b,c}.

Lozenge tilings of
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
sampled according to
\mathbb{P}^{N,q}_{a,b,c}
exhibit the following limit shape phenomenon.
4
4

4

The parameters

(r;\mathsf{q};\mathsf{N},\mathsf{T},a_{1})
in
[
22
, Theorem 1.6]
are equal to

(1;q;b,a+c,-c)
here. Also, the limit shape in
[
22
, Theorem 1.6]
is shifted by
(0,-c)
with respect to the one here.

Theorem 3.1

(
[
22
, Theorem 1.6]
)
.

Fix
q\in\mathbb{R},a,b,c>0
. There exists a deterministic function
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}:\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
such that the height function
H_{\eta}
of a random tiling sampled from
\mathbb{P}^{N,q}_{a,b,c}
tends in probability to
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}
as
N\to\infty
:
for every
\varepsilon>0
,

(3.2)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}^{N,q}_{a,b,c}(\exists z\in\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}:|H_{\eta}(z)-\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(z)|\geq\varepsilon)=0.

Remark 3.2
.

It is quickly verified that, if
q\geq q^{\prime}
, then the measure
\mathbb{P}^{N,q}_{a,b,c}
stochastically dominates
\mathbb{P}^{N,q^{\prime}}_{a,b,c}
. Together with (
3.2
), this implies that
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}\geq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime};a,b,c}
everywhere in
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
.

We drop
q
from the notation if
q=0
and
a,b,c
if

a=b=c=1
. Thus, we may write for example

\mathcal{H}_{0;a,b,c}=\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
,

\mathcal{H}_{q;1,1,1}=\mathcal{H}_{q}
,

\mathcal{H}_{0;1,1,1}=\mathcal{H}
, and

\mathfrak{X}_{1,1,1}=\mathfrak{X}
. For
q=0
,

3.1
is due to
[
17
,
16
]
. In that
specific case, we provide a more quantitative concentration bound
in
3.17
below.

The limit shape
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}
is rather explicit, though involved (see
Section

B.1
) and exhibits a
phase separation

between liquid and frozen regions. In order to precisely define these regions, as well as the arctic boundary separating them, we start by noting that
because of
Definition

2.2
, the gradient
\nabla\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}
(wherever defined) belongs to the closure
\overline{\mathcal{T}}
of the triangle

(3.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}=\big\{(r,s)\in(-1,0)\times(0,1):0<s+r<1\}.

Definition 3.3
.

[Liquid and frozen region, arctic boundary]
Given any open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we call a function
F:\overline{\Omega}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

admissible
if
F
is
1
-Lipschitz and satisfies
\nabla F(z)\in\overline{\mathcal{T}}
for Lebesgue almost every
z\in\Omega
. Let
\Adm(\Omega)
denote the set of admissible functions
F:\overline{\Omega}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and, for any function
f:\partial\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, let
\Adm(\Omega;f)
denote the set of
F\in\Adm(\Omega)
satisfying
F|_{\partial\Omega}=f
. Define the
liquid region

\mathfrak{L}_{F}\subseteq\Omega
associated with an admissible height function
F\in\Adm(\Omega)
by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{F}=\big\{z\in\Omega:\nabla F(z)\in\mathcal{T}\big\},

where the condition
\nabla F(z)\in\mathcal{T}
in particular implies that
\nabla F(z)
exists.

In particular, we define the liquid region of
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}
as

\mathfrak{L}_{q;a,b,c}:=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}}.

The boundary

\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}:=\partial\mathfrak{L}_{q;a,b,c}
is called the

arctic boundary
and the interior of

\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}\setminus\mathfrak{L}_{q;a,b,c}
is called the
frozen
region
.

In fact, for
q=0
,
3.1
is a particular case of a general limit shape theorem (
[
16
]
; see also
[
23
, Theorem 5.15]
) that we recall here for later convenience.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
D
be a bounded open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with piecewise smooth boundary and
f:\partial D\mapsto\mathbb{R}
be such that
\Adm(D;f)\neq\emptyset.
Let
(D_{N})_{N\geq 1}
be a sequence of tilable domains of
\mathbb{T}_{N}
. We write
D_{N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\to\infty}}{{\rightharpoonup}}(D,f)
if, as
N\to\infty
,
D_{N}\to D
in Hausdorff metric, and at the same time
\lim_{N\to\infty}\max\{|h_{N}(z)-f(z)|:z\in\partial D_{N}\}=0
, where
h_{N}:\partial D_{N}\mapsto N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
is the boundary height function associated to
D_{N}
.

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
D,f
be as in
Definition

3.4
. There exists
\phi\in\Adm(D;f)
such that, for any sequence
(D_{N})_{N\geq 1}
of tilable domains such that
D_{N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\to\infty}}{{\rightharpoonup}}(D,f)
and for every
\varepsilon>0
, it holds

(3.4)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\pi_{D_{N}}(\exists z\in D_{N}:|H_{\eta}(z)-\phi(z)|>\varepsilon)=0

where
\pi_{D_{N}}
is the uniform measure on
\Omega_{D_{N}}
.

The function
\phi
is called
limit shape
(in the domain
D
, with boundary condition
f
) and it satisfies the following monotonicity property and maximum principle:

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
D
be an open bounded subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with piecewise smooth boundary, let
f_{1},f_{2}:\partial D\mapsto\mathbb{R}
be such that
\Adm(D;f_{1}),\Adm(D;f_{2})\neq\emptyset
and let
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
be the associated limit shapes. Then, it holds:

•

If
f_{1}(z)\leq f_{2}(z)
for all
z\in\partial D
, then
\phi_{1}(z)\leq\phi_{2}(z)
for all
z\in D
;

•

The maximum of
\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}
in
\bar{D}
is realized on
\partial D
.

Proof.

The first statement is proven for instance in
[
4
, Lemma 2.5]
. The second immediately follows from the first, with the fact that
\partial D
is compact and that, if two boundary heights differ by an additive constant, the limit shapes do as well.
∎

Several facts about the limit shape
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}
and arctic boundary of the volume-tilted measure are proven in
Appendix

B
. We begin with the following lemma about the arctic boundary
\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
. This statement is known, but we are not aware of a proof written anywhere, so we provide one in
Section

B.2
below.

Lemma 3.7
.

The arctic boundary
\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
is a smooth curve that is tangent to each edge of
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
at exactly one point.

In view of
3.7
, we pose the following definition.

Definition 3.8
.

Removing the six locations at which
\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
is tangent to
\partial\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
disconnects
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}\setminus\overline{\mathfrak{L}}_{q;a,b,c}
into six frozen regions
\mathfrak{F}^{i}_{q;a,b,c},i\in\{N,NE,SE,S,SW,NW\}
(see
Figure

3
).
We let
p^{i}_{q;a,b,c},i\in\{NW,NE,E,SE,SW,W\}
denote the six tangency points; see Fig
3
, and
we let
p^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}=(x^{SW}_{q;a,b,c},0)
and
p^{W}_{q;a,b,c}=(0,y^{W}_{q;a,b,c})
.

Moreover, we let
\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}^{i},i\in\{N,NE,SE,S,SW,NW\}
denote the portion of
\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
adjacent to the frozen region
\mathfrak{F}^{i}_{q;a,b,c}
.

Note that
0<x^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}<a
and
0<y^{W}_{q;a,b,c}<b
.
The following lemma determines
\nabla\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}
in each of the six frozen regions; we establish it in
Section

B.2
below.

Lemma 3.9
.

The following statements hold for any
z\in\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}\setminus\overline{\mathfrak{L}}_{q;a,b,c}
.

(1)

If
z\in\mathfrak{F}^{S}_{q;a,b,c}\cup\mathfrak{F}^{N}_{q;a,b,c}
, then
\nabla\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}=(0,0)
.

(2)

If
z\in\mathfrak{F}^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}\cup\mathfrak{F}^{NE}_{q;a,b,c}
, then
\nabla\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}=(0,1)
.

(3)

If
z\in\mathfrak{F}^{SE}_{q;a,b,c}\cup\mathfrak{F}^{NW}_{q;a,b,c}
, then
\nabla\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}=(-1,1)
.

For
q=0
, the arctic curve
\mathfrak{A}_{a,b,c}

is the ellipse inscribed in
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}

[
16
]
. It is therefore natural to expect that
\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
is convex for
q
small. This is confirmed by the next result:

Lemma 3.10
.

There exists a constant
\varepsilon>0
such that, for any
q\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
and
a,b,c\in(1-\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon)
, the arctic boundary
\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
is a smooth, convex curve, whose curvature is bounded below by a positive constant
\kappa=\kappa(a,b,c)>0
.

Figure 3.
A pictorial view of the liquid region for
q>0
, in the case
a=b=c=1
(so that we drop the indices
a,b,c
). For small enough
q
, the liquid region is convex, for large
q
it is not. In the three larger frozen regions
\mathfrak{F}^{N}_{q},\mathfrak{F}^{SW}_{q},\mathfrak{F}^{SE}_{q}
the limit shape
\mathcal{H}_{q}
coincides with the maximal height function. In the three other (smaller) frozen regions, it coincides with the minimal height function.

In order to state more refined properties of the limit shape, we need some more geometric notations.

Definition 3.11

(Directional distance)
.

For any points
z,w\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, sets
\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and vector
V\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we set
\dist_{V}(z,w)=\big|(z-w)\cdot V\big|
;
\dist_{V}(z,\mathcal{S})=\inf_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\dist_{V}(z,s)
; and
\dist_{V}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S}^{\prime})=\inf_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\inf_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}\dist_{V}(s,s^{\prime})
.

Definition 3.12

(
\mathfrak{e}_{z;q},\mathfrak{d}_{z},\mathtt{d}_{z},\mathtt{e}_{z;q}
)
.

Given
z\in\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
let
\ell_{z}
denote the side of
\partial\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
closest to
z
(if two such sides exist, we select one arbitrarily) and
\mathfrak{d}_{z}=\dist(z,\ell_{z})^{1/2}
. We let
\bm{v}=\bm{v}_{z}\in\big\{(1,0),(-1,0),(0,1),(0,-1),(1,1),(-1,-1)\big\}
denote the vector
parallel to
\ell_{z}
; as for its orientation, we orient the boundary of the hexagon anti-clockwise, and we let
\bm{v}
be oriented like the side
\ell_{z}
that defines it. We also let
\bm{v}^{\perp}=\bm{v}_{z}^{\perp}
denote the vector obtained by a
\pi/2
clockwise rotation of
\bm{v}
(so that it is in particular orthogonal to
\bm{v}
, in the
(x,y)
coordinate system). Given
q\in\mathbb{R}
, we let
\bm{w}=\bm{w}_{z;q}
denote the
unit vector pointing from
z
to the closest point of

\mathfrak{A}_{q;{a},{b},{c}}
. Finally, we let
\bm{u}=\bm{u}_{z;q}
denote the
unit pointing from
z
and tangent to

\mathfrak{A}_{q;{a},{b},{c}}
(note that it is orthogonal to
\bm{w}
). The orientation of
\bm{u}
is chosen by orienting
\mathfrak{A}_{q;{a},{b},{c}}
anti-clockwise. If the points involved in the definition of
{\bm{w}},{\bm{u}}
are not unique, then choose one arbitrarily.
5
5

5

The distinction between the directions
{\bm{u}}
and
{\bm{w}}
will be only important for points
z
close to the arctic boundary, in which case the choice is unique.

Moreover, we let (see Fig.
4
)

(3.5)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}:=\dist_{\bm{v}}(z,\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}).

If
z
is outside the liquid region, we set
\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}=0
. We further set

(3.6)

\displaystyle\mathtt{d}_{z}=\max\{\mathfrak{d}_{z},N^{-1/2}\};\qquad\mathtt{e}_{z;q}=\max\{\mathfrak{e}_{z;q},\mathtt{d}_{z}^{-1/3}N^{-2/3}\}.

Figure 4.
A point
z
in
\mathfrak{L}_{q;a,b,c}
. The distance from
z
to
\partial\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
is
\mathfrak{d}_{z}^{2}
(in this case,
\ell_{z}
is the southwest side of the hexagon). The distance from
z
to the arctic boundary
\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
in the direction parallel to
\ell_{z}
is
\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}
. The unit vector
{\bm{w}}_{z;q}
points to the closest point on the arctic boundary, and
{\bm{u}}_{z;q}
is perpendicular to it.

Remark 3.13
.

Let
q,a,b,c
satisfy the conditions of
3.10
. Elementary geometric considerations (using Lemmas
3.7
and
3.10
) show that, if
z\in\mathfrak{L}_{q;a,b,c}
, then

(3.7)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}\lesssim\mathfrak{d}_{z}

and

(3.8)

\displaystyle\dist_{\bm{w}_{z;q}}(z;\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c})\asymp\mathfrak{d}_{z}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q},\quad\dist_{\bm{u}_{z;q}}(z;\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c})\asymp(\mathfrak{d}_{z}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q})^{1/2}.

Remark 3.14
.

Let us briefly (and informally) comment on the reason for the definition (
3.6
) of
\mathtt{d}_{z}
and
\mathtt{e}_{z;q}
. Given
z\in\mathfrak{L}_{q}
, let
z^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{A}_{q}
denote the closest point to
z
. Then
|z-z^{\prime}|\leq\mathfrak{d}_{z}^{2}
so, if
\mathfrak{d}_{z}<N^{-1/2}
, then
\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)-\mathcal{H}_{q}(z^{\prime})\leq N^{-1}
. Moreover, if
\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}<\mathtt{e}_{z;q}
, then we will show (see Lemma
3.18
) that
|\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)-\mathcal{H}_{q}(z^{\prime})|\asymp\mathfrak{d}_{z}^{1/2}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}^{3/2}\lesssim N^{-1}
. Thus, the values of
\mathtt{d}_{z}
and
\mathtt{e}_{z;q}
are essentially the minimal ones to “detect” that a point
z
is in the liquid region, up to an error of
N^{-1}
in the height function
\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)
evaluated at
z
.

Definition 3.15

(Augmented liquid region)
.

Given
\delta>0
and
N\geq 1
,
define the
augmented liquid region

\mathfrak{L}^{+}_{q;a,b,c}(\delta)=\mathfrak{L}_{q;a,b,c}(\delta;N)\subset\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
by setting

(3.9)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}^{+}_{q;a,b,c}(\delta)=\mathfrak{L}_{q;a,b,c}\cup\big\{z\in\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}:\dist(z,\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c})\leq\mathfrak{d}_{z}^{2/3}N^{\delta-2/3}\big\}.

Remark 3.16
.

Observe that, if
z\in\mathfrak{L}_{q;a,b,c}^{+}(\delta)\setminus\mathfrak{L}_{q;a,b,c}
, then
\dist_{\bm{v}_{z}}(z,\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c})\lesssim\mathfrak{d}_{z}^{-1/3}N^{\delta-2/3}
.

The following result gives precise bounds on the probability that, under the measure
\mathbb{P}^{N}_{a,b,c}
(with
q=0
), the height function deviates from the limit shape. Its first part, which is due to
[
45
]
(see also
[
33
]
), shows that the height function of a uniformly random tiling of a hexagon satisfies a nearly optimal concentration estimate with error
N^{\delta-1}
; its second part shows that this height function is frozen, in that it coincides with its limit shape, outside of the augmented liquid region. The latter is similar to
[
25
, Theorem 2.6]
, though there the augmentation of the liquid region was slightly larger than the one here (namely, there was no factor of
\mathfrak{d}_{z}^{2/3}
as in (
3.9
)).
We provide a proof of
3.17
in
Section

A.1
.

Proposition 3.17
.

For any real numbers
R>1
and
\delta\in(0,1)
, the following holds w.o.p. with respect to
\mathbb{P}^{N}_{a,b,c}
. Fix real numbers
a,b,c\in[R^{-1},R]
; sample a uniformly random tiling on a hexagon
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
and denote its height function by
H:\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{a,b,c}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.

(1)

We have
\sup_{z\in\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}}\big|H(z)-\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(z)\big|\leq N^{\delta-1}
.

(2)

We have
H(z)=\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(z)
, for each
z\notin\mathfrak{L}_{a,b,c}^{+}(\delta)
.

3.2.
Properties of the limit shape and of its level lines

This section collects several results about the behavior of
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}
and of its level lines, defined below, near the arctic boundary.
As mentioned in Section
3
, these limit shapes admit an explicit, though slightly intricate, formula in terms of elementary functions (provided in Appendix
B.1
). As such, the proofs of the limit shape results in this section will amount to a fairly direct (but tedious) Taylor expansion of those formulas.

The following result, proven in
Section

C.1.4
, indicates that the derivatives of
\mathcal{H}
exhibit a square root singularity as one nears the arctic boundary (with a correction that depends on
\mathfrak{d})
. Below, we recall the notation
x^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
from
Definition

3.8
.

Lemma 3.18
.

There exists a constant
C>1
such that the following holds, whenever
q\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
and
a,b,c\in(1-\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon)
, for sufficiently small
\varepsilon\in(0,C^{-1})
. Fix
z_{0}=(x_{0},y_{0})\in\mathfrak{L}_{q;a,b,c}
, and assume that
x_{0}\geq x^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
and that
\ell_{z_{0}}
is the southwest edge of
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
.

(1)

We have that
C^{-1}(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{{z_{0}};q})^{1/2}\leq-\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}({z_{0}})\leq C(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{{z_{0}};q})^{1/2}
.

(2)

We have that
C^{-1}(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1}\mathfrak{e}_{{z_{0}};q})^{1/2}\leq\partial_{y}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}({z_{0}})\leq C(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1}\mathfrak{e}_{{z_{0}};q})^{1/2}
.

(3)

We have that
C^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}^{1/2}\mathfrak{e}_{{z_{0}};q}^{3/2}\leq\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}({z_{0}})\leq C\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}^{1/2}\mathfrak{e}_{{z_{0}};q}^{3/2}
.

(4)

Assume that
z_{0}=(x_{0},y_{0})\in\mathfrak{L}_{q;a,b,c}
and
\ell_{z_{0}}
is the southwest edge of
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
, but now
x_{0}\leq x^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
. Then
C^{-1}(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{{z_{0}};q})^{1/2}\leq-\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}({z_{0}})\leq C(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{{z_{0}};q})^{1/2}
remains valid, but now
C^{-1}\leq\partial_{y}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}({z_{0}})\leq C
and
C^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}^{2}\leq\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}({z_{0}})\leq C\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}^{2}
.

In particular, under the assumptions of
3.18
we have

(3.10)

\displaystyle C^{-1}(\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}({z_{0}}))^{1/3}\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}^{-2/3}\leq\partial_{y}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}({z_{0}})\leq C(\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}({z_{0}}))^{1/3}\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}^{-2/3}.

The conditions that
x_{0}\geq x^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
and that
\ell_{z_{0}}
is the southwest edge of
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
essentially mean that
{z_{0}}
is close to
\mathfrak{A}^{S}_{q;a,b,c}
, and closer to
p^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
than to
p^{SE}_{q;a,b,c}
.

The next two lemmas compare the limit shapes (with
a=b=c=1
) for two different values of
q
. They are shown in
Section

C.2
.

Lemma 3.19
.

There exist constants
\varepsilon>0
and
C>1
such that the following holds for any
-\varepsilon\leq q^{\prime}\leq q\leq\varepsilon
. Let
z=(x,y)\in\mathfrak{A}_{q}^{S}
and
z^{\prime}=(x^{\prime},y)\in\mathfrak{A}_{q^{\prime}}^{S}
be such that
\ell_{z}=\ell_{z^{\prime}}
is the southwest edge of
\mathfrak{X}
. Then, we have
C^{-1}(q-q^{\prime})\leq x-x^{\prime}\leq C(q-q^{\prime})
.

Remark 3.20
.

By symmetry, an analogous statement holds, if we assume that
z\in\mathfrak{A}_{q}^{\mathrm{l}}
and
z^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{A}_{q^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{l}}
for some
\mathrm{l}\in\{NW,N,NE,SE,S,SW\}
; that
\ell_{z}=\ell_{z^{\prime}}
; and that
\mathfrak{d}_{z}=\mathfrak{d}_{z^{\prime}}
, and then replace
x-x^{\prime}
by
\dist_{\ell_{z}}(z,z^{\prime})
above.

Lemma 3.21
.

There exist constants
\varepsilon>0
and
C>1
such that the following holds for any
q,q^{\prime}\in[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]
, with
q\geq q^{\prime}
, and
z\in\mathfrak{X}
. For each
\mathfrak{e}_{z}\in\{\mathfrak{e}_{z;q},\mathfrak{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}\}
, we have

(3.11)

\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)+C^{-1}(\mathfrak{d}_{z}\mathfrak{e}_{z})^{1/2}(q-q^{\prime})-C\mathfrak{d}_{z}^{1/2}(q-q^{\prime})^{3/2}\\
\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)+C(\mathfrak{d}_{z}\mathfrak{e}_{z})^{1/2}(q-q^{\prime})+C\mathfrak{d}_{z}^{1/2}(q-q^{\prime})^{3/2}.

In addition, we have

(3.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)\geq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)+C^{-1}(\mathfrak{d}_{z}\mathfrak{e}_{z})^{1/2}(q-q^{\prime}).

Next, we need a precise control on the behavior of the level lines of
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}
, as well as of the random level lines of the height function sampled from
\mathbb{P}^{N}_{a,b,c}
.
In analogy with
Definition

2.6
,
the following definition (see also Figure
5
) provides level lines
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}
,
and the function
\mathfrak{l}
describing
\mathfrak{A}^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
(recall
Definition

3.8
).

Definition 3.22

(Level lines of the macroscopic shape)
.

For any
a,b,c>0
,
q\in\mathbb{R}
and
0<h<b
, define
\mathcal{U}_{q;a,b,c}^{h}:[0,a+c]\mapsto[0,b+c]
by setting

(3.13)

\mathcal{U}_{q;a,b,c}^{h}(x)=y\in[0,b+c]:\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)=h.

If instead
h=0
or
h=b
, then given
x
there may exist multiple
y
satisfying
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)=h
. In that case, we define

(3.14)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{U}_{q;a,b,c}^{0}(x)&=\sup\{y\in[0,b+c]:\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)=0\};\\
\mathcal{U}_{q;a,b,c}^{b}(x)&=\inf\{y\in[0,b+c]:\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)=b\}.\end{aligned}

Also, define

\mathfrak{l}_{q;a,b,c}:[0,y^{W}_{q;a,b,c}]\rightarrow[0,x^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}]
by letting

\mathfrak{l}_{q;a,b,c}(y)
be the
x
coordinate of the unique point of
\mathfrak{A}^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
with vertical coordinate
y
(namely, so that
(\mathfrak{l}_{q;a,b,c},y)\in\mathfrak{A}^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
).
Note that
\mathfrak{l}_{q;a,b,c}(0)=x^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
and
\mathfrak{l}_{q;a,b,c}(y^{W}_{q;a,b,c})=0
.
See Figure
5
. As usual, if either
q=0
or
a=b=c=1
, we drop these indices from the notation.

Figure 5.
The arctic boundary (in blue) and the level line
\mathcal{U}^{h}_{q;a,b,c}
(in red). For
x
smaller than
\mathfrak{l}_{q;a,b,c}
,
\mathcal{U}^{h}_{q;a,b,c}(x)
is constant equal to
h
.

The next lemma is shown in
Section

B.2
. We refer to Figure
5
for a depiction.

Lemma 3.23
.

There exists a constant
\varepsilon>0
such that, for any
q\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
,
a,b,c\in(1-\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon)
, and
h\in[0,\varepsilon]
, we have that
\mathcal{U}_{q;a,b,c}^{h}(x)=h
, for all
x\in[0,\mathfrak{l}_{q;a,b,c}(h)]
.

An immediate consequence of
3.18
is the following:

Corollary 3.24
.

There exists a constant
C>1
such that the following holds. Fix
z=(x,y)\in\mathfrak{L}_{q;a,b,c}
, such that
x\geq x^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
and
\ell_{z}
is the southwest edge of
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
. Then, for any nonnegative
h\in[\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(z)-N^{-1},\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(z)+N^{-1}]
and
\textbf{V}\in\{\bm{v}_{z}^{\perp},\bm{w}_{z;q}\}
, we have
\dist_{\textbf{V}}(z,\mathcal{U}_{q;a,b,c}^{h})\leq C(\mathfrak{d}_{z}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}^{-1})^{1/2}N^{-1}
.

Proof of
3.24
.

This follows quickly from
3.18
.
∎

The height concentration statements of
3.17
have the following immediate consequence on concentration of the level lines.

Corollary 3.25
.

Let
H
be the height function of a uniformly random tiling of
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
, and let
\delta>0
be a small but
N
-independent constant. The following holds w.o.p.:

(1)

For every
h\in[0,b]\cap(1/N)(\mathbb{Z}+1/2)
,
\mathtt{U}^{h}_{a,b,c}
is between
\mathcal{U}^{h+N^{\delta-1}}_{a,b,c}
and
\mathcal{U}^{h-N^{\delta-1}}_{a,b,c}
.

(2)

Let
z=(x,y)\in\mathfrak{L}_{a,b,c}

and assume that
x\geq x^{SW}_{a,b,c}
and that
\ell_{z}
is the southwest edge of
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
. The distance along the direction
{\bm{w}}_{z}
between
z
and

\mathtt{U}^{h}_{a,b,c}
is at most
\mathtt{d}_{z}^{2/3}h^{-1/3}N^{-1+\delta}
, with
h=\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(z)
.

Proof.

The first statement of the corollary is a consequence of
3.17
. Indeed, any point
z
above
\mathcal{U}^{h+N^{\delta-1}}_{a,b,c}
and below
\mathtt{U}^{h}_{a,b,c}
by definition satisfies
\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(z)\geq h+N^{\delta-1}
and
H(z)\leq h
. Thus, for such a point
z
we have
|H(z)-\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}|\geq N^{\delta-1}
. Since this w.o.p. happens nowhere in
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
, by the first item in
3.17
, no such point
z
exists, meaning that
\mathtt{U}_{a,b,c}^{h}
cannot go above
\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{h+N^{\delta-1}}
. Similarly,
\mathtt{U}^{h}_{a,b,c}
cannot go below
\mathcal{U}^{h-N^{\delta-1}}_{a,b,c}
.

To show the second, observe from
3.18
that
h\asymp\mathfrak{d}_{z}^{1/2}\mathfrak{e}_{z}^{3/2}
, so
\mathtt{d}_{z}^{2/3}h^{-1/3}N^{-1+\delta}\gg(\mathfrak{d}_{z}/\mathfrak{e}_{z})^{1/2}N^{\delta/2-1}.
By definition,
z
lies on the curve
\mathcal{U}^{h}_{a,b,c}
. Since
3.24
implies that the event
\{\dist_{\bm{w}_{z}}(z,\mathtt{U}_{a,b,c}^{h})\geq(\mathfrak{d}_{z}\mathfrak{e}_{z}^{-1})^{1/2}N^{\delta/2-1}\}

implies the event that
\mathtt{U}^{h}_{a,b,c}
is not
between
\mathcal{U}^{h+N^{-1+\delta/4}}_{a,b,c}
and
\mathcal{U}^{h-N^{-1+\delta/4}}_{a,b,c}
, one concludes using the first statement of the corollary (with that
\delta
replaced by
\delta/4
). ∎

The next proposition (shown in
Section

C.3
) will be
used to compare the level lines of
\mathcal{H}_{q}
and of

\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
. Equation (
3.19
) indicates the
level lines of the
q
-deformed limit shape are “more
convex” than those of the non-deformed limit shape, upon
approximately matching (after a shift in the hexagon, given
by
\mathfrak{r}
and
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
in the
x
and
y

coordinates, respectively) their gradients through
(
3.17
) and (
3.18
); (
3.21
)
allows a specific inequality between these derivatives. See
Figure

6
. A heuristic explanation for this increased convexity is as follows. Suppose one fixes the values of a level line at two
x
-coordinates,
x^{-}
and
x^{+}
. Then increasing
q
would, in order to maximize the volume of the associated height function, cause this level line to extend further to the right in the interval
(x^{-},x^{+})
, thereby increasing its convexity.

Simultaneously enabling
an approximate match between these gradients but a gain in
convexity when comparing these limit shapes can require a
slight change in the dimensions of the hexagon, from
1\times 1\times 1
for the
q
-deformed limit shape to some
a\times b\times c
for the non-deformed one. This gain (
3.19
)
in convexity is not imposed too close to the left edge of
the arctic boundary, where we instead only require that the
level lines of
\mathcal{H}_{q}
are above those of

\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
, by (
3.20
). The estimate (
3.23
) lower bounds the height function
\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
in terms of
\mathcal{H}_{q}
(observe that this corresponds to upper bounding the level lines
\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}
of
\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
in terms of the
\mathcal{U}_{q}
, which the estimates (
3.19
) and (
3.20
) do for the
h_{0}
-level line).

Proposition 3.26
.

There exist constants
\varepsilon>0
and
C>1
such that the following holds for any
q\in[0,\varepsilon]
. Let
h_{0}\in(0,\varepsilon]
and
0\leq s_{-}\leq s_{0}\leq s_{+}
be real numbers such that

(3.15)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{l}_{q}(0)-\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})\leq s_{0}\leq s_{+}\leq 1+\varepsilon-\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})

(3.16)

\displaystyle 0\leq s_{+}-s_{-}\leq C^{-1}s_{0}^{1/2}.

Then there exist real numbers

a,b,c\in[1-C\varepsilon,1+C\varepsilon]
and
\mathfrak{r}
such that

(3.17)

\displaystyle\partial_{s}\mathcal{U}^{h_{0}}_{a,b,c}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s_{0}+\mathfrak{r})=\partial_{s}\mathcal{U}^{h_{0}}_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s_{0}),

and, defining
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
via

(3.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{h_{0}}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s_{0}+\mathfrak{r})+\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}=\mathcal{U}_{q}^{h_{0}}(s_{0}+\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})),

the following statements hold.

(1)

For all
s\in[\max(s_{-},C^{-1}h_{0}^{1/2}),s_{+}]
, we have

(3.19)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{U}_{q}^{h_{0}}&(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s)-\mathcal{U}_{q}^{h_{0}}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s_{0})\\
&\geq\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{h_{0}}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s+\mathfrak{r})-\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{h_{0}}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s_{0}+\mathfrak{r})+C^{-1}qs_{0}|s-s_{0}|^{2}.\end{aligned}

(2)

For all
s\in[s_{-},C^{-1}h_{0}^{1/2}]
, we have

(3.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{q}^{h_{0}}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s)\geq\mathcal{U}^{h_{0}}_{a,b,c}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s+\mathfrak{r})+\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}.

(3)

For all
h\in[0,h_{0}]
, we have

(3.21)

\mathcal{U}_{q}^{h_{0}}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s_{0})-\mathcal{U}_{q}^{h}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s_{0})\geq\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{h_{0}}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s_{0}+\mathfrak{r})-\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{h}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s_{0}+\mathfrak{r}).

(4)

For all
s\in[C^{-1}h_{0}^{1/2},s_{+}]
and
h\in[0,h_{0}]
, we have

(3.22)

\big|\big(\mathcal{U}_{q}^{h_{0}}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s)-\mathcal{U}_{q}^{h}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s)\big)-\big(\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{h_{0}}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s+\mathfrak{r})-\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{h}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s+\mathfrak{r})\big)\big|\leq Cqs_{+}^{5/3}h_{0}^{2/3}.

(5)

For any point
z=(x,y)
with
x-\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})\in[s_{-},s_{+}]
and
\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)\leq h_{0}
, we have that

(3.23)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(x+\mathfrak{r},y+\mathfrak{r}^{\prime})-\mathcal{H}_{q}(x,y)\geq-Cq\mathfrak{d}_{z}h_{0}.

Figure 6.
A schematic illustration of
3.26
. The figure focuses on the south corners of two hexagons (of side-lengths
(1,1,1)
and
(a,b,c)
respectively, and translated by
(\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime})
one with respect to the other). The thick lines in blue/light blue are the corresponding arctic curves, while the thick lines in red/pink are the level lines at the same height
h_{0}
of the limit shapes
\mathcal{H}_{q},\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
. If
a,b,c,\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
are chosen as in
3.26
, the level line
\mathcal{U}^{h_{0}}_{q}
is tangent to
\mathcal{U}^{h_{0}}_{a,b,c}+(\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime})
, the former is above the latter and the curvature of the former is
qs_{0}
larger than that of the latter.

3.3.
Rescaled limit shape

In this section, we state a result that compares the macroscopic shapes
\mathcal{H}_{q}
and
\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
, up to a suitable rescaling. As in
Section

3.2
, the proofs of the statements in this section are based on an elementary Taylor expansion of the formulas for these limit shapes. First, while
3.18
suggests that second derivatives of
\mathcal{H}
diverge in the (inverse of the) distance to the arctic boundary, the following proposition (see also
Figure

7
) provides a rescaling of
\mathcal{H}
that remains smooth.

Figure 7.
In the definition (
3.24
) of the rescaled limit shape,
the coordinates around a point
z_{0}
are rescaled differently in direction
{\bm{u}}_{z_{0};q}
and
{\bm{w}}_{z_{0};q}
. The rectangular region centered at
z_{0}
and with sides parallel to
{\bm{u}}_{z_{0};q},{\bm{w}}_{z_{0};q}
is the cell
\mathfrak{Y}
(
Definition

6.2
, with
w_{0}
there equal to
z_{0}
here).

Proposition 3.27
.

The following holds for
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small. Fix
{a},{b},{c}\in(1-\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon)
,
q\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
and a point
z_{0}=(x_{0},y_{0})\in{\mathfrak{L}}_{q;a,b,c}
,
and assume that
x_{0}\geq x^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
and that
\ell_{z_{0}}
is the southwest edge of
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
.
Define the rescaled limit shape as

(3.24)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q;a,b,c}(\hat{z})=\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/2}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}^{-3/2}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(z_{0}+(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q})^{1/2}{\bm{u}}_{z_{0};q}\hat{x}+(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}){\bm{w}}_{z_{0};q}\hat{y}),\quad\hat{z}=(\hat{x},\hat{y}).

For every
B>1
there exists a constant
C>1
(depending only on
B
) such that for any differential operator
\partial_{\gamma}
(recall the notation in
Section

1.3.1
) with
|\gamma|\in[1,B]
and any
\hat{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
|\hat{z}|\leq C^{-1}
, we have
|\partial_{\gamma}\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q;{a},{b},{c}}(\hat{z})|\leq C
.

Note that the condition
|\hat{z}|\leq C^{-1}
, together with
3.13
, guarantees that the argument
z
of
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}
in (
3.24
) is within
\mathfrak{L}_{q;a,b,c}
(and that
\dist_{\bm{u}_{z_{0};q}}(z,\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c})\asymp(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q})^{1/2}
and
\dist_{\bm{w}_{z_{0};q}}(z,\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c})\asymp\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}
for such
\hat{z}
).

The next proposition will enable us to compare the rescaled limit shapes associated with
\mathcal{H}_{q}
and
\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
. Through (
3.26
), it essentially states (the plausible notion) that, fixing their gradients, the latter is “more concave” than the former, when
q\geq 0
.
As in
3.26
, simultaneously matching the gradients but gaining in concavity when comparing these limit shapes can require slightly changing the dimensions of the hexagon, from
1\times 1\times 1
for the
q
-deformed limit shape to some
a\times b\times c
for the non-deformed one. A similar notion (in the case when
q=0
and
\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}},\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}\asymp 1)

was witnessed and used in
[
35
, Section 7]
.

Proposition 3.28
.

There exist constants
\varepsilon>0
and
C>1

such that the following holds whenever
q\in[0,\varepsilon]
. Fix a point
z_{0}\in{\mathfrak{L}}_{q}
, and define
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}=\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q;1,1,1}
as in (
3.24
).
There exist
a,b,c\in[1-Cq,1+Cq]
and
z_{1}\in\mathfrak{L}_{{a},{b},{c}}
such that, defining

(3.25)

\displaystyle\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}_{a,b,c}(\hat{z})=\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/2}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}^{-3/2}\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(z_{1}+(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q})^{1/2}{\bm{u}}_{z_{0};q}\hat{x}+(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}){\bm{w}}_{z_{0};q}\hat{y}),\quad\hat{z}=(\hat{x},\hat{y}),

we have

(3.26)

\nabla\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}(0,0)=\nabla\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}_{a,b,c}(0,0),\qquad D^{2}\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}_{a,b,c}(0,0)-D^{2}\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}(0,0)\geq C^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}q\cdot\Id.

Moreover, for every
B\geq 0
there exists
C^{\prime}>1
such that
for any
\gamma
with
1\leq|\gamma|\leq B
we have

\displaystyle\big|\partial_{\gamma}\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}(0,0)-\partial_{\gamma}\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}_{a,b,c}(0,0)\big|\leq C^{\prime}\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}q.

Note that, in the definition of
\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}_{a,b,c}
, the quantities
\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}},\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}
are intentionally computed at
z_{0}
and not at
z_{1}
. The reason is that, otherwise, the equality
\nabla\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}(0,0)=\nabla\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}_{a,b,c}(0,0)
would not imply equality
\nabla\mathcal{H}_{q}(z_{0})=\nabla\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(z_{1})
of the gradients of the unrescaled functions.

4.
Proof of Theorem
1.1
: main steps

4.1.
Proof of Theorem
1.1

We aim at proving
t_{\rm mix}^{N}(1/4)\leq C(\delta)N^{2+\delta}
for every
\delta>0
.
Let

t^{q,N}_{\mix}(\epsilon)
be the mixing time (cf. (
2.3
)) of the Glauber dynamic in
\mathfrak{X}
,
constrained between floor
\mathcal{H}_{{-q}}
and ceiling

\mathcal{H}_{q}
, in the sense of
Definition

2.10
.
The first step, shown in Section
4.3
, is to prove:

Theorem 4.1
.

For any real numbers
q_{0}>0,\delta>0
, there exist constants
r=r(q_{0})\in(0,1)
and
\mathfrak{C}=\mathfrak{C}(q_{0},\delta)>1
such that the following holds.
Define

i_{max}=\left\lfloor\frac{9}{10}\frac{\log N}{\log(1/(1-r))}\right\rfloor

and
the sequence

N_{i}=\lfloor N(1-r)^{i}\rfloor,\quad 0\leq i\leq i_{max}

that
decreases from
N_{0}=N
to
N_{i_{max}}\asymp N^{1/10}
. Define also
the sequence

\epsilon_{i}=10^{-i}N^{-100},\quad 0\leq i\leq i_{max}.

For every
i\leq i_{max}-1
, we have

(4.1)

\displaystyle t_{\rm mix}^{N_{i}}(\epsilon_{i})\leq\mathfrak{C}{N_{i}}^{2+\delta}+t_{\rm mix}^{N_{i+1}}(\epsilon_{i+1})+t^{q_{0},N_{i}}_{\rm mix}\left(\frac{\epsilon_{i}}{4}\right).

Remark 4.2
.

Note that
\epsilon_{i}

decreases from
\epsilon_{0}=N^{-100}
to

\epsilon_{i_{max}}=N^{-L}
for some
L=L(q_{0})>100
.

For the following result, for
i\leq i_{max}
and
t>0
, let
H^{N_{i}}_{t}
denote
the height at time
t
of the tiling evolved according to the Glauber dynamic in
\mathfrak{X}
, with
N
equal
N_{i}
.
The main estimate leading to Theorem
4.1
, shown in Section

4.2.2
, is:

Theorem 4.3
.

Adopt the notation of
4.1
. For every
i\leq i_{max}-1
and for every initial condition, the following holds off of an event of probability at most
N^{-2L}+2\epsilon_{i+1}
(with
L
the same constant as in
4.2
):
for all times
t
in the interval

(4.2)

\displaystyle I^{N}_{i}=[\mathfrak{C}N_{i}^{2+\delta}+t_{\rm mix}^{N_{i+1}}(\epsilon_{i+1}),N^{5}],

we have that
\mathcal{H}_{-q_{0}}(z)\leq H^{N_{i}}_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}(z)
for all
z\in\mathfrak{X}
.

In
4.3
and in the following,
N^{5}
could have been replaced by any power of
N
with exponent larger than
2
, since we want this time to be much larger than our target for the mixing time of the Glauber dynamic.
The next step, shown in
Section

5.3
, is:

Theorem 4.4
.

There exists
\varepsilon>0
so that the following holds. For any real numbers
q_{0}\in(0,\varepsilon)
,
\delta>0
, and
\mathsf{L}>1
, there is a constant
C>1
such that

(4.3)

\displaystyle t^{q_{0},N}_{\rm mix}(N^{-\mathsf{L}})\leq CN^{2+\delta}.

Putting together the two results, we immediately have Theorem
1.1
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
, assuming Theorems
4.1
and
4.4
.

For
N_{i_{max}}\asymp N^{1/10}
we know from (
2.6
) that

(4.4)

\displaystyle t_{\rm mix}^{N_{i_{max}}}(\epsilon_{i_{max}})\lesssim(N_{i_{max}})^{4}(\log N_{i_{max}})^{2}|\log(\epsilon_{i_{max}})|\lesssim\sqrt{N}\leq N.

On the other hand, thanks to
4.2
there exists a constant
L=L(q_{0})>0
such that
\epsilon_{i}/4\geq N_{i}^{-L}
. Hence,

t^{q_{0},N_{i}}_{\rm mix}\left(\frac{\epsilon_{i}}{4}\right)\leq t^{q_{0},N_{i}}_{\rm mix}(N_{i}^{-L})\leq CN_{i}^{2+\delta/2}

where the second inequality holds by Theorem
4.4
. Then, (
4.1
) yields

t_{\rm mix}^{N_{i}}(\epsilon_{i})\leq(C+\mathfrak{C})N_{i}^{2+\delta/2}+t_{\rm mix}^{N_{i+1}}(\epsilon_{i+1}).

Solving the iteration and using the final condition (
4.4
), we get the desired bound

\displaystyle t_{\rm mix}^{N}(1/4)=t_{\rm mix}^{N_{0}}(1/4)\leq t_{\rm mix}^{N_{0}}(\epsilon_{0})\lesssim N^{2+\delta}+N\lesssim N^{2+\delta},

thereby establishing the theorem.
∎

The rest of the proof is organized as follows. In
Section

4.2
we will prove Theorem
4.3
. In Section

4.3
we show how this implies Theorem

4.1
. Finally, the proof
of Theorem
4.4
is contained in Sections

5
.

4.2.
Proof of Theorem
4.3

4.2.1.
Perturbing the boundary condition

The first step is to prove that, for any
\delta,\omega>0
, after time
of order
N^{2+\delta}
, the height function is w.o.p. within
\omega
from the macroscopic height. If the limit
shape had no frozen regions, this would follow from the main result in

[
33
]
. In our case this assumption fails, but the problem can
be resolved by a suitable perturbation of the boundary height. Let

f:\partial\mathfrak{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
denote the restriction to
\partial\mathfrak{X}
of the height
function of any lozenge tiling of
\mathfrak{X}
. Note that
f
is affine
along each side of the hexagon.

Proposition 4.5
.

Assume that there exists a sequence of boundary height functions
f^{(n)}:\partial\mathfrak{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, for each
n\geq 1
, such that
\Adm(\mathfrak{X};f^{(n)})
is not empty (recall
Definition

3.3
) and

(A)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{z\in\partial\mathfrak{X}}|f^{(n)}(z)-f(z)|=0

(B)

the limit shape
\mathcal{H}^{(n)}
in
\mathfrak{X}
with boundary condition
f^{(n)}
(cf.
3.5
)
has the property that
\nabla\mathcal{H}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{T}
and
\dist(\nabla\mathcal{H}^{(n)},\partial\mathcal{T})\geq 1/(2n)
hold almost everywhere in
\mathfrak{X}
, where
\mathcal{T}
was defined in (
3.3
).

Then, for the Glauber dynamic in
\mathfrak{X}
, for every
\delta,L,\omega>0
there exists
C<\infty
such that, for every
N
,

(4.5)

\displaystyle\max_{\eta\in\Omega^{N}}\mathbf{P}_{\eta}\Big(\exists t:CN^{2+\delta}\leq t\leq N^{L},z\in\mathfrak{X},|H_{t}(z)-\mathcal{H}(z)|\geq\omega\Big)\leq CN^{-L}.

Proof.

Let

\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}
denote the hexagon with the same center as
\mathfrak{X}
and with side-lengths
1-\omega/4
instead of
1
, and let
\psi^{(n)}
be the restriction of
\mathcal{H}^{(n)}
to
\partial\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}
. Let
(D_{N})_{N\geq 1}
be a sequence of tilable domains of
\mathbb{T}_{N}
such that
D\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\to\infty}}{{\rightharpoonup}}(\mathfrak{X}^{\prime},\psi^{(n)})
(cf.
Definition

3.4
). Observe that the limit shape in
\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}
with boundary height
\psi^{(n)}
is just the restriction of
\mathcal{H}^{(n)}
to
\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}
.

Consider now the Glauber dynamic
(\eta^{\prime}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
in
D_{N}
. Thanks to assumption (B) above about the gradient of
\mathcal{H}^{(n)}
, and since
\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}

is at positive distance
\omega/4
from
\partial\mathfrak{X}
,
it follows from
[
35
, Theorem 2.5]
that its mixing time is

O(N^{2+\delta^{\prime}})
for every
\delta^{\prime}>0
. Using the equilibrium height
fluctuation statement of
[
35
, Theorem 3.7 and Corollary 3.8]
, and the
submultiplicativity property (
2.4
), together with

2.14
, we conclude that, if
\delta>\delta^{\prime}
then, w.o.p.,

(4.6)

\displaystyle H_{\eta^{\prime}_{t}}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}^{(n)}(z)+\omega/4\quad\forall t\in[N^{2+\delta},N^{L}],\quad\forall z\in D_{N},

for any choice of the initial condition
\eta^{\prime}_{0}\in\Omega_{D_{N}}
.

Choose
n
large
enough so that

\sup\{|f^{(n)}(z)-f(z)|:z\in\partial\mathfrak{X}\}\leq\omega/4
, which is possible by assumption (A) above. By the first item of
3.6
, this implies that

|\mathcal{H}^{(n)}-\mathcal{H}|\leq\omega/4
everywhere in

\mathfrak{X}
. Note that, since the height is
1
-Lipschitz, the
maximal height function in the hexagon
\mathfrak{X}
, restricted to

\partial\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}
, is smaller than

\mathcal{H}^{(n)}|_{\partial\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}}+\omega/2
. Then, it follows from (
4.6
) together with
2.11
that the following holds w.o.p., for the
Glauber dynamic
(\eta_{t})_{t\geq 0}
in
\mathfrak{X}
: for any initial condition
\eta_{0}\in\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}}
,

(4.7)
