\displaystyle H_{t}(z):=H_{\eta_{t}}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}(z)+\omega,\quad\forall t\in[N^{2+\delta},N^{L}],\quad\forall z\in D_{N}.

On the other
hand, in
\mathfrak{X}\setminus D_{N}
,
H_{t}
is
deterministically smaller than
\mathcal{H}+\omega/2
, again because
it is
1
-Lipschitz and equals
\mathcal{H}
on
\partial\mathfrak{X}
(and
\dist(\partial\mathfrak{X},\partial\mathfrak{X}^{\prime})\leq\omega/2
). Altogether,
H_{t}
is w.o.p. smaller than
\mathcal{H}+\omega
everywhere in

\mathfrak{X}
, in the whole time interval and for any initial
condition. The statement that the height is larger than

\mathcal{H}-\omega
is proven analogously.
∎

The next important observation is that such sequence

\{f^{(n)}\}_{n\geqslant 1}
indeed exists:

Proposition 4.6
.

There exists a sequence of boundary height functions
\{f^{(n)}\}_{n\geq 1}
on
\partial\mathfrak{X}
that satisfies conditions (A) and (B) of Proposition
4.5
.

Proof.

We show how a small perturbation of the
hexagon boundary height function leads to its limit shape having no
frozen regions. We define

\phi^{(n)}:\mathfrak{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
as

\phi^{(n)}(x,y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}y\big(1-\frac{1}{n}\big)-\frac{x}{n}&\text{if}&0\leqslant x\leqslant 1,0\leq y\leq 1\\
&&\\
-x\big(1-\frac{2}{n}\big)+y\big(1-\frac{1}{n}\big)+\big(1-\frac{3}{n}\big)&\text{if}&1\leqslant x\leqslant 2,y\leqslant x\\
&&\\
\frac{2y}{n}-\frac{x}{n}+\big(1-\frac{3}{n})&\text{if}&1\leqslant y\leqslant 2,y\geqslant x\end{array}\right.

and
f^{(n)}:\partial\mathfrak{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
as the restriction of
\phi^{(n)}
to
\partial\mathfrak{X}
. Note that
f^{(n)}
is an admissible boundary height, that is,
\Adm(\mathfrak{X};f^{(n)})
in
Definition

3.3
is nonempty, since the gradient of
\phi^{(n)}
belongs to
\overline{\mathcal{T}}
(for sufficiently large
n
) wherever defined.
Moreover,
\phi^{(n)}
clearly satisfies condition (A) of Proposition
4.5
.

Call
\mathcal{H}^{(n)}
the limit shape associated to
f^{(n)}
. To prove that
f^{(n)}
also satisfies condition (B), we need to show that for every
r>0
one has

(4.8)

{r/n}\leqslant\mathcal{H}^{(n)}(x,y+r)-\mathcal{H}^{(n)}(x,y)\leqslant(1-1/n)r,\\
{r/n}\leqslant\mathcal{H}^{(n)}(x-r,y)-\mathcal{H}^{(n)}(x,y)\leqslant(1-1/n)r\\
{r/n}\leqslant\mathcal{H}^{(n)}(x+r,y+r)-\mathcal{H}^{(n)}(x,y)\leqslant(1-1/n)r.

whenever the argument of
\mathcal{H}^{(n)}
belongs to
\mathfrak{X}
.
We only discuss the proof of the upper bound in the first line, since those of the others are
entirely analogous.

Fix
r>0
, and let
(x,y)\in\mathfrak{X}
be such that

\mathcal{H}^{(n)}(x,y+r)-\mathcal{H}^{(n)}(x,y)
is
maximal. We first claim that either
(x,y+r)

or
(x,y)
can be assumed to be on the boundary of the hexagon

\mathfrak{X}
. Indeed, note that both
u(\cdot):=\mathcal{H}^{(n)}(\cdot)

and
v(\cdot):=\mathcal{H}^{(n)}(\cdot+(0,r))
are limit shapes of the tiling model in
\mathfrak{X}
and
\mathfrak{X}-(0,r)
, respectively. In
particular, they are both minimizers in
\mathfrak{X}\cap(\mathfrak{X}-(0,r))
(with
different boundary data). Let
(x,y),(x,y+r)\in\mathfrak{X}
, so that

(x,y)\in\mathfrak{X}\cap(\mathfrak{X}-(0,r))
. Then,

(4.9)

\mathcal{H}^{(n)}(x,y+r)-\mathcal{H}^{(n)}(x,y)=v(x,y)-u(x,y)\leq v(z_{1},z_{2})-u(z_{1},z_{2})\\
=\mathcal{H}^{(n)}(z_{1},z_{2}+r)-\mathcal{H}^{(n)}(z_{1},z_{2})

for some
(z_{1},z_{2})\in\partial\left(\mathfrak{X}\cap(\mathfrak{X}-(0,r))\right)
, by the second item of
3.6
. That is, either
(z_{1},z_{2})
or
(z_{1},z_{2}+r)
belongs to
\partial\mathfrak{X}
and the claim follows.

By symmetry, to prove the upper bound in the first of (
4.8
) we may restrict to the case when
(x,y)
is on
either the SW or SE side of
\mathfrak{X}
(it could be also
on a vertical side, in which case the bound

\mathcal{H}^{(n)}(x,y+r)-\mathcal{H}^{(n)}(x,y)=\phi^{(n)}(x,y+r)-\phi^{(n)}(x,y)\leqslant(1-1/n)r

holds with equality); let us assume it is on the SE side, as
the proof is very similar if it is on the SW one. Let
G
be
the affine function on
\mathfrak{X}
of slope

(-1+2/n,1-1/n)
, such that

G(x,y)=\mathcal{H}^{(n)}(x,y)
. It is quickly verified
that
G\geq f^{(n)}
on

\partial\mathfrak{X}
, with equality on the whole SE and E sides.
From
3.6
, it follows that

G\geq\mathcal{H}^{(n)}
holds also in the interior of

\mathfrak{X}
. In particular,

\mathcal{H}^{(n)}(x,y+r)-\mathcal{H}^{(n)}(x,y)=\mathcal{H}^{(n)}(x,y+r)-G(x,y)\leq G(x,y+r)-G(x,y)=(1-1/n)r,

from which the above upper bound follows.
∎

4.2.2.
Reducing to
\mathcal{H}_{-q_{0}}\leq H_{t}\leq\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}
with
q_{0}
small

In this section we prove Theorem
4.3
. Throughout we will implicitly assume that
q_{0}
is sufficiently small (so that all of the results in
Section

3
hold for
\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}
). This is allowed, since the statement we want to prove is weaker for larger
q_{0}
.
Recall that we are considering the Glauber dynamic in
\mathfrak{X}
, with the original
N
equal to
N_{i}
(so that the tile size is
1/N_{i}
) for
i\leq i_{max}
, so that
N_{i}\in[N^{1/10},N]
. The statements of Proposition
4.7
, Proposition
4.8
and Lemma
4.11
below refer to that situation, even if not explicitly stated. As before,
H_{t}^{N_{i}}
denotes the height function of the configuration at time
t
; we abbreviate
H_{t}^{N_{i}}=H_{t}
throughout this section.

By symmetry and monotonicity of the Glauber dynamics, it is enough to start from the maximal configuration
\eta^{\wedge}
(see
Definition

2.4
) and to prove that
H_{t}\leq\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}
off of an event of probability at most

(4.10)

\xi^{N}_{i}:=\frac{1}{2}N^{-2L}+3\epsilon_{i+1},

for all times in the interval
I^{N}_{i}
defined in (
4.2
). Fix

\omega>0
. Due to Proposition
4.5
and Proposition

4.6
, for

t\in I^{N}_{i}
,
the height function
H_{t}
is within
\omega
from

\mathcal{H}
, except with probability

(1/4)N^{-2L}
. Note that
H_{t}\leq\mathcal{H}+\omega
does not imply that
H_{t}\leq\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}

everywhere in
\mathfrak{X}
, even for small
\omega
and large
q_{0}
(since
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}
in the regions where both of these height functions are frozen).

Recall that the frozen region of
\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}
is composed of
six connected regions labeled
\mathfrak{F}^{i}_{q_{0}},i\in\{N,NE,SE,S,SW,NW\}
.
In
\mathfrak{F}^{N}_{q_{0}},\mathfrak{F}^{SW}_{q_{0}},\mathfrak{F}^{SE}_{q_{0}}
, the inequality

H_{t}\leq\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}
holds since
\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}
is
maximal there by
3.9
. Call
\mathfrak{T}_{q_{0}}=\mathfrak{X}\setminus(\mathfrak{F}^{N}_{q_{0}}\cup\mathfrak{F}^{SE}_{q_{0}}\cup\mathfrak{F}^{SW}_{q_{0}})
(see
Figure

8
): it includes the liquid region
\mathfrak{L}_{q_{0}}
of
\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}
and
the three frozen
\mathfrak{F}^{S}_{q_{0}},\mathfrak{F}^{NE}_{q_{0}},\mathfrak{F}^{NW}_{q_{0}}
, where
\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}
is minimal
instead (also by
3.9
). For
j\in\{S,NE,NW\}
, let
\mathfrak{B}^{j}_{q_{0}}=\{z\in\mathfrak{X}:\dist(z,\mathfrak{F}^{j}_{q_{0}})<c_{\omega}\}
denote the
c_{\omega}
-neighborhood of

\mathfrak{F}^{j}_{q_{0}}
, for some
c_{\omega}>0
to be chosen later. We first claim:

Proposition 4.7
.

There exists a real number
\delta_{0}=\delta_{0}(q_{0})>0
such that the following holds. For
\omega<\delta_{0}
, there exists a constant
c_{\omega}>0
such that
\lim_{\omega\rightarrow 0}c_{\omega}=0
and the inequality

\mathcal{H}+\omega\leq\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}},\qquad\text{holds on $\mathfrak{T}_{q_{0}}\setminus(\cup_{j\in\{S,NE,NW\}}\mathfrak{B}^{j}_{q_{0}})$}.

In particular, off of an event of probability at most
(1/4)N^{-2L}
, we have that
H_{t}\leq\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}
in
\mathfrak{T}_{q_{0}}\setminus(\cup_{j\in\{S,NE,NW\}}\mathfrak{B}^{j}_{q_{0}})
, for every
t\in I^{N}_{i}
(recall (
4.2
)).

The next result deals with the three regions
\mathfrak{B}^{j}_{q_{0}},j\in\{S,NE,NW\}
.

Proposition 4.8
.

Off of an event of probability at most
\epsilon_{i+1}+N^{-2L}/12
, we have that
H_{t}\leq\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}
on
\cup_{j\in\{S,NE,NW\}}\mathfrak{B}^{j}_{q_{0}}
, for each
t\in I_{i}^{N}
(recall (
4.2
)).

Proof of Theorem
4.3
.

By Propositions
4.7
and
4.8
, with a union bound, we deduce that
H_{t}^{N_{i}}(z)=H_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}(z)
for all
z\in\mathfrak{X}
and
t\in I_{i}^{N}
, with probability at least
1-(N^{-2L}/3+\epsilon_{i+1})
. By symmetry (or entirely analogous reasoning), we also have
H_{t}^{N_{i}}(z)\geq\mathcal{H}_{-q_{0}}(z)
for all such
(z,t)
, with the same probability. Thus, the theorem follows from a union bound.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.7
.

Throughout this proof, we refer to Figure
8
and use
the notations depicted there. Since
q_{0}>0
, we have by

3.2
that

\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}\geq\mathcal{H}
everywhere on
\mathfrak{X}
;
by
3.21
that

\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}>\mathcal{H}
on

\mathfrak{L}_{q_{0}}\setminus(\partial\mathfrak{F}^{NE}_{q_{0}}\cup\partial\mathfrak{F}^{NW}_{q_{0}}\cup\partial\mathfrak{F}^{S}_{q_{0}})
;
and by
3.9
that

\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}=\mathcal{H}
on

\partial\mathfrak{F}^{NE}_{q_{0}}\cup\partial\mathfrak{F}^{NW}_{q_{0}}\cup\partial\mathfrak{F}^{S}_{q_{0}}
.
By continuity of
z\mapsto\mathcal{H}(z)
and

z\mapsto\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}(z)
,
it follows that for sufficiently small

\omega>0
there exists a constant
c_{\omega}>0
(which we can
take to satisfy
\lim_{\omega\rightarrow 0}c_{\omega}=0
) for
which
\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}\geq\mathcal{H}+\omega
holds on

\mathfrak{L}_{q_{0}}\setminus(\cup_{j=S,NE,NW}\mathfrak{B}^{j}_{q_{0}})=\mathfrak{T}_{q_{0}}\setminus(\cup_{j=S,NE,NW}\mathfrak{B}^{j}_{q_{0}})
. This shows the first statement of the proposition; the second follows from the previously mentioned fact that
H_{t}\leq\mathcal{H}+\omega
with probability at least
1-N^{-2L}/4
.

Figure 8.
The arctic curve of
\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}
(in red) enclosing the liquid region
\mathfrak{L}_{q_{0}}
and the arctic curve of
\mathcal{H}
(in blue), enclosing the liquid region
\mathfrak{L}
. The shaded region is
\mathfrak{T}_{q_{0}}

∎

Proof of Proposition
4.8
.

By symmetry, it suffices to prove the statement in
\mathfrak{B}^{S}_{q_{0}}
, namely, that
H_{t}(z)=0
for
z\in\mathfrak{B}_{q_{0}}^{S}
and
t\in I_{i}^{N}
. Let us briefly outline how we will proceed. We will first embed a hexagon
\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
of side length
\rho\approx 1
within
\mathfrak{X}
and use monotonicity to bound the Glauber dynamics
(H_{t})
in
\mathfrak{X}
from above by those
(G_{t})
in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
, in a region containing
\mathfrak{B}_{q_{0}}^{S}
. After time
t\geq t_{\mix}^{N_{i+1}}
, the latter dynamics will have mixed. By the second part of Lemma
3.17
, we will have w.o.p. that
G_{t}=0
in the associated south frozen region, which we will see contains
\mathfrak{B}_{S}^{q_{0}}
. Hence, monotonicity will yield w.o.p. that
H_{t}=0
on
\mathfrak{B}_{S}^{q_{0}}
, proving the proposition.

To implement this, let us fix some notation. While
\mathfrak{X}
as usual
has side length
1
, we let
\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
denote
the smaller hexagon
\mathfrak{X}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
of side length
\rho=1-r
, translated so that it shares
the south corner of
\mathfrak{X}
. The constant
r
is the same as in

4.1
and it will be chosen
small enough later. We assume throughout that
(1-\rho)^{j}N\in\mathbb{Z}
if
j\leq i+1
, for notational convenience.

Remark 4.9
.

We will choose
r
sufficiently small (once
q_{0}
is given), then
\omega
sufficiently small (once
r
is given). None of these parameters depends on
N
.

Further let
\mathfrak{X}^{N_{i}}
and

\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}^{N_{i}}
denote the intersection of

\mathfrak{X}
and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
with
\mathbb{T}_{N_{i}}
(the triangular grid of mesh

1/N_{i}
). In accordance with
Definition

3.8
, let
\mathfrak{A}^{S}
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}^{S}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
) denote the south arc of the arctic boundary of
\mathcal{H}
(resp. of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
). See Figure
9
.
Note that
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
is a simple linear rescaling of

\mathcal{H}
.

Let
(G_{t})_{t\geq 0}
denote the height function associated to the Glauber dynamic on
\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
starting from the maximal
height function in
\Omega_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}}
and recall that
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is the height function of the Glauber dynamic on
\mathfrak{X}
starting from the maximal
height function in
\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}}
. In both cases, the tile size is
1/N_{i},i\leq i_{\rm max}
.
We couple the two processes as described in the proof of
2.11
(except that for the process in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
, the Poisson clocks and coin flips in
\mathfrak{X}\setminus\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
induce no update). We will show at end of the proof that the following holds except with probability
N^{-3L}
:

(4.11)

\displaystyle H_{\mathfrak{C}N_{i}^{2+\delta}+t}(z)\leq G_{t}(z),\;\text{ for every }t\leq N^{5}\text{ and }z\in\mathfrak{X}\;\text{ with }\dist(z,\mathfrak{A}^{S})\leq N_{i}^{-1}.

It follows easily from the definition of the monotone coupling in
2.11
that,
except with probability
N^{-3L}
,

H_{\mathfrak{C}N_{i}^{2+\delta}+t}(z)\leq G_{t}(z)
for all
z
below
\mathfrak{A}^{S}

and all
t\leq N^{5}
. The mixing time in

\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
is
t_{\rm mix}^{N_{i+1}}
, since
\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}^{N_{i}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
is nothing but

\mathfrak{X}^{N_{i+1}}
, up to a rescaling by

\rho=(1-r)
. After time

t_{mix}^{N_{i+1}}(\epsilon_{i+1})
we have (by the
concentration statement in Proposition
3.17
) that

G_{t}(z)=\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}(z)=0
for
z
at distance at least
c_{\omega}
below
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}^{S}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
, except with probability

\epsilon_{i+1}+N^{-3L}
. It follows that, except with probability

\epsilon_{i+1}+2N^{-3L}\leq(1/3)\xi^{N}_{i}
(recall (
4.10
)), for all
t\leq N^{5}
we have

H_{\mathfrak{C}N_{i}^{2+\delta}+t}(z)=0
for all
z
at distance at least
c_{\omega}

below
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}^{S}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
.
On the other hand,
c_{\omega}
sufficiently small (depending only on
q_{0}
and
r
) the region at distance at least
c_{\omega}
below
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}^{S}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
includes the region
\mathfrak{B}^{s}_{q_{0}}
(see also Figure
9
). In fact,
\partial\mathfrak{B}^{S}_{q_{0}}
is at distance
c_{\omega}
from
\mathfrak{A}^{S}_{q_{0}}
(by definition of
\mathfrak{B}^{S}_{q_{0}}
);
\mathfrak{A}^{S}_{q_{0}}
is strictly below

\mathfrak{A}^{S}
(by
3.19
), at a distance depending only on
q_{0}
; the distance between
\mathfrak{A}^{S}
and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}^{S}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
tends to zero as
r\to 0
(since the two are related via a scaling by
1-r
). Choosing the parameters as in
4.9
, it follows that the region of distance at least
c_{\omega}
below
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}^{S}
contains
\mathfrak{B}_{q_{0}}^{S}
; see
Figure

9
. The desired result then follows.

It remains to prove (
4.11
). Let
(F_{t})_{t\geq 0}
denote the height function of the stationary Glauber dynamic on

\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
, that is, the one started from the uniform measure on
\Omega_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}}
. Under the coupling of
2.11
,
G_{t}\geq F_{t}
almost surely. Therefore, it suffices to show that

(4.12)

\text{except with probability $N^{-3L}$},\quad H_{\mathfrak{C}N_{i}^{2+\delta}+t}(z)\leq F_{t}(z)\quad\text{for every}\quad t\leq N^{5}

and
for every
z\in\mathfrak{X}
such that
\dist(z,\mathfrak{A}^{S})\leq N_{i}^{-1}
. For
lightness, we say that
z
is
along
\mathfrak{A}^{S}
if
\dist(z,\mathfrak{A}^{S})\leq N_{i}^{-1}
.
By the concentration bounds for
the height under the stationary measure in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
(Theorem
3.17
) and
2.14
, the following two statements hold w.o.p.:

(i)

F_{t}(z)>\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}(z)-\omega
for all
z\in{\mathfrak{X}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}}
and
t\leq N^{5}
.

(ii)

F_{t}(z)=\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}(z)
for all
t\leq N^{5}
and for all
z
in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{F}}^{S}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
, with
\dist(z,\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}^{S}_{\rho,\rho,\rho})\geq c_{\omega}
.

Let us first prove (
4.12
), for all
z

along
\mathfrak{A}^{S}
that are sufficiently close to the boundary of
\mathfrak{X}
, namely, for
z
satisfying
\dist(z,\partial\mathfrak{X})\leq b(r)
, for some sufficiently small constant
b(r)>0
(to be chosen later). In fact, observe in this case that
z
is in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{F}}^{SW}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}\cup\widetilde{\mathfrak{F}}^{SE}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}

and bounded away from
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
(see Figure
9
). Thus, point (ii) above and the fact that we are taking
\omega
sufficiently small (recall
4.9
) so that
c_{\omega}<b(r)
implies that
F_{t}(z)=\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}(z)
w.o.p.. In
\widetilde{\mathfrak{F}}^{SW}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}\cup\widetilde{\mathfrak{F}}^{SE}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
, the limit shape
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
coincides with the maximal height function, so that

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}(z)\geq H_{\mathfrak{C}N_{i}^{2+\delta}+t}(z)
deterministically. This yields (
4.12
) for all such
z
.

Next, we must verify (
4.12
) for all
z
along

\mathfrak{A}^{S}
with
\dist(z,\partial\mathfrak{X})\geq b(r)
. Observe by Proposition
4.5
that w.o.p. we have
H_{\mathfrak{C}N_{i}^{2+\delta}+t}\leq\mathcal{H}+\omega
for every
t\leq N^{5}
. Since
\mathcal{H}(z)\leq N_{i}^{-1}\leq\omega
for
z
along
\mathfrak{A}^{S}
, it follows that
H_{\mathfrak{C}N_{i}^{2+\delta}+t}(z)\leq 2\omega
. Since
\mathfrak{A}^{S}
is above
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}^{S}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
by a distance
c(r)>0
, it follows that
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}(z)>\psi(r)
for some constant
\psi(r)>0
(dependent on
r
but not on
\omega
) for
z
along
\mathfrak{A}^{S}
with
\dist(z,\partial\mathfrak{X})\geq b(r)
. Together with item (i) above, we conclude that w.o.p.

F_{t}(z)\geq\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}(z)-\omega\geq\psi(r)-\omega\geq 2\omega\geq H_{\mathfrak{C}N_{i}^{2+\delta}+t}\quad\text{for all}\quad t\leq N^{5},

and for all
z
along
\mathfrak{A}^{S}
with
\dist(z,\partial\mathfrak{X})\geq b(r)
,
where the third inequality holds provided by taking
\omega
sufficiently small, as in
4.9
.
∎

Figure 9.
A zoom-in around the south corner of

\mathfrak{X}
. The line
\mathfrak{A}^{S}
(respectively
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}^{S}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
) is the south
arc of the arctic boundary of the limit shape in

\mathfrak{X}
(respectively
\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
). The dashed line is the boundary of the region at distance
c_{\omega}
below
\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}^{S}_{\rho,\rho,\rho}
.
The line
\mathfrak{A}^{S}_{q_{0}}
(respectively

\partial\mathfrak{B}^{S}_{q_{0}}
) is the south arc of the arctic
boundary of
\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}
(respectively, the boundary of

\mathfrak{B}^{S}_{q_{0}}
). For
c_{\omega},r,q_{0}
small, these four curves are
close to each other.

4.3.
Proof of
4.1

Theorem
4.3
implies the following. Let as usual
(\eta_{t})_{t\geq 0}
denote the Glauber dynamic in
\mathfrak{X}
, started from some
\eta_{0}\in\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}}
, and
let

(\tilde{\eta}_{t})_{t\geq\tau},\quad\tau:=\mathfrak{C}N_{i}^{2+\delta}+t_{\rm mix}^{N_{i+1}}(\epsilon_{i+1})

be the Glauber dynamic in
\mathfrak{X}
, constrained (in the sense of
Definition

2.10
) between floor

\mathcal{H}_{-q_{0}}
and ceiling
\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}
and with initial condition
\tilde{\eta}_{\tau}=\eta_{\tau}
. If the two processes are coupled using the same Poisson clocks and coin flips (as in
2.11
), then off of an event of probability
N^{-2L}+2\epsilon_{i+1}
, we have
\eta_{t}=\tilde{\eta}_{t}
for all
t\in I^{N}_{i}
.
Note also that, because of Proposition
2.13
, (
2.4
), and the facts that
\min_{i\leq i_{max}}\epsilon_{i}=N^{-L}
and
\max_{i\leq i_{max}}N_{i}=N
,

T^{N}_{i}:=\mathfrak{C}N_{i}^{2+\delta}+t_{\rm mix}^{N_{i+1}}(\epsilon_{i+1})+t_{\mix}^{q_{0},N_{i}}\left(\frac{\epsilon_{i}}{4}\right)\leq N^{5}.

Therefore, uniformly in the initial condition
\eta_{0}
, at time
T^{N}_{i}
the law of
\eta_{t}
has total variation distance at most

N^{-2L}+2\epsilon_{i+1}+\epsilon_{i}/4
from the invariant measure of
the constrained process
\tilde{\eta}
, that is denoted
\pi_{\mathcal{H}_{\pm q_{0}}}
, using the notation of
Definition

2.16
.
Next, note by
3.17
(and the bound
N_{i}\geq N^{1/10}
for every
i\leq i_{max}
) that the total variation distance between the uniform measure
\mathbb{P}^{N_{i}}
on
\Omega^{N_{i}}
and
\pi_{\mathcal{H}_{\pm q_{0}}}
is at most
N^{-2L}
. Hence, the total variation distance between
\mathbb{P}^{N_{i}}
and the distribution of
\eta_{T^{N}_{i}}
is at most

\xi^{N}_{i}:=2N^{-2L}+2\epsilon_{i+1}+\frac{\epsilon_{i}}{4}=\left(\frac{1}{5}+\frac{1}{4}\right)\epsilon_{i}+2N^{-2L}\leq\epsilon_{i}

(where the last inequality holds because
\epsilon_{i}\geq N^{-L}\gg N^{-2L}
).
This implies the desired bound
(
4.1
).

5.
Estimates for the height reduction

The goal of this section is to prove
4.4
. Throughout this section,
\varepsilon_{0}>0
is chosen small
enough (but independently of
N
). Also,
\delta\in(0,10^{-1000})
is fixed throughout to be a sufficiently small real number. Throughout, we will fix a constant
A>10^{10}
. Moreover,
M,K,D>0
will be large constants that are much larger one with respect to the other (and
A
), in a certain order. The values of the constants
(M,K,D)
need not be the same at each occurrence, but we will always impose that they satisfy

(5.1)

\displaystyle M>10^{5}K>10^{10}A>10^{15}D>10^{20}.

5.1.
Annulus decomposition of the hexagon

We begin with the following definition of annuli; see Figure
10
(that explains why we call these regions “annuli”).

Definition 5.1

(Annuli)
.

For any real numbers
q,u\geq 0
and integer
\ell\geq 1
, define the following subsets of the liquid region:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\ell}^{q}=\big\{z\in\mathfrak{L}_{q}:4^{-\ell}\leq\mathtt{d}_{z}\mathtt{e}_{z;q}\leq 4^{1-\ell}\};\qquad\mathcal{B}_{\ell}^{q}=\big\{z\in\mathfrak{L}_{q}:4^{1/2-\ell}\leq\mathtt{d}_{z}\mathtt{e}_{z;q}\leq 4^{1-\ell}\big\}.

as well as

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\ell}^{q}(u)=\big\{z\in\mathcal{A}_{\ell}^{q}:\mathtt{d}_{z}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z;q}^{3/2}\geq uN^{-1}\big\};\qquad\mathcal{B}_{\ell}^{q}(u)=\big\{z\in\mathcal{B}_{\ell}^{q}:\mathtt{d}_{z}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z;q}^{3/2}\geq uN^{-1}\big\}.

Figure 10.
A schematic view of the decomposition of

\mathfrak{L}_{q}
into
\mathcal{A}^{q}_{\ell}
. In the figure,
q
is assumed to be very small, so that
the liquid region is essentially a disk. For
\ell=1
,

\mathcal{A}_{\ell}^{q}
is approximately a disk and for
\ell>1
the
sets
\mathcal{A}_{\ell}^{q}
are approximately concentric annuli,
closer and closer to the arctic boundary. However, for
\ell

sufficiently large (such that
4^{-\ell}\lesssim N^{-2/3}
)
\mathcal{A}_{\ell}^{q}
consists of six disjoint
regions, one per tangency point (this can be checked via (
3.6
)). For
\ell>\log N
, the annuli are empty,
because by definition
\mathtt{d}_{z}\geq N^{-1/2}
and
\mathtt{e}_{z;q}\geq N^{-2/3}
. The sets
\mathcal{B}^{q}_{\ell}
are
similar.

Remark 5.2
.

The relevance of the parameter
\mathtt{d}_{z}\mathtt{e}_{z;q}
(in the definitions of
\mathcal{A}_{\ell}^{q}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\ell}^{q}
) is due to
3.21
, which indicates that it dictates how the limit shape
\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)
varies upon changing
q
. The relevance of
\mathtt{d}_{z}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z;q}^{3/2}
(in the definitions of
\mathcal{A}_{\ell}^{q}(u)
and
\mathcal{B}_{\ell}^{q}(u)
) is due to the third statement of
3.18
, which indicates that it dictates the value of
\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)
.

For any
q>0
, we will treat differently the
bulk liquid phase
, consisting of
the points
z\in\mathfrak{L}_{q}
such that

(5.2)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{z}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}\geq q^{-2/3}N^{-2/3+A\delta}

and the
edge phase
, consisting of the
z\in\bar{\mathfrak{X}}
such that

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{z}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}<4^{20}q^{-2/3}N^{-2/3+A\delta},

as the mechanisms for mixing in these two regions will be different (we will explain the reason for the definition (
5.2
) of the bulk liquid phase in Remark
6.3
below). Note that the bulk liquid and edge phases overlap on
20
“scales” of the form
4^{-\ell}
. Let
\ell_{0}=\ell_{0}(q)
denote the tenth such scale, defined to satisfy

(5.4)

\displaystyle 2^{-21}q^{2/3}N^{2/3-A\delta}\leq 4^{\ell_{0}(q)}<2^{-19}q^{2/3}N^{2/3-A\delta}.

Remark 5.3
.

Observe from the first bound in (
5.4
) that
\ell_{0}(q)\gtrsim\log N\gg 1
for
q>N^{2A\delta-1}
(in the discussion below, we will always take
q\geq N^{M\delta-1}
for a large constant
M\geq 2A
since, for all practical purposes,
q\in[0,N^{M\delta-1}]
will be indistinguishable from
q=0
).

The next lemma lower bounds
\mathcal{H}_{q}
in
\mathcal{A}_{k}^{q}
if
k
is not too large, and the following lemma shows containment properties for the
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
annuli.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
0<q\leq\varepsilon_{0}
be a real number;
k\leq\ell_{0}(q)+\delta\log N
be a positive integer; and
z\in\mathcal{A}_{k}^{q}
be a point satisfying
\dist(z,\mathfrak{A}_{q})=\dist(z,\mathfrak{A}_{q}^{S})
. Then,
\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)\geq N^{(5/4)A\delta-1}
and
(\mathfrak{d}_{z},\mathfrak{e}_{z;q})=(\mathtt{d}_{z},\mathtt{e}_{z;q})
.

Proof.

We have

(5.5)

\displaystyle\mathtt{d}_{z}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z;q}^{3/2}\geq(\mathtt{d}_{z}\mathtt{e}_{z;q})^{3/2}\geq 4^{-(3/2)\ell_{0}(q)-(3/2)\delta\log N}

\displaystyle\gg 2^{27}N^{(3A/2-3)\delta}(qN)^{-1}\geq N^{5A\delta/4-1},

where in the first inequality we used the fact that
\mathtt{d}_{z}\leq 1
; in the second we used the fact that
z\in\mathcal{A}^{q}_{k}
for some
k\leq\ell_{0}(q)+\delta\log N
; and in the last two we used (
5.4
), with the fact that
A\geq 30
and
q\leq 1
.
In particular,
\mathtt{e}_{z;q}\gg N^{-2/3}\mathtt{d}_{z}^{-1/3}
, which implies
\mathtt{e}_{z;q}=\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}
. Moreover, since (
5.5
) and
\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}\lesssim\mathfrak{d}_{z}\leq\mathtt{d}_{z}
(where we used (
3.7
) in the first bound) imply that
\mathtt{d}_{z}\gg N^{-1/2}
, we also have
\mathtt{d}_{z}=\mathfrak{d}_{z}
. Together with (
5.5
), these yield
\mathfrak{d}_{z}^{1/2}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}^{3/2}\gg N^{5A\delta/4-1}
, so the lemma follows from
3.18
.
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

Fix
\delta>0
.
Let
k\geq 1
be a positive integer and
q^{\prime},q\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, and
u\geq 0
be real numbers such that
|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 2^{k}N^{-1-4\delta}u
. We have

(5.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{q}_{k}(u):=\left[\mathcal{B}_{k+1}^{q}(u)\cup\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{q}(u)\right]\subseteq\bigcup_{j=1}^{k+1}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{q^{\prime}}\big((1-N^{-\delta})u\big),

and

(5.7)

\displaystyle 1-2N^{-2\delta}\leq\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}\mathtt{e}_{z;q}^{-1}\leq 1+2N^{-2\delta},\qquad\text{for every $z\in\mathcal{C}^{q}_{k}(u)$}.

Proof.

Fix
z\in\mathcal{C}^{q}_{k}(u)
and assume, by symmetry, that
\ell_{z}
is the southwest edge of
\mathfrak{X}
. To verify (
5.6
), it suffices to show that

(5.8)

\displaystyle\mathtt{d}_{z}\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}\geq 4^{-k-1},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathtt{d}_{z}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}^{3/2}\geq(1-N^{-\delta})uN^{-1}.

To show the former, observe that

(5.9)

\displaystyle\mathtt{d}_{z}\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}=\mathtt{d}_{z}\mathtt{e}_{z;q}+\mathtt{d}_{z}(\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}-\mathtt{e}_{z;q})\geq 4^{-k-1/2}-2^{k}N^{-1-3\delta}u\mathtt{d}_{z},

where the second statement follows from the fact that
z\in\mathcal{C}^{q}_{k}(u)
and
\dist_{\bm{v}}(\mathfrak{A}_{q},\mathfrak{A}_{q^{\prime}})\lesssim|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 2^{k}N^{-1-4\delta}u
(by
3.19
6
6

6

Recall that
3.20
states how to modify the statement of
3.19
if
\ell_{z}
is not the southwest edge of
\mathfrak{X}
.
).
Further observe that it is enough to prove (
5.8
) when
\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}
is minimal for a given value of
\mathtt{d}_{z}
, that is, when
z\in\mathcal{B}_{k+1}^{q}(u)\cup\mathcal{A}_{k}^{q}(u)
. In that case,
8^{1-k}\geq(\mathtt{d}_{z}\mathtt{e}_{z;q})^{3/2}\geq\mathtt{d}_{z}uN^{-1}
. Therefore,
4^{-k-1}\geq 2^{k-2}N^{-1}u\mathtt{d}_{z}
, which with (
5.9
) confirms the first statement in (
5.8
). It also (using (
5.9
)) implies that

\displaystyle 1-2N^{-2\delta}\leq\mathtt{d}_{z}\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}(\mathtt{d}_{z}\mathtt{e}_{z;q})^{-1}\leq 1+2N^{-2\delta},

which verifies (
5.7
). Since
\mathtt{d}_{z}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z;q}^{3/2}\geq uN^{-1}
, this gives
\mathtt{d}_{z}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}^{3/2}\gtrsim(1-2N^{-2\delta})^{3/2}uN^{-1}
, which verifies the second statement in (
5.8
). ∎

5.2.
Height reduction across annuli

In this section, we state three variants of the following statement. If a height function
H_{0}
initially satisfies w.o.p. an upper bound of the type
H_{0}\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}
(possibly with a small additive error) in a certain region
R\subseteq\mathfrak{X}
, then after a suitable amount of time under the Glauber dynamics,
H_{t}
satisfies w.o.p. the improved upper bound
H_{t}\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}
, with
q^{\prime}<q
, in a modified region
R^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{X}
. Lemma
5.6
addresses regions in the bulk liquid phase, while
5.7
and
5.8
address regions in the edge phase (close to or at the arctic boundary, respectively).

In all cases, the time
t
at which this improved bound holds will be at least
N^{2}(q-q^{\prime})
(and will attain this bound, up to powers of
N^{\delta}
, if
q-q^{\prime}
is made as large as permitted). This suggests that the parameter
q
defining the upper barrier
\mathcal{H}_{q}
decreases “at speed”
N^{-2-o(1)}
. As such,
q
should reduce from
\varepsilon_{0}
to
0
in time
N^{2+o(1)}
, which is consistent with the notion that the Glauber dynamics mix in time
N^{2+o(1)}
. Furthermore, in all of these statements, we must run for time
7
7

7

In Lemma
5.8
, one may identify
2^{k}
with
\mathtt{d}^{-1/3}N^{1/3+o(1)}
, as one can verify that this is approximately the value of
(\mathtt{d}_{z}\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime}})^{-1/2}
there.

2^{k}N
to “witness” a height reduction on the annulus
\mathcal{A}_{k}
. To heuristically see why this should be the case, observe that one expects
q
to change by
2^{k}N^{-1}
after time
2^{k}N
. Hence, Lemma
3.21
indicates that the height function
(H_{t})
on
\mathcal{A}_{k}^{q^{\prime}}
reduces by
(\mathfrak{d}_{z}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q^{\prime}})^{1/2}2^{k}N^{-1}
in this time. Since
(\mathfrak{d}_{z}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q^{\prime}})^{1/2}\sim 2^{-k}
on
\mathcal{A}_{k}^{q^{\prime}}
, this reduction is around
N^{o(1)-1}
, which is the minimal that can be “detected” (as it is the minimal height difference).

The following lemma, to be shown in
Section

6.1
, states the bulk liquid phase mixing bound. It indicates that, if a height function
H_{0}
is initially bounded by some
\mathcal{H}_{q}
, then running the Glauber dynamics
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
decreases this upper bound to
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}
at points
z
in the bulk liquid phase (see the bound
k\leq\ell_{0}(q)+5
in (
5.10
)), for some
q^{\prime}<q
.

See Fig.
11
.

Figure 11.
A pictorial illustration of Lemmas

5.6
,
5.7
and
5.8
. (a): in
5.6
, we assume an upper bound on the height function at time zero in
\cup_{\ell=1}^{k+1}\mathcal{A}^{q}_{\ell}
(union of red and blue regions) and we deduce an improved upper bound at time
\mathtt{t}
, in the smaller blue region
\cup_{\ell=1}^{k}\mathcal{A}^{q^{\prime}}_{\ell}
. (b): in
5.7
, we assume at time zero an upper bound in the whole hexagon, and we deduce an improved upper bound at time
\mathtt{t}
, in the blue region
\cup_{\ell=1}^{k+1}\mathcal{A}^{q^{\prime}}_{\ell}(N^{A\delta})
. (c): in
5.8
we assume at time zero an upper bound in the whole hexagon and a stronger upper bound in the complement of
\mathfrak{L}_{q}^{+}(K\delta),K>1
(red region), and we obtain at time
\mathtt{t}
improved upper bounds, both in the complement of
\mathfrak{L}_{q}^{+}(\delta)
(union of light blue and red regions), and in the region (dark blue) close to the arctic boundary, identified by condition (
5.18
). In (c), we zoom on a corner of the hexagon, to make the figure understandable, and the thick curve denotes the arctic boundary
\mathfrak{A}_{q}
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
A,D
satisfy (
5.1
). Also let
q
be a real number and
k\geq 0
be an integer satisfying

(5.10)

q\in[N^{A\delta-1},\varepsilon_{0}],\quad k\leq\ell_{0}(q)+5.

Further let
q^{\prime}\geq 0
satisfy

(5.11)

\displaystyle q^{\prime}\in[q-2^{k}N^{(D+2)\delta-1},q-2^{k}N^{(D-2)\delta-1}];\qquad\mathsf{t}=q^{\prime-1}k2^{k}N^{1+6A\delta}.

Consider the Glauber dynamics
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
starting from an initial condition
\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{+}
(recall Definition
2.16
) and assume that, w.o.p. under
\mu
,

(5.12)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &H_{0}(w)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}(w)+N^{\delta-1},\qquad\text{for each $w\in\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{k+1}\mathcal{A}_{\ell}^{q}$ }.\end{aligned}

Then we have w.o.p. that

(5.13)

\displaystyle H_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z),\qquad\text{for each $z\in\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{k}\mathcal{A}_{\ell}^{q^{\prime}}$ and $t\in[\mathsf{t},N^{5}]$}.

The next lemma, to be shown in Section
7.1
, states the following. Suppose a height function initially satisfies an upper bound on the full hexagon (observe that this assumption differs from that in
5.6
, which is indicative of the eventually different mechanisms of mixing in the bulk liquid and edge phases). Then, it satisfies an improved upper bound on a union of annuli, potentially within the edge phase. See Fig.
11
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
A,K,D
satisfy (
5.1
). Also let
k\geq 1
be an integer and
q\in[2^{k}N^{K\delta-1},\varepsilon_{0}]
be a real number. Further let
q^{\prime}\geq 0
satisfy

(5.14)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &q^{\prime}\in[q-2^{k}N^{(D+1)\delta-1},q-2^{k}N^{(D-1)\delta-1}];\qquad\mathsf{t}:=q^{-1}k2^{k+3}N^{1+11K\delta}.\end{aligned}

Consider the Glauber dynamics
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
starting from an initial condition
\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{+}
(recall Definition
2.16
) and assume that, w.o.p. under
\mu
,

(5.15)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} H_{0}(w)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}(w)+N^{\delta-1},\qquad\text{for each $w\in\mathfrak{X}$}.\end{aligned}

Then, w.o.p., we have

(5.16)

\displaystyle H_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z),\qquad\text{for each $z\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{k+1}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{q^{\prime}}(N^{A\delta})$ and $t\in[\mathsf{t},N^{5}]$.}

The next lemma, to be shown in
Section

7.3
, essentially states the following. Assume that a height function is initially bounded above by
\mathcal{H}_{q}+N^{K\delta-1}
on all of
\mathfrak{X}
and by
\mathcal{H}_{q}
outside of the augmented liquid region
\mathfrak{L}_{q}^{+}(K\delta)
(recall (
3.9
)). Then this height function will reduce under the Glauber dynamics to
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}+N^{\delta/4-1}
on all of
\mathfrak{X}
, and to
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}
outside of
\mathfrak{L}_{q^{\prime}}^{+}(\delta)
, for
q^{\prime}\leq q
satisfying the third bound in (
5.18
). In addition to reducing
q
, this effectively reduces the error
K
from a potentially large constant to
1
.

Let us provide a bit more explanation for the second condition in (
5.18
). It implies that

\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}\leq\mathtt{d}_{z}^{-1/3}N^{2K\delta/3-2/3}
. Since

\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}\geq\mathtt{d}_{z}^{-1/3}N^{-2/3}
, this
indicates that
\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}
is almost as small as
possible (recall (
3.6
)), meaning that
z
is essentially at the arctic
boundary. Thus, the below lemma indicates how height functions reduce very close to the arctic boundary. See Fig.
11
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Fix a real number
K
satisfying (
5.1
);
\mathtt{d}\in[N^{-1/2},1)
; and real numbers
q\geq q^{\prime}\geq 0
with
\varepsilon_{0}\geq q\geq N^{3500K\delta-2/3}\mathtt{d}^{-1/3}
. Consider the Glauber dynamics
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
starting from an initial condition
\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{+}
and assume that, w.o.p.,

(5.17)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &H_{0}(w)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}(w)+N^{K\delta-1},\qquad\text{for all $w\in\mathfrak{X}$};\\
&H_{0}(w)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}(w),\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{for all $w\notin\mathfrak{L}_{q}^{+}({K\delta})$}.\end{aligned}

Denoting
\mathsf{t}=q^{-1}\mathtt{d}^{-1/3}N^{4/3+15000K\delta}
, the following holds w.o.p. for any
t\in[\mathsf{t},N^{5}]
. For any
z\in\mathfrak{X}
satisfying

(5.18)

\displaystyle\mathtt{d}_{z}\geq N^{-K\delta}\mathtt{d};\qquad\mathtt{d}_{z}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}^{3/2}\leq N^{K\delta-1},\qquad\text{and}\qquad N^{K\delta}(q-q^{\prime})\leq N^{-2/3}\mathtt{d}_{z}^{-1/3},

we have

(5.19)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &H_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)+N^{\delta/4-1},\qquad\text{and}\qquad H_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z),\quad\text{if $z\notin\mathfrak{L}_{q^{\prime}}^{+}(\delta)$}.\end{aligned}

5.3.
Proof of
4.4

To show
4.4
, we will use the results from Section
5.2
in an inductive way, to successively improve the upper bound on the height function under the Glauber dynamics (in suitably chosen time intervals), to reduce it from the initial
\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}
to
\mathcal{H}_{q_{i_{\max}}}
, with
q_{i_{\max}}
so small that
\mathcal{H}_{q_{i_{\max}}}
is barely distinguishable from the equilibrium height. The three results from that section that we will use are
5.6
, which applies in the bulk liquid phase;
5.7
, which applies in the edge phase not at the arctic boundary; and
5.8
, which applies at the arctic boundary.

All of these decrease the value of
q
in the function
\mathcal{H}_{q}
upper bounding the Glauber dynamics. It will be beneficial to decrease
q
by as much as possible under each application of the above results; this is limited by
5.6
. Ignoring factors of
N^{\delta}
, after taking
k=\ell_{0}(q)+5
to the maximum permitted by (
5.10
), this decreases
q
by about
2^{\ell_{0}(q)}N^{-1}
, which is by (
5.4
) nearly
q^{1/3}N^{-2/3}
. This suggests the following definition for a sequence of
(q_{i})
that will serve as the parameters in the
\mathcal{H}_{q}
upper bounding the Glauber dynamics.

Definition 5.9
.

Fix constants
M,K
satisfying (
5.1
). Define the sequence of real numbers
q_{0},q_{1},\ldots
by, for each
i\geq 0
, setting
q_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}
and

(5.20)

\displaystyle q_{i+1}=q_{i}-\Delta_{i},\qquad\text{where}\qquad\Delta_{i}=q_{i}^{1/3}N^{-2/3-M\delta}.

We will always work with values of
i
for which
q_{i}>0
.

Now, recall that we seek for
\mathcal{H}_{q}
to be “indistinguishable” from
\mathcal{H}
for some sufficiently small
q
; in our context, this means
|\mathcal{H}_{q}-\mathcal{H}|
is at most
N^{-1}
, again ignoring factors of
N^{\delta}
. Since
\mathcal{H}_{q}
behaves differently across
\mathfrak{X}
, the value of
q
for which this “indistinguishability,” or “stabilization,” occurs varies across
\mathfrak{X}
. By Lemma
3.21
, this is governed by the annuli from Definition
5.1
; on
\mathcal{A}_{k}
, this “stabilizing” value of
q
is about
N^{-1}2^{k}
. This quantity ranges from
N^{-1}
(in the center of the hexagon) to
N^{-1/2}
(at the tangency locations).

Thus, we will analyze the Glauber dynamics on a sequence of times
(\mathsf{T}_{i,j})
. Here,
j
will denote the index of the annulus that we are attempting to stabilize during the time sequence
(\mathsf{T}_{i,j})_{i}
; as such, it will range from
1
to the integer
\mathfrak{j}
, for which
N^{-1}2^{\mathfrak{j}}
is approximately its maximal value of
N^{-1/2}
. Since the outermost annuli
\mathcal{A}_{k}
(with the largest values of
2^{k}N^{-1}
) will stabilize first, one should imagine
j
as decreasing from
\mathfrak{j}
to
1
. Then
i
will index the
q_{i}
for which the height function of our Glauber dynamics
(H_{t})
is bounded by
\mathcal{H}_{q_{i}}
at times
t\geq\mathsf{T}_{i,j}
(see
5.14
). It will range from
0
to the value
a_{j}
, for which
q_{a_{j}}
is approximately
2^{j}N^{-1}
. Lemma
5.6
implies that the amount of time it takes for
q_{i}
to decrease to
q_{i+1}
is (at least in the bulk liquid region) around
q_{i}^{-1}(q_{i}-q_{i+1})N^{2}
, which is nearly
q_{i}^{-2/3}N^{4/3}
. As such, we will have that
\mathsf{T}_{i+1,j}-\mathsf{T}_{i,j}
is about
q_{i}^{-2/3}N^{4/3}
.

These notions are made specific through the following definition.

Definition 5.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{j}:=\lfloor(\frac{1}{2}+\delta)\log_{2}N\rfloor
denote the largest integer such that
2^{\mathfrak{j}}\leq N^{1/2+\delta}
. For each
j\in[0,\mathfrak{j}]
, let
a_{j}
denote the minimal integer with
q_{a_{j}}<\max\{2^{j}N^{5000K\delta-1},N^{M\delta-1}\}
; observe that the sequence
(a_{j})
is non-increasing in
j
. For each
j\in[0,\mathfrak{j}]
and
i\in[0,a_{j}]
, define the sequence
\mathsf{T}_{i,j}
inductively (starting at
(i,j)=(0,\mathfrak{j})
, and then increasing in
i\in[0,a_{j}]
and decreasing in
j\in[0,\mathfrak{j}]
) by setting

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}_{0,\mathfrak{j}}=N^{2+M^{2}\delta};\quad\mathsf{T}_{i+1,j}=\mathsf{T}_{i,j}+q_{i}^{-2/3}N^{4/3+M^{2}\delta};\quad\mathsf{T}_{0,j}=\mathsf{T}_{a_{j+1},j+1},

observing that

(5.21)

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}_{i+1,j}=\mathsf{T}_{i,j}+\frac{q_{i}-q_{i+1}}{q_{i}}\cdot N^{2+(M^{2}+M)\delta}.

Remark 5.11
.

Observe that
q_{i}
and
\Delta_{i}
are decreasing in

i
, and that
N^{M\delta}\Delta_{i}\leq q_{i}
for each

i\in[1,a_{0}]
(as
q_{i}\geq N^{M\delta-1}
). Hence,

q_{i+1}\geq(1-N^{-M\delta})q_{i}
, meaning that

\Delta_{i+1}\geq(1-N^{-M\delta})\Delta_{i}
. Note
also that
\mathsf{T}_{i,j}
is increasing with
i
but
nonincreasing with
j
(specifically,
\mathsf{T}_{i,j}\geq\mathsf{T}_{i^{\prime},j+1}

for every
i\leq a_{j}
and
i^{\prime}\leq a_{j+1}
).

Lemma 5.12
.

We have for each
j\in[0,\mathfrak{j}]
and
i\in[0,a_{j}-1]
that

(5.22)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &(1-N^{-M\delta})q_{i}\leq q_{i+1}\leq q_{i};\qquad\quad q_{a_{j}}\geq 2^{j-1}N^{5000K\delta-1};\\
&a_{j}\leq a_{0}\leq N^{2/3+2M\delta};\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\mathsf{T}_{a_{j},j}\leq N^{2+2M^{2}\delta}.\end{aligned}

Proof.

The first statement follows from
5.11
. This in particular yields
q_{a_{j}}\geq q_{a_{j}-1}/2
; together with the fact that
q_{a_{j}-1}\geq 2^{j}N^{5000K\delta-1}
(by the minimality of
a_{j}
in its definition), this implies the second bound in (
5.22
). The fact that the
(a_{j})
are non-increasing implies the first estimate in the third bound of (
5.22
).

A heuristic for the second estimate
a_{0}\leq N^{2/3+2M\delta}
there is that one may view the
q_{i}
as an approximate solution, at time
i
, of the differential equation
q^{\prime}(t)=-q(t)^{1/3}\cdot N^{-2/3-M\delta}
, with
q(0)=\varepsilon_{0}
. Since this equation satisfies
q(t)\ll N^{-1}
for
t\gg N^{2/3+M\delta}
, it is plausible that
a_{0}
, the first integer
i
for which
q_{i}<N^{5000K\delta-1}
, should be smaller than
N^{2/3+2M\delta}
.

To prove this more carefully, first observe for each integer
k\geq 0
that there exist at most
2^{1-2k/3}N^{2/3+M\delta}
indices
i
(which are consecutive, as the
q_{i}
are decreasing) for which
q_{i}\in[2^{-k-1},2^{-k}]
. Indeed, assume to the contrary that this is false. Then there exist indices
i_{1}\leq i_{2}
with
i_{2}-i_{1}\geq 2^{1-2k/3}N^{2/3+M\delta}
and
q_{i_{1}},q_{i_{1}+1},\ldots,q_{i_{2}}\in[2^{-k-1},2^{-k}]
. This yields

\displaystyle 0\leq q_{i_{2}}\leq q_{i_{1}}-(i_{2}-i_{1})\Delta_{i_{2}}\leq q_{i_{1}}-2^{1-2k/3}q_{i_{2}}^{1/3}\leq 2^{-k}-2^{2/3-k}<0,

which is a contradiction (where in the second inequality we used (
5.20
); in the third we used (
5.20
) and the fact that
i_{2}-i_{1}\geq 2^{1-2k/3}N^{2/3+M\delta}
; and in the fourth we used that
q_{i_{1}},q_{i_{2}}\in[2^{-k-1},2^{-k}]
). Hence,
a_{0}\leq N^{2/3+M\delta}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{1-2k/3}\ll N^{2/3+2M\delta}
.

To confirm the fourth bound in (
5.22
), observe that

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}_{a_{j},j}

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{T}_{0,\mathfrak{j}}+N^{4/3+M^{2}\delta}\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\mathfrak{j}}\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{a_{j}}q_{i}^{-2/3}

\displaystyle\leq N^{2+M^{2}\delta}+(\mathfrak{j}+1)N^{4/3+M^{2}\delta}\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{N^{\delta}}2^{1-2k/3}N^{2/3+M\delta}\cdot 2^{2k/3}\leq N^{2+2M^{2}\delta},

where the first bound follows from the definition of
\mathsf{T}_{i,j}
; the second from applying the above fact, for any integer
k\geq 1
, there are at most
2^{1-2k/3}N^{2/3+M\delta}
many indices
i
for which
q_{i}\in[2^{-k},2^{1-k}]
; and the third follows from performing the sum (together with the fact that
\mathfrak{j}\lesssim\log N
).
∎

After each annulus stabilizes, it will be convenient to shift the Glauber dynamics height function by
N^{M\delta-1}
. Since there are at most
\log N\ll N^{\delta}
annuli, this produces an overall shift of at most
N^{(M+1)\delta-1}
, which will be negligible for our purposes.

Definition 5.13
.

Let
(H_{s})_{s\geq 0}
denote the height function at time
s
for the Glauber dynamic on
\mathfrak{X}
, with floor constraint
\mathcal{H}_{-q_{0}}
and ceiling constraint
\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}
(as in
Definition

2.10
), started from the maximal configuration compatible with these constraints (recall
Definition

2.4
). Then, for each
j\in[0,\mathfrak{j}]
and
w\in\mathfrak{X}
, let

(5.23)

\displaystyle H_{s,j}(w)=H_{s}(w)+(j-\mathfrak{j})N^{M\delta-1}.

Under the above notation we have the following inductive upper bounds on
H_{s,j}
, to be shown in
Section

5.4
, that quickly imply
4.4
.

Proposition 5.14
.

Fix integers
j_{0}\in[0,\mathfrak{j}]
and
i_{0}\in[0,a_{j_{0}}]
. Assume that, for any integers
j\in[j_{0},\mathfrak{j}]
and
i\in[0,a_{j}]
such that either
j>j_{0}
or
i<i_{0}
, the following holds w.o.p.:

(5.24)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &H_{\mathsf{T}_{i,j},j}(w)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q_{i}}(w)+N^{\delta-1},\qquad\text{for each $w\in\mathfrak{X}$};\\
&H_{\mathsf{T}_{i,j},j}(w)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q_{i}}(w),\qquad\qquad\quad\text{for each $w\notin\mathfrak{L}_{q_{i}}^{+}(\delta)$}.\end{aligned}

Then w.o.p., we have for any
t\in[\mathsf{T}_{i_{0},j_{0}},N^{5}]
that

(5.25)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &H_{t,j_{0}}(z_{0})\leq\mathcal{H}_{q_{i_{0}}}(z_{0})+N^{\delta-1},\qquad\text{for each $z_{0}\in\mathfrak{X}$};\\
&H_{t,j_{0}}(z_{0})\leq\mathcal{H}_{q_{i_{0}}}(z_{0}),\qquad\qquad\quad\text{for each $z_{0}\notin\mathfrak{L}_{q_{i_{0}}}^{+}(\delta)$}.\end{aligned}

Remark 5.15
.

By
2.17
, the law of
H_{s}
is in
\mathcal{P}^{+}
, for any
s\geq 0
. Thus
2.17
implies that, if the bounds (
5.24
) hold w.o.p. at time
\mathsf{T}_{i,j}
, then the same inequalities hold w.o.p. for every
t\in[\mathsf{T}_{i,j},N^{5}]
.

Proof of
4.4
.

Recall from
Definition

5.13
that
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
denotes the dynamic constrained between
\mathcal{H}_{\pm q_{0}}
.
By
5.14
and
5.15
, induction on increasing
i\geq 0
and decreasing
j\leq\mathfrak{j}
, (
5.22
), and the definition (
5.23
) of
H_{t,j}
, we have w.o.p. that
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
satisfies

(5.26)

\displaystyle H_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q_{a_{0}}}(z)+\mathfrak{j}N^{M\delta-1}+N^{\delta-1}\leq\mathcal{H}_{q_{a_{0}}}(z)+N^{(M+1)\delta-1}

for every
z\in\mathfrak{X}
and every

t\in[\mathsf{T}_{a_{0},0},N^{5}]
.
Note that

q_{a_{0}}\leq N^{M\delta-1}
(by definition of
a_{j}
) and

\mathcal{H}_{q_{a_{0}}}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}(z)+N^{\delta}q_{a_{0}}\leq\mathcal{H}(z)+N^{(M+1)\delta-1}
(by
3.21
).
In conclusion, w.o.p. the height function
H_{t}
satisfies
H_{t}\leq\mathcal{H}^{+}:=\min\{\mathcal{H}+2N^{(M+1)\delta-1},\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}\}
for all
t\in[\mathsf{T}_{a_{0},0},N^{5}]
. The analogous lower bound
H_{t}\geq\mathcal{H}^{-}:=\max\{\mathcal{H}-2N^{(M+1)\delta-1},\mathcal{H}_{-q_{0}}\}
follows by symmetry. Recall also that
\mathsf{T}_{a_{0},0}\leq N^{2+2M^{2}\delta}
from (
5.22
).
For some sufficiently large constant
C>1
, we have then the following facts:

(1)

\mathcal{H}^{-}\leq H_{t}\leq\mathcal{H}^{+}
, w.o.p., for all
t\in[N^{2+2M^{2}\delta},N^{5}]
. This allows to couple (w.o.p.) the dynamic
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
with the Glauber dynamics with ceiling constraint
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and floor constraint
\mathcal{H}^{-}
, in the whole interval
t\in[N^{2+2M^{2}\delta},N^{5}]
.

(2)

the mixing time of the Glauber dynamics, with ceiling constraint
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and floor constraint
\mathcal{H}^{-}
, is at most
CN^{2+2(M+2)\delta}
, by
2.13
;

(3)

the mixing time
t^{q_{0},N}_{\rm mix}
of the Glauber dynamics, with ceiling constraint
\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}
and floor constraint
\mathcal{H}_{-q_{0}}
, is at most
CN^{4+\delta}\ll N^{5}
, also by
2.13
.

From items (1)–(3) and the submultiplicativity (
2.4
) we deduce that, at time
t=N^{5}
, the law of the configuration
H_{t}
is, on the one hand, at total variation distance at most
N^{-2}
from the uniform measure
\pi_{\mathcal{H}^{\pm}}
and, on the other hand, at total variation distance at most
N^{-2}
from the uniform measure
\pi_{\mathcal{H}_{\pm q_{0}}}
(recall
Definition

2.16
). It follows that
\|\pi_{\mathcal{H}^{\pm}}-\pi_{\mathcal{H}_{\pm q_{0}}}\|\leq N^{-1}
. Then, item (2) (with item (1)) implies that
t^{q_{0},N}_{\rm mix}(1/4+N^{-1})\leq N^{2+4M^{2}\delta}+CN^{2+2(M+2)\delta}\leq 2N^{2+4M^{2}\delta}
. Finally, applying once more the submultiplicativity (
2.4
) of the mixing time we conclude

(5.27)

t^{q_{0},N}_{\rm mix}(N^{-\mathsf{L}})\lesssim N^{2+4M^{2}\delta}|\log(N^{-\mathsf{L}})|\lesssim N^{2+5M^{2}\delta}

which proves the theorem.
∎

5.4.
Proof of
5.14

Recall from
Definition

5.9
that
q_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}
and that the sequence
(q_{i})_{i}
is decreasing. We may assume that
i_{0}
is sufficiently large (and in particular
i_{0}\geq 1
) so that

q_{i_{0}}\leq\varepsilon_{0}/2
, since for
q_{i_{0}}\geq\varepsilon_{0}/2
the statement we want to prove follows from
4.3
(with the fact that
H_{s,j}(w)\leq H_{s}(w)
for all
j\in[0,\mathfrak{j}]
and
w\in\mathfrak{X}
).

Now fix
z_{0}\in\mathfrak{X}
, and set
\mathfrak{q}=q_{i_{0}}
and
\ell_{0}=\ell_{0}(\mathfrak{q})
(recall (
5.4
)).

Lemma 5.16
.

If
j_{0}<\mathfrak{j}
and
\mathfrak{q}\not\in[2^{j_{0}-1}N^{5000K\delta-1},2^{j_{0}+1}N^{5000K\delta-1}]
, then the statement of
5.14
holds.

Proof.

Note that
H_{s,j}
is increasing in
j
, and that
\mathsf{T}_{i,j}
is decreasing in
j
, so the statement (
5.25
) is weaker for smaller values of
j
(for the same
i
). Therefore (recalling also
5.15
), if
j_{0}<\mathfrak{j}
(
5.25
) follows from (
5.24
) applied at
(i,j)=(i_{0},j_{0}+1)
, except if the condition
i\leq a_{j}
is violated when we increase
j
from
j_{0}
to
j_{0}+1
, namely, if
i_{0}>a_{j_{0}+1}
.
Since
q_{i}
is decreasing in
i
, this implies that
\mathfrak{q}=q_{i_{0}}<q_{a_{j_{0}+1}}
.
Since
i\leq a_{j_{0}}
, we always have
\mathfrak{q}\geq q_{a_{j_{0}}}
, so this means

q_{a_{j_{0}}}\leq\mathfrak{q}<q_{a_{j_{0}+1}},

which upon recalling the definition of
a_{j}
(and the first statement in (
5.22
)) implies that
\mathfrak{q}\in[2^{j_{0}-1}N^{5000K\delta-1},2^{j_{0}+1}N^{5000K\delta-1}]
.
∎

From now on, we assume therefore that

(5.28)

\displaystyle j_{0}=\mathfrak{j},\quad\text{or}\quad\mathfrak{q}\in[2^{j_{0}-1}N^{5000K\delta-1},2^{j_{0}+1}N^{5000K\delta-1}].

The following lemma verifies
5.14
if
z_{0}
is close to the arctic boundary.

Lemma 5.17
.

If
\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};\mathfrak{q}}^{3/2}\leq N^{K\delta-1}
, then the statement of
5.14
holds.

Proof.

We distinguish between whether
\mathfrak{q}\leq N^{4000K\delta-2/3}\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/3}
or
\mathfrak{q}>N^{4000K\delta-2/3}\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/3}
. In the former case, we will show that (
5.25
) follows from (
5.24
) at a larger value of
j>j_{0}
. To that end, let us first verify that
j_{0}<\mathfrak{j}
. Indeed, we have
N^{4000K\delta-2/3}\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/3}\geq\mathfrak{q}\geq q_{a_{j_{0}}}\geq 2^{j_{0}-1}N^{5000K\delta-1}
, so that
\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/3}\geq 2^{j_{0}-1}N^{1000K\delta-1/3}
. Since
2^{\mathfrak{j}}\geq N^{1/2}
and
\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\geq N^{-1/2}
, it follows that
j_{0}<\mathfrak{j}
and thus
\mathfrak{q}\geq 2^{j_{0}-1}N^{5000K\delta-1}
because of (
5.28
).

Now, set
j_{0}^{\prime}=j_{0}+1
, and observe that
q_{a_{j_{0}^{\prime}}}\leq 2^{j_{0}+1}N^{5000K\delta-1}
. Hence, we have w.o.p. that

\displaystyle H_{t,j_{0}}(z_{0})

\displaystyle=H_{t,j_{0}+1}(z_{0})-N^{M\delta-1}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{H}_{q_{a_{j_{0}^{\prime}}}}(z_{0})-\frac{1}{2}\cdot N^{M\delta-1}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{q}}(z_{0})+N^{\delta}(q_{a_{j_{0}^{\prime}}}-\mathfrak{q})(\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};\mathfrak{q}})^{1/2}+N^{\delta}\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{1/2}q_{a_{j_{0}^{\prime}}}^{3/2}-\frac{1}{2}\cdot N^{M\delta-1}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{q}}(z_{0})+N^{5100K\delta-1}2^{j_{0}+1}(\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};\mathfrak{q}})^{1/2}+N^{7600K\delta-1}-\frac{1}{2}\cdot N^{M\delta-1},

where in the first statement we used the definition of
H_{t,j}
; in the second we used (
5.24
) together with
5.15
; in the third we used
3.21
;
and in the fourth we used the above fact that
q_{a_{j_{0}^{\prime}}}\leq 2^{j_{0}+1}N^{5000K\delta-1}
and that
q_{a_{j_{0}^{\prime}}}\leq 4\mathfrak{q}\leq N^{5000K\delta-2/3}\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/3}
. Since
\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};\mathfrak{q}}^{3/2}\leq N^{K\delta-1}
and
\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/3}\geq 2^{j_{0}-1}N^{1000K\delta-1/3}
, we have
\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0}}^{1/2}\leq 2^{1-j_{0}}N^{-900K\delta}
, and so

\displaystyle H_{t,j_{0}}(z_{0})\leq\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{q}}(z_{0})+N^{8000K\delta-1}-\frac{1}{2}\cdot N^{M\delta-1}\leq\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{q}}(z_{0}),

which verifies the lemma if
\mathfrak{q}\leq N^{4000K\delta-2/3}\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/3}
(recall that
M>10^{5}K
).

Now instead suppose that
\mathfrak{q}\geq N^{4000K\delta-2/3}\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/3}
; we will deduce (
5.25
) from (
5.24
) at a smaller value of
i<i_{0}
, with
5.8
. Set

(5.29)

\displaystyle r=\mathfrak{q}+N^{-2/3-K\delta}\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/3}\leq 2\mathfrak{q};

the bound holds since
\mathfrak{q}\geq N^{4000K\delta-2/3}\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/3}
.
Further let
m\leq i_{0}
be the smallest integer such that
q_{m}<r
. Observe that such an integer
m
exists since
r<\mathfrak{q}+o(1)\leq\varepsilon_{0}/2+o(1)<\varepsilon_{0}
and
m<i_{0}
since
r-\mathfrak{q}=N^{-K\delta-2/3}\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/3}\gg\Delta_{m-1}
(by the definition (
5.20
) of
\Delta_{m-1}
).

We will apply
5.8
with the
(\mathtt{d};z;q,q^{\prime};H_{s})
there equal to
(\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}};z_{0};r,\mathfrak{q},H_{s+\mathsf{T}_{m,j_{0}}})
here. These parameters satisfy (
5.18
), where (
5.24
) (with the
(i_{0},j_{0})
there equal to
(m,j_{0})
here) verifies (
5.17
). Therefore,
5.8
implies w.o.p. that
H_{t,j_{0}}(z_{0})\leq\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{q}}(z_{0})+N^{\delta/4-1}
and
H_{t,j_{0}}(z_{0})\leq\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{q}}(z_{0})
if
z_{0}\notin\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}(\delta)
for
\mathsf{T}_{m,j_{0}}+\mathfrak{q}^{-1}\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/3}N^{4/3+15000K\delta}\leq t\leq N^{5}
. Since
\mathsf{T}_{i_{0},j_{0}}-\mathsf{T}_{m,j_{0}}\geq(i_{0}-m)r^{-1}N^{2+M^{2}\delta}\Delta_{i_{0}}\geq(i_{0}-m)(2\mathfrak{q})^{-1}N^{2+M^{2}\delta}
and
i_{0}-m\geq(r-\mathfrak{q})\Delta_{m}^{-1}\geq(r-\mathfrak{q})(2\Delta_{i_{0}})^{-1}
(as
r\leq 2\mathfrak{q}
by (
5.29
)), we have that
\mathsf{T}_{i_{0},j_{0}}-\mathsf{T}_{m,j_{0}}\gg\mathfrak{q}^{-1}\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/3}N^{4/3+15000K\delta}
, and so this establishes (
5.25
).
∎

The next lemma verifies
5.14
if
z_{0}
is in the bulk liquid phase.

Lemma 5.18
.

If
z_{0}\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{\ell_{0}+4}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{\mathfrak{q}}
, then the statement of
5.14
holds.

Proof.

Recalling
D
from (
5.1
), set
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}=q_{i_{0}-1}-2^{\ell_{0}}N^{D\delta-1}
. We will apply
5.6
, with the
(k;q,q^{\prime};H_{s})

there equal to
(\ell_{0}+5;q_{i_{0}-1},\mathfrak{q}^{\prime};H_{s+\mathsf{T}_{i_{0}-1,j_{0}}})

here. These parameters satisfy (
5.11
), and (
5.24
) implies (
5.12
). Hence,
5.6
yields w.o.p. that
H_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}}(z)
for all
z\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{\ell_{0}+5}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}}
and
\mathsf{T}_{i_{0}-1,j_{0}}+(\mathfrak{q}^{\prime})^{-1}(\ell_{0}+5)2^{\ell_{0}+5}N^{1+10A\delta}\leq t\leq N^{5}
. Note that
\mathfrak{q}\geq\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
, since

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}-\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}=2^{\ell_{0}}N^{D\delta-1}-q_{i_{0}-1}^{1/3}N^{-2/3-M\delta}\geq\mathfrak{q}^{1/3}N^{-2/3-A\delta/2}-q_{i_{0}-1}^{1/3}N^{-2/3-M\delta}>0,

where in the first statement we used the
definitions of
\mathfrak{q}
and
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
; in
the second we used (
5.4
); and in the third
we used the fact from
5.11
that

2\mathfrak{q}>q_{i_{0}-1}
, together with the condition
M\geq 10^{6}A
(by (
5.1
)).

This implies that

H_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{q}}(z)
for all

z\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{\ell_{0}+5}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}}
and thus for all

z\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{\ell_{0}+4}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{\mathfrak{q}}
(by (
5.6
) and

5.4
, where we used the fact that

\mathfrak{q}-\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}\leq 2^{\ell_{0}}N^{D\delta-1}\leq 2^{\ell_{0}+5}N^{A\delta-4\delta-1}
). Thus, to show the lemma, it suffices to verify that
\mathsf{T}_{i_{0}-1,j_{0}}+(\mathfrak{q}^{\prime})^{-1}(\ell_{0}+5)2^{\ell_{0}+5}N^{1+10A\delta}\leq\mathsf{T}_{i_{0},j_{0}}
. This holds since

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}^{\prime-1}(\ell_{0}+5)2^{\ell_{0}+5}N^{1+10A\delta}

\displaystyle\leq 2q_{i_{0}-1}^{-1}(\ell_{0}+5)\mathfrak{q}^{1/3}N^{4/3+10A\delta}

\displaystyle\leq q_{i_{0}-1}^{-2/3}N^{4/3+7A\delta}\leq\mathsf{T}_{i_{0},j_{0}}-\mathsf{T}_{i_{0}-1,j_{0}},

by (
5.4
) and the bounds

q_{i_{0}-1}\leq 2\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
(as
q_{i_{0}-1}\geq\mathfrak{q}\geq 2^{\ell_{0}+1}N^{D\delta-1}
, where the last inequality holds since
(\mathfrak{q}N)^{1/3}\geq 2^{\ell_{0}+1}N^{A\delta/2}
, by (
5.4
) and (
5.10
)),
\mathfrak{q}\leq q_{i_{0}-1}
, and
M\geq 10^{10}A
.
∎

Proof of
5.14
.

By Lemmas
5.17
and
5.18
, it remains to consider the case when
z_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{k}^{\mathfrak{q}}(N^{K\delta})
for some
k\geq\ell_{0}+5=\ell_{0}(\mathfrak{q})+5
. The proof will be similar to that of
5.17
; as there, we will distinguish between whether
\mathfrak{q}\leq 2^{k}N^{K\delta-1}
or
\mathfrak{q}\geq 2^{k}N^{K\delta-1}
.

Case 1:
\mathfrak{q}\leq 2^{k}N^{K\delta-1}
.
We will verify that (
5.25
) follows from (
5.24
) at a larger value of
j>j_{0}
. To that end, we first confirm that
j_{0}<\mathfrak{j}
. Observe that
2^{j_{0}-1}N^{5000K\delta-1}\leq\mathfrak{q}\leq 2^{k}N^{K\delta-1}\leq 2N^{K\delta-1}(\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0}})^{-1/2}\leq N^{K\delta-1/2}
, where in the first inequality we used (
5.22
) and the fact that
\mathfrak{q}=q_{i_{0}}
; in the second we used our assumption that
\mathfrak{q}\leq 2^{k}N^{K\delta-1}
; in the third we used the fact that
z_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{k}^{\mathfrak{q}}
; and in the fourth we used the fact that
\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};\mathfrak{q}}\gtrsim\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};\mathfrak{q}}^{3/2}\geq N^{K\delta-1}
(by (
3.7
) and the fact that
z_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{k}^{\mathfrak{q}}(N^{K\delta})
). Hence,
2^{j_{0}}\leq N^{1/2-4900K\delta}
meaning since
2^{\mathfrak{j}}\asymp N^{1/2+\delta}
that
j_{0}\leq\mathfrak{j}-1
.

Now, set
j^{\prime}=j_{0}+1
. We claim (and prove below) that

(5.30)

q_{a_{j^{\prime}}}\leq 2^{j_{0}+1}N^{5000K\delta-1}\leq 4\mathfrak{q}.

Thus, we have w.o.p. that

\displaystyle H_{t,j_{0}}(z_{0})

\displaystyle=H_{t,j^{\prime}}(z_{0})-N^{M\delta-1}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{H}_{q_{a_{j^{\prime}}}}(z_{0})+N^{\delta-1}-N^{M\delta-1}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{q}}(z_{0})+N^{\delta}(q_{a_{j^{\prime}}}-\mathfrak{q})(\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};\mathfrak{q}})^{1/2}+N^{\delta}\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{1/2}q_{a_{j^{\prime}}}^{3/2}-N^{M\delta-1}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{q}}(z_{0})+N^{2\delta}2^{3-k}\mathfrak{q}+8N^{\delta}\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{1/2}\mathfrak{q}^{3/2}-N^{M\delta-1}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{q}}(z_{0})+N^{2K\delta-1}-N^{M\delta-1}\leq\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{q}}(z_{0}),

where in the first statement we used the definition of
H_{t,j}
; in the second we used the
(i,j)=(a_{j^{\prime}},j^{\prime})
case of (
5.24
) (together with
5.15
); in the third we used
3.21
; in the fourth we used (
5.30
), with the fact that
z\in\mathcal{A}_{k}^{\mathfrak{q}}
; in the fifth we used the facts that
\mathfrak{q}\leq 2^{k}N^{K\delta-1}
and
\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{1/2}\leq 2^{-3k/2}N^{1/2-K\delta/2}
(as
\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};\mathfrak{q}}^{3/2}\geq N^{K\delta-1}
and
\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0}}\leq 4^{-k}
for
z_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{k}^{\mathfrak{q}}
); and in the sixth we used the fact that
M\geq 10^{5}K
.

To establish the proposition when
\mathfrak{q}\leq 2^{k}N^{K\delta-1}
, it remains to verify (
5.30
).
Recall that
a_{j^{\prime}}
is the minimum integer such that
q_{a_{j^{\prime}}}<\max\{2^{j^{\prime}}N^{5000K\delta-1},N^{M\delta-1}\}
. If the maximum is realized by the first alternative, then we directly have
q_{a_{j^{\prime}}}<2^{j^{\prime}}N^{5000K\delta-1}\leq 4\mathfrak{q}
using (
5.22
). So, assume instead that
2^{j^{\prime}}N^{5000K\delta-1}\leq q_{a_{j^{\prime}}}<N^{M\delta-1}
. Then, since
j^{\prime}=j_{0}+1
, we have
2^{j_{0}}N^{5000K\delta-1}\leq q_{a_{j^{\prime}}}\leq N^{M\delta-1}
. Therefore, since
a_{j^{\prime}}
is the minimal integer for which
q_{a_{j^{\prime}}}<N^{M\delta-1}
, it follows that
a_{j^{\prime}}=a_{j_{0}}
, so
q_{a_{j_{0}}}=q_{a_{j^{\prime}}}
, meaning that

(5.31)

\mathfrak{q}=q_{i_{0}}\geq q_{a_{j_{0}}}=q_{a_{j^{\prime}}},

where the first statement holds by the definition of
\mathfrak{q}
; the second since
q_{i}
is decreasing in
i
; and the third holds by the above. This verifies the claim.

Case 2:
\mathfrak{q}\geq 2^{k}N^{K\delta-1}
.
We will use
(
5.24
) applied at a smaller value of
i<i_{0}
, together with

5.7
. More specifically, recalling
D
from (
5.1
), let
m<i_{0}
be any integer such
that

q_{m}-\mathfrak{q}\in[2^{k}N^{D\delta-1},2^{k+2}N^{D\delta-1}]
. In particular,
\mathfrak{q}\geq 2^{k}N^{K\delta-1}\geq q_{m}/2
, since
K>10^{15}
by (
5.1
). To see that such an integer
m
exists, note first of all
that

(5.32)

\displaystyle q_{i_{0}-1}-\mathfrak{q}=q_{i_{0}-1}-q_{i_{0}}\asymp\mathfrak{q}^{1/3}N^{-2/3-M\delta}\ll 2^{k}N^{-1+D\delta}

by the definition (
5.20
) of the
(q_{i})
(with
5.11
), and the assumption
k>\ell_{0}(\mathfrak{q})+4
(with (
5.4
)). This, together with the fact that
q_{j}-q_{j+1}\asymp q_{i_{0}-1}-q_{i_{0}}=q_{i_{0}-1}-\mathfrak{q}
whenever
\mathfrak{q}\leq q_{j}\leq 2\mathfrak{q}
(which holds by (
5.20
)) yields the existence of such an integer
m
.

Let us apply
5.7
, with the
(q,q^{\prime};H_{s})
there equal to
(q_{m},\mathfrak{q};H_{s+\mathsf{T}_{m,j_{0}}})
here, where we observe that (
5.15
) is verified by the
(i,j)=(m,j_{0})
case of (
5.24
). This implies that, w.o.p.,
H_{s,j_{0}}(z_{0})\leq\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{q}}(z_{0})
for all

\mathsf{T}_{m,j_{0}}+q_{m}^{-1}k^{2}2^{k+3}N^{1+11K\delta}\leq s\leq N^{5}.

The proof is then concluded by noting that
\mathsf{T}_{i_{0},j_{0}}-\mathsf{T}_{m,j_{0}}\gg k^{2}2^{k+3}q_{m}^{-1}N^{1+11K\delta}
, as

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}_{i_{0},j_{0}}-\mathsf{T}_{m,j_{0}}\gtrsim(q_{m}-\mathfrak{q})\mathfrak{q}^{-1}N^{2+M\delta}\geq\mathfrak{q}^{-1}2^{k}N^{D\delta-1}\cdot N^{2+M\delta}\gg q_{m}^{-1}k^{2}2^{k+3}N^{1+11K\delta}.

where we used (
5.21
) and the bounds
q_{m}\leq 2\mathfrak{q}
and
M>10^{5}K
.
∎

6.
Height reduction in the bulk liquid phase

6.1.
Proof of
5.6

In this section we establish
5.6
using the below lemma, to be shown in Section
6.2
. It explains how the height function under the Glauber dynamics reduces in a single annulus (corresponding to the outermost one in the union of annuli considered in Lemma
5.6
) in the bulk liquid phase. Observe that the reduction
q-q^{\prime}
of
q
and time
\mathsf{t}
given by (
6.1
) below are similar to those in (
5.11
). See
Figure

12
.

Figure 12.
(a): a schematic illustration of
6.1
. We assume an upper bound on the height function at time zero in
\mathcal{C}^{q}_{k}
(union of red and blue regions) and we deduce a stronger upper bound at time
\mathtt{t}
in the annulus
\mathcal{A}^{q^{\prime}}_{k}
(blue region). (b): a schematic illustration of
7.1
. We assume an upper bound on the height function at time zero in the whole hexagon, and a better upper bound in the red region
\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{A}^{q^{\prime}}_{j}(N^{A\delta})
(red region) and we deduce an improved upper bound at time
\mathtt{t}
in the blue region.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
A,D
satisfy (
5.1
); let
q\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
k\leq\ell_{0}(q)+5
; and set

(6.1)

\displaystyle q^{\prime}\in[q-2^{k}N^{D\delta-1},q];\qquad\mathsf{t}=2^{k}q^{-1}N^{1+6A\delta}.

Consider the Glauber dynamics
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
starting from an initial condition
\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{+}
(recall
Definition

2.16
) and assume that, w.o.p. under
\mu
,

(6.2)

\displaystyle H_{0}(w)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}(w)+N^{\delta-1},\quad\text{for each $w\in\mathcal{C}^{q}_{k}:=\mathcal{B}_{k+1}^{q}\cup\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{q}$}.

Then we have w.o.p. that, for any
t\in[\mathsf{t},N^{5}]
,

(6.3)

\displaystyle H_{t}(z_{0})\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z_{0}),\qquad\text{for each $z_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{k}^{q^{\prime}}$}.

Proof of
5.6
.

To establish the lemma, we induct on
k\in[0,\ell_{0}+5]
. The statement (
5.13
) is empty for
k=0
; fix
k\in[1,\ell_{0}+5]
. Assume that (
5.11
) and (
5.12
) imply (
5.13
) w.o.p., with the
k
there replaced by
k-1
; we will then show that (
5.11
) and (
5.12
) w.o.p. imply (
5.13
).
To that end, since
\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{+}
, it suffices by (
6.2
) and
2.17
to show (
5.13
) when the Glauber dynamics
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
are replaced by the Glauber dynamics restricted (in the sense of
Definition

2.15
) the region
\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{k+1}\mathcal{A}_{\ell}^{q}
, and with ceiling constraint given by
\mathcal{H}_{q}+N^{\delta-1}
(as in Definition
2.10
). Thus,
6.1
(applied with
D
there equal to
D+2
here) yields, for any
r\in[q^{\prime},q]
,

(6.4)

\displaystyle H_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z),\qquad\text{for each $t\in[q^{\prime-1}2^{k}N^{1+6A\delta},N^{5}]$ and $z\in\mathcal{A}_{k}^{r}$},

w.o.p. (where we used the fact that
q\geq q^{\prime}
). Set
\mathtt{s}=q^{\prime-1}2^{k}N^{1+6A\delta}
.

By the
r=q^{\prime}
case of (
6.4
), it remains to verify that
H_{s}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)
holds w.o.p., for each
s\in[\mathsf{t},N^{5}]
and
z\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{k-1}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{q^{\prime}}
. This will follow from two applications of the inductive hypothesis. More specifically, set
\mathsf{q}_{j}=q-j(q-q^{\prime})/2
for each
j\in\{0,1,2\}
. Then, we have that
\mathsf{q}_{0}=q
and
\mathsf{q}_{2}=q^{\prime}
; that
\mathsf{q}_{i}\in\big[\mathsf{q}_{i-1}-2^{k-1}N^{(D+2)\delta-1},\mathsf{q}_{i-1}-k2^{k-1}N^{(D-2)\delta-1}\big]
for
i\in\{1,2\}
(where the inclusion follows from (
5.11
)). By (
5.12
), the inductive hypothesis (
5.13
) (with the
(k,q,q^{\prime})
there given by
(k-1,\mathsf{q}_{0},\mathsf{q}_{1})
here) yields, w.o.p.,

(6.5)

\displaystyle H_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{q}_{1}}(z),\quad\text{for $t\in\big[\mathtt{s}+\mathsf{q}_{1}^{-1}(k-1)2^{k-1}N^{1+6A\delta},N^{5}\big]$ and $z\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{k-1}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{\mathsf{q}_{1}}$}.

Now by (
6.4
) (with the
r
there equal to
\mathsf{q}_{1}
here) and (
6.5
)
the inductive hypothesis (
5.13
) (with the
(k,q,q^{\prime})
there given by
(k-1,\mathsf{q}_{1},\mathsf{q}_{2})
here) yields w.o.p. that
H_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{q}_{2}}(z)=\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)
, for each
z\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{k-1}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{q^{\prime}}
and each
t\in[\mathtt{s}^{\prime},N^{5}]
. Here, we have denoted

\displaystyle\mathtt{s}^{\prime}=\mathtt{s}+\mathsf{q}_{1}^{-1}(k-1)2^{k-1}N^{1+6A\delta}+\mathsf{q}_{2}^{-1}(k-1)2^{k-1}N^{1+6A\delta}\leq q^{\prime-1}2^{k}kN^{1+6A\delta}=\mathsf{t},

where in the last inequality we recalled the definitions of
\mathtt{s}
and
\mathsf{t}
, and we also used the facts that
\mathsf{q}_{1},\mathsf{q}_{2}\geq q^{\prime}
. This establishes the lemma.
Note that, in applying the inductive hypothesis the second time, we have implicitly assumed that
k-1\leq\ell_{0}(\mathsf{q}_{1})+5
, which is needed in (
5.10
).
The claim follows if
\ell_{0}(\mathsf{q}_{1})\geq\ell_{0}(q)-1
, since
k
satisfies
k\leq\ell_{0}(q)+5
.
First observe by (
5.4
) and (
5.10
)

\displaystyle q\geq(qN)^{1/3}N^{A\delta/2-1}\geq 2^{\ell_{0}(q)+5}N^{A\delta-1}\geq 2^{k}N^{A\delta-1}.

Hence, it follows from (
5.11
) that
\mathsf{q}_{1}\leq q\leq 2\mathsf{q}_{1}
.
Using again (
5.4
) we deduce
\ell_{0}(\mathsf{q}_{1})>\ell_{0}(q)-2
which implies
\ell_{0}(\mathsf{q}_{1})\geq\ell_{0}(q)-1
,
since
\ell_{0}
is an integer.
∎

6.2.
Mixing in bulk liquid phase cells

In this section we show
6.1
, by reducing it to a “more local” height reduction statement on suitably defined mesoscopic rectangular cells. Throughout this section, we let
q\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
and
z_{0}\in\mathfrak{L}_{q}
satisfy

(6.6)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}\geq q^{-2/3}N^{-2/3+A\delta}.

Recall from
Section

5.1
that this assumes
z_{0}
is in the bulk liquid phase. Since
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\leq 1
,
(
6.6
) implies

(6.7)

\displaystyle q\geq\frac{N^{-1+(3/2)A\delta}}{(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q})^{3/2}}\geq\frac{N^{-1+(3/2)A\delta}}{(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q})^{1/2}}.

The following definition provides the mesoscopic cells in which we will locally analyze the Glauber dynamics in the bulk liquid phase. The reasoning behind the specific choice of dimensions in (
6.8
) will be explained in
6.6
below. See also
Figure

7
.

Definition 6.2
.

Define the cell
\mathfrak{Y}\subset\bar{\mathfrak{X}}
centered at
z_{0}
by

(6.8)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathfrak{Y}=\bigg\{z\in\overline{\mathfrak{X}}:&\dist_{\bm{u}_{z_{0};q}}(z,z_{0})\leq q^{-1/2}N^{(\frac{3}{4}A-\frac{1}{2})\delta-1/2}(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q})^{-1/4},\\
&\dist_{\bm{w}_{z_{0};q}}(z,z_{0})\leq q^{-1/2}N^{(\frac{3}{4}A-\frac{1}{2})\delta-1/2}(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q})^{1/4}\bigg\}.\end{aligned}

Further define
\mathfrak{Y}^{-}\subset\mathfrak{X}
by
\mathfrak{Y}^{-}=(1/L)\cdot(\mathfrak{Y}-z_{0})+z_{0}
(namely, to be its shrinking by a factor
L
), where
L
is a sufficiently large constant that depends only on the constant
C
in
3.28
.

Remark 6.3
.

By (
6.6
) and (
6.8
) (with the fact that
A\geq 30
), we have that

\displaystyle\dist_{\bm{u}_{z_{0};q}}(z_{0},\partial\mathfrak{Y})\leq N^{-\delta/2}(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q})^{1/2};\qquad\dist_{\bm{w}_{z_{0};q}}(z_{0},\partial\mathfrak{Y})\leq N^{-\delta/2}\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}.

By (
3.8
) and the smoothness of
\mathfrak{A}
(
3.10
), it quickly follows that, for any
z\in\mathfrak{Y}
, we have

(6.9)

\displaystyle(1-N^{-\delta/4})\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}\leq\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}\leq(1+N^{-\delta/4})\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q};\qquad(1-N^{-\delta/4})\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\leq\mathfrak{d}_{z}\leq(1+N^{-\delta/4})\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}.

In particular, the cell
\mathfrak{Y}\subseteq\mathfrak{L}_{q}
is contained in the liquid region. Observe that the derivation of (
6.9
) used (and in fact required) the condition (
6.6
), which is why we are using the latter to define the bulk liquid phase.

The following proposition, to be shown in Section
6.3
, provides a rate of decrease for the height function of tilings under Glauber dynamics in
\mathfrak{Y}
. See
Figure

13
. Observe below that
\bar{q}\geq 0
, by (
6.7
) and (
6.10
).

Proposition 6.4

(Bulk mixing)
.

The following holds if the constant
L
in
Definition

6.2
is large enough. Fix real numbers
q\geq\overline{q}
and a point
z_{0}\in\mathfrak{L}_{q}
satisfying (
6.6
) and

(6.10)

\displaystyle 0\leq q-\overline{q}<N^{5A\delta/4-1}(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q})^{-1/2}.

Further assume that
H_{0}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)+N^{\delta-1}
for each
z\in\mathfrak{Y}
.
Under the Glauber dynamics restricted to
\mathfrak{Y}
, with the ceiling constraint
\mathcal{H}_{q}+N^{\delta-1}
on
\mathfrak{Y}
,
we have w.o.p.

(6.11)

\displaystyle H_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{\bar{q}}(z),

for all

(6.12)

z\in\mathfrak{Y}^{-},\qquad t\in[\mathtt{t},N^{5}],\quad\mathtt{t}:=q^{-1}N^{1+6A\delta}(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q})^{-1/2}.

Figure 13.
Proposition
6.4
assumes the bound
H\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}+N^{\delta-1}
in the cell
\mathfrak{Y}
(the union of the blue and red regions), and deduces w.o.p. the stronger bound
H_{t}\leq\mathcal{H}_{\bar{q}}
in the smaller cell
\mathfrak{Y}^{-}
(the blue region), after time
\mathtt{t}
.

Remark 6.5
.

Let us briefly, and heuristically, explain the choices of
\bar{q}
and
\mathsf{t}
in
6.4
(omitting factors of
N^{\delta}
in this informal discussion). The choice (
6.10
) of
\bar{q}
arises since it, with
3.21
, implies that
0\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)-\mathcal{H}_{\bar{q}}(z)\leq N^{o(1)-1}
for
z
near
z_{0}
. Thus, by imposing a floor constraint of
\mathcal{H}_{\bar{q}}
and a ceiling constraint of
\mathcal{H}_{q}+N^{o(1)-1}
for the Glauber dynamics, the factor of
H_{\max}
appearing in (
2.5
) will be
N^{o(1)-1}
, which is essentially minimal. Then the mixing time in (
2.5
) will be
N^{2+o(1)}\diam(\mathfrak{Y})^{2}\leq q^{-1}N^{1+o(1)}(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q})^{-1/2}
, by (
6.8
); this is consistent with the choice of
\mathsf{t}
in (
6.12
).

Lemma
6.1
is a quick consequence of
6.4
.

Proof of
6.1
.

To show (
6.3
), fix a point
z_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{k}^{q^{\prime}}
, and let
\mathfrak{Y}
denote the cell associated to
z_{0}
as in
(
6.8
), with the constant
A
in the definition of the cell chosen to be
D+2/3
here. Observe that
\mathfrak{Y}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{k}^{q}
, as for any
z\in\mathfrak{Y}
we have

\displaystyle\mathtt{d}_{z}\mathtt{e}_{z;q}\geq(1-N^{-\delta/4})\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q}\geq(1-2N^{-\delta/4})^{2}\geq 4^{-k-1/2},

where the first bound holds by (
6.9
), the second holds by (
5.7
) (in
5.5
at
u=N^{(D+5)\delta}
, which applies by
5.4
), and the third holds since
z_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{k}^{q^{\prime}}
.

Since
\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{+}
,
it suffices by (
6.2
) and
2.17
to show (
6.3
) when the Glauber dynamics
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
are replaced by the Glauber dynamics restricted (in the sense of
Definition

2.15
) to
\mathfrak{Y}
, and with ceiling constraint given by
\mathcal{H}_{q}+N^{\delta-1}
(as in Definition
2.10
). Hence, (
6.2
) verifies w.o.p. the assumptions in
6.4
. The lemma then follows from that proposition with (
6.1
), using the fact that the
\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}
in
6.4
is equal (by
5.4
) to
\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q}\in[4^{-k},4^{1-k}]
.
∎

6.3.
Proof of
6.4

In this section we show
6.4
, whose proof will be similar to that of
[
33
, Claim 6.3]
, upon replacing the use of
[
33
, Theorem 4.4]
in the proof of
[
33
, Proposition 6.7]
with
3.28
here. Specifically (omitting factors of
N^{\delta}
in this informal discussion), we will first use
3.28
to match at
z_{0}
the values of
\mathcal{H}_{q}
and its gradient with those of some
q=0
limit shape
\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
, so that the latter is “less concave” than
\mathcal{H}_{q}
at
z_{0}
. This difference in concavity will ensure that
\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}-\mathcal{H}_{q}\gg N^{-1}
on the boundary
\partial\mathfrak{Y}
of the cell containing
z_{0}
. Hence, monotonicity will imply that the stationary measure for
(H_{t})
on
\mathfrak{Y}
, with boundary data
\mathcal{H}_{q}
on
\partial\mathfrak{Y}
, is lower than
\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
by at least
N^{-1}
. Since
\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
and
\mathcal{H}_{q}
are shifted to match at
z_{0}
, this will imply that
H_{t}
decreases by at least
N^{-1}
at
z_{0}
. By
3.21
, this corresponds (as in (
6.10
)) to decreasing
q
by
N^{-1}(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q})^{-1/2}
, in agreement with (
6.11
). We next implement this in detail.

Proof of
6.4
.

Let
z_{1},

a
,
b
, and
c
be as in
3.28
and, for lightness of notation, let
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
,
{\bm{u}}:={\bm{u}}_{z_{0};q}
, and
{\bm{w}}:={\bm{w}}_{z_{0};q}
. We let
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}
and
\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
be the macroscopic shapes
\mathcal{H}_{q}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
rescaled according to (
3.24
) and (
3.25
), respectively, namely,

(6.13)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}(\hat{x},\hat{y})&=\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/2}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}^{-3/2}\cdot\mathcal{H}_{q}(z_{0}+(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q})^{1/2}{\bm{u}}\hat{x}+\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}{\bm{w}}\hat{y})\\
\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}(\hat{x},\hat{y})&=\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/2}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}^{-3/2}\cdot\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(z_{1}+(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q})^{1/2}{\bm{u}}\hat{x}+\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}{\bm{w}}\hat{y}).\end{aligned}

If the argument of
\mathcal{H}_{q}
in (
6.13
) belongs to the
cell
\mathfrak{Y}
from (
6.8
), then the rescaled coordinates
\hat{x},\hat{y}
run
in the range

(6.14)

|\hat{x}|,|\hat{y}|\leq q^{-1/2}N^{(\frac{3}{4}A-\frac{1}{2})\delta-1/2}(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q})^{-3/4}=:R\leq N^{-\delta/2}\ll 1,

(where we used assumptions (
6.6
)). Thus, in these coordinates,
\mathfrak{Y}^{-}
and
\mathfrak{Y}
are square
\mathfrak{S}^{-}
and
\mathfrak{S}
of side-lengths
2L^{-1}R
and
2R
, respectively.

By Proposition
3.28
, we have that
\nabla\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}(0,0)=\nabla\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}(0,0)
and that
|\partial_{\gamma}\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}-\partial_{\gamma}\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}|\lesssim\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}q
on
\mathfrak{S}
, whenever
|\gamma|\geq 2
. Therefore, for any
\rho\in[0,1]
, we have

(6.15)

\displaystyle\sup_{|w|\leq\rho R}|\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}(w)-\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}(w)-\Delta\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/2}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}^{-3/2}|\lesssim\rho^{2}R^{2}\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}q\leq\rho^{2}N^{(\frac{3}{2}A-1)\delta-1}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}^{-3/2}\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/2},

where
\Delta=\mathcal{H}_{q}(z_{0})-\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(z_{1})
. The value of
\Delta
will play no role in the proof, so without loss of generality (and to lighten notations) we assume that it is zero. As such,
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}(0,0)=\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}(0,0)
.
The inequality (
6.15
), with (
6.13
), implies that the supremum norm difference between the two original functions
\mathcal{H}_{q}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
in the cell
\mathfrak{Y}

is bounded as

(6.16)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathfrak{Y}}|\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)-\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(z+z_{1}-z_{0})|=\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}^{1/2}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}^{3/2}\displaystyle\sup_{w\in\mathfrak{S}}|\widehat{\mathcal{H}}(w)-\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}(w)|\lesssim N^{(\frac{3}{2}A-1)\delta-1}.

We next estimate
\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}(w)-\widehat{\mathcal{H}}(w)
for
w\in\partial\mathfrak{S}
. To that end, Taylor expanding
\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
yields for any integer
K\geq 2\delta^{-1}
and point
w\in\partial\mathfrak{S}
that

\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}(w)-\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}(0,0)=w\cdot\nabla\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}(0,0)+\displaystyle\frac{w}{2}\cdot D^{2}\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}(0,0)\cdot w+\sum_{3\leq|\gamma|\leq K}w^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}(0,0)+O(|w|^{K+1}),

where
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
is a multi-index and
w^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}(0,0)
symbolically denotes the corresponding term in the Taylor expansion. By

3.27
and
3.28
(and recalling that
q^{\prime}=0
) we deduce for
w\in\mathfrak{S}
and a sufficiently large constant
C>1
that

(6.17)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \widecheck{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}(w)-\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}(0,0)&\geq w\cdot\nabla\widehat{\mathcal{H}}(0,0)+\displaystyle\frac{w}{2}\cdot D^{2}\widehat{\mathcal{H}}(0,0)\cdot w+\frac{1}{2C}\cdot\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}qR^{2}\\
&\qquad+\displaystyle\sum_{3\leq|\gamma|\leq K}w^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}\widehat{\mathcal{H}}(0,0)+O\big(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}qR^{3}\big)+O\big(R^{K+1}\big)\\
&\geq\widehat{\mathcal{H}}(w)-\widehat{\mathcal{H}}(0,0)+\frac{1}{4C}\cdot\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/2}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}^{-3/2}N^{(\frac{3}{2}A-1)\delta-1}+O(R^{K+1})\\
&\geq\widehat{\mathcal{H}}(w)-\widehat{\mathcal{H}}(0,0)+\frac{1}{8C}\cdot\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/2}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}^{-3/2}N^{(\frac{3}{2}A-1)\delta-1},\end{aligned}
