where in the second and third inequalities we absorbed

O\big(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}qR^{3}\big)

into
(2C)^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}qR^{2}
by
changing
(2C)^{-1}
to
(4C)^{-1}
, and then we absorbed
O(R^{K+1})=O(N^{-\delta K/2})
by
changing
(4C)^{-1}
to
(8C)^{-1}
(using the facts that
R\leq N^{-\delta/2}
and that
K\geq 2\delta^{-1}
).
Going back to the unrescaled limit shapes using (
6.13
), (
6.17
) implies for
z\in\partial\mathfrak{Y}
that

(6.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(z+z_{1}-z_{0})\geq\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)+\frac{1}{8C}\cdot N^{(\frac{3}{2}A-1)\delta-1}.

Therefore, by the assumptions of the proposition, the initial condition of the dynamic satisfies

(6.19)

\displaystyle H_{0}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)+N^{\delta-1}\leq\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(z+z_{1}-z_{0})-\frac{1}{10C}\cdot N^{(\frac{3}{2}A-1)\delta-1},\quad\text{for $z\in{\partial\mathfrak{Y}}$},

where the error term
N^{\delta-1}
has been absorbed by changing the constant
8
to
10
.
Let us now upper bound
H_{t}(z)
for
z\in\mathfrak{Y}^{-}
. Recall by assumption that the dynamics
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
are restricted to
\mathfrak{Y}
(in the sense of
Definition

2.15
; in particular, the height function
H_{t}
coincides with
H_{0}
at all times, outside of
\mathfrak{Y}
) and have the ceiling constraint
H_{+}:=\mathcal{H}_{q}+N^{\delta-1}
in
\mathfrak{Y}
. To upper bound
H_{t}
, we may by monotonicity (
2.17
) further impose on
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
the floor constraint
H_{-}:=\mathcal{H}^{\prime}-N^{\frac{3}{2}A\delta-1}
in
\mathfrak{Y}
. The distance
between this floor and ceiling is by (
6.16
) at most

CN^{\frac{3}{2}A\delta-1}
, for some constant
C>1
. So, the mixing time for these constrained dynamics
is by
2.13
at most

(6.20)

\displaystyle N^{2+\delta/2}\diam(\mathfrak{Y})^{2}\cdot N^{3A\delta}\leq q^{-1}N^{5A\delta+1}(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q})^{-1/2}\leq N^{-A\delta}\mathtt{t}.

To conclude the proof, let us introduce some notation. Let
\pi
denote the uniform measure on tilings
\eta
of the hexagon such that
H_{\eta}(z)=H_{0}(z)
for
z\notin\mathfrak{Y}
and
H_{-}(z)\leq H_{\eta}(z)\leq H_{+}(z)
for
z\in\mathfrak{Y}
. Also let
(H^{\eq}_{t})_{t\geq\mathtt{t}}
denote the Glauber dynamics restricted to
\mathfrak{Y}
, with ceiling constraint
H_{+}
and floor constraint
H_{-}
, with initial data
H_{\mathtt{t}}^{\eq}
at
t=\mathtt{t}
sampled under
\pi
; observe that this process is stationary. Further let
\bar{\eta}
be the tiling of
\mathfrak{X}
whose height function is maximal, subject to the constraints that
H_{\bar{\eta}}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(z+z_{1}-z_{0})-(10C)^{-1}\cdot N^{(\frac{3}{2}A-1)\delta-1}
for all
z\in\partial\mathfrak{Y}
. Additionally let
\mu
denote the uniform measure on tilings of
\mathfrak{X}
that coincide with
\bar{\eta}
outside of
\mathfrak{Y}
, and let
\mu^{+}
denote the measure obtained by conditioning
\mu
on the event that
H(z)\geq H_{-}(z)
for all
z\in\mathfrak{Y}
.

Thanks to (
6.20
) and (
2.4
), at time
t=\mathtt{t}
the law of
H_{t}
is of total variation distance smaller than any inverse power of
N
from
\pi
. Hence, we can couple the processes
(H_{t})_{t\geq\mathtt{t}}
and
(H^{\eq}_{t})_{t\geq\mathtt{t}}
so that, w.o.p., they coincide for all
t\geq\mathtt{t}
. Proposition
2.14
implies that the statement (
6.11
) holds w.o.p. simultaneously for all
t\in[\mathtt{t},N^{5}]
, provided that

(6.21)

\displaystyle H(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{\bar{q}}(z)\quad\forall z\in\mathfrak{Y}^{-},

holds w.o.p., where
H
is sampled under
\pi
.

To prove (
6.21
) under
\pi
, note that the distribution of the height function
H
in
\mathfrak{Y}
sampled from
\pi
is stochastically dominated (
Definition

2.5
), because of the ceiling constraint, by that of the height in
\mathfrak{Y}
, sampled from
\mu^{+}
. Next, the height fluctuation bound
8
8

8

Recall that
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
and therefore the right-hand side of (
6.19
), as a function of
z
, is the limit shape in
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
, up to a translation
z_{1}-z_{0}
of the hexagon, and to a global additive shift of the height. Therefore, we apply
3.17
to the hexagon
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
.

3.17
implies on one hand that
\|\mu-\mu^{+}\|
is smaller than any inverse power of
N
and on the other that, w.o.p. under
\mu
,

\big|H(z)-\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(z+z_{1}-z_{0})+\frac{1}{10C}\cdot N^{(\frac{3}{2}A-1)\delta-1}\big|\leq N^{\delta/4-1}\;\text{ for every }z\in\mathfrak{Y}.

Putting everything together we deduce that, w.o.p. under
\pi
and
for every

z\in\mathfrak{Y}
,

\displaystyle{H}(z)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(z+z_{1}-z_{0})+N^{\delta/4-1}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{10C}\cdot N^{(\frac{3}{2}A-1)\delta-1}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(z+z_{1}-z_{0})-\frac{1}{16C}\cdot N^{(\frac{3}{2}A-1)\delta-1}.

Now, if
z\in\mathfrak{Y}^{-}
then by (
6.15
) (with the
\rho
there to be
L^{-1}
defining
\mathfrak{Y}^{-}
here) and (
6.13
), we have
|\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(z+z_{1}-z_{0})-\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)|\leq N^{(\frac{3}{2}A-1)\delta-1}/(32C)
, if
L
is sufficiently large. This implies
H(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)-N^{(\frac{3}{2}A-1)\delta-1}/(32C)
. Finally, the fact that
3A/2-1\geq 5A/4
(as
A\geq 30
), together with
3.21
, (
6.10
), and (
6.9
) (with (
5.5
) and (
6.7
) to verify that
\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}\gg q-\bar{q}
, thereby enabling us to neglect the
\mathfrak{d}_{z}^{1/2}(q-\bar{q})^{3/2}
error terms in (
3.11
)) imply that
\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)-N^{(\frac{3}{2}A-1)\delta-1}/(32C)\leq\mathcal{H}_{\bar{q}}(z)
whenever (
6.10
) holds;
this establishes (
6.21
) and hence the proposition.
∎

Remark 6.6
.

The dimensions of the cell
\mathfrak{Y}
have been chosen in (
6.8
) exactly in such a way that the supremum norm difference between
\mathcal{H}_{q}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{Y}
is of order
N^{o(1)-1}
((
6.16
) provides the upper bound, and (
6.18
) provides the lower bound).

7.
Height reduction in the edge phase

7.1.
Proof of Lemma
5.7

In this section we show Lemma
5.7
using the following lemma, to be shown in
Section

7.4
. It essentially states the following. Assume that a height function is initially bounded above by
\mathcal{H}_{q}
on all of
\mathfrak{X}
, and by
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}
up to an annulus
\mathcal{A}_{k}^{q^{\prime}}(N^{A\delta})
in the edge phase (see the condition
k\geq\ell_{0}
below), for some
q^{\prime}\leq q
satisfying (
7.1
) (which is similar to the condition (
5.14
) from Lemma
5.7
). Then this height function will reduce under the Glauber dynamics to
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}+N^{\delta-1}
, on all of
\mathfrak{X}
. In this way, the stronger height upper bound on inner annuli “extends” (up to a small additive term
N^{\delta-1}
) to outer ones, unlike in the bulk mixing phase result
6.1
, where the weaker height upper bound “improves” on inner annuli (indeed, to show
5.7
, we will first use
5.6
to obtain a stronger height bound on the bulk liquid phase annuli, and then “extend them out” using
7.1
). See
Figure

12
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
K,D
satisfy (
5.1
).
Let
0<q\leq\varepsilon_{0}
,
denote
\ell_{0}=\ell_{0}(q)
and let
k\geq\ell_{0}
be an integer such that
q\geq 2^{k}N^{K\delta-1}
. Set

(7.1)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &q^{\prime}\in[q-2^{k}N^{D\delta-1},q];\qquad\mathsf{t}=2^{k}q^{-1}N^{1+11K\delta}\end{aligned}

and denote
\ell_{0}^{\prime}=\ell_{0}(q^{\prime})
. Consider the Glauber dynamics
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
starting from an initial condition
\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{+}
(recall
Definition

2.16
) and assume that, w.o.p. under
\mu
,

(7.2)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &H_{0}(w)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(w),\qquad\text{for each $w\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{q^{\prime}}(N^{A\delta})$};\\
&H_{0}(w)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}(w),\qquad\text{for each $w\in\mathfrak{X}$}.\end{aligned}

Then we have w.o.p. that, for any
t\in[\mathsf{t},N^{5}]
,

(7.3)

\displaystyle H_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)+N^{\delta-1},\qquad\text{for each $z\in\mathfrak{X}$}.

Proof of
5.7
.

We induct on
k
, under the assumption (less restrictive than (
5.14
)) that

(7.4)

\displaystyle q^{\prime}\in I_{k}:=\big[q-(N^{\delta}-k)2^{k}N^{(D+2)\delta-1},q-(k+1)2^{k}N^{(D-2)\delta-1}\big].

Note that we can assume that
k<N^{\delta}
, as otherwise
\mathsf{t}\geq N^{5}
and the claim of the lemma is empty.
Setting
\ell_{0}=\ell_{0}(q)
, first observe by
5.6
that the lemma holds for
k\leq\ell_{0}+4
. So, fix
k\geq\ell_{0}+5
, and assume that (
5.16
) (with the
k
there replaced by
k-1
) holds for all
q^{\prime}\in I_{k-1}
and
t\in[\mathsf{t}_{k-1},N^{5}]
, where
\mathsf{t}_{k-1}=q^{-1}(k-1)2^{k+2}N^{1+11K\delta}
.

Now assume that
q^{\prime}\in I_{k}
. Set
\mathsf{q}_{0}=q^{\prime}-2^{k+1}N^{D\delta/2-1}
and
q^{\prime\prime}=(q+\mathsf{q}_{0})/2
, observing that
\mathsf{q}_{0}\leq q^{\prime}\leq q^{\prime\prime}\leq q\leq 2\mathsf{q}_{0}
; also let
\mathsf{r}=2^{k+1}q^{-1}N^{1+11K\delta}
. Observe that
q-q^{\prime\prime}=q^{\prime\prime}-\mathsf{q}_{0}\in\big[k2^{k-1}N^{(D-2)\delta-1},(N^{\delta}-k+1)2^{k-1}N^{(D+2)\delta-1}\big]
. Thus, the inductive hypothesis (with the
(q,q^{\prime})
there equal to the
(q,q^{\prime\prime})
here) yields w.o.p. that
H_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime\prime}}(z)
for each
z\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{q^{\prime\prime}}(N^{A\delta})
and
t\in[\mathsf{t}_{k-1},N^{5}]
. Therefore,
7.1
(with the
(q,q^{\prime})
there equal to
(q,q^{\prime\prime})
here) yields w.o.p. that
H_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime\prime}}(z)+N^{\delta-1}
for all
z\in\mathfrak{X}
and
t\in[\mathsf{t}_{k-1}+\mathsf{r},N^{5}]
. Applying the inductive hypothesis again (now with the
(q,q^{\prime})
there equal to the
(q^{\prime\prime},\mathsf{q}_{0})
here) yields w.o.p. that
H_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{q}_{0}}(z)
for all
z\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{\mathsf{q}_{0}}(N^{A\delta})
and
t\in[2\mathsf{t}_{k-1}+\mathsf{r},N^{5}]
.
Again applying
7.1
(with the
(q,q^{\prime})
there equal to
(q^{\prime\prime},\mathsf{q}_{0})
here, and replacing
H_{t}
by
H_{t}-N^{\delta-1}
) then gives
H_{t}(z)-N^{\delta-1}\leq\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{q}_{0}}(z)+N^{\delta-1}
for all
z\in\mathfrak{X}
and
t\in[2\mathsf{t}_{k-1}+2\mathsf{r},N^{5}]
.

Since
2\mathsf{t}_{k-1}+2\mathsf{r}\leq\mathsf{t}
, it suffices to show that
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{q}_{0}}(z)+2N^{\delta-1}\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)
for each
z\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{k+1}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{q^{\prime}}(N^{A\delta})\subseteq\mathfrak{L}_{q^{\prime}}
. To do so, we consider two cases. First suppose that
z\in\mathfrak{L}_{\mathsf{q}_{0}}
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)-\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{q}_{0}}(z)

\displaystyle\gtrsim(q^{\prime}-\mathsf{q}_{0})(\mathtt{d}_{z}\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime}})^{1/2}\gtrsim N^{D\delta/2-1}\gg N^{\delta-1},

where in the first bound we used (
3.12
), in the second we used the facts that
q^{\prime}-\mathsf{q}_{0}=2^{k+1}N^{D\delta/2-1}
and
\mathtt{d}_{z}\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}\geq 4^{-k-1}
(as
z\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{k+1}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{q^{\prime}}
), and in the third we used that
D>5
. If
z\notin\mathfrak{L}_{\mathsf{q}_{0}}
, then since
z\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{k+1}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{q^{\prime}}(N^{A\delta})
, we have
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)-\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{q}_{0}}(z)\gtrsim\mathtt{d}_{z}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}^{3/2}-N^{\delta-1}\gtrsim N^{A\delta-1}
, where for the second inequality we used
3.18
(and the fact that, if
(\mathfrak{d}_{z},\mathfrak{e}_{z;q^{\prime}})\neq(\mathtt{d}_{z},\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime}})
, then
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)\lesssim N^{-1}
, which is quickly verified also by
3.18
). Hence, again
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)\geq\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{q}_{0}}(z)+2N^{\delta-1}
, which confirms the lemma.
∎

7.2.
Mixing in edge phase cells

As in
Section

6.2
, we will show
7.1
and
5.8
using “more local” height reduction results on certain mesoscopic cells; in this section we state those results (whose proofs will appear in Sections
8
and
9
). We begin with some notation. Throughout, we fix real numbers
B>A\geq 30
,
L\geq 10
, and

(7.5)

q\in(N^{5B\delta-1},\varepsilon_{0})

where we recall that
\varepsilon_{0}
is chosen small enough, but independent of
N
(so that the statements of
3.23
and
4.4
with
q_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}
hold, using
4.3
).
We further let
\mathfrak{d},\omega>0
be real numbers satisfying (the below conditions and choices will be explained in
7.4
)

(7.6)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}\in[N^{-1/2},1)

(7.7)

\displaystyle N^{-2A\delta}\max\{(qN)^{1/3},\mathfrak{d}^{-1}\}\leq\omega\leq\displaystyle\min\{\mathfrak{d}^{-1/3}N^{1/3-A\delta},qN^{1-3B\delta}\}.

Also set

(7.8)

\displaystyle{\bm{d}}=\max\big\{\mathfrak{d},L^{10}N^{B\delta}\omega^{1/2}(qN)^{-1/2}\big\},\quad{\bm{e}}=\min\big\{N^{2B\delta}\omega(q\mathfrak{d}N)^{-1},L^{-10}N^{B\delta}\omega^{1/2}(qN)^{-1/2}\big\},

which satisfy

(7.9)

\displaystyle{\bm{e}}\geq L^{-20}\mathfrak{d}^{-1/3}N^{-2/3},\qquad{\bm{d}}{\bm{e}}=N^{2B\delta}\omega(qN)^{-1}.

The second statement in (
7.9
) holds since
{\bm{d}}
takes the first alternative in (
7.8
) if and only if
{\bm{e}}
does. To see that the first statement in (
7.9
) also holds, note that when
{\bm{e}}=N^{2B\delta}\omega(q\mathfrak{d}N)^{-1}
, we have

\mathfrak{d}^{1/3}{\bm{e}}=N^{2B\delta}\omega(qN)^{-1}\mathfrak{d}^{-2/3}\geq N^{2B\delta}\omega(qN)^{-1}
,
since
\mathfrak{d}\leq 1
. This, together with the lower bound on
\omega
in (
7.7
) (using the fact that
B>A
and
q\leq 1
), implies that
\mathfrak{d}^{1/3}{\bm{e}}\geq N^{-2/3}
.
In the alternative case, we have from the minimum defining
{\bm{e}}
that

{\bm{e}}=L^{-10}N^{B\delta}\omega^{1/2}(qN)^{-1/2}\leq N^{2B\delta}\omega(q\mathfrak{d}N)^{-1}=(L^{10}{\bm{e}})^{2}\mathfrak{d}^{-1}.

Thus,

{\bm{e}}\geq L^{-20}\mathfrak{d},

and so

{\bm{e}}\mathfrak{d}^{1/3}\geq L^{-20}\mathfrak{d}^{4/3}\geq L^{-20}N^{-2/3},

where we have used the fact that
\mathfrak{d}\geq N^{-1/2}.

Definition 7.2
.

Given
(\mathfrak{d},q,\omega,L)
as above, we let
w_{0}
be a point in
\mathfrak{L}_{q}
satisfying the following conditions:

•

When
{\bm{d}}=\mathfrak{d}
, let
w_{0}\in\mathfrak{L}_{q}
be any point (which is not necessarily unique) satisfying

(7.10)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}={\bm{d}};\qquad L^{-1}{\bm{e}}\leq\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q}\leq L{\bm{e}}

so that, by (
7.9
), we have

(7.11)

\displaystyle L^{-1}N^{2B\delta}\omega(qN)^{-1}\leq\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q}\leq LN^{2B\delta}\omega(qN)^{-1}.

•

When
{\bm{d}}>\mathfrak{d}
, let
w_{0}\in\mathfrak{L}_{q}
denote the point with

\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}={\bm{d}}
, such that the orthogonal projection of
w_{0}
to
\ell_{w_{0}}
is the tangency point
\mathfrak{A}_{q}\cap\ell_{w_{0}}
.

The distinction of cases in (
7.8
) will be relevant in defining the following cells
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{Y}^{-}
around
w_{0}
, on which we will analyze the Glauber dynamics. See also
Figure

14
.

Definition 7.3
.

Given
(\mathfrak{d},q,\omega,L)
as above, and a point
w_{0}
satisfying the properties in
Definition

7.2
,
we define two subsets
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\overline{\mathfrak{X}}
and
\mathcal{Y}^{-}\subseteq\overline{\mathfrak{X}}
as follows. Set

(7.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}=\big\{z\in\overline{\mathfrak{X}}:\dist_{\bm{v}_{w_{0}}}(z,w_{0})\leq L^{2}(\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q})^{1/2},\dist_{\bm{w}_{w_{0}}}(z,w_{0})\leq L^{5}\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q}\big\},\qquad\text{if ${\bm{d}}=\mathfrak{d}$};

(7.13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}=\big\{z\in\overline{\mathfrak{X}}:\dist_{\bm{v}_{w_{0}}}(z,w_{0})\leq L^{2}N^{B\delta}{\bm{d}},\mathfrak{d}_{z}^{2}\leq L^{5}N^{2B\delta}{\bm{d}}^{2}\},\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\text{otherwise}.

Then define
\mathcal{Y}^{-}\subseteq\mathcal{Y}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}^{-}=(1/L)\cdot(\mathcal{Y}-w_{0})+w_{0},\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\text{if $\bm{d}=\mathfrak{d}$};

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}^{-}=\big\{z\in\overline{\mathfrak{X}}:\dist_{\bm{v}_{w_{0}}}(z,w_{0})\leq L{\bm{d}},\mathfrak{d}_{z}^{2}\leq L^{4}{\bm{d}}^{2}\},\qquad\text{otherwise}.

Figure 14.
The cell
\mathcal{Y}
of
Definition

7.3
, with the point
w_{0}
marked as a dot. In the case
\mathfrak{d}<{\bm{d}}
(a), the long sides of the cell are parallel to the boundary of the hexagon. In the case where
\mathfrak{d}={\bm{d}}
(b) they are parallel to
{\bm{u}}_{w_{0};q}
. The short sides are in both cases vertical (that is, orthogonal to the boundary of the hexagon, in the
(x,y)
coordinate system). Note that the cell is not entirely contained in the liquid region; in particular, in case (a) the short sides are in the frozen region.

Note that, while the sides of the cell
\mathfrak{Y}
are parallel to
{\bm{w}}_{w_{0};q},{\bm{u}}_{w_{0};q}
, those of
\mathcal{Y}
are parallel to
{\bm{v}}^{\perp}_{w_{0}},{\bm{u}}_{w_{0};q}
. This different choice is made for technical convenience. Note also that the angle between
{\bm{v}}^{\perp}_{w_{0}}
and
{\bm{w}}_{w_{0};q}
is of order
\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\lesssim 1
, so this modification will make little qualitative difference.

Remark 7.4
.

Let us briefly explain some of the choices and bounds above, by indicating how we will eventually proceed; in this informal discussion, we omit powers of
N^{\delta}
. We will
view
\omega N^{-1}
as the distance, in the
\bm{v}_{w_{0}}

direction, between the
N^{-1}
-quantiles of the height
function
\mathcal{H}_{q}
around some point

z\in\mathfrak{L}_{q}
in the edge phase; we will also interpret

\mathfrak{d}
as being of order
\mathfrak{d}_{z}
. Then, by the first statement of
3.18
, we have
\omega\sim(\mathfrak{d}_{z}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q})^{-1/2}
.

Since
z
is in the edge phase, we have by (
5.3
) that
\omega\sim(\mathfrak{d}_{z}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q})^{-1/2}\gtrsim q^{1/3}N^{1/3}
, as in the first lower bound on
\omega
in (
7.7
). Since
\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}\lesssim\mathfrak{d}_{z}
, we also have
\omega\sim(\mathfrak{d}_{z}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q})^{-1/2}\gtrsim\mathfrak{d}^{-1}
, as in second lower bound in (
7.7
). Since
\mathtt{e}_{z;q}\gtrsim\mathfrak{d}^{-1/3}N^{-2/3}
, we obtain
\omega\sim(\mathfrak{d}_{z}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q})^{-1/2}\lesssim\mathfrak{d}^{-1/3}N^{1/3}
, as in the first upper bound in (
7.7
). Since the dimensions of our cells in
Definition

7.3
are of order
(\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q})^{1/2}\times(\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q})
, and the second upper bound in (
7.7
) implies
\bm{d}\bm{e}\sim\omega(qN)^{-1}\ll 1
, which ensures that these cells are mesoscopic.

These cell dimensions
(\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q})^{1/2}\times(\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q})
are fixed so that, by (
3.8
), the cell is both partially inside and partially outside of the liquid region
\mathfrak{L}_{q}
. To explain the choice (
7.11
) of
\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q}
, observe that the “long dimension” (in the
\bm{u}_{w_{0}}
direction) of these cells is
(\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q})^{1/2}
. Upon identifying
\omega\sim(\mathfrak{d}_{z}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q})^{-1/2}
, (
7.11
) ensures that this is of order
(qN)^{-1/2}(\mathfrak{d}_{z}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q})^{-1/4}
, consistent with the long dimension of the cells
\mathfrak{Y}
from (
6.8
).

The parameter
{\bm{d}}
distinguishes between whether
w_{0}
is reasonably distant (
{\bm{d}}=\mathfrak{d}
) or too close (
{\bm{d}}>\mathfrak{d}
) to a tangency location. In particular, the specific choice (
7.8
) of
\bm{d}
is fixed to ensure that, in the first case (
\bm{d}=\mathfrak{d}
) of
Definition

7.3
, we have
\mathfrak{d}_{z}\asymp\mathfrak{d}
for all
z\in\mathcal{Y}
; see
8.2
below.

The following lemma verifies that certain points in the liquid region, whose values of
\mathfrak{d}
coincide with those of
w_{0}
, are contained in the above cells (observe that the condition (
7.14
) is in agreement with the beginning of
7.4
).

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
z\in\mathfrak{L}_{q}
be any point in the liquid region with

\mathfrak{d}_{z}=\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}=\mathfrak{d}
and
\ell_{z}=\ell_{w_{0}}
. In the case
\mathfrak{d}={\bm{d}}
, assume furthermore that

(7.14)

\omega\leq(\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q})^{-1/2}.

Then, if
L
in
Definition

7.3
is large enough,
z\in\mathcal{Y}^{-}
.

Proof.

First suppose that
\bm{d}<\mathfrak{d}
. Since
\mathfrak{d}_{z}=\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}
and
\dist(z,w_{0})\lesssim\mathfrak{d}
(as
z,w_{0}\in\mathfrak{L}_{q})
, we have from
Definition

7.3
that
z\in\mathcal{Y}^{-}
. So, suppose instead that
\bm{d}=\mathfrak{d}
.

Without loss of generality, assume that the closest tangency
point to
w_{0}=(x,y)=(x,\mathfrak{d}^{2})
is the SW one
(x^{SW}_{q},0)
and that
w_{0}
is to the right
of it (namely,
x\geq x^{SW}_{q}
). Note that
w_{0}
and
z
have the same
y
-coordinate
\mathfrak{d}^{2}
; let
\ell
be the (horizontal) line containing these points, and let
\mathbf{l}=(l,\mathfrak{d}^{2})
and
\mathbf{r}=(r,\mathfrak{d}^{2})
denote the left and right intersections of

\ell
with
\partial\mathcal{Y}^{-}
, respectively. Denoting
z=(x^{\prime},y)=(x^{\prime},\mathfrak{d}^{2})
, we then must show that
x^{\prime}\in[l,r]
. From
Definition

7.3
of
\mathcal{Y}^{-}
and (
3.8
), it follows (for sufficiently large
L
) that
\mathbf{r}\notin\mathfrak{L}_{q}
, so
x^{\prime}<r
since
z\in\mathfrak{L}_{q}
. It therefore suffices to verify that
x\geq l
, or equivalently that
\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}\leq\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q}
. Since
\mathfrak{d}_{z}=\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}
, we thus must show
\mathfrak{d}_{z}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}\leq\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q}
, which holds since
\mathfrak{d}_{z}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}\leq\omega^{-2}
(from (
7.14
)), since
L^{-1}N^{2B\delta}\omega(qN)^{-1}\leq\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q}
(from (
7.11
)), and by (
7.7
).
∎

Under this notation, the following results state how the tiling height function reduces in
\mathcal{Y}^{-}
, under the Glauber dynamics on
\mathcal{Y}
; see
Figure

15
and
Figure

16
for depictions. Proposition
7.6
and Proposition
7.7
will be proven in Sections
8
and
9
, respectively.

Proposition
7.6
essentially states that, assuming some weaker height upper bound (
7.17
) in the full cell
\mathcal{Y}
and stronger upper bound (
7.18
) in a part of it, we are able to “extend” this stronger upper bound to a larger region (
7.19
) within the smaller cell
\mathcal{Y}^{-}
(which is analogous to
7.1
). While
7.6
considers the edge phase in general, Proposition
7.7
specifically considers the arctic boundary, through the condition (
7.21
) (which is analogous to the second bound in (
5.18
)). It in fact improves the bounds there, by effectively reducing the constant
R
governing the upper bounds to
1
(which is analogous to
5.8
). The reason for the choices of
\mathsf{t}
(and
q^{\prime}
, for
7.6
) in the below propositions are similar to those explained in Remark
6.5
.

The statements of
7.6
and
7.7
, have been set up to be as analogous to each other as possible, so that their proofs will be quite similar. However, this will come at the expense of having to verify their hypotheses whenever we apply them in
Section

7.4
.

Proposition 7.6
.

The following holds if the constant
L
entering
Definition

7.3
is chosen to be sufficiently large. Let the constants
A,B,R
satisfy

(7.15)

\displaystyle B\geq 1000R\geq 1000A\geq 30000.

Let
q
satisfy (
7.5
); let
\mathfrak{d},\omega>0
satisfy (
7.6
) and (
7.7
); and let the point
w_{0}
around which the cell
\mathcal{Y}
is centered satisfy the assumptions in
Definition

7.2
. Also let
q^{\prime}
satisfy

(7.16)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &q^{\prime}\in[q-N^{R\delta-1}\omega,q].\end{aligned}

Further assume:

(i)

For all
w\in\mathcal{Y}
, we have

(7.17)

\displaystyle H_{0}(w)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}(w).

(ii)

For all
w\in\mathcal{Y}
such that
(\mathtt{d}_{w}\mathtt{e}_{w;q^{\prime}})^{1/2}\geq\omega^{-1}
and
\mathtt{d}_{w}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{w;q^{\prime}}^{3/2}\geq N^{A\delta-1}
, we have

(7.18)

\displaystyle H_{0}(w)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(w).

Apply the Glauber dynamics restricted to
\mathcal{Y}
with ceiling constraint
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}
in the region
\mathcal{R}:=\{w:(\mathtt{d}_{w}\mathtt{e}_{w;q^{\prime}})^{1/2}\geq\omega^{-1},\mathtt{d}_{w}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{w;q^{\prime}}^{3/2}\geq N^{A\delta-1}\}
and ceiling constraint
\mathcal{H}_{q}
in
\mathcal{Y}\setminus\mathcal{R}
. Then, w.o.p., it holds

(7.19)

\displaystyle H_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)+N^{\delta-1}

for each
t\in[q^{-1}N^{1+10B\delta}\omega,N^{5}]
and
z\in\mathcal{Y}^{-}
such that
\mathtt{d}_{z}\in(L^{-2}\mathfrak{d},L^{2}\mathfrak{d})
.

Figure 15.
Proposition
7.6
assumes the weaker bound
H\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}
in the (union of the blue and red) region in
\mathcal{Y}
below the dashed line (corresponding to points
z
where
(\mathfrak{d}_{z}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q^{\prime}})^{1/2}=\omega^{-1}
), and it assumes the stronger bound
H\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}
in the (yellow) region in
\mathcal{Y}
above the dashed line. It then deduces w.o.p. the improved bound
H_{t}\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}+N^{\delta-1}
in the (blue) region in
\mathcal{Y}^{-}
below the dashed line, after time
t\geq q^{-1}\omega N^{1+10B\delta}
.

Proposition 7.7
.

The following holds if the constant
L
entering
Definition

7.3
is chosen to be sufficiently large. Let the constants
A,B,R
satisfy

(7.20)

\displaystyle B\geq 1000R\geq 1000A\geq 30000.

Let
q
satisfy (
7.5
); let
\mathfrak{d},\omega>0
satisfy (
7.6
) and (
7.7
); and let the point
w_{0}
around which the cell
\mathcal{Y}
is centered satisfy the assumptions in
Definition

7.2
. Also suppose that

(7.21)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \omega\geq\mathfrak{d}^{-1/3}N^{1/3-R\delta}.\end{aligned}

Further assume:

(i)

For all
w\in\mathcal{Y}
, we have

(7.22)

\displaystyle H_{0}(w)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}(w)+N^{R\delta-1}.

(ii)

For all
w\in\mathcal{Y}\setminus\mathfrak{L}_{q}^{+}(R\delta)
, we have

(7.23)

\displaystyle H_{0}(w)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}(w).

Apply the Glauber dynamics restricted to
\mathcal{Y}
, with ceiling constraint
\mathcal{H}_{q}
on
\mathcal{Y}\setminus\mathfrak{L}_{q}^{+}(R\delta)
and
\mathcal{H}_{q}+N^{R\delta-1}
on
\mathcal{Y}\cap\mathfrak{L}_{q}^{+}(R\delta)
. Then, w.o.p., the following two statements hold for each
t\in[q^{-1}N^{1+10B\delta}\omega,N^{5}]
.

(1)

For each
z\in\mathcal{Y}^{-}
with
(\mathtt{d}_{z}\mathtt{e}_{z;q})^{1/2}\leq N^{R\delta}\omega^{-1}
and
\mathtt{d}_{z}\in(L^{-2}\mathfrak{d},L^{2}\mathfrak{d})
, we have

(7.24)

\displaystyle H_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)+N^{\delta-1}.

(2)

For each
z\in\mathcal{Y}^{-}\setminus\mathfrak{L}_{q}^{+}(\delta)
with
\mathtt{d}_{z}\in(L^{-2}\mathfrak{d},L^{2}\mathfrak{d})
, we have

\displaystyle H_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}(z).

Figure 16.
From top to bottom, the three dashed lines indicate the set of
z
satisfying
(\mathtt{d}_{z}\mathtt{e}_{z;q})^{1/2}=N^{R\delta}\omega^{-1}
; the boundary of
\mathfrak{L}_{q}^{+}(\delta)
; and the boundary of
\mathfrak{L}_{q}^{+}(R\delta)
. Proposition
7.7
assumes the weaker bound
H\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}+N^{R\delta-1}
in the (union of the red and blue) regions in
\mathcal{Y}
above the bottommost dashed line and the stronger bound
H\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}
in the (yellow) region below this dashed line. It then deduces w.o.p. in
\mathcal{Y}^{-}
the improved bounds
H_{t}\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}+N^{\delta-1}
in the (light blue) region (between the top two dashed lines), and
H_{t}\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}
in the (dark blue) region (between the bottom two dashed lines), after time
t\geq q^{-1}\omega N^{1+10B\delta}
.

7.3.
Proof of
5.8

As in the proof of
6.1
, it suffices by Proposition
2.17
to show (
5.19
), with the Glauber dynamic
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
replaced by the Glauber dynamics with ceiling constraint
\mathcal{H}_{q}+N^{K\delta-1}
on the whole
\mathfrak{X}
and
\mathcal{H}_{q}
on the complement of
\mathfrak{L}_{q}^{+}({K\delta})
. So, we let
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
denote the latter dynamic for the remainder of this section.

Fix a point
z_{0}\in\mathfrak{X}
satisfying (
5.18
). We must show w.o.p. that, for all
t\in[\mathsf{t},N^{5}]
, we have
H_{t}(z_{0})\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z_{0})+N^{\delta/4-1}
and
H_{t}(z_{0})\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z_{0})
if
z_{0}\notin\mathfrak{L}_{q^{\prime}}^{+}(\delta)
. It suffices to show w.o.p. that, for all
t\in[\mathsf{t},N^{5}]
, we have

(7.25)

\displaystyle H_{t}(z_{0})\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}(z_{0})+\frac{1}{2}\cdot N^{\delta/4-1},\qquad\text{and}\qquad H_{t}(z_{0})\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}(z_{0}),\quad\text{if $z_{0}\notin\mathfrak{L}_{q}^{+}(\delta/2)$}.

Indeed, the sufficiency of the first bound in (
7.25
) follows from the bounds

(7.26)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q}(z_{0})-\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z_{0})

\displaystyle\lesssim(q-q^{\prime})(\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q^{\prime}})^{1/2}+(q-q^{\prime})^{3/2}\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{1/2}\lesssim N^{K\delta/3-1/3}\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{1/3}(q-q^{\prime})\leq N^{-1},

where the first statement follows from
3.21
; the second and the third from (
5.18
). The sufficiency of the second bound in (
7.25
) follows from the fact that the distance between the arctic boundaries
\mathfrak{A}_{q^{\prime}}
and
\mathfrak{A}_{q}
on the level
\{z:\mathtt{d}_{z}=\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\}
is at most
N^{\delta}(q-q^{\prime})\leq\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/3}N^{-2/3-\delta}
, again by
3.19
. Therefore, by Definition
3.15
of augmented liquid region (together with
3.16
), for
z_{0}\not\in\mathfrak{L}_{q}^{+}(\delta/2)
, we have
\mathcal{H}_{q}(z_{0})=\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z_{0})
, so the second statement of (
5.19
) follows from that of (
7.25
).

To show (
7.25
) (and thus
5.8
), we will use
7.7
, whose hypotheses are verified through the below lemma.

Lemma 7.8
.

The assumptions of
7.7
are satisfied with the parameters
(\mathfrak{d},\omega;A,B,R;\delta)
there given by
(\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}},N^{-K\delta/2}(\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q})^{-1/2};2K,4000K,4K;\delta/4)
here.

Proof of
5.8
.

By
7.8
, we may apply
7.7
; taking
w_{0}
as in
Definition

7.2
and
the associated cells
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{Y}^{-}
as in
Definition

7.3
, it
is quickly verified via
7.5
that

z_{0}\in\mathcal{Y}^{-}
. Since the initial condition
\mu
belongs to

\mathcal{P}^{+}
, by Proposition
2.17
it suffices to show (
5.19
), with the Glauber dynamic
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
replaced by the Glauber dynamics restricted (in the sense of
Definition

2.15
) to
\mathcal{Y}
. Then,
7.7
yields
(
7.25
) w.o.p. for

q^{-1}N^{1+10B\delta/4}\omega\leq t\leq N^{5}
. Since

B=4000K
and

\omega\leq(\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q})^{-1/2}\leq\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/3}N^{1/3}
, and we also have

\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\geq N^{-K\delta}\mathtt{d}
, we have from the
definition of
\mathsf{t}
that

\mathsf{t}\geq q^{-1}N^{1+10000K\delta}\omega
, which implies the
lemma.
∎

Proof of
7.8
.

We need to check that

(7.27)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \max\big\{N^{-K\delta}(qN)^{1/3},N^{-K\delta}\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1},\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/3}N^{1/3-K\delta}\big\}&\leq N^{-K\delta/2}(\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q})^{-1/2}\\
&\leq\min\{\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/3}N^{1/3-K\delta/2},qN^{1-3000K\delta}\}.\end{aligned}

First observe that
\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/3}N^{1/3-K\delta}\leq N^{-K\delta/2}\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q}^{-1/2}\leq\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/3}N^{1/3-K\delta/2}
follows from the facts that
\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q}\geq\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/3}N^{-2/3}
(by its definition) and
\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q}^{3/2}\leq N^{K\delta-1}
by (
5.18
).
We also have
N^{-K\delta/2}(\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q})^{-1/2}\geq N^{-K\delta}\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1}
, as
\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q}\lesssim\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}
(by (
3.7
)). We have that
N^{-K\delta}(qN)^{1/3}\leq N^{-K\delta/2}(\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q})^{-1/2}
since
q\leq 1
and
(\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q})^{-1/2}\geq(\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q}^{3/2})^{-1/3}\geq N^{1/3-K\delta/3}
(the first since
\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\leq 1
and the second by (
5.18
)). Finally,
N^{-K\delta/2}(\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q})^{-1/2}\leq qN^{1-3000K\delta}
since
q\geq\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/3}N^{3500K\delta-2/3}
and
\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q}^{3/2}\geq N^{-1}
. This verifies (
7.27
).
∎

7.4.
Proof of
7.1

Since
\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{+}
, to prove
7.1
it suffices (as in the proof of
6.1
) by
2.17
to show (
7.3
) when the Glauber dynamics
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
are replaced by the Glauber dynamics with ceiling constraint
\mathcal{H}_{q}
on all of

\mathfrak{X}
, and the stronger ceiling constraint
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}
in
\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{q^{\prime}}(N^{A\delta})
. Therefore, for the remainder of this section, we will let
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
denote the latter dynamics. In what follows, we fix a point
z_{0}\in\mathfrak{X}
.

The following lemma directly addresses several “boundary cases” for
z_{0}
.

Lemma 7.9
.

The bound (
7.3
) holds for any
t\in[\mathsf{t},N^{5}]
, w.o.p., either if
z_{0}\in\overline{\mathfrak{L}}_{q^{\prime}}
and
\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q^{\prime}}\leq N^{-6D\delta}4^{-k}
, or if
z_{0}\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{q^{\prime}}(N^{A\delta})
. Additionally, if w.o.p. (
7.3
) holds for any
t\in[\mathsf{t},N^{5}]
and
z_{0}\in\overline{\mathfrak{L}}_{q^{\prime}}
, then w.o.p. (
7.3
) holds for any
t\in[\mathsf{t},N^{5}]
and
z_{0}\in\mathfrak{X}
.

Proof.

The fact that (
7.3
) holds if
z_{0}\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{q^{\prime}}(N^{A\delta})
follows on the ceiling constraint for
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
mentioned above. Next, if
z_{0}\in\overline{\mathfrak{L}}_{q^{\prime}}
and
\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q^{\prime}}\leq N^{-6D\delta}4^{-k}
, then

\displaystyle H_{t}(z_{0})\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}(z_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z_{0})+N^{\delta}(q-q^{\prime})(\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q^{\prime}})^{1/2}+N^{\delta}\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{1/2}(q-q^{\prime})^{3/2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z_{0})+N^{\delta-D\delta-1}+2^{3k/2}N^{4D\delta-3/2}\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{1/2},

where in the first bound we used the ceiling constraint mentioned above on
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
; in the second we used
3.21
; in the third we used the facts that
0\leq q-q^{\prime}\leq 2^{k}N^{2D\delta-1}
and
\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0}}\leq N^{-6D\delta}4^{-k}
. Since
\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q^{\prime}}^{3/2}\geq N^{-1}
and
\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q^{\prime}}^{1/2}\leq N^{-3D\delta}2^{-k}
, we have that
\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\leq 8^{-k}N^{1-9D\delta}
, and so insertion into the above inequality yields
H_{t}(z_{0})\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z_{0})+N^{-1-\delta}+N^{-1-D\delta/2}\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z_{0})+N^{\delta-1}
, confirming (
7.3
).

Next, suppose that
z_{0}\notin\overline{\mathfrak{L}_{q^{\prime}}}
. If
z_{0}
is
in a connected component of

\mathfrak{X}\setminus\overline{\mathfrak{L}}_{q^{\prime}}
for which

\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z_{0})
is maximal, then we have

H_{t}(z_{0})\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z_{0})<\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z_{0})+N^{\delta-1}
. Otherwise, if

z_{0}\notin\mathfrak{L}_{q^{\prime}}
, then there is a point

z_{0}^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathfrak{L}}_{q^{\prime}}
(obtained by intersecting
\mathfrak{A}_{q^{\prime}}
with the line through
z_{0}
orthogonal to
\ell_{z_{0}}
) for which

\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z_{0})=\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z_{0}^{\prime})
and

H(z_{0})\leq H(z_{0}^{\prime})
for any admissible height function

H:\mathfrak{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Hence, if

H_{t}(z_{0}^{\prime})\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z_{0}^{\prime})+N^{\delta-1}
holds w.o.p., then we would have

H_{t}(z_{0})\leq H_{t}(z_{0}^{\prime})\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z_{0}^{\prime})+N^{\delta-1}=\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z_{0})+N^{\delta-1}
w.o.p., confirming (
7.3
).
∎

In view of Lemma
7.9
, for the remainder of this section, we will assume that

(7.28)

\displaystyle z_{0}\in\overline{\mathfrak{L}}_{q^{\prime}};\qquad z_{0}\notin\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{q^{\prime}}(N^{A\delta});\qquad N^{-6D\delta}4^{-k}\leq\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q^{\prime}}.

Now set

(7.29)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}=\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}};\qquad\omega=\min\{2^{k},\mathfrak{d}^{-1/3}N^{1/3-A\delta}\}.

Lemma 7.10
.

The parameters
(q,\mathfrak{d},\omega)
satisfy the inequalities (
7.7
), with the
(A,B)
there equal to
(A,K/3)
here.

Proof.

The bound
\omega\leq 2^{k}\leq qN^{1-K\delta}
follows from our assumed lower bound on
q
; the bound
\omega\leq\mathfrak{d}^{-1/3}N^{1/3-A\delta}
follows from our definition of
\omega
;
the bound
N^{-2A\delta}\mathfrak{d}^{-1}\leq\omega
follows from the facts that
\mathfrak{d}\geq N^{-1/2}
and that
N^{-2A\delta}\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1}\lesssim N^{-2A\delta}(\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q^{\prime}})^{-1/2}\leq N^{(3D-2A)\delta}2^{k}\ll 2^{k}
(the first by (
3.7
), the second by the last bound in (
7.28
), and the third by (
5.1
)); and the bound
q^{1/3}N^{1/3-2A\delta}\leq\omega
follows from the bounds
q^{1/3}N^{1/3-2A\delta}\leq N^{1/3-2A\delta}\leq\mathfrak{d}^{-1/3}N^{1/3-A\delta}
and
2^{k}\geq 2^{\ell_{0}}\gtrsim q^{1/3}N^{1/3-A\delta/2}
by the fact that
k\geq\ell_{0}
and (
5.4
), with the fact that
\omega=\min\{2^{k},\mathfrak{d}^{-1/3}N^{1/3-A\delta}\}
.
∎

Now let
L\geq 10
be a sufficiently large real number so that Lemma
7.5
holds. Further let
w_{0}
be a point in
\mathfrak{L}_{q}
satisfying the properties in
Definition

7.2
. Then define the rectangular cells
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{Y}^{-}
with respect to
(\mathfrak{d},q,\omega,L)
and
w_{0}
, as in
Definition

7.3
.

Lemma 7.11
.

We have that
z_{0}\in\mathcal{Y}^{-}
.

Proof.

The claim holds by
7.5
if either we are in the second case of
Definition

7.3
(
\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\neq\bm{d}
), or if

\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q}\leq 4^{-k}
(as, in the latter case, (
7.14
) holds by our definition (
7.29
) of
\omega
). Therefore, we can assume that we are in the first case of
7.5
, i.e.,
\bm{d}=\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}=\mathfrak{d}
. Observe that

(7.30)

\displaystyle N^{A\delta-1}\geq\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q^{\prime}}^{3/2}\gtrsim\mathfrak{d}^{1/2}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}^{3/2},

where the first bound holds by the second statement in (
7.28
) and the second holds by (
5.7
) (applied with the
u
there equal to
N^{A\delta}
here, using (
7.1
)).

As in the proof of
7.5
, it is enough to show that
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}\leq\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q}
. To do so, observe by (
7.11
) (at
B=K/3
) that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q}\geq L^{-1}N^{2K/3\delta}\omega(q\mathfrak{d}N)^{-1}.

Since (
7.30
) implies that
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}\lesssim\mathfrak{d}^{-1/3}N^{2A\delta/3-2/3}
, it suffices to show for a sufficiently large constant
C>1
that

(7.31)

\displaystyle\omega\geq CLq\mathfrak{d}^{2/3}N^{1/3+\delta(2A/3-2K/3)}.

Since
\omega\geq N^{-2A\delta}(qN)^{1/3}
by (
7.7
), this holds by (
5.1
) and bound
d,q\lesssim 1
.
∎

By
7.11
(and the fact that
\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{+}
), to show
7.1
it suffices by
2.17
to prove (
7.3
) (with the
z
there equal to
z_{0}
here) when the dynamics
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
are further restricted to
\mathcal{Y}
(as in Definition
2.15
). Therefore, in the remainder of this section, we will let
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
denote the latter dynamics. Then the next lemma verifies (
7.3
) for certain
z
(satisfying (
7.32
) below).

Lemma 7.12
.

Setting
\mathsf{t}^{\prime}=\mathsf{t}/2
, we have w.o.p. that, for any
z\in\mathfrak{X}
and
t\in[\mathsf{t}^{\prime},N^{5}]
,

(7.32)

\displaystyle H_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z) +N^{\delta-1},\quad\text{if either $q-q^{\prime}\leq\mathtt{d}_{z}^{-1/3}N^{A\delta-2/3}$ or $\mathtt{d}_{z}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}^{3/2}\geq N^{4A\delta-1}$.}

Proof.

It suffices to show the lemma at
z=z_{0}
. This would follow from
7.6
(with the
R
there equal to
2A
here), upon verifying the assumptions there; by (
5.1
) and
7.10
, we must confirm (
7.16
), (
7.17
), and (
7.18
). Since
\omega^{-1}\geq 2^{-k}
by (
7.29
), the bounds (
7.17
) and (
7.18
) follow from (
7.2
). Moreover, (
7.16
) holds whenever
q-q^{\prime}\leq N^{2A\delta-1}\omega
. If
\mathtt{d}_{z}^{-1/3}N^{1/3-A\delta}\geq 2^{k}
, then
\omega=2^{k}
, so by (
7.1
) we have
q-q^{\prime}\leq 2^{k}N^{D\delta-1}\leq N^{2A\delta-1}\omega
, confirming (
7.16
) and thus the lemma.

So, assume instead that
\omega=\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/3}N^{1/3-A\delta}<2^{k}
. If
q-q^{\prime}\leq\mathtt{d}_{z}^{-1/3}N^{A\delta-2/3}
, then
q-q^{\prime}\leq N^{2A\delta-1}\omega
, again confirming (
7.16
) and thus the lemma; this addresses the first case in (
7.32
). To address the second, it suffices to show that
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z_{0})<N^{4A\delta-1}
(when
\omega<2^{k}
). To that end, since
z_{0}\notin\mathcal{A}_{j}^{q^{\prime}}(N^{A\delta})
for any
j\leq k
(by (
7.28
)), we may assume that
z_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}^{q^{\prime}}
for some
j>k
. In that case,
\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q^{\prime}}\leq 4^{-k}\leq\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{2/3}N^{2A\delta-2/3}
(as
2^{k}>\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1/3}N^{1/3-A\delta}
), so
\mathtt{d}_{z_{0}}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z_{0};q^{\prime}}^{3/2}\ll N^{4A\delta-1}
, confirming that the second case in (
7.32
) does not hold.
∎

If
q-q^{\prime}>\mathtt{d}_{z}^{-1/3}N^{A\delta-2/3}
(and
\mathtt{d}_{z}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}^{3/2}<N^{A\delta-1}
), then
q-q^{\prime}
is too large to directly satisfy (
7.16
), so we cannot immediately apply
7.6
. In this case, we will inductively increase the “range” for
q-q^{\prime}
, by a certain increment
u(z)
, at which we can obtain improved estimates.

More precisely, given
z\in\mathfrak{X}
, let
u(z)=N^{-25A\delta-2/3}\mathtt{d}_{z}^{-1/3}
if
q-q^{\prime}\leq N^{A\delta-2/3}\mathtt{d}_{z}^{-1/3}
. If instead
q-q^{\prime}>N^{A\delta-2/3}\mathtt{d}_{z}^{-1/3}
then let
u(z)\in[N^{-25A\delta-2/3}\mathtt{d}_{z}^{-1/3}/2,N^{-25A\delta-2/3}\mathtt{d}_{z}^{-1/3}]
be any real number satisfying
(q-q^{\prime})/u(z)\in\mathbb{Z}
. Under this notation, we have the following lemma that quickly implies
7.1
; it will be shown by induction.

Lemma 7.13
.

For any point
z\in\mathfrak{X}
and integer
m\in[0,(q-q^{\prime})/u(z)]
, the following two statements hold w.o.p., for each
t\in[\mathsf{t}^{\prime}+mN^{10K\delta+2}u(z),N^{5}]
. First,

(7.33)

\displaystyle H_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q-mu(z)}(z) +N^{\delta-1}.

Second, we have

(7.34)

\displaystyle H_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q-mu(z)}(z),\qquad\text{if $z\notin\mathfrak{L}_{q-mu(z)}^{+}(\delta)$}.

Proof of
7.1
.

Recall we have fixed
z_{0}\in\mathfrak{X}
. If
q-q^{\prime}\leq u(z_{0})
, then (
7.3
) follows from (
7.32
). If instead
z_{0}\in\mathfrak{X}
satisfies
q-q^{\prime}\geq u(z_{0})
, then taking
m=(q-q^{\prime})/u(z_{0})\in\mathbb{Z}
in (
7.33
) yields (
7.3
), since
\mathsf{t}^{\prime} +(q-q^{\prime})N^{10K\delta+2}<\mathsf{t}
.
∎

It remains to show (
7.33
) and (
7.34
). They hold when
m=0
because we imposed the ceiling
\mathcal{H}_{q}
everywhere in
\mathfrak{X}
. So, it suffices to show them for
m>0
; we also know them if
q-q^{\prime}\leq N^{A\delta-2/3}\mathsf{d}_{z}^{-1/3}
(and
m>0
), by (
7.32
).
Let
\mathcal{S}
denote the set of real numbers of the form
\mathsf{t}^{\prime}+mN^{10K\delta+2}u(z)
that are less than
N^{5}
, where
m>0
ranges over all positive integers and
z\in\mathfrak{X}\cap\mathbb{T}_{N}
ranges across all points such that
q-q^{\prime}>N^{A\delta-2/3}\mathsf{d}_{z}^{-1/3}
.
Observe that
|\mathcal{S}|\leq N^{11}
, since
(q-q^{\prime})/u(z)\in\mathbb{Z}
and
u(z)\geq N^{-25A\delta-2/3}\mathsf{d}_{z}^{-1/3}/2\geq N^{-6/7}
.

It suffices to show that, for any
s\in\mathcal{S}
, (
7.33
) and (
7.34
) hold w.o.p. for all
t\in[s,N^{5}]
, where the
m
in those equations is given by
\lfloor(s-\mathsf{t}^{\prime})/(N^{10K\delta+2}u(z))\rfloor
.
To that end, induct on
s
. More specifically, we fix
s\in\mathcal{S}
with
s=\mathsf{t}^{\prime}+mN^{10K\delta+2}u(z)
, for some point
z\in\mathfrak{X}
and integer
m\in[1,(q-q^{\prime})/u(z)]
. We then prove (
7.33
) and (
7.34
) for all
t\in[s,N^{5}]
, assuming they hold for all
(m^{\prime},z^{\prime},t)
such that
\mathsf{t}^{\prime}+m^{\prime}N^{10K\delta+2}u(z^{\prime})<s
and
t\in[\mathsf{t}^{\prime}+m^{\prime}N^{10K\delta+2}u(z^{\prime}),N^{5}]
.

Set

\displaystyle q^{\prime\prime}=q-(m-1)u(z);\qquad s^{\prime}=s-N^{10K\delta+2}u(z).

By symmetry, we may assume that the closest tangency location, between
\mathfrak{A}_{q^{\prime\prime}}
and
\partial\mathfrak{X}
, to
z
is the SW one
p_{q^{\prime\prime}}^{SW}
; we may also assume that
z\in\mathfrak{L}_{q^{\prime\prime}}^{+}(2\delta)
(as otherwise (
7.34
) would follow from applying it to the point where
\mathfrak{L}_{q^{\prime\prime}}^{+}(2\delta)
meets the line through
z
parallel to
\ell_{z}
).

Lemma 7.14
.

The assumptions of
7.7
are verified if the
(A,R,B,\delta)
there equal to
(2A,10A,K/3,\delta/2)
here; the
(q,\mathfrak{d},\omega)
there equal to
(q^{\prime\prime},\mathtt{d}_{z},\mathtt{d}_{z}^{-1/3}N^{1/3-2A\delta})
here; and the
H_{0}
there equals to
H_{s^{\prime}}
here. Moreover,
z
is in the cell
\mathcal{Y}^{-}
of the proposition.

Proof of
7.13
.

Applying
7.7
using
7.14
, we obtain by its second statement that
H_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime\prime}}(z)
, for any
t\in[\mathsf{t}^{\prime}+mN^{10K\delta+2}u(z),N^{5}]
and
z\notin\mathfrak{L}_{q^{\prime\prime}}^{+}(\delta/2)
. Also, since
\mathfrak{L}_{q^{\prime\prime}}^{+}(\delta/2)\subseteq\mathfrak{L}_{q^{\prime\prime}-u(z)}^{+}(\delta)
(by
3.19
and
3.16
, with
3.20
, as
u(z)\leq\mathsf{d}_{z}^{-1/3}N^{-25A\delta-2/3}\ll\mathsf{d}_{z}^{-1/3}N^{\delta/2-2/3}
), we have for
z\notin\mathfrak{L}_{q^{\prime\prime}-u(z)}^{+}(\delta)
that
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime\prime}-u(z)}(z)=\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime\prime}}(z)
. Hence,
H_{t}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime\prime}}(z)=\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime\prime}-u(z)}(z)=\mathcal{H}_{q-mu(z)}(z)
, so (
7.34
) holds.

To show (
7.33
), we may assume that
\mathtt{d}_{z}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime\prime}-u(z)}^{3/2}\leq N^{4A\delta-1}
, as otherwise (
7.34
) would follow from (
7.32
). Then, we have
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime\prime}-u(z)}(z)\geq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime\prime}}(z)-N^{-1}
by
3.21
, as
u(z)\leq\mathsf{d}_{z}^{-1/3}N^{-25A\delta-2/3}
and
\mathtt{d}_{z}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime\prime}-u(z)}^{3/2}\leq N^{4A\delta-1}
implies that
u(z)(\mathfrak{d}_{z}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q^{\prime\prime}-u(z)})^{1/2}+u(z)^{3/2}\mathfrak{d}_{z}^{1/2}\ll N^{-1}
. Therefore, we would deduce (
7.33
) from (
7.24
) if we have
(\mathtt{d}_{z}\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime\prime}})^{1/2}\leq N^{4A\delta}\omega^{-1}
. Recalling that
\omega=\mathtt{d}_{z}^{-1/3}N^{1/3-2A\delta}
, this is equivalent to
\mathtt{d}_{z}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime\prime}}^{3/2}\leq N^{18A\delta-1}
, which holds since
\mathtt{d}_{z}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime\prime}-u(z)}^{3/2}\leq N^{4A\delta-1}
. This establishes (
7.33
) and (
7.34
), and hence the lemma, in both cases.
∎

Proof of
7.14
.

Observe under this choice of parameters that (
7.21
) holds, and as does (
7.7
), where the last bound
qN^{1-3B\delta}\geq\omega
in (
7.7
) holds since
\omega\leq N^{1+30A\delta}u(z)\leq N^{1+30A\delta}(q-q^{\prime})\leq 2^{k}N^{31A\delta}\leq N^{1-K\delta/2}q
, by (
5.1
) and our assumptions on
(q,q^{\prime})
(and the estimates in (
7.7
) are quickly verified). It remains to verify (
7.22
) and (
7.23
). For the former, observe by (
7.32
) that
H_{s^{\prime}}(w)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(w) +N^{5A\delta-1}\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime\prime}}(w) +N^{5A\delta-1}
holds w.o.p. for all
w\in\mathfrak{X}
; this confirms (
7.22
) under our parameter choices.

Next we verify (
7.23
). By the inductive hypothesis (
7.34
) (applied with that
z
equal to
w
here) we have that
H_{s^{\prime}}(w)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime\prime\prime}}(w)
w.o.p. whenever
w\notin\mathfrak{L}_{q^{\prime\prime\prime}}^{+}(\delta)
, where
q^{\prime\prime\prime}=q-\lfloor(s^{\prime}-\mathsf{t}^{\prime})N^{-10K\delta-2}/u(w)\rfloor u(w)
. Since
s^{\prime}-\mathsf{t}^{\prime}=s-\mathsf{t}^{\prime}-N^{10K\delta+2}u(z)=(m-1)N^{10K\delta+2}u(z)
and
(m-1)u(z)=q-q^{\prime\prime}
, we have that

\displaystyle(s^{\prime}-\mathsf{t}^{\prime})N^{-10K\delta-2}u(w)^{-1}=(m-1)u(z)u(w)^{-1}=(q-q^{\prime\prime})u(w)^{-1}.

As such,
q^{\prime\prime\prime}\leq q^{\prime\prime}+2u(w)
, so
H_{s^{\prime}}(w)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime\prime}+2u(w)}(w)
. By
3.19
(and
3.20
), for some constant
C>1
, the distance in the
\bm{v}_{w}
-direction between
\mathfrak{A}_{q^{\prime\prime}+2u(w)}
and
\mathfrak{A}_{q^{\prime\prime}}
is at most
Cu(w)\leq C\mathtt{d}_{w}^{-1/3}N^{-25A\delta-2/3}
. It quickly follows that, if
w\notin\mathfrak{L}_{q^{\prime\prime}}^{+}(10A\delta)
, then
w\notin\mathfrak{L}_{q^{\prime\prime}+2u(w)}^{+}(\delta)
. So,
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime\prime}+2u(w)}(w)=\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime\prime}}(w)
for all
w\notin\mathfrak{L}_{q^{\prime\prime}}^{+}(10A\delta)
. Therefore,
H_{s^{\prime}}(w)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime\prime}+2u(w)}(w)=\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime\prime}}(w)
for
w\notin\mathfrak{L}_{q^{\prime\prime}}^{+}(10A\delta)
, confirming (
7.23
) under our parameter choices. This shows the first statement of the lemma. The second follows from
7.11
.
∎

8.
Proof of Proposition
7.6

In this section, we prove
7.6
. Recall from
Section

6.3
that the proof of
6.4
was based on the notion (justified by
3.28
) that the normalized limit shape
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q}
from (
3.24
) is “more concave” than
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{a,b,c}
. This reasoning does not seem to directly apply to show
7.6
, since
\mathfrak{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}
is not approximately constant within the cell
\mathcal{Y}^{-}
. Thus the scaling required to define this renormalized height function
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q^{\prime}}
will vary considerably, thereby skewing the notion of concavity, across
\mathcal{Y}^{-}
.

To remedy this, we will instead study level lines of
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}
, using
3.26
(in place of
3.28
) to realize that those of
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}
are “more convex” than are those of
\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
. We will discuss this in more detail in the beginning of
Section

8.1.2
below.

Remark 8.1
.

Because of the definition (
7.8
) of
{\bm{e}}
and of the upper bound
(
7.7
) on
\omega
, we have
{\bm{e}}\leq N^{-B\delta/2}
. The same considerations show that the product

\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q}
in (
7.11
) is at most
N^{-B\delta}
. Therefore, for
N
large the whole cell
\mathcal{Y}
(recall
Definition

7.3
) is within a distance
o(1)
(with respect to
N
)
from the arctic boundary. Without loss of generality, we will
prove Propositions
7.6
and
7.7
only in the case where

w_{0}
is closer to
p^{SW}_{q}
than to any of
the other tangency points of
\mathfrak{A}_{q}
. In fact, all other cases can be reduced to this one by
symmetry. In addition, we will only consider the case where the
x
-coordinate
of
w_{0}
is at least
x^{SW}_{q}
(recall
Definition

3.8
). The proof of the propositions in
the alternative case is entirely analogous.

In view of this discussion, the cell
\mathcal{Y}
is close to the frozen
region
\mathfrak{F}^{S}_{q}
, where the limit
shape
\mathcal{H}_{q}
is zero. By
3.18
, we
have then, for
z\in\mathcal{Y}\cap\mathfrak{L}_{q}
whose
x
-coordinate is at least
x^{SW}_{q}
, that

(8.1)

\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)\asymp\mathfrak{d}_{z}^{1/2}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}^{3/2}.

Note for future reference that

(8.2)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{q-q^{\prime}}{q}\right|\leq N^{-B\delta}

as follows from conditions (
7.16
) and (
7.15
), together with the upper bound in (
7.7
).
Recall that, as in the statement of
7.6
, the constant
L
is assumed to be large enough (but independent of
N
).

8.1.
Case I:
\bm{d}=\mathfrak{d}

In this section we show
7.6
when
\mathfrak{d}=\bm{d}
.

8.1.1.
Properties of cells and level lines

We start with a few preparatory lemmas.

Lemma 8.2
.

For every
z=(x,y)\in\mathcal{Y}
, we have

\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}/2\leq\mathfrak{d}_{z}\leq 2\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}
and

x\geq x^{SW}_{q}
. Letting
h=\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)
, we have

s:=x-\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h)\asymp\mathfrak{d}
. If
q^{\prime}
satisfies (
7.16
) and
h^{\prime}:=\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)>0
, then
s^{\prime}:=x-\mathfrak{l}_{q^{\prime}}(h^{\prime})\asymp\mathfrak{d}
.

Proof.

Write
w_{0}=(x_{0},y_{0})
.
Recall that

(8.3)

\displaystyle y_{0}={\bm{d}}^{2}=\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}^{2}\geq L^{20}N^{2B\delta}\frac{\omega}{qN},

where the bound follows from (
7.8
) and the assumption
\mathfrak{d}={\bm{d}}
. As for
x_{0}
, note that
\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q}\leq L{\bm{e}}\leq L^{-19}\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}
(using (
7.10
) and (
7.8
)), which
implies that

(8.4)

\displaystyle x_{0}-x^{SW}_{q}\geq\mathfrak{d}L^{-1},

if
L
is sufficiently large, since the point of
\mathfrak{A}_{q}
closest to, and with the same
y
-coordinate as,
w_{0}
has
x
-coordinate larger than
x^{SW}_{q}
by
c\mathfrak{d}
, for some constant
c>0
.

Since the slope of the vector
{\bm{u}}_{w_{0};q}
is of order
\mathfrak{d}
, the diameter of
\mathcal{Y}
in the
y
direction is of order

L^{5}\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q}+\mathfrak{d}L^{2}(\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q})^{1/2}\leq L^{6}N^{2B\delta}\frac{\omega}{qN}+L^{5/2}{\bm{d}}N^{B\delta}\sqrt{\frac{\omega}{qN}}\leq\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{\bm{d}^{2}}}{2L}=\displaystyle\frac{y_{0}}{2L},

where the first bound uses (
7.11
) and the second uses (
7.8
) (with the fact that
\bm{d}=\mathfrak{d}
). Together with (
8.3
),
this proves the first claim of the lemma for
L
sufficiently large.

Similarly, the diameter
of
\mathcal{Y}
in the
x-
direction is, for some constant
C>1
, at most

CL^{2}\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q}}\leq CL^{5/2}N^{B\delta}\sqrt{\omega/(qN)}\leq CL^{-5}\mathfrak{d}
. Together with (
8.4
), the claim

x\geq x^{SW}_{q}
follows. The statement
x-\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h)\asymp\mathfrak{d}
follows from the fact that
x-x^{SW}_{q}\asymp\mathfrak{d}
(as proven above) and
x^{SW}_{q}-\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h)\asymp\sqrt{h}\lesssim\mathfrak{d}
(the first bound follows from the definition of
\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h)
, together with the fact that the curvature of the arctic curve is bounded below, by
3.10
, and the second from (
8.1
)).
As for the statement about
s^{\prime}
, note that
s^{\prime}\geq x-x^{SW}_{q^{\prime}}\geq x-x^{SW}_{q}\asymp\mathfrak{d}
, where the second bound follows from
3.19
and the third one was proven just above. Moreover, if
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)>0
then
z\in\mathfrak{L}_{q^{\prime}}
and
x-x^{SW}_{q^{\prime}}\lesssim\mathfrak{d}
follows from the fact that the curvature of
\mathfrak{A}_{q^{\prime}}
is uniformly positive. Finally,
x^{SW}_{q^{\prime}}-\mathfrak{l}_{q^{\prime}}(h^{\prime})\asymp\sqrt{h^{\prime}}\leq\sqrt{h}\lesssim\mathfrak{d}
, where the first inequality follows from
q^{\prime}\leq q
and the second was proven above. The bound
s^{\prime}\asymp\mathfrak{d}
then follows.
∎

Next, we require the following geometric definitions. Below,
\mathbf{S}
prescribes a strip containing the cell
\mathcal{Y}
; we will focus on decreasing the height function
H_{t}
in the part in
\mathbf{S}
below the
k
-th level line
\mathcal{U}_{q^{\prime}}^{k/N}
of
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}
, where
k\sim(\omega^{3}\mathfrak{d})^{-1}
(see (
8.5
)) as, above this level line, the hypotheses of
7.6
quickly imply that it holds (with the underlying reason being that, if
(\mathtt{d}_{z}\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime}})^{1/2}\leq\omega^{-1}
, then by
3.18
we have
\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)\asymp\mathtt{d}_{z}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}^{3/2}\lesssim(\omega^{3}\mathfrak{d})^{-1}
, meaning that
z
lies below
\mathcal{U}_{q^{\prime}}^{k/N}
; see
8.5
for further details). Here, as always,
w_{0}=(x_{0},y_{0})
is the point in
\mathfrak{L}_{q}
that defines the center of the cell
\mathcal{Y}
.

Definition 8.3
.

Let
{\bf L}^{\pm}
denote the line parallel to the
y
-axis and with
x
-coordinate
x^{\pm}:=x_{0}\pm L^{2}(\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q})^{1/2}
. Let
\bf S
denote the strip between
\bf L^{-}
and
\bf L^{+}
. Given
z\in\mathcal{Y}
, we let
{\bf L}^{z}
denote the line parallel to the
y
-axis and containing
z
. Note that the vertical sides of
\mathcal{Y}
are subsets of
\bf L^{-},\bf L^{+}
.

Moreover, as in
Definition

3.22
, let
9
9

9

With a minor abuse of notation, “the level line
\mathcal{U}_{q^{\prime}}^{k/N}
” refers to the graph of the function
x\mapsto\mathcal{U}_{q^{\prime}}^{k/N}(x)
.

\mathcal{U}_{q^{\prime}}^{k/N}
be the level line of
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}
at height
k/N
, where

(8.5)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{k}{N}=\frac{L^{3}}{\omega^{3}\mathfrak{d}}.

Figure 17.
The cell
\mathcal{Y}
with the vertical lines
{\bf L}_{\pm},{\bf L}_{z}
where
z\in\mathcal{Y}
. The strip
\bf S
is contained between
{\bf L}_{-}
and
{\bf L}_{+}
.

See
Figure

17
. Note that, because of the bounds
\omega\geq(qN)^{1/3}N^{-A\delta}
,
\mathfrak{d}\geq N^{B\delta}\sqrt{\omega/Nq}
and
Nq\geq N^{5B\delta}
together with the fact that
B
is sufficiently larger than
A
, we have
(k/N)\ll 1
.

Lemma 8.4
.

The intersection
\mathcal{U}_{q^{\prime}}^{k/N}\cap\bf S
is contained in
\mathcal{Y}
.

Proof of
8.4
.

Let
z\in\mathcal{Y}
denote any point on the top boundary of
\mathcal{Y}
. It suffices to show that
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)\geq kN^{-1}
. To do so, let us first compare
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)
to
\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)
, to which end we compare
\mathfrak{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}
to
\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}
. By
3.18
(with
8.2
), we have that
\mathfrak{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}\asymp(\mathfrak{d}^{-1/2}\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z))^{2/3}\asymp L^{2}\mathfrak{d}^{-1}\omega^{-2}
. We also have that
|q-q^{\prime}|\leq N^{R\delta-1}\omega,
by (
7.16
). Thus, by (
7.7
) it follows that
|q-q^{\prime}|\leq N^{R\delta}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}
, meaning (via
3.19
) that
\mathfrak{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}\leq\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}\leq N^{R\delta}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}
. Applying
3.18
again, this implies that
\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)\ll N^{2R\delta}\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)
, so it suffices to show that
\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)\gg N^{2R\delta-1}k
.

By
3.18
(with
8.2
), we have
\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)\asymp\mathfrak{d}^{-1}(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q})^{3/2}.

Next, by (
3.8
) and the fact that
\dist(z;\mathfrak{A}_{q})\gtrsim\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q}
(by the definition of
\mathcal{Y}
, with the smoothness of
\mathfrak{A}_{q}
), we have

\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}\gtrsim\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q}
.
To lower bound the latter, observe that the lower bound on
\omega
from (
7.7
) yields that
\omega^{3}\geq N^{1-6A\delta}q
. Together with (
7.11
) and the fact that
B\geq 1000A
, it follows that

\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q}\geq L^{-1}N^{2B\delta}\omega(qN)^{-1}\gg LN^{B\delta}\omega^{-2}
. Combining these bounds (and using the fact that
B\geq 1000R
) yields
\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)\gg N^{B\delta}\mathfrak{d}^{-1}\omega^{-3}\gg N^{2R\delta-1}k
, verifying the lemma.
∎

Remark 8.5
.

As a consequence of
8.4
,
\mathcal{U}_{q^{\prime}}^{k/N}\cap\bf S
splits
\mathcal{Y}
into a region
\mathcal{R}^{\uparrow}
above it where

\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}\geq\frac{k}{N}
and the complement
\mathcal{R}^{\downarrow}
where
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}<\frac{k}{N}
.
The statement of
7.6
is obvious in the region
\mathcal{R}^{\uparrow}
(defined in
8.5
), since
\mathcal{R}^{\uparrow}\subset\mathcal{R}
(the latter being defined in
7.6
). Indeed,
because of Remark
8.1
and
8.2
and the upper bound in (
7.7
), for
w\in\mathcal{R}^{\uparrow}
one has
\mathfrak{e}_{w;q^{\prime}}^{3/2}\mathfrak{d}_{w}^{1/2}\gtrsim L^{3}(\omega^{3}\mathfrak{d})^{-1}\asymp L^{3}(\omega^{3}\mathfrak{d}_{w})^{-1}\geq N^{3A\delta-1}
so that, in particular,
(\mathtt{e}_{w;q^{\prime}}\mathtt{d}_{w})^{1/2}\geq(\mathfrak{e}_{w;q^{\prime}}\mathfrak{d}_{w})^{1/2}\geq\omega^{-1}
and
\mathtt{d}_{w}^{1/2}\mathtt{e}_{w;q^{\prime}}^{3/2}\geq N^{A\delta-1}
, that is,
w\in\mathcal{R}
.

Conversely, note that
\mathfrak{d}_{z}^{1/2}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}^{1/2}\lesssim L\omega
for all
z\in\mathcal{R}^{\downarrow}
. Indeed, by (
8.1
) and (
8.5
), we have that
\mathfrak{d}_{z^{\prime}}^{1/2}\mathfrak{e}_{z^{\prime};q^{\prime}}^{1/2}\lesssim(k\mathfrak{d}/N)^{1/3}\lesssim L\omega
for all
z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}_{q^{\prime}}^{k/N}
, and for every
z\in\mathcal{R}^{\downarrow}
there exists some
z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}_{q^{\prime}}^{k/N}
above
z
, with the same
x
-coordinate, so that
\mathfrak{d}_{z}^{1/2}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}^{1/2}\leq\mathfrak{d}_{z^{\prime}}^{1/2}\mathfrak{e}_{z^{\prime};q^{\prime}}^{1/2}\lesssim L\omega
.

Note also that, in the case
{\bm{d}}=\mathfrak{d}
, the condition
\mathtt{d}_{z}\in(L^{-2}\mathfrak{d},L^{2}\mathfrak{d})
is automatically satisfied in the whole cell
\mathcal{Y}
for
L
large enough, in view of (
7.6
) and
8.2
.

8.1.2.
Comparison of level lines

For the remainder of the proof of Proposition
7.6
when
\mathfrak{d}=\bm{d}
, we fix a point
\bar{z}=(\bar{x},\bar{y})\in\mathcal{Y}^{-}
; we then verify the bound (
7.19
) w.o.p. at
\bar{z}
.

Let us briefly outline how we will proceed. We will eventually (see the beginning of the proof of
7.6
in
Section

8.1.3
) censor the Glauber dynamics
(H_{t})
outside of
\mathcal{R}^{\downarrow}
and run them inside of
\mathcal{R}^{\downarrow}
(with a floor constraint) until they mix, which will by
2.13
take time at most
q^{-1}N^{1+5B\delta}\omega
. Since the hypotheses of
7.6
imply that
H_{t}
is bounded above by
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}
along the upper boundary of
\mathcal{R}^{\downarrow}
and by
\mathcal{H}_{q}
along its left and right boundaries, monotonicity implies that
H_{t}
is bounded above by the uniform measure on tilings of
\mathcal{R}^{\downarrow}
with these boundary conditions. To analyze this stationary measure, we will compare to random tilings of a suitably chosen hexagon.

Specifically, we will first apply Proposition
3.26
to find
(a,b,c)
, such that the following holds. The
k
-th level line
\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{k/N}
of
\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
is (after a shift by
(\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime})
) tangent to, and less convex than, the
k
-th level line
\mathcal{U}_{q^{\prime}}^{k/N}
of
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}
at
\bar{z}
. This difference in convexity will imply that the level lines of
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}
are above those of
\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
along the boundary of
\mathcal{R}^{\downarrow}
; see
8.9
10
10

10

The idea of comparing two random tilings by tuning the curvatures of their limit shape level lines was also used in
[
1
, Section 3]
(though in the distinct context of accessing local statistics).
. Together with monotonicity and the concentration estimate
3.17
for random tilings of
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
, this will imply that the height function for the uniform measure described above is w.o.p. bounded above by
\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
(up to an error of
N^{\delta-1}
). Using (
3.21
), it will be verified (see
8.13
) that the latter is at most
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(\bar{z})
, showing the proposition.

To implement this, we must first verify that
3.26
applies here. Below, given
x\in[x^{-},x^{+}]
, set
s_{x}:=x-\mathfrak{l}_{q^{\prime}}(k/N)
.

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
s_{\pm}:=s_{x^{\pm}},s_{0}:=s_{\bar{x}}
. Then, the assumptions of
3.26
are verified, with
q,\varepsilon,h_{0}
there equal to
q^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0},(k/N)
here.

Proof of
8.6
.

The condition
k/N\leq\varepsilon_{0}
holds since
k/N\ll 1
, as noted just after (
8.5
). Also, condition
q^{\prime}\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
holds thanks to (
7.16
), (
7.5
) and (
8.2
).

The first bound in (
3.15
) holds because
\bar{x}\geq x^{SW}_{q}
(by
8.2
) and therefore
x\geq x^{SW}_{q^{\prime}}
as well (by
3.19
). The last bound in (
3.15
) holds because
\bar{z}
is closer to the
p^{SW}_{q}
than to
p^{SE}_{q}
(recall
8.1
), and parameters in the definition of the cells ensure that their size tends to zero as
N\to\infty
(recall
7.4
).

As for condition (
3.16
),
the upper bound on
s_{+}-s_{-}
is satisfied in our case (if
L
in the definition of
\mathcal{Y}
is large enough) since the width of the cell
\mathcal{Y}
in the
x
-direction is at most
L^{-7}\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\lesssim L^{-7}s_{0}\lesssim L^{-7}s_{0}^{1/2}
, as follows from (
7.8
), (
7.10
), (
7.12
). For the bound
\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\lesssim s_{0}
, we used
8.2
.
∎

Thanks to
3.26
, we can find
a,b,c\in[1-C\varepsilon_{0},1+C\varepsilon_{0}]

and
\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
such that the statements of the proposition hold.

Remark 8.7
.

The specific value of the constants
\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
plays no role in the subsequent arguments. Therefore, to lighten formulas, we will drop them (assume they equal zero).

In particular, we have by (
3.17
) and (
3.18
) that

(8.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{k/N}_{q^{\prime}}(\bar{x})=\mathcal{U}^{k/N}_{a,b,c}(\bar{x}),\quad\partial_{x}\mathcal{U}^{k/N}_{q^{\prime}}(\bar{x})=\partial_{x}\mathcal{U}^{k/N}_{a,b,c}(\bar{x})

and by (
3.19
),
8.2
and (
8.2
) that, for
x\in[x^{-},x^{+}]
,

(8.7)

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{U}_{q^{\prime}}^{k/N}(x)-\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{k/N}(x)\big)\geq C^{-1}q^{\prime}\mathfrak{d}|x-\bar{x}|^{2}\geq(2C)^{-1}q\mathfrak{d}|x-\bar{x}|^{2}.

Definition 8.8
.

For
j\leq k
, with
k
as in (
8.5
), let
y^{\pm,j}_{q}:=\mathcal{U}^{j/N}_{q}(x^{\pm})
,
y^{\pm,j}_{q^{\prime}}:=\mathcal{U}^{j/N}_{q^{\prime}}(x^{\pm})
, and
y^{\pm,j}_{a,b,c}:=\mathcal{U}^{j/N}_{a,b,c}(x^{\pm})
.

Note that these are the
y
-coordinates of the intersections of
\bf L^{\pm}
with the
(j/N)
-level line of
\mathcal{H}_{q}
and
\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
(the latter up to a translation of the hexagon). The next lemma compares the boundary data of the level lines of
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
on
\mathcal{R}^{\downarrow}
.

Lemma 8.9
.

For
1\leq j\leq k
, we have that
y^{\pm,j}_{q}-y^{\pm,j}_{a,b,c}\geq{L^{2}}N^{2B\delta-1}\omega\mathfrak{d}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{U}^{k/N}_{q^{\prime}}(x)\geq\mathcal{U}^{k/N}_{a,b,c}(x)
for all
x\in[x^{-},x^{+}]
.

Proof.

First of all, we claim that

(8.8)

\displaystyle y^{\pm,k}_{q^{\prime}}-y^{\pm,k}_{a,b,c}\geq(100C)^{-1}L^{3}N^{2B\delta-1}\omega\mathfrak{d}.

In fact, this follows from (
8.6
), (
8.7
), and the fact that
\min(|\bar{x}-x^{+}|,|\bar{x}-x^{-}|)\geq\frac{1}{4}(x^{+}-x^{-})
(because
\bar{z}\in\mathcal{Y}^{-}
and
L
is large) and lastly

(x^{+}-x^{-})^{2}=4L^{4}(\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q})\geq 4L^{3}N^{2B\delta}\omega(qN)^{-1}
(by (
7.12
) and (
7.11
)). All these facts, together, imply (
8.8
).
Next, we claim that

(8.9)

\displaystyle y^{\pm,j}_{q^{\prime}}-y^{\pm,j}_{a,b,c}\geq(200C)^{-1}L^{3}N^{2B\delta-1}\omega\mathfrak{d}

for every
j\leq k-1
. This follows if we prove that

(8.10)

\displaystyle|(y^{\pm,k}_{a,b,c}-y^{\pm,j}_{a,b,c})-(y^{\pm,k}_{q^{\prime}}-y^{\pm,j}_{q^{\prime}})|\ll L^{3}N^{2B\delta-1}{\omega\mathfrak{d}}

for

1\leq j<k-1
.
Using (
3.21
), (
3.22
) and recalling from
8.2
that

s_{0}\asymp\mathfrak{d}
, we see
that

(8.11)

|(y^{\pm,k}_{a,b,c}-y^{\pm,j}_{a,b,c})-(y^{\pm,k}_{q^{\prime}}-y^{\pm,j}_{q^{\prime}})|\lesssim q\mathfrak{d}^{5/3}(k/N)^{2/3}=q\mathfrak{d}\Big(\mathfrak{d}\frac{k}{N}\Big)^{2/3}\\
=L^{2}\mathfrak{d}\omega\times\frac{q}{\omega^{3}}\leq L^{2}N^{6A\delta-1}{\omega\mathfrak{d}}

where in the last inequality we used the lower bound (
7.7
) for
\omega
. The claim (
8.9
) then follows since
6A<2B
.

Finally, the claim of the lemma follows from (
8.9
) since,
by
3.21
and the second part of
3.18
, we have that
|y^{\pm,j}_{q}-y^{\pm,j}_{q^{\prime}}|\asymp\mathfrak{d}(q-q^{\prime})\lesssim\mathfrak{d}\omega N^{R\delta-1}\ll L^{3}N^{2B\delta-1}{\omega\mathfrak{d}}

(the last bound holds because of (
7.15
)).
∎

8.1.3.
Proof of
7.6
if
\mathfrak{d}=\bm{d}

As mentioned in the beginning of
Section

8.1.2
, we can (after running the Glauber dynamics until they mix on
\mathcal{R}^{\downarrow}
) bound
(H_{t})
above by the uniform measure on random tilings of
\mathcal{R}^{\downarrow}
, with upper level line given by
\mathcal{U}_{q^{\prime}}^{a,b,c}
and left and right level line endpoints given by
(y_{q}^{\pm,j})
. By
8.9
and monotonicity, we may replace the upper boundary condition by
\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{k/N}
and the left and right ones by
\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{j/N}
. The following definition provides notation for the associated Bernoulli path ensemble (here,
\widehat{U}
involves an additional floor condition to be briefly explained below
Definition

8.10
).
See also
Figure

18
.

Definition 8.10

(
\widehat{U},\widecheck{U}
)
.

For
j\leq k-1
, let
(x^{\pm},\bar{y}^{\pm,j}_{a,b,c})
be a point in
\mathbb{T}_{N}+(0,1/(2N))
at minimal distance from
(x^{\pm},y^{\pm,j}_{a,b,c})
. We
let
\widehat{U}=\{\widehat{U}_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq k-1}
denote the Bernoulli path ensemble
with endpoints given by

\{(x^{\pm},\bar{y}^{\pm,j}_{a,b,c})\}_{1\leq j\leq k-1}
, conditioned to
the event that the top path
\widehat{U}_{k-1}
is weakly below

\mathcal{U}^{k/N}_{a,b,c}
and that each
\widehat{U}_{j},j\leq k-1

is weakly below
\mathcal{U}^{(j/N+N^{-1+(6A+R)\delta})}_{q^{\prime}}
.
Similarly, we let
\widecheck{U}=\{\widecheck{U}_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq k-1}
denote the
Bernoulli path ensemble defined like
\widehat{U}
, but without the constraint
that
\widecheck{U}_{j}
is below

\mathcal{U}^{(j/N+N^{-1+(6A+R)\delta})}_{q^{\prime}}
.

Figure 18.
A schematic illustration of
Definition

8.10
. The top Bernoulli path
\widehat{U}_{k-1}
is weakly below
\mathcal{U}^{k/N}_{a,b,c}
and the Bernoulli path
\widehat{U}_{j},j<k-1
, is weakly below
\mathcal{U}^{h_{j}}_{q^{\prime}}
, where
h_{j}:=j/N+N^{-1+(6A+R)\delta}
.

Note that the condition that each
\widehat{U}_{j}
is below
\mathcal{U}^{N^{-1}(j/N+N^{-1+(6A+R)\delta})}_{q^{\prime}}
is equivalent to the condition that the height function associated to the Bernoulli path ensemble
\widehat{U}
is above the floor

\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}-N^{-1+(6A+R)\delta}
. This floor constraint will be a convenience when applying
2.13
, as it will indicate that the quantity
H_{\max}
from (
2.5
) is nearly its minimal value
N^{o(1)-1}
(see (
8.15
) below). Since the concentration bound
3.25
applies to
\widecheck{U}
and not
\widehat{U}
, the following lemma, stating that this constraint is irrelevant, will be useful.

Lemma 8.11
.

The Bernoulli path ensembles
\widehat{U},\widecheck{U}
can be coupled in such a way that, w.o.p.,
\widehat{U}=\widecheck{U}
.

Proof of
8.11
.

The claim follows once we prove

(8.12)

\displaystyle\text{w.o.p., }\widecheck{U}_{j}\text{ is weakly below }\mathcal{U}^{(j/N+N^{-1+(6A+R)\delta})}_{q^{\prime}},\text{ for every }j\leq k-1.

In agreement with
Definition

2.6
, let
11
11

11

With a minor abuse of notation and in order to lighten formulas, whenever we write
\mathtt{U}^{h}_{a,b,c}
we actually mean
\mathtt{U}^{h^{\prime}}_{a,b,c}
, with
h^{\prime}
the largest element of
(1/N)(\mathbb{Z}+1/2)
smaller than
h
.

\mathtt{U}^{h}_{a,b,c}
denote the level line at height
h
of the height function of a uniformly random tiling of
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
. As above, we assume that the hexagon
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
is suitably translated, so that the level line at height
j/N
of its limit shape coincides with
\mathcal{U}^{j/N}_{a,b,c}
.
In the cell
\mathcal{Y}
, we have that
\widecheck{U}_{k-1}
is below
\mathcal{U}^{k/N}_{a,b,c}
(by definition of the Bernoulli path ensemble
\widecheck{U}
), and the latter is w.o.p. below

\mathtt{U}^{k/N+N^{\delta-1}}_{a,b,c}
, by

3.25
12
12

12

When applying
3.25
, note that

\mathtt{d}_{z}\asymp\mathfrak{d}
in
\mathcal{Y}
, since
\mathfrak{d}\geq N^{-1/2}
by assumption (
7.6
).
.
Also by
3.25
and by definition of
\widecheck{U}
, the endpoints of
\widecheck{U}_{j},j\leq k-1
, are w.o.p. below the intersections of
\mathtt{U}^{j/N+N^{\delta-1}}_{a,b,c}
with
\bf L^{\pm}
. Applying
2.12
, we conclude that the Bernoulli path ensemble
\widecheck{U}
can be coupled with the level lines
\mathtt{U}_{a,b,c}
so that, w.o.p.,

\widecheck{U}_{j}
is below
\mathtt{U}^{j/N+N^{\delta-1}}_{a,b,c}
for every
j\leq k-1
, in the cell
\mathcal{Y}
.
Applying once more
3.25
, we conclude w.o.p. that

\widecheck{U}_{j}
is below
\mathcal{U}^{j/N+2N^{\delta-1}}_{a,b,c}
.
It remains to prove that the latter is below

\mathcal{U}^{(j/N+N^{-1+(6A+R)\delta})}_{q^{\prime}}
. To this purpose, we apply (
3.23
) (with
h_{0},\mathfrak{d}_{z}
there replaced by
k/N,\mathfrak{d}
here) and note that
Cq\mathfrak{d}kN^{-1}\asymp L^{3}q\omega^{-3}\lesssim L^{3}N^{6A\delta-1}
by (
7.7
). In particular,
N^{\delta-1}+Cq\mathfrak{d}kN^{-1}\leq N^{-1+(6A+R)\delta}
. Then, (
3.23
) implies that

\mathcal{U}^{j/N+2N^{\delta-1}}_{a,b,c}
is below
\mathcal{U}^{(j/N+N^{-1+(6A+R)\delta})}_{q^{\prime}}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

The next lemma indicates that the Bernoulli path ensemble
(\widehat{U}_{j})
is closely approximated by the level lines
(\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{j/N})
of
\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
; it quickly follows from first comparing
\widehat{U}
to
\widecheck{U}
through
8.11
and then using
3.25
(which follows from the concentration bound
3.17
) to approximate the latter. The subsequent lemma compares the level lines
(\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{j/N})
to
\mathcal{U}_{q^{\prime}}^{j/N}
at
x
-coordinate
\bar{x}
(where we recall that we have fixed a point
\bar{z}=(\bar{x},\bar{y})\in\mathcal{Y}^{-}
, and that
{\bf L}^{\bar{z}}
is the vertical line containing
\bar{z}
).

Lemma 8.12
.

W.o.p., the following holds for every
1\leq j<k
:
the Bernoulli path
\widehat{U}_{j}
is between
\mathcal{U}^{j/N-N^{-1+(3/4)\delta}}_{a,b,c}
and
\mathcal{U}^{j/N+N^{-1+(3/4)\delta}}_{a,b,c}
if
j-N^{(3/4)\delta}\geq 0
, or below
\mathcal{U}^{j/N+N^{-1+(3/4)\delta}}_{a,b,c}
if
j-N^{(3/4)\delta}<0
.

Proof of
8.12
.

By
8.11
it is enough to prove the statement for
\widecheck{U}
. Then, the statement follows from

3.25
.
∎

Lemma 8.13
.

For every
1\leq j<k
the following holds:

\mathcal{U}^{j/N}_{a,b,c}(\bar{x})\geq\mathcal{U}^{j/N}_{q^{\prime}}(\bar{x})
.

Proof of
8.13
.

Since (
8.6
) implies that
\mathcal{U}_{q^{\prime}}^{k/N}(\bar{x})=\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{k/N}(\bar{x})
, the lemma follows from (
3.21
) (with the
h
there equal to
j/N
here).
∎

Finally we can prove
7.6
(following the outline in the beginning of
Section

8.1.2
) in the case
{\bm{d}}=\mathfrak{d}
.

Proof of
7.6
if
{\bm{d}}=\mathfrak{d}
.

As noted in

8.5
,
we need only to prove that w.o.p, for all times in the time interval
[q^{-1}N^{1+10B\delta}\omega,N^{5}]
the event

(8.13)

H_{t}(\bar{z})\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(\bar{z})+N^{\delta-1}\quad\text{for all}\quad\bar{z}\in\mathcal{Y}^{-}\cap\mathcal{R}^{\downarrow}

occurs. Since the event in (
8.13
) is decreasing, by
monotonicity (
2.11
) and by
8.5
we can
and will impose that the height function
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
in

\mathcal{Y}\cap\mathcal{R}
(and in particular in

\mathcal{Y}\cap\mathcal{R}^{\uparrow}
, that is the region above the
line
\mathcal{U}^{k/N}_{q^{\prime}}
) is frozen to the maximal configuration
lower than the ceiling
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}
, and that
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
in

\mathcal{Y}
is constrained (in the sense of
Definition

2.10
)
to be above the floor
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}-N^{-1+(6A+R)\delta}
.

For the dynamic
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
in the cell
\mathcal{Y}
with these floor/ceiling constraints, we can upper bound via
2.13
its mixing time
T_{\rm mix}^{\mathcal{Y}}
by a constant times

(8.14)

\displaystyle N^{2}\times L^{4}(\mathfrak{e}_{w_{0};q}\mathfrak{d}_{w_{0}})N^{2}(\log N)^{2}N^{(12A+3R)\delta-2}\lesssim L^{5}N^{1+2(B+(3/2)R+6A)\delta}\frac{\omega}{q}\lesssim N^{1+5B\delta}\frac{\omega}{q},

where we used also (
7.11
) and (
7.15
).
In fact, in applying
2.13
we use the fact that
the difference
|\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)-\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)|
is (by
3.21
) at most of order

(8.15)

(q-q^{\prime})(\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}\mathfrak{d}_{z})^{1/2}+(q-q^{\prime})^{3/2}\mathfrak{d}_{z}^{1/2}\lesssim(\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}\mathfrak{d}_{z})^{1/2}N^{R\delta-1}\omega+N^{-1+(3/2)R\delta}\lesssim N^{(3/2)R\delta-1}

for
z\in\mathcal{Y}\setminus\mathcal{R}
, where the first bound follows from the assumption (
7.16
) on
q^{\prime}
and the upper bound (
7.7
) on
\omega
, while the last bound follows from the fact that
z\in\mathcal{Y}\setminus\mathcal{R}
, where
(\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}\mathfrak{d}_{z})^{1/2}\lesssim\omega
by
8.5
(recall that we are restricting our attention to points in
\mathcal{R}^{\downarrow}
). Because of (
2.4
), we deduce that the law of the dynamic at time
t=N^{1+10B\delta}\frac{\omega}{q}\geq N^{B\delta}T_{\rm mix}^{\mathcal{Y}}
is at variation distance smaller than any inverse power of
N
from its stationary measure
\pi
.

We prove now that, w.o.p., if
H^{\eq}
denotes the discrete height of the configuration sampled according to
\pi
,

(8.16)

H^{\eq}(\bar{z})\leq\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(\bar{z})+N^{\delta-1}\quad\text{for all}\quad\bar{z}\in\mathcal{Y}^{-}\cap\mathcal{R}^{\downarrow}.

Once that is proven, it follows by
2.14
and (
8.16
) that the event (
8.13
) holds w.o.p. in the whole time interval
[q^{-1}N^{1+10B\delta}\omega,N^{5}]
, which concludes the proof of the proposition.

To prove (
8.16
), given
j\in\mathbb{N},
with
j\leq k
, call
W^{j}
the level line of
H^{\eq}
at height
j/N-1/(2N)
and

(x^{\pm},Y^{\pm,j})
the intersection of
W^{j}
with
\bf L^{\pm}
.
Because of assumption (i) of
7.6
and
8.9
, we have

Y^{\pm,j}\geq y^{\pm,j}_{q}\geq y^{\pm,j}_{a,b,c}
for all
j\leq k
.
By assumption (ii) of
7.6
and
8.5
,
the level line
W^{k}
is deterministically weakly above

\mathcal{U}^{k/N}_{q^{\prime}}
. Hence, by
2.12
, the Bernoulli path ensembles
W=\{W^{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq k-1}
and
\widehat{U}
(cf.
Definition

8.10
) can be coupled in such a way that
W^{j}
is weakly above
\widehat{U}_{j}
for every
j\leq k-1
(note that the constraint that the
j
-th line is weakly below
\mathcal{U}_{q^{\prime}}^{j/N+N^{-1+(6A+R)\delta}}
is present in both ensembles). Note also that the event in (
8.16
) is decreasing in terms of height function, but increasing in terms of Bernoulli paths. Therefore, we can replace the ensemble
W
by
\widehat{U}
.

Call
j\leq k
the index such that

\lfloor N\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(\bar{z})\rfloor=j
(recall that, as noted in
8.5
,
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(\bar{z})\leq k/N
).
The event (
8.16
) is implied by the event that the
level line
U_{j+N^{\delta}}
stays above the point
\bar{z}
, that is, at the
intersection with
{\bf L}^{\bar{z}}
the level line
\widehat{U}_{j+N^{\delta}}
is above

\mathcal{U}^{j}_{q^{\prime}}
. We can assume that
j+N^{\delta}<k
, since (as noted above) the level line at height
k/N
is above
\mathcal{U}_{q^{\prime}}^{k/N}
, hence above
\bar{z}
.
On the other hand, for
j<k-N^{\delta}
we have
that
\widehat{U}_{j+N^{\delta}}
is w.o.p. above
\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{j/N}
(by

8.12
), which at the intersection with
{\bf L}^{\bar{z}}
is above
\mathcal{U}^{j/N}_{q^{\prime}}
(by

8.13
), as desired.
∎

8.2.
Case II:
{\bm{d}}>\mathfrak{d}

In this section we establish
7.6
in the case when
\bm{d}>\mathfrak{d}
. First observe since
\bm{d}>\mathfrak{d}
and since
\bm{d}\leq N^{(B+1)\delta}(\omega/qN)^{1/2}\leq N^{(1-B/2)\delta}
(by (
7.7
) and (
7.8
)) that we have

(8.17)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}<N^{-B\delta/4}.

8.2.1.
Inductive setup

The proof of
7.6
if
\bm{d}>\mathfrak{d}
will have a number of similarities to that when
\mathfrak{d}=\bm{d}
. However, one relevant difference relates to the estimates (
3.19
) and (
3.22
). When
\mathfrak{d}=\bm{d}
, we used that the former “increase in curvature” dominates the latter “error,” when showing that
y_{a,b,c}^{+,j}\leq y_{q}^{+,j}
(recall Definition
8.8
, where the right endpoints will be the main relevant ones when
\bm{d}>\mathfrak{d}
) in the proof of Lemma
8.9
. Now, when
\bm{d}>\mathfrak{d}
, a direct attempt to follow the same reasoning could have us take
s_{0}=\mathfrak{d}
(from reducing
H_{t}(z)
where
\mathfrak{d}_{z}=\mathfrak{d}
) and
s_{+}=\bm{d}
(from the length of the cell
\mathcal{Y}
) in Proposition
3.26
. However, if
\mathfrak{d}\ll\bm{d}
, then the error in (
3.22
) could dominate the increase in curvature from (
3.19
), which would prevent us from ordering
\mathcal{H}_{q}
with respect to
\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
. To circumvent this, one would like to reduce the value of
s_{+}
in Proposition
3.26
, by applying that proposition on a smaller length scale now only slightly larger than
\mathfrak{d}
. This would enable us to show that
y_{a,b,c}^{\pm,j}\leq y_{q^{\prime}}^{\pm,j}
. However, if the length scale is too small, then we would not be able to deduce that
