U_{1}(x)\geq\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(x)-K\mathtt{d}_{x}^{2/3}N^{\delta/3-2/3}
. This is equivalent to verifying that w.o.p.
\Omega_{K}
holds, which is true by the fact that
\Omega_{1}
holds w.o.p., together with Lemma
A.2
and induction on
j
.
∎

To show
A.2
, we fix
j\in[1,K-1]
and restrict to
\Omega=\Omega_{j}
. Then, conditional on
U_{K-j+1}
, the law of
\bm{U}=(U_{1},U_{2},\ldots,U_{K-j})
is given the uniform measure on families of
K-j
non-intersecting Bernoulli paths on
[0,a+c]
, conditioned to satisfy, for each integer
i\in[1,K-j]
,

U_{i}(0)=\frac{i-(1/2)}{N};\qquad U_{i}(a+c)=c+\frac{i-(1/2)}{N}\\
U_{K-j}(x)\leq U_{K-j+1}(x),\quad\text{for all $x\in[0,a+c]$}.

Next let
V:[\mathfrak{l},a+c]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
denote a uniformly random Bernoulli path conditioned to start at
V(\mathfrak{l})=0
, end at
V(a+c)=c
, and satisfy
V(x)<\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(x)
for each
x\in(\mathfrak{l},a+c)
.

Since we have restricted to the event
\Omega_{j}
, monotonicity (Lemma
2.12
) yields a coupling between
V
and
\bm{U}
such that

(A.1)

\displaystyle U_{K-j}(x)\geq V(x)-(j-1)d_{x}^{2/3}N^{\delta/3-2/3},\qquad\text{for each $x\in[0,a+c]$}.

Lemma A.3
.

We have w.o.p. that
V(x)\geq\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(x)-d_{x}^{2/3}N^{\delta/3-2/3}
, for all
x\in[\mathfrak{l},a+c]
.

Proof of
A.2
.

By
A.3
, (
A.1
) yields
U_{K-j}\geq\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(x)-jd_{x}^{2/3}N^{\delta/3-2/3}
holds w.o.p., meaning that w.o.p.
\Omega_{j+1}
holds. ∎

To establish
A.3
, for any real numbers
x\in[\mathfrak{l},a+c]
and
r>0
, define the events

(A.2)

\displaystyle\Omega(x;r)=\big\{V(x)\geq\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(x)-rd_{x}^{2/3}N^{-2/3}\big\};\qquad\Omega(r)=\bigcap_{x\in[\mathfrak{l},a+c]}\Omega(x;r).

Lemma
A.3
indicates that
\Omega(x;r)
very likely holds for
r\geq N^{\delta/3}
. The following lemma, to be established in
Section

A.2
below, indicates that if
\Omega(r)
holds for
r\geq N^{\delta/3}
, then
\Omega((1-N^{-\delta})r)
holds w.o.p.; repeatedly applying this statement yields
A.3
.

Lemma A.4
.

If
\mathfrak{R}\geq N^{\delta/3}
, then w.o.p.
\Omega(\mathfrak{R})^{\complement}\cup\Omega((1-N^{-\delta})\mathfrak{R})
holds.

Proof of
A.3
.

Observe that
\Omega(N^{2})
holds deterministically, as since
d_{x}\geq N^{-1/2}
we have
rd_{x}^{2/3}N^{-2/3}\gg 1
and thus
\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(x)-rd_{x}^{2/3}N^{-2/3}<0
for
r\gg N
. Thus, applying
A.4

N^{2\delta}
times, since
N^{\delta/3}\geq(1-N^{-\delta})^{N^{2\delta}}\cdot N^{2}
, we deduce that
\Omega(N^{\delta/3})
holds w.o.p.; this implies the lemma. ∎

A.2.
Proof of
A.4

In this section we establish
A.4
. The proof uses ideas reminiscent of the tangent method introduced in
[
18
]
; see also
[
3
, Section 5.2]
.

Proof.

It suffices to show for any fixed
x_{0}\in[\mathfrak{l},a+c]
that w.o.p.
(\Omega(\mathfrak{R})\cap\Omega(x_{0};\mathfrak{R}-\mathfrak{R}N^{-\delta})^{\complement})^{\complement}
holds. We will assume in what follows that
x_{0}\leq a
(as the case when
x_{0}\geq a
follows similarly, by rotating the hexagon clockwise). We also assume
\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(x_{0})\geq(1-N^{-\delta})\mathfrak{R}d_{x_{0}}^{2/3}N^{-2/3}
, as otherwise
\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(x_{0})-(1-N^{-\delta})\mathfrak{R}d_{x_{0}}^{2/3}N^{-2/3}\leq 0\leq V(x_{0})
and so
\Omega(\mathfrak{R}-\mathfrak{R}N^{-\delta})
(and thus
(\Omega(\mathfrak{R})\cap\Omega(x_{0};\mathfrak{R}-\mathfrak{R}N^{-\delta})^{\complement})^{\complement}
) holds deterministically.

Then, since the strict convexity of
\mathfrak{A}_{a,b,c}
gives
\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(x_{0})\asymp d_{x_{0}}^{2}
, it follows that
d_{x_{0}}^{2}\gtrsim\mathfrak{R}d_{x_{0}}^{2/3}N^{-2/3}
. This yields

(A.3)

\displaystyle d_{x_{0}}\gtrsim\mathfrak{R}^{1/2}d_{x_{0}}^{1/3}N^{-1/3},

which in particular implies that
d_{x_{0}}\geq N^{\delta/4-1/2}
(as
\mathfrak{R}\geq N^{\delta/3}
) and hence
d_{x_{0}}=x_{0}-\mathfrak{l}
.

Now, let
u>0
be a small constant and
U>1
be a large constant, both to be fixed later, and set

(A.4)

\displaystyle x_{1}=x_{0}-u\mathfrak{R}^{1/2}d_{x_{0}}^{1/3}N^{-1/3};\qquad x_{2}=\min\{x_{0}+U\mathfrak{R}^{1/2}d_{x_{0}}^{1/3}N^{-1/3},a+c\}.

Observe for sufficiently small
u>0
that
x_{1}>\mathfrak{l}
, as
x_{1}-\mathfrak{l}=x_{0}-\mathfrak{l}-u\mathfrak{R}^{1/2}d_{x_{0}}^{1/3}N^{-1/3}\gtrsim d_{x_{0}}
, by (
A.3
). By similar reasoning, we have
x_{2}-x_{0}\lesssim Ud_{x_{0}}
. Moreover, we have

(A.5)

\displaystyle d_{x_{1}}<d_{x_{0}}<d_{x_{2}}\lesssim Ud_{x_{0}},

where the first two statements follow from (
A.4
), and the third from (
A.3
) (with (
A.4
)). In what follows, we restrict to the event
\Omega(x_{1};\mathfrak{R})\cap\Omega(x_{2};\mathfrak{R})
. We must then show that w.o.p. that

(A.6)

\displaystyle V(x_{0})\geq\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(x_{0})-(1-N^{-\delta})\mathfrak{R}d_{x_{0}}^{2/3}N^{-2/3}.

Now, let
\ell
denote the line tangent to
\mathfrak{A}_{a,b,c}
at the point
(x_{0},\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(x_{0}))
. For any
x\in\mathbb{R}
, let
\ell(x)
be such that
(x,\ell(x))\in\ell
; set
\rho=\ell^{\prime}(x_{0})
, which is the slope of
\ell
at
x_{0}
. By the strict convexity of
\mathfrak{A}_{a,b,c}
, we have

(A.7)

\displaystyle\rho\asymp d_{x_{0}}.

Again by the strict convexity of
\mathfrak{A}_{a,b,c}
there exists a constant
v>0
such that
\ell(x_{1})\leq\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(x_{1})-2v(x_{1}-x_{0})^{2}
. By (
A.4
), it follows that

(A.8)

\displaystyle\ell(x_{1})\leq\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(x_{1})-2vu^{2}\mathfrak{R}d_{x_{0}}^{2/3}N^{-2/3}.

By similar reasoning, we have

(A.9)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\ell(x_{2})\leq\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(x_{2})-2vU^{2}\mathfrak{R}d_{x_{0}}^{2/3}N^{-2/3},\qquad\text{if $x_{2}=x_{0}+U\mathfrak{R}^{1/2}d_{x_{0}}^{1/3}N^{-1/3}$}.\end{aligned}

Next observe that, conditional on
V(x)
for
x\notin(x_{1},x_{2})
, the law of
V(x)
for
x\in[x_{1},x_{2}]
is that of a uniformly random Bernoulli path on
[x_{1},x_{2}]
, with endpoints given by
14
14

14

Here and in the rest of the proof, endpoints of Bernoulli paths should be taken as points in
\mathbb{T}_{N}+(0,1/(2N))
at minimal distance from the points mentioned in the text.

(x_{1},V(x_{1}))
and
(x_{2},V(x_{2}))
, conditioned to satisfy
V(x)\leq\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(x)
for each
x\in[x_{1},x_{2}]
. We will compare
V
to a uniformly random Bernoulli path without any such conditioning. To set this up, let
\ell_{1}
denote the vertical shift of
\ell
down by
(1-vu^{2})\mathfrak{R}d_{x_{0}}^{2/3}N^{-2/3}
, that is, set

(A.10)

\displaystyle\ell_{1}(x)=\ell(x)-(1-vu^{2})\mathfrak{R}d_{x_{0}}^{2/3}N^{-2/3},

for each
x\in\mathbb{R}
. We then have

\displaystyle\ell_{1}(x_{1})\leq\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(x_{1})-\mathfrak{R}d_{x_{0}}^{2/3}N^{-2/3}\leq\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(x_{1})-\mathfrak{R}d_{x_{1}}^{2/3}N^{-2/3}\leq V(x_{1}),

where the first statement follows from (
A.10
) and (
A.8
); the second from (
A.5
); and the third from our restriction to
\Omega(x_{1};\mathfrak{R})
. We further have for sufficiently large
U
that

\displaystyle\ell_{1}(x_{2})\leq V(x_{2}).

Indeed, if
x_{2}=a+c
, then this follows from the fact
V(a+c)=\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(a+c)\geq\ell_{1}(a+c)
(the first by the boundary conditions of
V
, imposed by those of the tiling, and the second by the convexity of
\mathfrak{A}_{a,b,c}
). If instead
x_{2}=x_{0}+U\mathfrak{R}^{1/2}d_{x_{0}}^{1/3}N^{-1/3}
, then this holds, as for sufficiently large
U
we have for some constant
C>1
that

\displaystyle\ell_{1}(x_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(x_{2})-(1-vu^{2}+2vU^{2})\mathfrak{R}d_{x_{0}}^{2/3}N^{-2/3}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(x_{2})-(1+vU^{2})(CU)^{-1}\mathfrak{R}d_{x_{2}}^{2/3}N^{-2/3}\leq\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(x_{2})-\mathfrak{R}d_{x_{2}}^{2/3}N^{-2/3}\leq V(x_{2}),

where the first statement follows from (
A.10
) and (
A.9
); the second from (
A.5
); the third from the fact that
U
is sufficiently large; and the fourth from our restriction to
\Omega(x_{2};\mathfrak{R})
.

Let
W_{1}:[x_{1},x_{2}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
denote a uniformly random Bernoulli path starting at
(x_{1},W_{1}(x_{1}))
with
W_{1}(x_{1})=\ell_{1}(x_{1})
, ending at
(x_{2},W_{1}(x_{2}))
with
W_{1}(x_{2})=\ell_{1}(x_{2})
, and satisfying
W_{1}(x)\leq\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(x)
. By monotonicity (
2.12
), we may couple
(V,W_{1})
such that
W_{1}(x)\leq V(x)
for all
x\in[x_{1},x_{2}]
. Also let
W_{2}:[x_{1},x_{2}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
denote a uniformly random Bernoulli walk satisfying
W_{2}(x_{1})=\ell_{1}(x_{1})
and
W_{2}(x_{2})=\ell_{1}(x_{2})
. We would like to couple
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
to coincide w.o.p., to which end it suffices to show that w.o.p.
W_{2}
remains below
\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}
.

To that end, observe that, w.o.p.,

(A.11)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in[x_{1},x_{2}]}|W_{2}(x)-\ell_{1}(x)|\leq N^{\delta/200}\cdot\rho^{1/2}\cdot\Big(\displaystyle\frac{x_{2}-x_{1}}{N}\Big)^{1/2}\leq N^{\delta/100}\mathfrak{R}^{1/2}d_{x_{0}}^{2/3}N^{-2/3}.

where the first statement follows from a concentration bound for uniformly random Bernoulli walks (with the fact that the slope of
\ell_{1}
is
\rho
) and the second follows from (
A.4
) and (
A.7
). Since
\dist(\ell_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{a,b,c})\gtrsim\mathfrak{R}d_{x_{0}}^{2/3}N^{-2/3}
and
\mathfrak{R}\geq N^{\delta/3}
, it follows that
W_{2}
remains w.o.p. below
\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}
. Hence, we may couple
W_{1}=W_{2}
w.o.p., meaning that w.o.p.
V(x_{0})\geq W_{2}(x_{0})
.

Thus, we have w.o.p. that

\displaystyle V(x_{0})\geq W_{2}(x_{0})

\displaystyle\geq\ell_{1}(x_{0})-N^{\delta/100}\mathfrak{R}^{1/2}d_{x_{0}}^{2/3}N^{-2/3}

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(x_{0})-(1-vu^{2}+N^{\delta/100}\mathfrak{R}^{-1/2})\cdot\mathfrak{R}d_{x_{0}}^{2/3}N^{-2/3}

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(x_{0})-(1-N^{-\delta})\mathfrak{R}d_{x_{0}}^{2/3}N^{-2/3},

where the second statement follows from (
A.11
); the third from the definition of
\ell_{1}
(and the fact that
\ell(x_{0})=\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{0}(x_{0})
); the fourth from the fact that
\mathfrak{R}\geq N^{\delta/3}
and that
N
is sufficiently large. This establishes (
A.6
) and thus the lemma.
∎

Appendix B
Properties of limit shapes

Recall from
Section

3
that
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}
denotes the limit shape for the volume-tilted random tiling on
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
. In this section we prove the properties stated in
Section

3
. Throughout, we fix
q\in\mathbb{R}
and
a,b,c>0
. IWe also set
\mathfrak{q}=e^{q}
and let
\mathbb{H}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\Imaginary z>0\}
denote the upper half plane.

B.1.
Formula for the limit shape

In this section we recall from
[
8
]
(though we will use the conventions from
[
22
]
) a formula for
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}
. To that end, the boundary values of
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}
along
\partial\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
are fixed, as depicted in
Figure

1
. Specifically,

(B.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }y=0\text{ or if }x-y=a\\
b&\text{ if }y-x=b\text{ or if }y=b+c\\
y&\text{ if }x=0\\
y-c&\text{ if }x=a+c\end{cases}.

It remains to determine its gradient
\nabla\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}
. Following
[
22
]
, we do this through its complex slope, defined below. Here, (
B.2
) is
[
22
, Equations (14) and (15)]
, and (
B.4
) and (
B.3
) are
[
22
, Theorem 1.6]
. Moreover, the first part of
B.2
is
[
22
, Equation (17)]
; its second part is
[
22
, Proposition 5.19]
; and its third part is explained in the proof of
[
22
, Theorem 1.3]
. The parameters
(a_{1},b_{1},\mathsf{N},\mathsf{T};\kappa;\mathfrak{q})
from
[
22
]
are taken to be
(-c,b-c,b,a+c;0;\mathfrak{q}^{-1})
here, the coordinates
(t,x)
from
[
22
]
are taken to be
(x,y)
here, and the parameter
u
from
[
22
]
is taken to be
u-c
here.

Definition B.1

(Complex slope)
.

For any
u\in\mathbb{C}
, define

(B.2)

\displaystyle f_{0}(u)=\displaystyle\frac{(\mathfrak{q}^{u-b-c}-1)(\mathfrak{q}^{u}-1)}{(\mathfrak{q}^{u+a}-1)(\mathfrak{q}^{u-b}-1)};\qquad U(u)=\mathfrak{q}^{u}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{f_{0}(u)}{1-f_{0}(u)};\qquad V(u)=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{u}}{1-f_{0}(u)}.

For any
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, define
u=u(x,y)=u_{q}(x,y)
to be the unique solution in
\overline{\mathbb{H}}
to the equation

(B.3)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}^{y}=V(u)-\mathfrak{q}^{x}\cdot U(u),

whenever (
B.3
), which is quickly verified to be a quadratic equation in
\mathfrak{q}^{u}
, has at most one real root. In this case, define the
complex slope

f_{x}(y):=f(x,y)
by

(B.4)

\displaystyle f(x,y)=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{u}-\mathfrak{q}^{y}}{\mathfrak{q}^{u}-\mathfrak{q}^{y-x}}.

Lemma B.2

(
[
22
]
)
.

The following statements hold for any
(x,y)\in\overline{\mathfrak{L}}_{q;a,b,c}
.

(1)

We have that

(B.5)

\displaystyle\arg f(x,y)=\pi(1-\partial_{y}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y));\qquad\arg(f(x,y)-1)=\pi(1+\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)).

(2)

We have that
(x,y)\notin\mathfrak{L}_{q;a,b,c}
if and only if
f(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}
.

(3)

We have that
(x,y)\in\mathfrak{L}_{q;a,b,c}
if and only if both roots of (
B.3
) are not real. Moreover,
(x,y)\in\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
if and only if (
B.3
) has a double (real) root for
u
.

Remark B.3
.

Using (
B.5
), if
f(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}
, we can quickly determine
\nabla\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)
depending on the interval in which
f(x,y)
resides. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle\nabla\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)=(0,1),\qquad\text{if $f(x,y)\in(0,1)$};

\displaystyle\nabla\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)=(-1,1),\qquad\text{if $f(x,y)\in(1,\infty)$};

\displaystyle\nabla\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)=(0,0),\qquad\text{if $f(x,y)\in(-\infty,0)$}.

Together,
B.2
and
3.9
determine the gradient
\nabla\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}
everywhere in
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
. This fixes the function
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}:\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by its boundary condition (
B.1
).

We next provide the Euler–Lagrange equation satisfied by
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}
. The first part of the below lemma is
[
23
, Proposition 9.4]
(which appeared earlier as
[
30
, Equation (3)]
); the second follows quickly from the explicit form of the
(\mathfrak{a}_{ij})
below, provided by
[
33
, Equations (5), (6), and (7)]
. Recall the definition of
\mathcal{T}
in (
3.3
).

Lemma B.4

(
[
30
,
23
,
33
]
)
.

For any indices
i,j\in\{x,y\}
, there exists
\mathfrak{a}_{ij}:\mathcal{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

satisfying
\mathfrak{a}_{xy}=\mathfrak{a}_{yx}
as well as the following two statements:

(1)

For any
z\in\mathfrak{L}_{q;a,b,c}
, we have

(B.6)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i,j\in\{x,y\}}\mathfrak{a}_{ij}(\nabla\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(z))\cdot\partial_{ij}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(z)=-q.

(2)

For any
\omega>0
, there exists a constant
C=C(\omega)>1
such that the following holds. Whenever
\gamma\in\mathcal{T}
with
\dist(\gamma,\partial\mathcal{T})\geq\omega
, we have

\displaystyle C^{-1}\leq\mathfrak{a}_{xx}(\gamma)\leq C;\qquad|\mathfrak{a}_{xy}(\gamma)|\leq C;\qquad C^{-1}\leq\mathfrak{a}_{yy}(\gamma)\leq C.

B.2.
Reformulation of the complex slope

In this section we change variables to express the quadratic equation (
B.3
) defining
u
in a slightly cleaner way. In what follows, for any
z\in\mathbb{C}
, define
[z]=[z]_{\mathfrak{q}}
by

\displaystyle[z]=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{-z}-1}{\mathfrak{q}^{-1}-1},

where we let
\mathfrak{q}^{z}=e^{z\log\mathfrak{q}}=e^{qz}
. Note that

\lim_{q\to 0}[z]=z.

Then, define
v=v(x,y)
by

(B.7)

\displaystyle v=[u(x,y)],

and denote

(B.8)

\displaystyle A=-[-a];\qquad B=[b];\qquad C=[b+c]-[b];\qquad X=-[-x];\qquad Y=-[-y],

Observe, by (
B.2
), that we have under this notation

(B.9)

\displaystyle f_{0}(u)=\displaystyle\frac{v(v-B-C)}{(v+A)(v-B)}.

Lemma B.5
.

We have that

(B.10)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &v^{2}\big(A+C-X+(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-1})(A+C)Y\big)\\
&\qquad+v\big(X(B+C)-Y(A+C)-AB+(\mathfrak{q}^{-1}-1)ABY\big)+ABY=0.\end{aligned}

Proof.

Using the definitions (
B.2
) of
U
and
V
, and recalling (
B.7
) and (
B.8
), the equation (
B.3
) defining
u
is equivalent to

(B.11)

\displaystyle(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-1})Yv+v=\mathfrak{q}^{y}v=X\cdot\displaystyle\frac{f_{0}(u)}{1-f_{0}(u)}+Y.

Inserting (
B.9
) into (
B.11
) then yields the lemma.
∎

Corollary B.6
.

We have that
(x,y)\in\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
if and only if

(B.12)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \big(X(B+C)-Y(&A+C)-AB+(\mathfrak{q}^{-1}-1)ABY\big)^{2}\\
&-4ABY\big(A+C-X+(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-1})(A+C)Y\big)=0.\end{aligned}

Proof.

This follows from the third part of
B.2
, with
B.5
(and the fact that (
B.3
) having a double root for
u
is equivalent to (
B.10
) having a double root for
v
).
∎

Proof of
3.7
.

By
B.6
, the arctic boundary
\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
defines a conic equation for
(X,Y)
; this arctic boundary therefore defines a smooth curve in the coordinates
(X,Y)
. Since (by (
B.8
)) the map from
(X,Y)
to
(x,y)
and its inverse are smooth, it follows that
\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
is a smooth curve (in the coordinates
(x,y)
).

It remains to show that
\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
is tangent to each edge of
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
at exactly one point. To that end, it suffices (since
\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
is smooth) to show that
\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
meets any edge of the hexagon
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
at exactly one point. All edges of
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
can be analyzed similarly, so let us only focus on its southwest edge
\{y=0\}
. Since
y=0
, we have by (
B.8
) that
Y=0
, and so (
B.12
) implies that
\big(X(B+C)-AB\big)^{2}=0
, meaning that
X=AB/(B+C)
. Hence,
\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
only intersects the southwest edge of
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
at one point
p^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}=(x,0)
, which is the solution to
X=AB/(B+C)
; this establishes the lemma.
∎

Remark B.7
.

Observe from the proof of
3.7
that the tangency point
p^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
is defined by the solution
x
to the equation
X=AB/(B+C)
.

Proof of
3.9
.

The identification of
\nabla\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(z)
is entirely analogous for each of the six frozen regions in which
z
is contained. So, let us assume that
z\in\mathfrak{F}^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
. First observe that it suffices to show that
\nabla\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)=(0,1)
for any
z\in\mathfrak{A}^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
(recall
Definition

3.8
). Indeed, denoting
z=(x,y)
, integrating this equality along
\mathfrak{A}^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
(and using the fact that
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,0)=0
, by (
B.1
)), it would follow that
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)=y
for each
(x,y)\in\mathfrak{A}^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
. Since
\partial_{y}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(z)\in[0,1]
for all
z\in\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
, it follows that we must have
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)\leq y
for all
(x,y)\in\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
. Since equality
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)=y
is attained for
(x,y)
on the boundary of
\mathfrak{F}^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
, it follows that
\partial_{y}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)=1
(and
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(z)=y
) for all
(x,y)\in\mathfrak{F}^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
. Hence,
\nabla\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(z)=(0,1)
for all
z\in\mathfrak{F}^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
, which yields the lemma.

To establish
\nabla\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(z)=(0,1)
for any
z\in\mathfrak{A}^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
, it suffices by
B.3
to verify that for such
z
we have
f(z)\in[0,1]
; by (
B.9
), this is equivalent to
v(z)\in[0,AB/(A+C)]
. It is also quickly verified from (
B.10
) that
v=0
only if
Y=0
(equivalently,
y=0
), and
v=AB/(A+C)
only if
X=0
(equivalently,
x=0
). Therefore, it suffices to show that
v(z_{0})=0
for
z_{0}=p^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
, and that
v(z_{1})=AB/(A+C)
for
z_{1}=p^{W}_{q;a,b,c}
. The two statements are proven very similarly, so let us only address the former. By
B.7
, we have at
p^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
that
X=AB/(B+C)
. Inserting this value of
X
(and
Y=0
) into (
B.10
) yields
(B+C)^{-1}C(A+B+C)\cdot v^{2}=0
, so
v=0
; this establishes the lemma. ∎

Proof of
3.10
.

By
B.6
, the arctic boundary
\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
defines a conic equation for
(X,Y)
, whose coefficients are smooth in the parameters
(q,a,b,c)
(since
\mathfrak{q}
and
(A,B,C)
are). Setting
q=0
and
(a,b,c)=(1,1,1)
, this conic equation takes the form
(X+Y-2)^{2}+3(X-Y)^{2}=3
, which defines a smooth, convex curve, with curvature uniformly bounded above and below. Hence, for
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small, it follows for any
q\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
and
a,b,c\in(1-\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon)
that the conic equation from (
B.12
) defining
(X,Y)
is a smooth, convex curve, with curvature uniformly bounded above and below. Since, by (
B.8
), we have that
X=x+O(\varepsilon)
, that
\partial_{x}X=1+O(\varepsilon)
, and that
\partial_{x}^{2}X=O(\varepsilon)
(and similarly for
Y
), it quickly follows for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small that
\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
is a smooth, convex curve (in the coordinates
(x,y)
), with curvature uniformly bounded above and below; this establishes the claim.
∎

Proof of
3.23
.

Observe since
(\mathfrak{l}_{q;a,b,c}(h),h)\in\mathfrak{A}^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
that
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(\mathfrak{l}_{q;a,b,c}(h),h)=h
, by integrating the second statement of
3.9
(from
z
starting at
(x^{SW}_{q;a,b,c},0)
, and ending at
(\mathfrak{l}_{q;a,b,c}(h),h)
, along
\mathfrak{A}^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
). Moreover, since
\mathfrak{A}^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
is convex by
3.10
, we have that
(x,y)\in\mathfrak{F}^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
for any
(x,y)\in[0,\mathfrak{l}_{q;a,b,c}(h)]\times[0,h]
. Therefore, it follows again from integrating the second part of
3.9
that
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)=y
for such
(x,y)
. Recalling the definition of
\mathcal{U}_{q;a,b,c}^{h}
from (
3.14
), this implies that
\mathcal{U}_{q;a,b,c}^{h}(x)=h
for any
x\in[0,\mathfrak{l}_{q;a,b,c}(h)]
; this establishes
3.23
.
∎

B.3.
Complex slope behavior away from the arctic boundary

Throughout this section, we let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
denote a sufficiently small constant, and we fix
q\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
and
a,b,c\in(1-\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon)
. The proof of
3.27
,
3.18
and
3.28
require distinct arguments according to whether one considers the region mesoscopically far from the arctic boundary (this is dealt with in the present section) or the one close to it (this is the topic of
Section

C.1
).

In what follows, we define
\xi,\eta,\zeta:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
as

(B.13)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \xi(X,Y)&=\big(X(B+C)-Y(A+C)-AB+(\mathfrak{q}^{-1}-1)ABY\big)^{2}\\
&\qquad-4ABY\big(A+C-X+(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-1})(A+C)Y\big);\\
\eta(X,Y)&=Y(A+C)-X(B+C)+AB+(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-1})ABY;\\
\zeta(X,Y)&=2\big(A+C-X+(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-1})(A+C)Y\big);\end{aligned}

in particular,
\xi
is defined as the left side of (
B.12
). Then,

(B.14)

\displaystyle v=\displaystyle\frac{\eta(X,Y)+\xi(X,Y)^{1/2}}{\zeta(X,Y)},

whenever
\xi(X,Y)\leq 0
(if
\xi(X,Y)<0
, then we take the root above with positive imaginary part).

We next require the following lemma. Its first statement is
[
33
, Theorem 4.4]
, and its second follows quickly from
[
33
, Equations (28), (29), and (30)]
.

Lemma B.8

(
[
33
]
)
.

Set
c=3-a-b
and define the sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}=\{(s,t,u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:(s,t)\in\mathcal{T}\};

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}=\{(a,b,x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:0<\min\{a,b\}<a+b<3,(x,y)\in\mathfrak{L}_{a,b,c}\},

and define the map
\Phi:\mathcal{W}\mapsto\mathcal{A}
by setting

\displaystyle\Phi(a,b,x,y)=(\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(x,y),\partial_{y}\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(x,y),\partial_{xx}\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(x,y),\partial_{xy}\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(x,y)).

(1)

The map
\Phi:\mathcal{W}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
is a diffeomorphism.

(2)

For any compact subset
\mathcal{W}_{0}\subset\mathcal{W}
, there exists a constant
C=C(\mathcal{W}_{0})
such that the Jacobian of
\Phi
is bounded above by
C
and below by
C^{-1}
everywhere on
\mathcal{W}_{0}
.

Lemma B.9
.

Fix
\omega>0
. For
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small (dependent on
\omega
),
3.27
,
3.18
, and
3.28
hold (with their constant
C
dependent on
\omega
) whenever
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}\geq\omega
.

Proof.

Throughout this proof, we set
z_{0}=(x_{0},y_{0})
. Let
\mathfrak{B}=\mathfrak{B}(\omega)>1
be a constant, to be fixed later, and let
z=(x,y)\in\mathfrak{L}_{q;a,b,c}
be a point satisfying
|z-z_{0}|\leq\mathfrak{B}^{-1}
. Denote the
(X,Y)
associated with
(x_{0},y_{0})
and
(x,y)
by
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X,Y)
, respectively.

We begin by verifying
3.27
. Observe since
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}\geq\omega\gtrsim 1
, we have
\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\gtrsim\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}\gtrsim 1
, where the first statement is a consequence of
3.10
(with the fact that
\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
is tangent to each edge of
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
). Therefore,

(B.15)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\asymp\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}\asymp 1.

By (
3.24
), it therefore suffices to show that
|\partial_{\gamma}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(z)|\lesssim 1
for each differential operator
\partial_{\gamma}
with
|\gamma|\in[1,B]
. One way of deducing this would be through Morrey’s theorem, using the Euler–Lagrange equation given by
B.4
. However, let us do this more directly (as it will be anyways useful for confirming the second part of the lemma), which will proceed by showing that
v
is uniformly smooth in
(a,b,c,x,y)
.

To that end, we will require several bounds on
(\xi,\eta,\zeta)
. Since
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}\asymp 1
(and
\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
is defined by the set of
(x,y)
for which
\xi(X,Y)=0
), it is quickly verified that
-\xi(X_{0},Y_{0})\asymp 1
; by the continuity of
\xi
, it follows that
-\xi(X,Y)\asymp 1
. One also clearly has
\eta(X,Y)\lesssim 1
. By (
B.15
), we also have
b+c-x\gtrsim 1
(where the bound follows from the fact that
b+c-x
is the distance from
z
to the northeast edge of
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
). Therefore, for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small,
\zeta(X,Y)=2(A+C-X+(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-1})(A+C)Y)\gtrsim 1
, as
\zeta(X,Y)\approx 2-x\approx b+c-x
for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small; we also have that
\zeta(X,Y)\lesssim 1
, since
(A,B,C,X,Y,q)
are bounded. Together, these give

(B.16)

\displaystyle-\xi(X,Y)\asymp 1;\qquad\eta(X,Y)\lesssim 1;\qquad\zeta(X,Y)\asymp 1.

By the explicit forms of
(\xi,\eta,\zeta)
from (
B.13
),
\xi
,
\eta
, and
\zeta
are all uniformly smooth (meaning that their derivatives of order at most
m
are bounded by a constant depending on
m
) in the parameters
(A,B,C,X,Y,q)
, whenever
|z-z_{0}|\leq\mathfrak{B}^{-1}
. Together with (
B.14
) (and the fact that the map from
(A,B,C,X,Y)
to
(a,b,c,x,y)
is uniformly smooth for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small), this implies that
v
is uniformly smooth in
(a,b,c,x,y,q)
for such
z=(x,y)
.

We also have from (
B.16
) that
|v|\lesssim 1
and that
\Imaginary v\asymp 1
. These two facts, with (
B.5
), imply that
\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)\asymp 1
and
\partial_{y}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)\asymp 1
whenever
z=(x,y)
satisfies
\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}\geq\omega
. This confirms the first two statements of
3.18
in this case; the third follows from integrating the first. The proof of the fourth is entirely analogous.

Moreover, by (
B.9
), the map from
v=v(x,y)
to
f=f(x,y)
is (uniformly) smooth whenever
\Imaginary v\asymp 1
and
|v|\lesssim 1
, in which case
\Imaginary f\asymp 1
and
|f|\lesssim 1
. Furthermore, we have from (
B.5
) that the map from
f=f(x,y)
to
\nabla\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)
is (uniformly) smooth whenever
\Imaginary f\asymp 1
and
|f|\lesssim 1
. Composing these maps, we deduce that
\nabla\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)
is (uniformly) smooth in
(a,b,c,x,y,q)
, if
|z-z_{0}|\leq\mathfrak{B}^{-1}
. The smoothness in
(x,y)
in particular verifies
3.27
whenever
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}\geq\omega
.

To confirm
3.28
in the same regime, we use
B.8
. Let
\nu=\nu(\omega)>0
denote some sufficiently small constant to be fixed later, and set

(B.17)

\displaystyle(s_{0},t_{0},u_{0},v_{0})=(\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}_{q}(z_{0}),\partial_{y}\mathcal{H}_{q}(z_{0}),\partial_{xx}\mathcal{H}_{q}(z_{0})+\nu q,\partial_{xy}\mathcal{H}_{q}(z_{0})).

Observe that
(s_{0},t_{0},u_{0},v_{0})
is in a compact subset of
\mathcal{A}
(only dependent on
\omega
). Indeed, since
\Imaginary f(z_{0})\asymp 1
and
|f(z_{0})|\lesssim 1
, we have from (
B.5
) that

(B.18)

\displaystyle\dist(\nabla\mathcal{H}_{q}(z_{0}),\partial\mathcal{T})\asymp 1.

We also have by the first part of the lemma
|\partial_{\gamma}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(z_{0})|\lesssim 1
, whenever
|\gamma|\leq 2
.
Recalling the function
\Phi
from
B.8
, set
(a,b,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=\Phi^{-1}(s_{0},t_{0},u_{0},v_{0})
, set
c=3-a-b
and
z_{1}=(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
; let us show that
3.28
holds for this choice of parameters.

To that end, set

(B.19)

\displaystyle(s_{1},t_{1},u_{1},v_{1})=(\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}(z_{0}),\partial_{y}\mathcal{H}(z_{0}),\partial_{xx}\mathcal{H}(z_{0}),\partial_{xy}\mathcal{H}(z_{0})),

which satisfies
\Phi(1,1,x_{0},y_{0})=(s_{1},t_{1},u_{1},v_{1})
. From the above fact that
\mathcal{H}_{q}(x,y)
is uniformly smooth in
(x,y,q)
, we have

(B.20)

\displaystyle|s_{1}-s_{0}|+|t_{1}-t_{0}|+|u_{1}-u_{0}|+|v_{1}-v_{0}|\lesssim q.

Observe that
(s_{1},t_{1},u_{1},v_{1})
is also in a compact subset of
\mathcal{A}
that only depends on
\omega
(as
(s_{0},t_{0},u_{0},v_{0})
is, by (
B.20
) and the fact that
|q|\leq\varepsilon
is sufficiently small). Therefore, by the second part of
B.8
, the Jacobian of
\Phi^{-1}
is bounded in a neighborhood of
(s_{0},t_{0},u_{0},v_{0})
and
(s_{1},t_{1},u_{1},v_{1})
. Together with (
B.20
) and the fact that
\Phi^{-1}(s_{1},t_{1},u_{1},v_{1})=(1,1,x_{0},y_{0})
, it follows that

(B.21)

\displaystyle|a-1|+|b-1|+|x^{\prime}-x_{0}|+|y^{\prime}-y_{0}|\lesssim q.

Since
c=3-a-b
, it also follows that
|c-1|\lesssim q
, which with (
B.15
) yields the claim in
3.28
about
a,b,c
being close to
1
. The first statement in (
3.26
) follows from the fact that
(s_{0},t_{0})=\nabla\mathcal{H}_{q}(z_{0})
by (
B.17
), with the definition of
(a,b,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=\Phi^{-1}(s_{0},t_{0},u_{0},v_{0})
.

By (
B.15
) and (
3.24
), to establish the second statement in (
3.26
), we must show that
D^{2}{\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(z_{1})}-D^{2}{\mathcal{H}_{q}(z_{0})}\gtrsim q\cdot\Id
. Since

(B.22)

\displaystyle\partial_{xx}\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(z_{1})=u_{0}=\partial_{xx}\mathcal{H}_{q}(z_{0})+\nu q;\qquad\partial_{xy}\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(z_{1})=v_{0}=\partial_{xy}\mathcal{H}_{q}(z_{0}),

it suffices to verify that

(B.23)

\displaystyle\partial_{yy}\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(z_{1})-\partial_{yy}\mathcal{H}_{q}(z_{0})\gtrsim q,

if
\nu>0
is sufficiently small. To do this, observe from the first statement of
B.4
(with the fact that
\nabla\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(z_{1})=(s_{0},t_{0})=\nabla\mathcal{H}_{q}(z_{0})
) that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{xx}(s_{0},t_{0})\cdot\partial_{xx}\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(z_{1})+2\mathfrak{a}_{xy}(s_{0},t_{0})\cdot\partial_{xy}\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(z_{1})+\mathfrak{a}_{yy}(s_{0},t_{0})\cdot\partial_{yy}\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(z_{1})=0;

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{xx}(s_{0},t_{0})\cdot\partial_{xx}\mathcal{H}_{q}(z_{0})+2\mathfrak{a}_{xy}(s_{0},t_{0})\cdot\partial_{xy}\mathcal{H}_{q}(z_{0})+\mathfrak{a}_{yy}(s_{0},t_{0})\cdot\partial_{yy}\mathcal{H}_{q}(z_{0})=-q.

Subtracting these equations and using (
B.22
) gives

(B.24)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{yy}(s_{0},t_{0})\cdot\big(\partial_{yy}\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(z_{1})-\partial_{yy}\mathcal{H}_{q}(z_{0})\big)=q(1-\mathfrak{a}_{xx}(s_{0},t_{0})\cdot\nu\big).

By the second part of
B.4
, together with (
B.18
) (and the fact that
\nabla\mathcal{H}_{q}(z_{0})=(s_{0},t_{0})
), there exists a constant
C=C(\omega)>1
such that
C^{-1}\leq\mathfrak{a}_{yy}(s_{0},t_{0})\leq C
and
\mathfrak{a}_{xx}(s_{0},t_{0})\leq C
. Setting
\nu=(2C)^{-1}
then yields (
B.23
), by (
B.24
).

The last statement of
3.28
follows from the above-mentioned fact that each derivative of
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(z)
is uniformly smooth in the parameters
(q,a,b,c)
, whenever
|z-z_{0}|\leq\mathfrak{B}^{-1}
, together with the bound (
B.21
) (and
|c-1|\lesssim q
). ∎

Appendix C
Behavior of limit shapes near the arctic boundary

Throughout this section, we let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
denote a sufficiently small constant, and we fix
q\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
and
a,b,c\in(1-\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon)
.

C.1.
Scaling of limit shapes

Remark C.1
.

In view of
B.9
, to establish
3.27
,
3.18
and
3.28
we may restrict our attention to the case when
z_{0}=(x_{0},y_{0})
is close to the arctic boundary
\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
; in particular,
\dist(z_{0},\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c})\leq 1/100
. Let us abbreviate
(\mathfrak{d},\mathfrak{e})=(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}},\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q})
.

We will assume in what follows that the closest edge
\ell_{z_{0}}
of
\mathfrak{X}_{a,b,c}
to
z_{0}
is its south one
\{y=0\}
, and that
x_{0}\geq\mathfrak{l}_{q;a,b,c}(0)=x^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
, as the other cases are entirely analogous. Then, it is quickly verified for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small, which we assume throughout this section (hence,
q
close to
0
and
a,b,c
close to
1
) that

(C.1)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}-O(\varepsilon)\leq x_{0}\leq 1+O(\varepsilon);\qquad y_{0}\leq\displaystyle\frac{49}{100},

as otherwise we would not have
\dist(z_{0},\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c})\leq 1/100
. This is the reason why some statements in this section are given for
x_{0}\in[1/4,5/4]
.

C.1.1.
Curvature and tangent approximations

In this section, we obtain exact and asymptotic expressions for the curvature and the slope of the arctic boundary of
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}
. This involves asymptotic expressions for the
functions
f_{0}
and
f_{x}
. These functions are in principle explicit, but a bit intricate, and so it will be more direct to study
f_{0}
and
f_{x}
through the method of characteristics
[
22
, Section 6.2]
.
The computations here are similar to those in
[
2
, Sections 10.2 and 10.4]
.

First, following
[
22
, Proposition 5.5]
, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
z\in\overline{\mathbb{H}}
, let

(C.2)

\displaystyle f_{x}(z)=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{u}-\mathfrak{q}^{z}}{\mathfrak{q}^{u}-\mathfrak{q}^{z-x}},

where
u=u_{x}(z)\in\overline{\mathbb{H}}
solves (see
[
22
, Remark 5.6]
for the choice of solution, if there is more than one)

(C.3)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}^{z-x}=\mathfrak{q}^{u}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{f_{0}(u)}{f_{0}(u)-1}+\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{u-x}}{1-f_{0}(u)}=\mathfrak{q}^{-x}V(u)-U(u).

If
z=y\in\mathbb{R}
, then
u_{x}(z)
coincides with
u(x,y)
defined by (
B.3
) and
f_{x}(z)
coincides with
f(x,y)
defined by (
B.4
). Given this, the below definition (
C.4
) coincides with
[
22
, Equation (214)]
(with the
(\mathsf{q},\kappa)
there equal to
(\mathfrak{q}^{-1},0)
here).

Definition C.2
.

For any
x\in[0,a]
and
u\in\mathbb{C}
, let
z_{x}=z_{x}(u)=z(x,u)\in\mathbb{C}
solve the equation (with
f_{x}(z)
as in (
C.2
))

(C.4)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}z_{x}=\displaystyle\frac{f_{x}(z_{x})}{f_{x}(z_{x})-1},\quad\text{where $z_{0}=u$}.

Definition C.3
.

For any
x\in[0,a]
, let
\gamma_{x}=\gamma(x)\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
denote the minimal real number such that
(x,\gamma_{x})\in\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
is on the arctic boundary. In this way, we parameterize the (union of the southwest and south parts of) the arctic boundary by
(x,\gamma_{x})_{x\in[0,a]}
. We further define
\xi_{x}=\xi(x)
by

\displaystyle\xi_{x}=u_{x}(\gamma_{x}),\quad\text{so that}\quad\gamma_{x}=z_{x}(\xi_{x})

where the latter holds since we more generally have
z_{x}(u_{x}(y))=y
, by
[
22
, Equation (222)]
.
15
15

15

Indeed,
[
22
, Equation (222)]
(with the
(\mathsf{q},\kappa)
there equal to
(\mathfrak{q}^{-1},0)
here) gives
q^{z_{x}(u_{x}(y))}=V(u_{x}(y))-q^{x}U(u_{x}(y))
; by (
B.3
) (and
[
22
, Remark 5.6]
), this yields
z_{x}(u_{x}(y))=y
.
We frequently abbreviate
\xi_{x}^{\prime}=\xi^{\prime}(x)
,
\gamma_{x}^{\prime}=\gamma^{\prime}(x)
, and
\gamma_{x}^{\prime\prime}=\gamma^{\prime\prime}(x)
.

Remark C.4
.

When
\mathfrak{q}=1
, it can be verified that
\xi_{x}
denotes the
y
-coordinate of the point where the tangent line to
\mathfrak{A}_{a,b,c}
at
(x,\gamma_{x})
meets the
y
-axis. Indeed, (
C.6
) below indicates that
f_{x}(z_{x})
is constant in
x
when
\mathfrak{q}=1
, and so the right side of the equation (
C.4
) defining
z_{x}
is constant in
x
. Moreover, by
C.5
below, this right side is equal to
\gamma_{x}^{\prime}
. Therefore, since
z_{x}=\gamma_{x}
, the path
(z_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{R}}
follows the line tangent to
\mathfrak{A}_{a,b,c}
at
(x,\gamma_{x})
. In particular,
\xi_{x}=z_{0}
is where this line meets the
y
-axis.

Recall the definition (
B.4
) of
f_{x}(y)
. By
[
22
, Equation (215)]
(with the
(\mathsf{q},\kappa)
there equal to
(\mathfrak{q}^{-1},0)
here), we have for any
(x,y)\in\mathfrak{L}_{q;a,b,c}
that

(C.5)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}(f_{x}(z_{x}))=\log\mathfrak{q}\cdot f_{x}(z_{x}),

and integrating over
x
yields

(C.6)

\displaystyle f_{x}(z_{x})=\mathfrak{q}^{x}\cdot f_{0}(z_{0})=\mathfrak{q}^{x}f_{0}(u).

Then,

(C.7)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}^{z_{x}}=\mathfrak{q}^{u}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{1-\mathfrak{q}^{x}f_{0}(u)}{1-f_{0}(u)}=\mathfrak{q}^{u}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{1-f_{x}(z_{x})}{1-\mathfrak{q}^{-x}f_{x}(z_{x})},

where the first equality follows from inserting (
C.6
) into (
C.5
) and integrating over
x
, and the second follows from (
C.6
).

The following lemma expresses the slope
\gamma_{x}^{\prime}
of the tangent line to the arctic boundary at
(x,\gamma_{x})
in terms of
f_{x}(\gamma_{x})
.

Lemma C.5
.

For any
x\in[0,a]
, we have

\displaystyle\gamma_{x}^{\prime}=\displaystyle\frac{f_{x}(\gamma_{x})}{f_{x}(\gamma_{x})-1}.

Remark C.6
.

If
(x,\gamma_{x})\in\mathfrak{A}^{S}_{q;a,b,c}
and is bounded away from
p^{SE}_{q;a,b,c}
, then
\gamma^{\prime}_{x}
is positive and strictly smaller than
1
(the value
1
is the slope
\gamma^{\prime}_{x}
at
p^{SE}_{q;a,b,c}
). It also follows from the statement of
C.5
that
f_{x}(\gamma_{x})<0
.

Proof.

Observe that the slope of the arctic boundary
\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
at
(x,\gamma_{x})
satisfies
\gamma_{x}^{\prime}\partial_{y}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}+\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}=0
. Near this point, we have by (
B.5
)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\Imaginary f}{f}\sim-\pi\partial_{y}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c};\qquad\displaystyle\frac{\Imaginary f}{f-1}\sim\pi\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c},

(where
\sim
indicates that the ratio of the two sides tends to
1
as the argument
z
of
f
converges to
(x,\gamma_{x})
) which together with the equality
\gamma_{x}^{\prime}=-\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}\cdot(\partial_{y}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c})^{-1}
yields the lemma.
∎

Now, we seek to understand the limit shape
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)
for
(x,y)\in\mathfrak{L}_{q;a,b,c}
near the arctic boundary
\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
. To do so, we must analyze
f_{x}(y)
for
y\approx\gamma_{x}
. The exact form (
B.4
) of
f_{x}(y)
involves the function
q^{u_{x}(y)}
that solves the quadratic equation (
B.3
). It will be more direct to approximate
f_{x}(y)
without solving the latter equation, to which end we will instead use (
C.6
) to express
f_{x}(y)
in terms of
f_{0}(u)
, where
u
is such that
z_{x}(u)=y
. If
y\approx\gamma_{x}
, so
u\approx\xi_{x}
, by Definition
C.3
. Therefore, we will be interested in Taylor expanding
f_{0}(u)
around
u\approx\xi_{x}
, which will involve the first two derivatives of
f_{0}
at
\xi_{x}
. As these derivatives will be the underlying constants underlying our approximation for the limit shape, it will be convenient to express quantities of interest on the arctic boundary (such as its curvature) through these derivatives, as well.

The next lemma expresses the second derivative
\gamma_{x}^{\prime\prime}
of the arctic boundary at
(x,\gamma_{x})
in
x
,
\xi_{x}
,
\gamma_{x}
,
f_{0}
, and
f_{x}(\gamma_{x})
. The quantity
\Gamma_{2;x}
will appear in
C.11
below.

Lemma C.7
.

For any
x\in[0,a]
, define
A=A_{x}
by

(C.8)

\displaystyle A=f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{x})-\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}+1)}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{3}}\cdot(\mathfrak{q}^{-x}-1)(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2}.

Further denoting

(C.9)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{x;1}=-\displaystyle\frac{f_{x}(\gamma_{x})}{(f_{x}(\gamma_{x})-1)^{2}}\cdot\log\mathfrak{q};\qquad\Gamma_{x;2}=(f_{x}(\gamma_{x})-1)^{2}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{3\xi_{x}-3\gamma_{x}-x}(\log\mathfrak{q})^{3}}{A(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-x})^{3}},

we have

(C.10)

\displaystyle\gamma_{x}^{\prime\prime}=\Gamma_{x;1}+\Gamma_{x;2}.

In particular,

(C.11)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{x;2}=\gamma_{x}^{\prime\prime}-\gamma_{x}^{\prime}(1-\gamma_{x}^{\prime})\log\mathfrak{q}.

To show the above lemma, we require the below one that evaluates
f_{0}(\xi_{x})
and
f_{0}^{\prime}(\xi_{x})
as explicit rational functions of
\mathfrak{q}^{x}
,
\mathfrak{q}^{\xi_{x}}
, and
\mathfrak{q}^{\gamma_{x}}
.

Lemma C.8
.

For any
x\in[0,a]
, we have

(C.12)

\displaystyle f_{0}(\xi_{x})=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1}{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1};\qquad f_{0}^{\prime}(\xi_{x})=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{2}}\cdot(\mathfrak{q}^{x}-1)\log\mathfrak{q}.

Proof.

The first statement of the lemma follows from (
C.7
) at
(z_{x},u)=(\gamma_{x},\xi_{x})
. The second follows from the equality

\displaystyle f_{0}^{\prime}(\xi_{x})

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\lim_{s\rightarrow x}\displaystyle\frac{f_{0}(\xi_{x})-f_{0}(\xi_{s})}{\xi_{x}-\xi_{s}}=(\xi_{x}^{\prime})^{-1}\cdot\partial_{x}(f_{0}(\xi_{x}))=(\xi_{x}^{\prime})^{-1}\cdot\partial_{x}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1}{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1}\Big)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}\log\mathfrak{q}}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{2}\xi_{x}^{\prime}}(1-\mathfrak{q}^{\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}+(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-x})\cdot\xi_{x}^{\prime}+(\mathfrak{q}^{-x}-1)\cdot\gamma_{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{2}}\cdot(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-x})\log\mathfrak{q},

where we used the first statement of (
C.12
) and the fact that

(C.13)

\displaystyle\gamma_{x}^{\prime}=\displaystyle\frac{f_{x}(\gamma_{x})}{f_{x}(\gamma_{x})-1}=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x}f_{0}(\xi_{x})}{\mathfrak{q}^{x}f_{0}(\xi_{x})-1}=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-\mathfrak{q}^{x}}{1-\mathfrak{q}^{x}}.

∎

We additionally require the below lemma evaluating the derivative of
\xi
; it can be viewed as the source of the quantity
A=A_{x}
from (
C.8
).

Lemma C.9
.

Let
x\in[0,a]
. Recalling
A=A_{x}
from (
C.8
), we have

\displaystyle\xi_{x}^{\prime}=-\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+2\xi_{x}-2\gamma_{x}}(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2}}{A(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{2}}.

Proof.

By
C.5
, we have

\displaystyle\xi_{x}^{\prime}f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{x})

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}(f_{0}^{\prime}(\xi_{x}))

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2}}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{2}}+\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(\mathfrak{q}^{-x}-1)(\gamma_{x}^{\prime}-\xi_{x}^{\prime}-1)(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2}}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad-\displaystyle\frac{2\mathfrak{q}^{2x+2\xi_{x}-2\gamma_{x}}(\mathfrak{q}^{-x}-1)(\gamma_{x}^{\prime}-\xi_{x}^{\prime}-1)(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2}}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{3}}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2}}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{2}}+\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}+1}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{3}}(\xi_{x}^{\prime}-\gamma_{x}^{\prime}+1)(\mathfrak{q}^{-x}-1)(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2}.

Thus,

\displaystyle A\xi_{x}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\xi_{x}^{\prime}\bigg(f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{x})-\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}+1)}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{3}}(\mathfrak{q}^{-x}-1)(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2}}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{2}}+\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}+1)(1-\gamma_{x}^{\prime})}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{3}}(\mathfrak{q}^{-x}-1)(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2}}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{2}}-\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}+1)}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{2}}(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2}=-\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+2\xi_{x}-2\gamma_{x}}(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2}}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{2}},

where we used the fact from (
C.13
) that

\displaystyle 1-\gamma_{x}^{\prime}=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{-x}(1-\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}})}{\mathfrak{q}^{-x}-1}.

This yields the lemma.
∎

Now we can establish
C.7
.

Proof of
C.7
.

By
C.5
, (
C.6
),
C.9
, the second statement of (
C.12
), we obtain

\displaystyle\gamma_{x}^{\prime\prime}=\partial_{x}\Big(1+\displaystyle\frac{f_{x}(\gamma_{x})}{f_{x}(\gamma_{x})-1}\Big)

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}\bigg(1+\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{-x}}{f_{0}(\xi_{x})-\mathfrak{q}^{-x}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=-\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{-x}f_{0}(\xi_{x})\log\mathfrak{q}}{(f_{0}(\xi_{x})-\mathfrak{q}^{-x})^{2}}-\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{-x}\xi_{x}^{\prime}f_{0}^{\prime}(\xi_{x})}{(f_{0}(\xi_{x})-\mathfrak{q}^{-x})^{2}}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+3\xi_{x}-3\gamma_{x}}(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-x})(\log\mathfrak{q})^{3}}{A(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{4}(f_{0}(\xi_{x})-\mathfrak{q}^{-x})^{2}}-\displaystyle\frac{f_{x}(\gamma_{x})\log\mathfrak{q}}{(f_{x}(\gamma_{x})-1)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+3\xi_{x}-3\gamma_{x}}(f_{0}(\xi_{x})-\mathfrak{q}^{-x})^{2}(\log\mathfrak{q})^{3}}{A(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-x})^{3}}-\displaystyle\frac{f_{x}(\gamma_{x})\log\mathfrak{q}}{(f_{x}(\gamma_{x})-1)^{2}},

from which (
C.10
) follows by the fact from (
C.6
) that
f_{0}(\xi_{x})=\mathfrak{q}^{-x}f_{x}(\gamma_{x})
. By
C.5
, we have that
\Gamma_{x;1}=\gamma_{x}^{\prime}(1-\gamma_{x}^{\prime})\log\mathfrak{q}
. Therefore, (
C.11
) follows from (
C.10
).
∎

Lemma C.10
.

If
z=(x,\gamma_{x})
with
x\in[x^{SW}_{q},a]
, then

\xi_{x}\asymp\mathfrak{d}_{z}
.

Proof.

First recall the first of (
C.12
).
Since
x
is bounded away from
0
, we have that

(C.14)

\displaystyle f_{0}(\xi_{x})=\frac{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1}{{\mathfrak{q}}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1}\asymp\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}.

Since
\gamma_{x}\asymp\mathfrak{d}_{z}^{2}
(because the arctic boundary has positive and bounded curvature),
it suffices to show that
f_{0}(\xi_{x})\asymp\mathfrak{d}_{z}
. To that end, observe that

(C.15)

\displaystyle f_{0}(\xi_{x})={\mathfrak{q}}^{-x}f_{x}(z_{x}(\xi_{x}))={\mathfrak{q}}^{-x}f_{x}(\gamma_{x}),

where the first statement follows from (
C.6
) and the second from Definition
C.3
. Hence, it suffices to show that
f_{x}(\gamma_{x})\asymp\mathfrak{d}_{z}
. By Lemma
C.5
and
\gamma_{x}^{\prime}\asymp\mathfrak{d}_{z}
, it follows that
f_{x}(\gamma_{x})\asymp\mathfrak{d}_{z}
, showing the bound.
∎

C.1.2.
Arctic curve at
q=0

Here, we derive the values of the functions from the previous sections at
\mathfrak{q}=1
. We have from (
C.4
) (and the fact that
f_{x}(z_{x})
is constant in
x
for
\mathfrak{q}=1
, by (
C.6
)) that

(C.16)

\displaystyle\gamma_{x}=\xi_{x}+x\gamma_{x}^{\prime}.

We also have from
C.5
that

(C.17)

\displaystyle\gamma_{x}^{\prime}=\displaystyle\frac{f_{0}(\xi_{x})}{f_{0}(\xi_{x})-1}=\frac{(b+c-\xi_{x})\xi_{x}}{c\xi_{x}+a(\xi_{x}-b)}.

By (
C.12
), we have

\displaystyle f_{0}(\xi_{x})=\displaystyle\frac{\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}};\qquad f_{0}^{\prime}(\xi_{x})=\displaystyle\frac{x}{(x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x})^{2}},

from which it follows using (
C.16
) and (
C.17
) that

(C.18)

x=\displaystyle\frac{(f_{0}(\xi_{x})-1)^{2}}{f_{0}^{\prime}(\xi_{x})};\\
\gamma_{x}=\xi_{x}+f_{0}(\xi_{x})(f_{0}(\xi_{x})-1)f_{0}^{\prime}(\xi_{x})^{-1}=\frac{c(a+b+c)\xi_{x}^{2}}{c\xi_{x}^{2}+a(b^{2}+b(c-2\xi_{x})+\xi_{x}^{2})}.

From the first line of (
C.18
), together with the fact that

(C.19)

\displaystyle f_{0}(u)=\frac{u(u-b-c)}{(u+a)(u-b)}

for
\mathfrak{q}=1
, that follows from (
B.2
), we can express
\xi^{\prime}_{x}
in terms of
\xi_{x}
itself, and then through (
C.17
) we can compute the derivatives (of any order) of
\gamma_{x}
with respect to
x
, as function of
\xi_{x}
. In particular, one finds the explicit expressions

(C.20)

\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{x}=\frac{(c\xi_{x}^{2}+a(b^{2}+b(c-2\xi_{x})+\xi_{x}^{2}))^{3}}{2abc(a+b+c)(a(b-\xi_{x})-c\xi_{x})^{3}},\\
\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}_{x}=\frac{3(c\xi_{x}^{2}+a(b^{2}+b(c-2\xi_{x})+\xi_{x}^{2}))^{4}(c^{2}\xi_{x}^{2}+a^{2}(b^{2}+\xi_{x}^{2}-b(c+2\xi_{x}))-ac(b^{2}-2\xi_{x}^{2}+b(c+2\xi_{x})))}{4(abc)^{2}(a+b+c)^{2}(a(b-\xi_{x})-c\xi_{x})^{5}}.

The above holds for arbitrary
(a,b,c)
. Now let us set
(a,b,c)=(1,1,1)
. Then, we have

(C.21)

\displaystyle x=\displaystyle\frac{(1-2\xi_{x})^{2}}{2(\xi_{x}^{2}-\xi_{x}+1)};\qquad\gamma_{x}=\displaystyle\frac{3\xi_{x}^{2}}{2(\xi_{x}^{2}-\xi_{x}+1)}.

This prescribes an arc of ellipse as we vary
\xi_{x}\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
.

C.1.3.
Scaling of the complex slope near
\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}

The following lemma approximates the complex slope
f_{x}(y)
for
(x,y)
in the liquid region but
y
close to
\gamma_{x}
. Underlying its proof is the fact that, while the function
f_{x}(y)
is not differentiable in
y
around
y=\gamma_{x}
for general
x>0
(as is indeed implied by the below lemma), the function
f_{0}
from (
B.2
) is. Thus, we will use the fact from (
C.6
) that
f_{x}(y)=\mathfrak{q}^{x}f_{0}(u)
, for
u
defined by setting
y=z_{x}(u)
, observing that
u\approx\xi_{x}
as
y\approx\gamma_{x}
; Taylor expand
f_{0}
around
\xi_{x}
; and use the results from
Section

C.1.1
to evaluate the resulting coefficients.

Lemma C.11
.

Let
C>1
be a constant.
Letting
x\in[1/4,5/4]
, the following two statements hold.

(1)

Recalling
\Gamma_{x;2}
from (
C.9
), we have for
\gamma_{x}\leq y
that

(C.22)

\displaystyle f_{x}(y)=f_{x}(\gamma_{x})+\mathrm{i}(f_{x}(\gamma_{x})-1)^{2}\cdot(2\Gamma_{x;2}(y-\gamma_{x}))^{1/2}+(y-\gamma_{x})\cdot\varsigma_{1}(x,(y-\gamma_{x})^{1/2}),

where
\varsigma_{1}:[1/4,5/4]\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is a function dependent on
(a,b,c,q)
that satisfies the following. For any derivative
\partial_{\ell}
in
(x,z,a,b,c,q)
, there exists a constant
C_{\ell}>1
such that
|\partial_{\ell}\varsigma_{1}(x,z)|\leq C_{\ell}
, for all
(x,z)\in[1/4,5/4]\times[0,C]
;
q\in[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]
; and
a,b,c\in[1-\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon]
.

(2)

The asymptotics can be refined close to
p^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
. That is, assume that
\gamma_{x}\leq y\leq C\mathfrak{d}^{2}
and denote

(C.23)

\displaystyle D^{\prime}

\displaystyle=f_{0}^{\prime}(0)=\displaystyle\frac{\log\mathfrak{q}}{\mathfrak{q}^{x}-1};

(C.24)

\displaystyle A^{\prime}

\displaystyle=f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(0)+\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x}+1}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x}-1)^{2}}(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2};

(C.25)

\displaystyle B^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\cdot\Bigg(f_{0}^{\prime\prime\prime}(0)+\displaystyle\frac{2(\mathfrak{q}^{2x}+\mathfrak{q}^{x}+1)}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x}-1)^{3}}(\log\mathfrak{q})^{3}+3f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(0)\cdot\displaystyle\frac{(\mathfrak{q}^{x}+1)}{\mathfrak{q}^{x}-1}\log\mathfrak{q}\Bigg).

Then, we have

(C.26)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} f_{x}(y)-f_{x}(\gamma_{x})&=\mathrm{i}(f_{x}(\gamma_{x})-1)^{2}\cdot(2\Gamma_{x;2}(y-\gamma_{x}))^{1/2}\\
&\qquad+\mathfrak{q}^{x}A^{\prime-1}D^{\prime}(A^{\prime-1}B^{\prime}D^{\prime}-f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(0))\cdot(y-\gamma_{x})+\mathfrak{d}^{3}\cdot\varsigma_{2}(x,(y-\gamma_{x})^{1/2}),\end{aligned}

where
\varsigma_{2}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is a function dependent on
(a,b,c,q)
that satisfies the following. For any derivative
\partial_{\ell}
in
(x,z,a,b,c,q)
, there exists a constant
C_{\ell}>1
such that
|\partial_{\ell}\varsigma_{2}(x,z)|\leq C_{\ell}
, for all
(x,z)\in[1/4,5/4]\times[0,C\mathfrak{d}]
;
q\in[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]
; and
a,b,c\in[1-\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon]
.

In the second claim, one should have in mind the case of
\mathfrak{d}
small, so that
(x,\gamma_{x})
is indeed close to
x^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
.

Proof.

Throughout this proof, for any parameter
\varkappa
, we let
\mathcal{O}(\varkappa)
denote a quantity of the form
\kappa\cdot\varsigma(x,(y-\gamma_{x})^{1/2})
, where
\varsigma
is a bounded function (that can depend on
\kappa
), that also smoothly depends on
(a,b,c,q)
, such that the following holds. For any derivative
\partial_{\gamma}
in the variables
(x,z,a,b,c,q)
, there exists a constant
C_{\gamma}>1
such that
|\partial_{\gamma}\varsigma(z)|\leq C_{\gamma}
.

Now let
y=z_{x}(u)
. Since by (
C.7
) we have

\displaystyle f_{0}(u)=\mathfrak{q}^{-x}+\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{-x}-1}{\mathfrak{q}^{u+x-y}-1},

it follows that

(C.27)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} f_{0}&(\xi_{x})+(u-\xi_{x})\cdot f_{0}^{\prime}(\xi_{x})+\displaystyle\frac{(u-\xi_{x})^{2}}{2}\cdot f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{x})\\
&=f_{0}(u)+\mathcal{O}(|u-\xi_{x}|^{3})=\mathfrak{q}^{-x}+\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{-x}-1}{\mathfrak{q}^{x+u-y}-1}+\mathcal{O}(|u-\xi_{x}|^{3})\\
&=\mathfrak{q}^{-x}+\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{-x}}{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\gamma_{x}-\xi_{x}}-1}+(y-\gamma_{x})\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{2}}\cdot(\mathfrak{q}^{-x}-1)\log\mathfrak{q}\\
&\qquad+(u-\xi_{x})\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{2}}(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-x})\log\mathfrak{q}\\
&\qquad+(u-\xi_{x})^{2}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}+1)}{2(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{3}}(\mathfrak{q}^{-x}-1)(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2}+\mathcal{O}(|y-\gamma_{x}|^{2}+|u-\xi_{x}|^{3}),\end{aligned}

where we used the facts that

(C.28)

\displaystyle\partial_{y}(\mathfrak{q}^{x+u-y}-1)^{-1}|_{(u,y)=(\xi_{x},\gamma_{x})}=\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{-2}\log\mathfrak{q};

(C.29)

\displaystyle\partial_{u}(\mathfrak{q}^{x+u-y}-1)^{-1}|_{(u,y)=(\xi_{x},\gamma_{x})}=-\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{-2}\log\mathfrak{q};

(C.30)

\displaystyle\partial_{u}^{2}(\mathfrak{q}^{x+u-y}-1)^{-1}|_{(u,y)=(\xi_{x},\gamma_{x})}=\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}+1)(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{-3}(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2}.

Thus, by
C.5
, we have

\displaystyle(u-\xi_{x})^{2}

\displaystyle\bigg(f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{x})-\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}+1)}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{3}}(\mathfrak{q}^{-x}-1)(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\displaystyle\frac{2(\gamma_{x}-y)\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{2}}(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-x})\log\mathfrak{q}+\mathcal{O}(|y-\gamma_{x}|^{2}+|u-\xi_{x}|^{3}),

and so
|u-\xi_{x}|=\mathcal{O}(|y-\gamma_{x}|^{1/2})
and

\displaystyle u-\xi_{x}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\Bigg(\displaystyle\frac{2\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(y-\gamma_{x})(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-x})\log\mathfrak{q}}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\bigg(f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{x})-\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}+1)}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{3}}(\mathfrak{q}^{-x}-1)(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2}\bigg)^{-1}\Bigg)^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}(y-\gamma_{x}).

Therefore, recalling the quantity
A=A_{x}
from (
C.8
), we obtain

\displaystyle f_{x}(y)-f_{x}(\gamma_{x})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{q}^{x}\big(f_{0}(u)-f_{0}(\xi_{x})\big)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{q}^{x}f_{0}^{\prime}(\xi_{x})\cdot(u-\xi_{x})+\mathcal{O}(|u-\xi_{x}|^{2})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{2x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-x})\log\mathfrak{q}}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{2}}\Bigg(\displaystyle\frac{2\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(y-\gamma_{x})(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-x})\log\mathfrak{q}}{A(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{2}}\Bigg)^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}(y-\gamma_{x})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}(f_{x}(\gamma_{x})-1)^{3}\Bigg(\displaystyle\frac{2(y-\gamma_{x})\mathfrak{q}^{3\xi_{x}-3\gamma_{x}-x}(\log\mathfrak{q})^{3}}{(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-x})^{3}A}\Bigg)^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}(y-\gamma_{x}).

where we used the fact that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{1}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{3}}=\displaystyle\frac{(f_{0}(\xi_{x})-\mathfrak{q}^{-x})^{3}}{(\mathfrak{q}^{-x}-1)^{3}}=\mathfrak{q}^{-3x}\displaystyle\frac{(f_{x}(\gamma_{x})-1)^{3}}{(\mathfrak{q}^{-x}-1)^{3}}.

We therefore obtain (
C.22
) by recalling the definition (
C.9
) of
\Gamma_{x;2}
.

Next, we prove (
C.26
). Since from the argument above it follows that
|u-\xi_{x}|=O(\mathfrak{d})
, we can improve (
C.27
) to

\displaystyle f_{0}

\displaystyle(\xi_{x})+(u-\xi_{x})\cdot f_{0}^{\prime}(\xi_{x})+\displaystyle\frac{(u-\xi_{x})^{2}}{2}\cdot f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{x})+\displaystyle\frac{(u-\xi_{x})^{3}}{6}\cdot f_{0}^{\prime\prime\prime}(\xi_{x})

\displaystyle=f_{0}(u)+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{4})=\mathfrak{q}^{-x}+\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{-x}-1}{\mathfrak{q}^{x+u-y}-1}+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{4})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{q}^{-x}+\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{-x}-1}{\mathfrak{q}^{x-\gamma_{x}+\xi_{x}}-1}+(y-\gamma_{x})\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{2}}\cdot(\mathfrak{q}^{-x}-1)\log\mathfrak{q}

\displaystyle\qquad+(u-\xi_{x})\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{2}}(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-x})\log\mathfrak{q}

\displaystyle\qquad+(u-\xi_{x})^{2}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}+1)}{2(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{3}}(\mathfrak{q}^{-x}-1)(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+(u-\xi_{x})(y-\gamma_{x})\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}+1)}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{3}}(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-x})(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+(u-\xi_{x})^{3}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(\mathfrak{q}^{2x+2\xi_{x}-2\gamma_{x}}+4\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}+1)}{6(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{4}}(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-x})(\log\mathfrak{q})^{3}+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{4}),

where we used (
C.30
) together with

\displaystyle\partial_{u}\partial_{y}(\mathfrak{q}^{x+u-y}-1)^{-1}|_{(u,y)=(\xi_{x},\gamma_{x})}=-\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}+1)(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{-3}(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2};

\displaystyle\partial_{u}^{3}(\mathfrak{q}^{x+u-y}-1)^{-1}|_{(u,y)=(\xi_{x},\gamma_{x})}=-\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(\mathfrak{q}^{2x+2\xi_{x}-2\gamma_{x}}+4\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}+1)}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{4}}\cdot(\log\mathfrak{q})^{3}.

Thus, by
C.8
, it follows that

\displaystyle(u-\xi_{x}

\displaystyle)^{2}\cdot\Bigg(f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{x})-\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}+1)}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{3}}(\mathfrak{q}^{-x}-1)(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2}\Bigg)+\displaystyle\frac{(u-\xi_{x})^{3}}{3}\cdot f_{0}^{\prime\prime\prime}(0)

\displaystyle=2(\gamma_{x}-y)\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{2}}\cdot(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-x})\log\mathfrak{q}

\displaystyle\qquad+2(u-\xi_{x})(y-\gamma_{x})\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}+1)}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{3}}(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-x})(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+(u-\xi_{x})^{3}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(\mathfrak{q}^{2x+2\xi_{x}-2\gamma_{x}}+4\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}+1)}{3(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{4}}(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-x})(\log\mathfrak{q})^{3}+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{4})

\displaystyle=2(\gamma_{x}-y)\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{2}}\cdot(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-x})\log\mathfrak{q}

\displaystyle\qquad+2(u-\xi_{x})(y-\gamma_{x})\cdot\displaystyle\frac{(\mathfrak{q}^{x}+1)}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x}-1)^{2}}(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+(u-\xi_{x})^{3}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{(\mathfrak{q}^{2x}+4\mathfrak{q}^{x}+1)}{3(\mathfrak{q}^{x}-1)^{3}}(\log\mathfrak{q})^{3}+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{4}).

We have replaced
\xi_{x},\gamma_{x}
with
0
in the terms proportional to
(u-\xi_{x})^{3}
and
(u-\xi_{x})^{2}(y-\gamma_{x})
, that are
O(\mathfrak{d}^{3})
. In fact, recall from Lemma

that
\xi_{x}\asymp\mathfrak{d}
, and that
\gamma_{x}\asymp\mathfrak{d}^{2}
. So, we have

\displaystyle\gamma_{x}-y=(u-\xi_{x})^{2}\cdot\bigg(f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(0)\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x}-1}{2\log\mathfrak{q}}+\displaystyle\frac{(\mathfrak{q}^{x}+1)}{2(\mathfrak{q}^{x}-1)}\log\mathfrak{q}\bigg)+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{3}),

meaning that

\displaystyle A(u-\xi_{x})^{2}+B(u-\xi_{x})^{3}=2D(\gamma_{x}-y)+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{4}).

where we have denoted

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{x})-\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}+1)}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{3}}(\mathfrak{q}^{-x}-1)(\log\mathfrak{q})^{2};

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\cdot\Bigg(f_{0}^{\prime\prime\prime}(0)+\displaystyle\frac{2(\mathfrak{q}^{2x}+\mathfrak{q}^{x}+1)}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x}-1)^{3}}(\log\mathfrak{q})^{3}+3f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(0)\cdot\displaystyle\frac{(\mathfrak{q}^{x}+1)}{\mathfrak{q}^{x}-1}\log\mathfrak{q}\Bigg);

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=f_{0}^{\prime}(\xi_{x})=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}}{(\mathfrak{q}^{x+\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}}-1)^{2}}\cdot(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-x})\log\mathfrak{q}=(f_{x}(\gamma_{x})-1)^{2}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{\xi_{x}-\gamma_{x}-x}}{1-\mathfrak{q}^{-x}}\log\mathfrak{q}.

So,
|u-\xi_{x}|=\mathcal{O}(|y-\gamma_{x}|^{1/2})
and

\displaystyle u-\xi_{x}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}(2A^{-1}D(y-\gamma_{x}))^{1/2}+A^{-2}BD(y-\gamma_{x})+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{3}).

Therefore, recalling the quantity
A=A_{x}
from (
C.8
) and
\Gamma_{x;2}
from (
C.9
), and using the identity
{\mathfrak{q}}^{x}f^{\prime}_{0}(\xi_{x})\sqrt{2D/A}=(f_{x}(\gamma_{x})-1)^{2}\sqrt{2\Gamma_{x;2}}
that follows from
Lemma
C.8
and (
C.6
),
we obtain

(C.31)

\displaystyle f_{x}(y)-f_{x}(\gamma_{x})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{q}^{x}\big(f_{0}(u)-f_{0}(\xi_{x})\big)

(C.32)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{q}^{x}f_{0}^{\prime}(\xi_{x})\cdot(u-\xi_{x})-A^{-1}D\mathfrak{q}^{x}f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{x})\cdot(y-\gamma_{x})+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{3})

(C.33)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}(f_{x}(\gamma_{x})-1)^{2}\cdot(2\Gamma_{x;2}(y-\gamma_{x}))^{1/2}+{\mathfrak{q}}^{x}\frac{D^{\prime}}{A^{\prime}}(y-\gamma_{x})\Big(-f^{\prime\prime}_{0}(0)+\frac{D^{\prime}B^{\prime}}{A^{\prime}}\Big)+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{3}),

where
A^{\prime},B^{\prime},D^{\prime}
are as in the statement of the lemma.
∎

From
C.11
we can get an approximated expression for the complex slope, in the rescaled coordinates
\hat{x},\hat{y}
of (
3.24
).
In particular,
C.13
is relevant when we look at points

, while
C.15
is relevant when
\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}\asymp\mathfrak{d}_{z}\ll 1
, which is the case very close to a tangency location.

Remark C.12
.

Throughout the remainder of this section, for any parameter
\varkappa
, we let
\mathcal{O}(\varkappa)
denote a smooth function
\varsigma
, often in two arguments
16
16

16

Observe that our convention here is slightly different from that in the proof of
C.11
, where
\varsigma
was a function of
x
and
(y-\gamma_{x})^{1/2}
.

(\hat{x},\hat{y})
(on some specified compact domain) and also dependent on
(a,b,c,q)
, such that
|\partial_{\ell}\varsigma(\hat{x},\hat{y})|\leq C_{\ell}\varkappa
, where
\partial_{\ell}
denotes a derivative (of any order) of
\varsigma
in the variables
(\hat{x},\hat{y},a,b,c,q)
(and
C_{\ell}
is a finite constant depending only on
\ell
).

Corollary C.13
.

Let
\mathfrak{d},\mathfrak{e}\lesssim 1
be positive numbers. There exists a constant

C>1
such that the following holds.
Fix
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=(x^{\prime},\gamma_{x^{\prime}})
on the arctic boundary
\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
with
x^{\prime}\in[1/4,5/4]
, and denote
f=f_{x^{\prime}}(\gamma_{x^{\prime}})
;
\gamma=\gamma_{x^{\prime}}
;
\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma_{x^{\prime}}^{\prime}
; and
\gamma^{\prime\prime}=\gamma_{x^{\prime}}^{\prime\prime}
.
Let
z=(x,y)
with

(C.34)

\displaystyle x=x^{\prime}+(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}\hat{x}-\gamma^{\prime}\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}\hat{y};\qquad y=\gamma_{x^{\prime}}+\gamma^{\prime}(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}\hat{x}+\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}\hat{y}

and assume that

(C.35)

\frac{\mathfrak{d}}{|\gamma^{\prime}|},\frac{\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}}{\mathfrak{e}},\frac{\mathfrak{d}_{z}}{\mathfrak{d}}\in[C^{-1},C].

Denoting

(C.36)

\displaystyle\hat{f}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{-1/2}(f(x,y)-f);\qquad\Gamma=\gamma^{\prime\prime}-\gamma^{\prime}(1-\gamma^{\prime})\log\mathfrak{q},

we have

(C.37)

\displaystyle\hat{f}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{i}\Gamma^{1/2}}{(\gamma^{\prime}-1)^{2}}\big(2(\gamma^{\prime 2}+1)\hat{y}-\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}^{2}\big)^{1/2}-\displaystyle\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}}{(\gamma^{\prime}-1)^{2}}+\mathcal{O}((\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}).

Remark C.14
.

Note that the assumption (
C.35
) implies in particular that the argument of the square root in (
C.37
) is positive and of order
1
.

Proof.

By (
C.11
), we have that
\Gamma=\Gamma_{x^{\prime};2}
. By
C.11
and
C.5
it follows

(C.38)

\displaystyle f_{x}(\gamma_{x})-1=(\gamma^{\prime}-1)^{-1}-\frac{1}{(\gamma^{\prime}-1)^{2}}\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}}+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})

(C.39)

\displaystyle=f-1-\frac{1}{(\gamma^{\prime}-1)^{2}}\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}}+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})

and
\Gamma_{x^{\prime};2}=\Gamma_{x;2}+\mathcal{O}((\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2})
. Therefore,

\displaystyle f(x,y)=f+\displaystyle\frac{(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}}{(\gamma^{\prime}-1)^{2}}\Bigg(\mathrm{i}\cdot\bigg(2\Gamma\Big((\gamma^{\prime 2}+1)\hat{y}-\displaystyle\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}^{2}}{2}\Big)\bigg)^{1/2}-\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}\Bigg)+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})

where we used the fact that

\displaystyle y-\gamma_{x}=\gamma_{x^{\prime}}-\gamma_{x}+\gamma^{\prime}(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}\hat{x}+\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}\hat{y}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\prime}\big(x^{\prime}-x+(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}\hat{x}\big)-\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}^{2}}{2}+\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}\hat{y}+\mathcal{O}((\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{3/2})

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}}{2}\cdot\big(2\hat{y}(\gamma^{\prime 2}+1)-\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}^{2}\big)+\mathcal{O}\big((\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{3/2}).

Thus, the lemma follows from the definition (
C.36
) of
\hat{f}
.
∎

Corollary C.15
.

Let
C>1
be a fixed constant.
Denote
\gamma^{\prime\prime}=\gamma_{x^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}}^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}=\gamma_{x^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
, and let

(C.40)

\displaystyle x=x^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}+\mathfrak{d}\hat{x};\qquad y=\mathfrak{d}^{2}\hat{y}.

Assume that
z=(x,y)\in\mathfrak{L}_{q;a,b,c}
with
\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}\geq(1/C)\mathfrak{d},\mathfrak{d}_{z}\asymp\mathfrak{d}
. Denoting

(C.41)

\displaystyle\hat{f}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\mathfrak{d}^{-1}\cdot f(x,y);\qquad\Gamma_{\hat{x}}=\gamma^{\prime\prime}+\mathfrak{d}\hat{x}(\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}-\gamma^{\prime\prime}\log{\mathfrak{q}}+4\gamma^{\prime\prime 2}).

we have

(C.42)

\displaystyle\hat{f}(\hat{x},\hat{y})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\Gamma_{\hat{x}}^{1/2}\Big(2\hat{y}-\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}^{2}-\displaystyle\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}}{3}\mathfrak{d}\hat{x}^{3}\Big)^{1/2}-\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}-\mathfrak{d}\Big(\gamma^{\prime\prime 2}+\displaystyle\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}}{2}\Big)\hat{x}^{2}+\mathfrak{d}g\Big(\hat{y}-\gamma^{\prime\prime}\displaystyle\frac{\hat{x}^{2}}{2}\Big)+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{2}),

where
A^{\prime},B^{\prime},D^{\prime}
are as in (
C.23
) with
x=x^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
and

(C.43)

\displaystyle g=g(q,a,b,c):=\mathfrak{q}^{x_{q;a,b,c}^{SW}}A^{\prime-1}D^{\prime}(D^{\prime}A^{\prime-1}B^{\prime}-f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(0)).

Remark C.16
.

Note that, with respect to
C.13
, in the definition of
\hat{f}
we do not subtract
f=f_{x^{\prime}}(\gamma_{x^{\prime}})
since in the present case we have
x^{\prime}=x_{q;a,b,c}^{SW}
, where
f_{x^{\prime}}(\gamma_{x^{\prime}})=0
. Also, the assumption
\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}\geq(1/C)\mathfrak{d}
implies that imaginary part of
\hat{f}
is positive and bounded away from zero and that
\hat{y}
is bounded away from zero and infinity.

Proof.

By
C.5
it follows

(C.44)

\displaystyle f_{x}(\gamma_{x})-1=-1-\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}\mathfrak{d}-(\gamma^{\prime\prime 2}+\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}/2)\mathfrak{d}^{2}\hat{x}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{3}).

We also have by (
C.11
)

(C.45)

\Gamma_{x;2}=\gamma^{\prime\prime}+\hat{x}\mathfrak{d}(\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}-\gamma^{\prime\prime}\log{\mathfrak{q}})+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{2})

as well as

(C.46)

\displaystyle y-\gamma_{x}=\mathfrak{d}^{2}\Big(\hat{y}-\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}{2}\hat{x}^{2}-\mathfrak{d}\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}}{6}\hat{x}^{3}\Big)+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{4}).

The results then follows from
C.11
with
x=x_{q;a,b,c}^{SW}

and the definition (
C.36
) of
\hat{f}
.
∎

The following two lemmas use
C.13
and
C.15
to approximate derivatives of the (rescaled) height function in terms of the (rescaled) complex slope
\hat{f}
from (
C.36
).

Lemma C.17
.

Adopt the notation and assumptions of
C.13
, and denote

(C.47)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\mathfrak{d}^{-1/2}\mathfrak{e}^{-3/2}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)\quad\text{if}\quad\gamma^{\prime}>0,

(C.48)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\mathfrak{d}^{-1/2}\mathfrak{e}^{-3/2}(y-\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y))\quad\text{if}\quad\gamma^{\prime}<0.

Then, we have

(C.49)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\partial_{\hat{x}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{d}(1-\gamma^{\prime})^{3}}{\pi|\gamma^{\prime}|}\cdot\Real\hat{f}(\hat{x},\hat{y})\cdot\Imaginary\hat{f}(\hat{x},\hat{y})+\mathcal{O}((\mathfrak{d}^{-1}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2});\\
&\partial_{\hat{y}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{d}(1-\gamma^{\prime})(\gamma^{\prime 2}+1)}{\pi|\gamma^{\prime}|}\cdot\Imaginary\hat{f}(\hat{x},\hat{y})+\mathcal{O}((\mathfrak{d}^{-1}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}).\end{aligned}

Proof.

We give the proof in the case
\gamma^{\prime}>0
, the other case being analogous (one just needs to replace (
C.55
) below with
\arg v=|v|^{-1}\Imaginary v+\mathcal{O}((\Imaginary v)^{3}/|v|^{3})
for
\Real v>0
). Throughout, we abbreviate
\hat{f}=\hat{f}(\hat{x},\hat{y})
. Recall from
C.13
that
f:=f_{x^{\prime}}(\gamma_{x^{\prime}})
and in particular from
C.6
that

(C.50)

\displaystyle f\asymp-\mathfrak{d}<0,1-f\asymp 1.

We begin by showing the second statement in (
C.49
). To that end, by (
C.34
), we have

(C.51)

\displaystyle\partial_{\hat{y}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}^{-1})^{1/2}\cdot(\partial_{y}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)-\gamma^{\prime}\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)).

Using (
B.5
) and (
C.36
), so that

(C.52)

\displaystyle\Real(f(x,y))<0,\qquad\Real(f(x,y)-1)<0,

we obtain that

(C.53)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \partial_{x}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)&=\pi^{-1}\big(\arg(f(x,y)-1)-\pi\big)=-\displaystyle\frac{\Imaginary f(x,y)}{\pi|f(x,y)-1|}+{\mathcal{O}((\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{3/2})}\\
&=(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\Imaginary\hat{f}}{\pi(f-1)^{2}}\cdot(f-1-(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}\Real\hat{f})+{\mathcal{O}((\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{3/2})},\end{aligned}

and

(C.54)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \partial_{y}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y)&=\pi^{-1}(\pi-\arg f(x,y))\\
&=\pi^{-1}\sin^{-1}\bigg((\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\Imaginary\hat{f}}{|f(x,y)|}\bigg)\\
&=\pi^{-1}\sin^{-1}\big((\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}f^{-2}\Real\hat{f}-(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}f^{-1})\cdot\Imaginary\hat{f}\big)+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{-1/2}\mathfrak{e}^{3/2}).\end{aligned}

Here, in the first statement of (
C.53
), we used (
B.5
); in the second we used (
C.50
) and (
C.52
) and the fact that, for any
v\in\overline{\mathbb{H}}
with
\Real v<0
,

(C.55)

\displaystyle\arg v=\pi-|v|^{-1}\cdot\Imaginary v+\mathcal{O}((\Imaginary v/|v|)^{3});

and in the third we used the definition (
C.36
) of
\hat{f}
, with the fact that

\displaystyle|f(x,y)-1|^{-1}

\displaystyle=\big|1-f-(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}\Real\hat{f}-\mathrm{i}(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}\Imaginary\hat{f}\big|^{-1}

\displaystyle=\big(1-f-(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}\Real\hat{f}\big)^{-1}+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})

\displaystyle=(f-1)^{-2}\cdot\big(1-f+(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}\Real\hat{f}\big)+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}),

again by (
C.50
). In the first statement of (
C.54
), we used (
B.5
); in the second we used (
C.36
) and (
C.50
), (
C.52
); and in the third we used the facts that
-f(x,y)\asymp\mathfrak{d}
and that

\displaystyle|f(x,y)|^{-1}=\big|f+(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}\Real\hat{f}+\mathrm{i}(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}\Imaginary\hat{f}\big|^{-1}

\displaystyle=\big|f+(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}\Real\hat{f}\big|^{-1}+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{-1}\mathfrak{e})

\displaystyle=f^{-2}\cdot((\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}\Real\hat{f}-f)+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{-1}\mathfrak{e}).

Inserting (
C.53
) and (
C.54
) into (
C.51
), using
C.5
, denoting

(C.56)

\displaystyle\Upsilon=(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}f^{-2}\Real\hat{f}-(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}f^{-1})\cdot\Imaginary\hat{f},

and since by
C.5
we have

\displaystyle-\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{d}}{f}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{f^{2}+(f-1)^{2}}{\pi(f-1)^{2}}\cdot\Imaginary\hat{f}=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{d}(1-\gamma^{\prime})(\gamma^{\prime 2}+1)}{\pi\gamma^{\prime}}\cdot\Imaginary\hat{f},\quad\frac{f^{3}+(f-1)^{3}}{f^{2}(f-1)^{3}}=(1+\gamma^{\prime 3})\frac{(1-\gamma^{\prime})^{2}}{\gamma^{\prime 2}}

we obtain

(C.57)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \partial_{\hat{y}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}&=\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\mathfrak{d}}{\mathfrak{e}}}\frac{\sin^{-1}(\Upsilon)-\Upsilon}{\pi}+\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{d}(1-\gamma^{\prime})(\gamma^{\prime 2}+1)}{\pi\gamma^{\prime}}\cdot\Imaginary\hat{f}\\
&\qquad+\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{d}^{2}}{\pi}\cdot\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\mathfrak{e}}{\mathfrak{d}}}\cdot(1+\gamma^{\prime 3})\frac{(1-\gamma^{\prime})^{2}}{\gamma^{\prime 2}}\cdot\Real\hat{f}\cdot\Imaginary\hat{f}+\mathcal{O}((\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}),\end{aligned}

where we have abbreviate
\partial_{\hat{y}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\partial_{\hat{y}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
.
We then deduce the second bound in (
C.49
) by combining (
C.57
) with

\sin^{-1}(\Upsilon)-\Upsilon=O(\Upsilon^{3})

(since
|\Upsilon|=\mathcal{O}((\mathfrak{e}/\mathfrak{d})^{1/2})
, as
|f|\asymp\mathfrak{d}
).

To address the
\hat{x}
derivative, observe again by (
C.34
) that

(C.58)

\displaystyle\partial_{\hat{x}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\mathfrak{e}^{-1}\cdot(\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}_{q}(x,y)+\gamma^{\prime}\partial_{y}\mathcal{H}_{q}(x,y)).

Thus, inserting (
C.53
) and (
C.54
) into (
C.58
), using
C.5
, and recalling (
C.56
), we obtain

(C.59)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \partial_{\hat{x}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}&=\displaystyle\frac{\gamma^{\prime}}{\pi\mathfrak{e}}\big(\sin^{-1}(\Upsilon)-\Upsilon\big)+\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{d}(1-\gamma^{\prime})^{3}}{\pi\gamma^{\prime}}\cdot\Real\hat{f}\cdot\Imaginary\hat{f}+\mathcal{O}((\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}),\end{aligned}

where we have abbreviated
\partial_{\hat{x}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\partial_{\hat{x}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
. We then deduce the first bound in (
C.49
) by combining (
C.59
) with
\sin^{-1}(\Upsilon)-\Upsilon=O(\Upsilon^{3})

(since
|\Upsilon|=\mathcal{O}((\mathfrak{e}/\mathfrak{d})^{1/2})
, as
|f|\asymp\mathfrak{d}
).
∎

Lemma C.18
.

Adopt the notation and assumptions of
C.15
, and denote

(C.60)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\mathfrak{d}^{-2}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(x,y).

Then, we have
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\mathfrak{H}_{q,\gamma^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{2})
, where

(C.61)

\mathfrak{H}_{q,\gamma^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\mathtt{H}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})+\frac{\mathfrak{d}}{\pi\sqrt{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}}\left[\left(\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}{3}-\frac{q}{6}+\frac{2\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}}{9\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\right)(2\hat{y}-\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}^{2})^{3/2}-\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}}{3\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\hat{y}\sqrt{2\hat{y}-\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}^{2}}\right],\\
\mathtt{H}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\displaystyle\frac{\hat{y}}{2}-\frac{1}{\pi}\left[\frac{\hat{x}}{2}\sqrt{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\,\sqrt{2\hat{y}-\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}^{2}}+\hat{y}\,\arctan\!\left(\frac{\sqrt{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\hat{x}}{\sqrt{2\hat{y}-\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}^{2}}}\right)\right].

Some useful facts about the functions
\mathfrak{H}_{q,\gamma^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})
and
\mathtt{H}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}
are collected in
Section

C.3.6
.

Proof.

It follows immediately from
(
C.40
), (
C.41
) and (
B.5
) that

(C.62)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\partial_{\hat{x}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\frac{1}{\mathfrak{d}}\left(-1+\frac{1}{\pi}\arg(\mathfrak{d}\hat{f}(\hat{x},\hat{y})-1)\right)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\Imaginary(\hat{f})[1+\mathfrak{d}\Real(\hat{f})]+{\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{2})}\\
&=-\frac{1}{\pi}\Imaginary(\hat{f})[1-\mathfrak{d}\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}]+{\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{2})}\\
&\partial_{\hat{y}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=1-\frac{1}{\pi}\arg(\hat{f}(\hat{x},\hat{y}))=1-\frac{1}{\pi}\arctan\Big(\frac{\Imaginary(\hat{f})}{\Real(\hat{f})}\Big).\end{aligned}

From (
C.42
) and (
C.62
) one deduces, letting
S=2\hat{y}-\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\partial_{\hat{x}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=-\frac{1}{\pi}\sqrt{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\,\sqrt{S}-\frac{\mathfrak{d}}{\pi}\left[\sqrt{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\,\hat{x}\left(\gamma^{\prime\prime}-\frac{q}{2}+\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}}{2\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\right)\sqrt{S}-\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}\sqrt{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}}{6}\frac{\hat{x}^{3}}{\sqrt{S}}\right]+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{2}).

Integrating with respect to
\hat{x}
gives

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})

\displaystyle=C(\hat{y})-\displaystyle\frac{\hat{y}}{2}-\frac{1}{\pi}\left[\frac{\hat{x}}{2}\sqrt{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\,\sqrt{S}+\hat{y}\,\arctan\!\left(\frac{\sqrt{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\hat{x}}{\sqrt{S}}\right)\right]
