\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\mathfrak{d}}{\pi\sqrt{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}}\left[\left(\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}{3}-\frac{q}{6}+\frac{2\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}}{9\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\right)S^{3/2}-\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}}{3\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\hat{y}\sqrt{S}\right]+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{2})

where
C
depends only on
\hat{y}
. Differentiating with respect to
\hat{y}
and comparing with the second of (
C.62
) implies that
\partial_{\hat{y}}C(\hat{y})=1+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{2})
and (
C.61
) follows.
∎

C.1.4.
Proof of
3.27
,
3.18
and
3.28

Proof of
3.18
.

The proof of the fourth statement is very similar to that of the first three and is therefore omitted. The third statement of the lemma follows from integrating the first from
z_{0}=(x_{0},y_{0})
to
(x_{1},y_{0})
, where
x_{1}>x_{0}
is chosen so that
(x_{1},y_{0})\in\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
(which satisfies
x_{1}-x_{0}=\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}
). It thus suffices to prove the first and second statements of the lemma.

When
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}\geq\omega
for a fixed constant
\omega>0
, these follow from
B.9
.
Therefore, we can assume that
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}\leq\omega
for some sufficiently small but fixed constant
\omega>0
.
We distinguish two situations: either
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}\leq(1/{\bf K})\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}
with
{\bf K}
a sufficiently large constant, or
\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\leq(1/{\bf K})\wedge({\bf K}\mathfrak{e}_{{z_{0}};q})
.
In the former case, we apply
C.13
and (
B.5
).
In particular, we have

(C.63)

\displaystyle\partial_{y}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}=1-\frac{1}{\pi}\arg(f+\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{{z_{0}};q}}\hat{f});

together with (
C.50
) and
|\Real(\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{{z_{0}};q}}\hat{f})|\lesssim\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}/\sqrt{{\bf K}}
that is negligible with respect to
f
(provided that
{\bf K}
is large enough), item (2) of
3.18
follows. Item (1) follows, similarly, from

(C.64)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}=-1+\frac{1}{\pi}\arg(-1+f+\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\mathfrak{e}_{{z_{0}};q}}\hat{f})

and the fact that
-1+f\asymp-1
(again by (
C.50
)).

In the case
\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\leq(1/{\bf K})\wedge({\bf K}\mathfrak{e}_{{z_{0}};q})
, we use
C.15
instead. For item (2), note that we need just to prove that
\partial_{y}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}
is bounded away from zero, which follows from
\partial_{y}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}=1-\pi^{-1}\arg(\hat{f})
together with
C.16
. Finally, for item (1) we need to prove that
-\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}\asymp-\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}
, which quickly follows from
\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}=-1+\frac{1}{\pi}\arg(-1+\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\hat{f})
,
C.15
and
C.16
.
∎

Proof of
3.27
.

By
B.9
, we can restrict to the case where
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}\leq\omega
for some sufficiently small but fixed constant
\omega>0
.

When
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q}\leq(1/{\bf K})\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}
, the statement is a consequence of
C.17
, together with
C.12
, which guarantees that the error terms in (
C.49
) are themselves smooth functions of the coordinates, and
C.14
, that guarantees that there is no singularity related to the square root in (
C.37
). In fact, note that the function
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
in
C.17
differs from the function
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}
in
3.27
only by a space translation (coordinates are centered at
z_{0}
for
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}
and at a point on the arctic boundary for
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
) and by a bounded linear transformation of coordinates (with bounded inverse). Indeed, the latter follows from recalling the definitions of the vectors
{\bm{u}}_{z_{0};q},{\bm{w}}_{z_{0};q}
, so that the change of coordinates in (
3.24
) is

(C.65)

\displaystyle x=x_{0}+\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}}\sqrt{1-\gamma^{\prime 2}}\hat{x}+\gamma^{\prime}\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}\hat{y},\quad y=y_{0}+\gamma^{\prime}\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}}\hat{x}-\sqrt{1-\gamma^{\prime 2}}\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}\hat{y},

to be compared with (
C.34
).

When
\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\leq(1/{\bf K})\wedge({\bf K}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q})
, the statement follows a similar reasoning, using this time
C.18
,
C.16
and
C.12
.

∎

Proof of
3.28
.

We rename
q
in the statement as
q_{0}
, because we will use
q
to indicate either the value
q_{0}
or
0
.
By
B.9
, we can restrict to the case where
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q_{0}}\leq\omega
for some sufficiently small but fixed constant
\omega>0
.
As in the proof of
3.18
and
3.27
, we distinguish the case
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q_{0}}<(1/{\bf K})\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}
and the case
\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}\leq(1/{\bf K})\wedge({\bf K}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q})
.
{\bf K}
is large but independent of any other parameter.

Case I:
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q_{0}}<(1/{\bf K})\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}
. We seek to locally compare the limit shape
\mathcal{H}_{q_{0};1,1,1}=\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}
around some point
z_{0}=(x_{0},y_{0})
close to its arctic boundary (call
z^{\prime}_{0}=(x_{0}^{\prime},y_{0}^{\prime})
the point of the boundary closest to
z_{0}
) to the limit shape
\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
of the
\mathfrak{q}=1
measure (with suitable side-lengths
(a,b,c)
) around some point
z_{1}=(x_{1},y_{1})
whose closest point on the associated arctic boundary is denoted by
z^{\prime}_{1}=(x_{1}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime})
.

Let
\mathfrak{d}:=\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}
and
\mathfrak{e}:=\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q_{0}}
.
For each
i\in\{0,1\}
, we denote the derivatives of the arctic boundary (for
\mathcal{H}_{q_{0}}
if
i=0
and
\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}
if
i=1
) at
(x_{i}^{\prime},y_{i}^{\prime})
by
(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{i}^{\prime},\gamma_{i}^{\prime\prime})
. We will later impose that
\gamma^{\prime}:=\gamma_{1}^{\prime}=\gamma_{0}^{\prime}
, which fixes
(x_{1}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime})
on the arctic boundary of the
\mathfrak{q}=1
limit shape.
We write
z_{0},z_{1}
as

(C.66)

\displaystyle x_{i}=x^{\prime}_{i}+(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}\hat{x_{i}}-\gamma^{\prime}\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}\hat{y_{i}};\qquad y_{i}=\gamma_{x^{\prime}_{i}}+\gamma^{\prime}(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}\hat{x_{i}}+\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}\hat{y_{i}},\quad i=0,1.

Note that we use the same
\mathfrak{d},\mathfrak{e}
for both, and that the rescaling of variables is the same as in
C.13
. Thanks to
3.13
, the coordinates
\hat{x}_{0},\hat{y}_{0}
are of order
1
(with
\hat{y}_{0}>0
since
z_{0}
is inside the liquid region). Our construction (see
C.21
below) will make sure that the same claims are true for
i=1
.

Define, for any
q\geq 0
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
using the notations of Lemma
C.17
.

Remark C.19
.

For
q=q_{0}
and
a=b=c=1
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
is nothing but
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q_{0};1,1,1}
in (
3.24
) (up to a shift and a bounded, linear transformation of coordinates, as remarked in the proof of
3.27
), and we denote it
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{0}
.
For
q=0
, instead,
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
is nothing but
{\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}}_{a,b,c}
in (
3.25
), again up to a shift and to the same linear change of coordinates, and we denote it
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. Since we are aiming at matching derivatives of the two rescaled height functions, these bounded affine transformations are inessential and it suffices to show that the statements of
3.28
hold with the functions
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q},\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}_{a,b,c}
appearing there replaced by
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{0}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
here.

Next, we verify that the map from
(a,x)
to
(\gamma^{\prime}_{x},\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{x})
is a bijection with bounded Jacobian for
a
around
1
, when
b,c
are fixed as
b=c=(3-a)/2
(so that
a+b+c=3
). It suffices to do so at
\mathfrak{q}=1
; this is permitted since is quickly seen from
B.5
that the arctic boundary is uniformly smooth in
\mathfrak{q}.

From (
C.17
) and (
C.20
) (with
b=c=(3-a)/2
) we have
(\gamma^{\prime}_{x},\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{x})
as an explicit function of
(a,\xi_{x})
. The Jacobian matrix
\mathcal{J}
of the map
(a,\xi_{x})\mapsto(\gamma^{\prime}_{x},\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{x})
and its determinant can be computed explicitly with the result that (for
a=1
)

(C.67)

\displaystyle\det(\mathcal{J})=\frac{2(1+(-1+\xi_{x})\xi_{x})^{2}(-2+\xi_{x}(4+\xi_{x}(6+(-8+\xi_{x})\xi_{x})))}{(-1+2\xi_{x})^{5}}.

The denominator vanishes for
\xi_{x}=1/2
, but by the geometric characterization of Remark
C.4
we know that
\xi_{x}<0
if
x>x^{SW}>1/2
. Recall from
C.1
that we have
1/2-O(\varepsilon)\leq x_{0}\leq 1+O(\varepsilon)
. Therefore, the denominator is non-zero in the range of parameters we are interested in. As for the numerator,
note
from (
C.21
) and from
x\in[1/2,1]
that
\xi_{x}
ranges between
\frac{1}{2}(1-\sqrt{3})
and
0
. On the other hand, one can quickly verify that the numerator does not vanish in this range.

Since the map
(a,x)\mapsto(\gamma^{\prime}_{x},\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{x})
is a local bijection, we fix
(a,x=:x^{\prime}_{1})
such that

(C.68)

\gamma^{\prime}_{1}=\gamma^{\prime}_{0},\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}=\gamma_{0}^{\prime\prime}-\mathfrak{c}_{0}q_{0}\mathfrak{d}

for some small constant
\mathfrak{c}_{0}>0
to be fixed later.
As a consequence, defining (according to (
C.36
))
\Gamma_{0}=\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{0}-\gamma^{\prime}(1-\gamma^{\prime})q_{0},\Gamma_{1}=\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{0}-\mathfrak{c}_{0}q_{0}\mathfrak{d}
, we have that
\Gamma_{1}-\Gamma_{0}=\gamma^{\prime}(1-\gamma^{\prime})q_{0}-\mathfrak{c}_{0}q_{0}\mathfrak{d}\asymp q_{0}\mathfrak{d}
, as
\gamma^{\prime}\asymp\mathfrak{d}
, provided that
\mathfrak{c}_{0}
is small enough. Since
z_{1}^{\prime}
lies on the arctic boundary, fixing
x_{1}^{\prime}
fixes also its second coordinate.

Up to now, we have fixed
z^{\prime}_{1}
. Now we want to fix
z_{1}
(that is,
\hat{x}_{1},\hat{y}_{1}
). To this purpose, we make the following observation.

Remark C.20
.

A look at the proof of
C.17
and
C.12
shows that, under the assumptions of
C.13
we have the following:
if
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, then

(C.69)

\displaystyle\partial^{a}_{\hat{x}}\partial^{b}_{\hat{y}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})

\displaystyle=\partial^{a}_{\hat{x}}\partial^{b}_{\hat{y}}\mathfrak{G}(\hat{x},\hat{y})+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}})+\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\mathfrak{e}/\mathfrak{d}}),

(C.70)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}(\hat{x},\hat{y})

\displaystyle=\Xi\cdot\big(2(\gamma^{\prime 2}+1)\hat{y}-\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}^{2}\big)^{3/2},\qquad\Xi:=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{d}\Gamma^{1/2}}{3\pi|\gamma^{\prime}|(1-\gamma^{\prime})}

where the error terms are smooth in
q,a,b,c,\hat{x},\hat{y}
(hence also in
\Gamma,\gamma^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime}
) and, in addition,
\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\mathfrak{e}/\mathfrak{d}})
are smooth functions of the arguments
\mathfrak{d},\mathfrak{e},\hat{f},\gamma^{\prime}
only. This is the reason why we distinguish the two types of error, despite the fact that
\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}}\ll\sqrt{\mathfrak{e}/\mathfrak{d}}
.

This should be compared with
C.18
, that holds under the assumptions of
C.15
instead (an aspect to be noted is that, while level lines of
\mathfrak{G}
are simply parabolas, those of
\mathfrak{H}
are more intricate).

We claim that the map from
(\hat{x},\hat{y})
to
\nabla\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})
is a bijection with bounded Jacobian. Indeed, in view of
C.20
, it suffices to do this for
\mathfrak{G}
, provided that
{\bf K}
is chosen large enough (recall that we are working under the assumption
that
\mathfrak{e}<(1/{\bf K})\mathfrak{d}
).
The Jacobian of the map
(\hat{x},\hat{y})\mapsto\nabla\mathfrak{G}(\hat{x},\hat{y})
is

(C.71)

J(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{d}\Gamma^{1/2}}{\pi|\gamma^{\prime}|(\gamma^{\prime}-1)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2(\gamma^{\prime 2}+1)\hat{y}-\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}^{2}}}\begin{pmatrix}2\gamma^{\prime\prime}(-\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}^{2}+(1+\gamma^{\prime 2})\hat{y})&(1+\gamma^{\prime 2})\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}\\
(1+\gamma^{\prime 2})\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}&-(1+\gamma^{\prime 2})^{2}\end{pmatrix}

with

(C.72)

\det(J)=-\frac{\mathfrak{d}^{2}\Gamma(1+\gamma^{\prime 2})^{2}\gamma^{\prime\prime}}{\pi^{2}\gamma^{\prime 2}(1-\gamma^{\prime})^{2}}

Note that this expression is strictly negative and bounded away from zero and minus infinity since the curvature
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
and
\Gamma
are positive and bounded uniformly if
q_{0}
is small and
a,b,c
are close to
1
. Recall also from Remark
C.6
that
\gamma^{\prime}
is bounded away from
1
, and that
\mathfrak{d}/\gamma^{\prime}\asymp 1
.

Remark C.21
.

Since the map from
(\hat{x},\hat{y})
to
\nabla\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})
is a bijection with bounded Jacobian, we fix
(\hat{x}_{1},\hat{y}_{1})
by imposing
\nabla\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{1}(\hat{x}_{1},\hat{y}_{1})=\nabla\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{0}(\hat{x}_{0},\hat{y}_{0})
.
Since
\Gamma_{1}-\Gamma_{0}\asymp\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{0}-\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{1}\asymp q_{0}\mathfrak{d}
, it follows from (
C.69
) that

|\hat{x}_{1}-\hat{x}_{0}|+|\hat{y}_{1}-\hat{y}_{0}|=\mathcal{O}(q_{0}\mathfrak{d})
.
This (together with Remark
C.20
) implies that
\partial_{\gamma}\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{1}(\hat{x}_{1},\hat{y}_{1})=\partial_{\gamma}\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}(\hat{x}_{0},\hat{y}_{0})+\mathcal{O}(q_{0}\mathfrak{d})
for any differential operator
\partial_{\gamma}
, which verifies the last statement in the proposition.

To complete the proof, it then suffices to show that
\nabla^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{1}(\hat{x}_{1},\hat{y}_{1})-\nabla^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{0}(\hat{x}_{0},\hat{y}_{0})\gtrsim q_{0}\mathfrak{d}\cdot\Id
. To do so, by
C.20
, we can replace
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
with
\mathfrak{G}
, provided that
{\bf K}
is chosen large enough. Recall that going from
(\hat{x}_{0},\hat{y}_{0})
to
(\hat{x}_{1},\hat{y}_{1})
,
\Gamma
increases by
\asymp q_{0}\mathfrak{d}
while
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
decreases by
\mathfrak{c}_{0}q_{0}\mathfrak{d}
.
It then suffices to show that

(C.73)

\displaystyle\partial_{\Gamma}\nabla^{2}\mathfrak{G}(\tilde{x}(\Gamma,\gamma^{\prime\prime}),\tilde{y}(\Gamma,\gamma^{\prime\prime}))-\mathfrak{c}_{0}\partial_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\nabla^{2}\mathfrak{G}(\tilde{x}(\Gamma,\gamma^{\prime\prime}),\tilde{y}(\Gamma,\gamma^{\prime\prime}))\gtrsim\Id,

where
(\tilde{x}(\Gamma,\gamma^{\prime\prime}),\tilde{y}(\Gamma,\gamma^{\prime\prime}))
is chosen so that
(\gamma^{\prime\prime},\Gamma)\mapsto\nabla\mathfrak{G}(\tilde{x}(\Gamma,\gamma^{\prime\prime}),\tilde{y}(\Gamma,\gamma^{\prime\prime}))
is constant.
Write
\tilde{x}=\tilde{x}(\Gamma,\gamma^{\prime\prime}),\tilde{y}=\tilde{y}(\Gamma,\gamma^{\prime\prime})
for lightness and let
\eta=2(\gamma^{\prime 2}+1),\psi=\mathfrak{d}/(3\pi\gamma^{\prime}(1-\gamma^{\prime}))
. Note that
\eta,\psi
are positive and bounded away from zero and infinity, since
\gamma^{\prime}/\mathfrak{d}\asymp 1
.
From the explicit formula for
\mathfrak{G}
, it is immediate to see that

(C.74)

\displaystyle\tilde{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa_{1}}{\gamma^{\prime\prime}},\quad\kappa_{1}:=-\frac{\eta\partial_{\hat{x}}\mathfrak{G}}{2\partial_{\hat{y}}\mathfrak{G}}

(C.75)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\eta\tilde{y}-\gamma^{\prime\prime}\tilde{x}^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa_{2}}{\sqrt{\Gamma}},\quad\kappa_{2}:=\frac{2\partial_{\hat{y}}\mathfrak{G}}{3\psi\eta}>0.

Since
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}
are independent of
\Gamma,\gamma^{\prime\prime}
(because
(\gamma^{\prime\prime},\Gamma)\mapsto\nabla\mathfrak{G}(\tilde{x}(\Gamma,\gamma^{\prime\prime}),\tilde{y}(\Gamma,\gamma^{\prime\prime}))
is constant), it follows that

(C.76)

\partial_{\Gamma}\nabla^{2}\mathfrak{G}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})=\psi\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{3\kappa_{1}^{2}}{\kappa_{2}}&-\frac{3\eta\kappa_{1}}{2\kappa_{2}}\\
-\frac{3\eta\kappa_{1}}{2\kappa_{2}}&\frac{3\eta^{2}}{4\kappa_{2}}\end{array}\right),\quad\partial_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\nabla^{2}\mathfrak{G}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})=\psi\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-3\kappa_{2}&0\\
0&0\end{array}\right).

The former has eigenvalues
0
and
3\psi(\eta+4\kappa_{1}^{2})/(4\kappa_{2})>0

and
\partial_{\Gamma}\nabla^{2}\mathfrak{G}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})-\mathfrak{c}_{0}\partial_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\nabla^{2}\mathfrak{G}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})
has determinant
9\eta^{2}\psi^{2}\mathfrak{c}_{0}/4>0
. Therefore, (
C.73
) follows since we have taken

\mathfrak{c}_{0}>0
.

Case II:
\mathfrak{d}_{z}\leq(1/{\bf K})\wedge({\bf K}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q_{0}})
.
We let as usual
\mathfrak{q}=e^{q}
and
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(q,a,b,c)
and
\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}(q,a,b,c)
denote the second and third derivative of the arctic boundary at the tangency point
p_{q;a,b,c}^{SW}
(given by (
C.82
)). Using
B.6
to express
\gamma_{x}
as a function of
(x,q,a,b,c)
, then computing its second and third derivatives at
p^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
and finally expanding in
q
and in
a-1,b-1
(with
a+b+c
fixed to
3
), one gets

(C.77)

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime\prime}(q,1+\bar{a},1+\bar{b},{1-(\bar{a}+\bar{b})})=\frac{4}{3}-2\bar{a}+O(\bar{a}^{2}+\bar{b}^{2}+q^{2}),

(C.78)

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}(q,1+\bar{a},1+\bar{b},{1-(\bar{a}+\bar{b})})=6\Big(-\frac{4}{9}+\frac{8}{9}(q+2\bar{a}+\bar{b})\Big)+O(\bar{a}^{2}+\bar{b}^{2}+q^{2}).

In particular, the map
(a,b)\mapsto(\gamma^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime})
(with
c
fixed to be
3-a-b
) is a local bijection with uniformly bounded Jacobian. Given
q>0
, we fix
a_{q},b_{q},c_{q}=3-a_{q}-b_{q}
so that

(C.79)

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime\prime}(q,1,1,1)-\gamma^{\prime\prime}(0,a_{q},b_{q},c_{q})=0,\quad\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}(q,1,1,1)-\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}(0,a_{q},b_{q},c_{q})=rq+O(q^{2})

where
{r\in\mathbb{R}}
will be chosen later. It is quickly verified from (
C.77
)-(
C.78
) that

(C.80)

\displaystyle a_{q}-1=O(q^{2}),\quad b_{q}-1=q\Big(1-\frac{3}{16}r\Big)+O(q^{2}).

Remark C.22
.

Note that
\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}
changes by order
q
, but
\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}
appears in the expressions of
C.15
always multiplied by
\mathfrak{d}
, so the effect on
D^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
will be of order
\mathfrak{d}q
.

Recall the definition (
C.43
) of
g
.
We have

(C.81)

\displaystyle g(q,1+\bar{a},1+\bar{b},1-(\bar{a}+\bar{b}))=2+\frac{1}{3}(q-7\bar{a}+4\bar{b})+O(\bar{a}^{2}+\bar{b}^{2}+q^{2})

To see this, just express
A^{\prime},B^{\prime},D^{\prime}
through the rational function
f_{0}
of (
B.9
), note that

(C.82)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}^{x_{q;a,b,c}^{SW}}=\frac{(\mathfrak{q}^{a}-1)(\mathfrak{q}^{-b}-1)}{\mathfrak{q}^{-b-c}-1}+1,

which follows directly from
B.7
, and expand the resulting explicit function in
q,a-1,b-1
.

One deduces from (
C.81
) and (
C.77
)-(
C.78
) that

(C.83)

\displaystyle g(q,1,1,1)=2+\frac{q}{3}+O(q^{2}),\quad g(0,a_{q},b_{q},c_{q})=2+q\Big(\frac{4}{3}-\frac{r}{4}\Big)+O(q^{2}).

We define
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
as in
C.18
and we call it
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{0}
if
(q,a,b,c)=(q_{0},1,1,1)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
if
(q,a,b,c)=(0,a_{q},b_{q},c_{q})
with
a_{q},b_{q},c_{q}
chosen as in (
C.79
) (with
q
there equal to
q_{0}
here). Let again
\mathfrak{d}:=\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}
and
call
\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{0}=\gamma^{\prime\prime}(q,1,1,1),\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{1}:=\gamma^{\prime\prime}(0,a_{q},b_{q},c_{q})
(and similarly for
\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0},\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}
) the values of
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
(resp.
\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}
) appearing in
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{0},\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
.
Recall that our goal is to prove (
3.26
) for a suitable
choice of
z_{1}
. Reasoning similarly as in Case I, this is
equivalent to proving (
3.26
) with

\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{q},\widecheck{\mathcal{H}}_{a,b,c}
there replaced by

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{0},\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
here.

Remark C.23
.

Since we want to bound the difference
D^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{0}-D^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
rather than the two Hessians individually, we can and will replace the expression for
\nabla\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
in
C.18
by the expression
\nabla\breve{\mathcal{H}}
below, where we neglect both the terms of order
\mathfrak{d}^{2}
(since in the difference (
3.26
) they would give a contribution of order
\mathfrak{d}^{2}q_{0}\leq(1/{\bf K})\mathfrak{d}q_{0}
and we are assuming that
{\bf K}
is large), as well as the terms of order
\mathfrak{d}
, except those that depend linearly in
q_{0}
(where the
q
-dependence can be hidden in
\gamma^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime},g
because of (
C.79
) and (
C.83
)):

(C.84)

\displaystyle\breve{f}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\mathrm{i}\sqrt{D_{1}}-D_{2}

(C.85)

\displaystyle\partial_{\hat{x}}\breve{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=-\frac{1}{\pi}\sqrt{D_{1}},\qquad\partial_{\hat{y}}\breve{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\frac{1}{\pi}\arctan\left(\frac{\sqrt{D_{1}}}{D_{2}}\right)

(C.86)

\displaystyle D_{1}=(\gamma^{\prime\prime}+\mathfrak{d}\hat{x}(\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}-q\gamma^{\prime\prime}))\Big(2\hat{y}-\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}^{2}-\displaystyle\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}}{3}\mathfrak{d}\hat{x}^{3}\Big),

(C.87)

\displaystyle D_{2}=\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}-\mathfrak{d}\breve{g}\,\Big(\hat{y}-\gamma^{\prime\prime}\displaystyle\frac{\hat{x}^{2}}{2}\Big)+\mathfrak{d}\hat{x}^{2}\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}}{2}.

We then call
\breve{\mathcal{H}}_{i},i=0,1
the expression obtained replacing
(\gamma^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime})
with
\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{i},\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i}
,
q
with either
q_{0}
(if
i=0
) or with
0
(if
i=1
) and

\breve{g}
with
\breve{g}_{i}
, where (compare with (
C.83
))

(C.88)

\displaystyle\breve{g}_{0}=2+\frac{q}{3},\quad\breve{g}_{1}=2+q\Big(\frac{4}{3}-\frac{r}{4}\Big).

Note that the derivatives in (
C.85
) are not quite consistent (they do not verify
\partial_{\hat{x}}(\partial_{\hat{y}}\breve{\mathcal{H}})=\partial_{\hat{y}}(\partial_{\hat{x}}\breve{\mathcal{H}})
). This is due to neglecting the error of
{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{d}\hat{f})
in (
C.62
). Despite that, we will formally replace the derivative of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
by (
C.85
) and its second derivatives by (
C.89
)-(
C.91
) below, since (as explained at the beginning of this remark) the error involved is negligible, for the purposes of the present proof.

Note that
D_{1}
is positive and bounded away from zero; see
C.16
.
The map
(\hat{x},\hat{y})\mapsto\nabla\breve{\mathcal{H}}
is a local bijection; this can be seen by computing its Jacobian matrix (that is, the Hessian of
\breve{\mathcal{H}}
) and checking that it is not degenerate. In fact,
the matrix elements of the Hessian of
\breve{\mathcal{H}}
are

(C.89)

\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{\hat{x}}\breve{\mathcal{H}}=-\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{D_{1}}}\partial_{\hat{x}}D_{1}

(C.90)

\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{\hat{x}\hat{y}}\breve{\mathcal{H}}=-\frac{1}{\pi\sqrt{D_{1}}}(\gamma^{\prime\prime}+\mathfrak{d}\hat{x}(\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}-q\gamma^{\prime\prime}))

(C.91)

\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{\hat{y}}\breve{\mathcal{H}}=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{D_{2}(\gamma^{\prime\prime}+\mathfrak{d}\hat{x}(\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}-q\gamma^{\prime\prime}))+D_{1}\mathfrak{d}\breve{g}}{(D_{1}+D_{2}^{2})\sqrt{D_{1}}}.

With manipulations using

\displaystyle 2\hat{y}-\gamma^{\prime\prime}{\hat{x}^{2}}=\frac{D_{1}}{\gamma^{\prime\prime}+\mathfrak{d}\hat{x}(\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}-q\gamma^{\prime\prime})}+\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}}{3}\mathfrak{d}\hat{x}^{3},\qquad\hat{x}\gamma^{\prime\prime}=D_{2}+\mathfrak{d}\breve{g}\frac{D_{1}}{2\gamma^{\prime\prime}}-\mathfrak{d}\hat{x}^{2}\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}}{2}+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{2}),

we can write

(C.92)

\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{\hat{x}}\breve{\mathcal{H}}=\frac{1}{\pi\sqrt{D_{1}}}\left\{\frac{\mathfrak{d}}{2\gamma^{\prime\prime}}[(\breve{g}+q)\gamma^{\prime\prime}-\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}]D_{1}+D_{2}[\gamma^{\prime\prime}+\mathfrak{d}\hat{x}(\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}-q\gamma^{\prime\prime})]\right\}+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{2}).

At zeroth order in
\mathfrak{d},q
, the Hessian of
\breve{\mathcal{H}}
is then (using
D_{1}+D_{2}^{2}=2\hat{y}\gamma^{\prime\prime}+O(\mathfrak{d})
)

(C.93)

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}{\pi\sqrt{D_{1}}}\begin{pmatrix}\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}&-1\\
-1&\frac{\hat{x}}{2\hat{y}}\end{pmatrix}

which is non-singular (recall from
C.16
that
D_{1}
, and therefore also
\hat{y}
, is bounded away from zero).

Since
(\hat{x},\hat{y})\mapsto\nabla\breve{\mathcal{H}}
is a local bijection, we fix
(\hat{x}_{1}(q_{0}),\hat{y}_{1}(q_{0}))
in such a way that
\nabla\breve{\mathcal{H}}_{1}(\hat{x}_{1}(q_{0}),\hat{y}_{1}(q_{0}))=\nabla\breve{\mathcal{H}}_{0}(\hat{x},\hat{y})
. In particular,
D_{1},D_{2}
are constant with respect to
q_{0}
.

Note that

(C.94)

\displaystyle|\hat{x}_{1}(q_{0})-\hat{x}|+|\hat{y}_{1}(q_{0})-\hat{y}|=O(\mathfrak{d}q_{0}).

This follows noting that between
\breve{\mathcal{H}}_{0}
and
\breve{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
,
\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}
changes by
O(q)
, and
\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}
is multiplied by
\mathfrak{d}
in the expression (
C.84
) for
D_{1},D_{2}
. It remains to check that

(C.95)

\displaystyle D^{2}\breve{\mathcal{H}}_{1}-D^{2}\breve{\mathcal{H}}_{0}\gtrsim\mathfrak{d}q_{0}\Id.

From (
C.92
) and (
C.94
) we deduce that

(C.96)

\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{\hat{x}}\breve{\mathcal{H}}_{1}-\partial^{2}_{\hat{x}}\breve{\mathcal{H}}_{0}=\frac{q_{0}\mathfrak{d}}{\pi\sqrt{D_{1}}}\left\{D_{2}^{2}+r\Big[D_{1}(4-\gamma^{\prime\prime})/(8\gamma^{\prime\prime})-D_{2}\hat{x}\Big]\right\}+O(\mathfrak{d}^{2}q_{0}).

Similarly, from (
C.90
), (
C.91
) we have

(C.97)

\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{\hat{x}\hat{y}}\breve{\mathcal{H}}_{1}-\partial^{2}_{\hat{x}\hat{y}}\breve{\mathcal{H}}_{0}=\frac{q_{0}\mathfrak{d}}{\pi\sqrt{D_{1}}}\left[-D_{2}+r\hat{x}\right]+O(\mathfrak{d}^{2}q_{0})

(C.98)

\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{\hat{y}}\breve{\mathcal{H}}_{1}-\partial^{2}_{\hat{y}}\breve{\mathcal{H}}_{0}=\frac{q_{0}\mathfrak{d}}{\pi\sqrt{D_{1}}}\left[1-\frac{r}{2\hat{y}\gamma^{\prime\prime}}(\frac{D_{1}}{4}+D_{2}\hat{x})\right]+O(\mathfrak{d}^{2}q_{0}).

We conclude that

(C.99)

\displaystyle D^{2}\breve{\mathcal{H}}_{1}-D^{2}\breve{\mathcal{H}}_{0}=\frac{q_{0}\mathfrak{d}}{\pi\sqrt{D_{1}}}M+O(\mathfrak{d}^{2}q_{0}),

(C.100)

\displaystyle M=\begin{pmatrix}D_{2}^{2}+r\Big[{D_{1}}\frac{4-\gamma^{\prime\prime}}{8\gamma^{\prime\prime}}-D_{2}\hat{x}\Big]&-D_{2}+r\hat{x}\\
-D_{2}+r\hat{x}&1-\frac{r}{2\hat{y}\gamma^{\prime\prime}}(\frac{D_{1}}{4}+D_{2}\hat{x})\end{pmatrix}.

Using
\gamma^{\prime\prime}=4/3+O(q_{0})
,

D_{1}=(4/3)(2\hat{y}-(4/3)\hat{x}^{2})+O(q_{0}+\mathfrak{d})
and
D_{2}=(4/3)\hat{x}+O(q_{0}+\mathfrak{d})
we get that

(C.101)

\displaystyle\det(M)=\frac{r}{3\hat{y}}\,(2\hat{y}-\frac{4}{3}\hat{x}^{2})(\hat{y}+\frac{4}{3}\hat{x}^{2})+O(r(r+q_{0}+\mathfrak{d})).

Recall from Remark
C.16
that under our assumptions,
(2\hat{y}-\frac{4}{3}\hat{x}^{2})
and
\hat{y}
are bounded away from zero and infinity. Then, we deduce from (
C.99
) and (
C.101
) (together with the positivity of the trace of
M
for
r
small) that, provided that
r
is positive and small enough, the inequality (
C.95
) holds.

∎

C.2.
Comparison between
\mathcal{H}_{q}
at different
q

In this section we show
3.19
and
3.21
.

Lemma C.24
.

Fix real numbers
q,q^{\prime},\omega,\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
with
q^{\prime}\leq q
. If
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)-\omega
for all
z\in\mathfrak{L}_{q}
with
\dist(z,\mathfrak{A}_{q})=\kappa
, then
\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)\leq\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)-\omega
for all
z\in\mathfrak{L}_{q}
with
\dist(z,\mathfrak{A}_{q})\geq\kappa
.

Proof.

The limit shape is monotone with respect to
q
(by
3.2
) and with respect to boundary conditions (this follows from monotonicity of the solution of the elliptic partial differential equation (
B.6
) with respect to the boundary data). The lemma then follows applying these monotonicities in the domain
\Lambda=\{z\in\mathfrak{L}_{q}:\dist(z,\mathfrak{A}_{q})\geq\kappa\}
.
∎

Fix
y\in[0,49/100]
(recall (
C.1
)), and let
\mathfrak{r}_{q}(y):=\max\{x\in\mathbb{R}:(x,y)\in\mathfrak{A}_{q}\}
.

Lemma C.25
.

There exists a constant
C>1
such that
C^{-1}(q-q^{\prime})\leq\mathfrak{r}_{q}(y)-\mathfrak{r}_{q^{\prime}}(y)\leq C(q-q^{\prime})
, for any
q,q^{\prime}\in[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]
with
q\geq q^{\prime}
, if
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small.

Proof.

We begin by setting some notation. Set
\mathfrak{q}=e^{q}
and
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}=e^{q^{\prime}}
; also denote
x=\mathfrak{r}_{q}(y)
and
x^{\prime}=\mathfrak{r}_{q^{\prime}}(y)
. As in the
(a,b,c)=(1,1,1)
case of (
B.8
), set
(A,B,C)=(-[-1]_{\mathfrak{q}},[1]_{\mathfrak{q}},[2]_{\mathfrak{q}}-[1]_{\mathfrak{q}})
and
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime},C^{\prime})=(-[-1]_{\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}},[1]_{\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}},[2]_{\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}}-[1]_{\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}})
. Also following (
B.8
), let
X=X_{q}(x)
,
X^{\prime}=X_{q^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})
,
Y=Y_{q}(y)
, and
Y^{\prime}=Y_{q^{\prime}}(y)
; further denote
\Delta=q-q^{\prime}
.

Now, observe that if
y=0
then, by
B.7
and the fact that
|q|\leq\varepsilon
,
x=1/2+O(\varepsilon)
and
x^{\prime}=1/2+O(\varepsilon)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle x-x^{\prime}+\displaystyle\frac{3\Delta}{8}+O(\Delta^{2}+\varepsilon\Delta)=X-X^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{AB}{B+C}-\displaystyle\frac{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}{B^{\prime}+C^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}-1}{1-\mathfrak{q}^{-2}}-\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}-1}{1-\mathfrak{q}^{\prime-2}}=\displaystyle\frac{3\Delta}{4}+O(\varepsilon\Delta).

where the first statement follows from Taylor expansion and (
B.8
) (with the fact that
x,x^{\prime}=1/2+O(\varepsilon)
); the second follows from
B.7
; the third follows from (
B.8
); and the fourth follows from the fact that
|q|,|q^{\prime}|=O(\varepsilon)
. Therefore,
x-x^{\prime}=3\Delta/8+O(\varepsilon\Delta)
, which for sufficiently small
\varepsilon
yields the lemma when
y=0
.

Next observe from the explicit form (
B.12
) of the arctic boundary that it is smooth in the parameter
q
. Therefore,
|x-x^{\prime}|\lesssim\Delta
, so it suffices to show that
x-x^{\prime}\gtrsim\Delta
. By this smoothness, we also have that the difference between the slope of the tangent line to
\mathfrak{A}_{q}
at
(x,y)
and the slope of the tangent line to
\mathfrak{A}_{q^{\prime}}
at
(x^{\prime},y)
is
O(\Delta)
. It follows since
x-x^{\prime}\gtrsim\Delta
at
y=0
that
x-x^{\prime}\gtrsim\Delta
for
y\leq C^{-1}
, if
C>1
is sufficiently large. Therefore, we may assume in what follows that
y\gtrsim 1
.

Next, we have from
B.6
that

\displaystyle\big(X(B+C)-Y

\displaystyle(A+C)-AB+(\mathfrak{q}^{-1}-1)ABY\big)^{2}

\displaystyle-4ABY\big(A+C-X+(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-1})(A+C)Y\big)=0,

and

\displaystyle\big(X^{\prime}(B^{\prime}+C^{\prime})-Y^{\prime}

\displaystyle(A^{\prime}+C^{\prime})-A^{\prime}B^{\prime}+(\mathfrak{q^{\prime}}^{-1}-1)A^{\prime}B^{\prime}Y^{\prime}\big)^{2}

\displaystyle-4A^{\prime}B^{\prime}Y^{\prime}\big(A^{\prime}+C^{\prime}-X^{\prime}+(1-\mathfrak{q^{\prime}}^{-1})(A^{\prime}+C^{\prime})Y^{\prime}\big)=0.

Since
(a,b,c)=(1,1,1)
and
q\in[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]
, by (
B.8
) we have

(C.102)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &X=X^{\prime}+x-x^{\prime}+\displaystyle\frac{\Delta}{2}\cdot x(x+1)+O(\Delta^{2});\qquad Y=Y^{\prime}+\displaystyle\frac{\Delta}{2}\cdot y(y+1)+O(\Delta^{2});\\
&\qquad\quad A=A^{\prime}+\Delta+O(\varepsilon\Delta);\qquad B=B^{\prime}=1;\qquad C=C^{\prime}-\Delta+O(\varepsilon\Delta).\end{aligned}

Set
\breve{X}=X^{\prime}+\Delta x(x+1)/2
. It suffices to show that
-\xi(\breve{X},Y)\gtrsim\Delta
, as
-\xi(\breve{X},Y)=\xi(X,Y)-\xi(\breve{X},Y)\lesssim X-\breve{X}=x-x^{\prime}+O(\varepsilon\Delta)
, where the first statement holds by (
B.12
), the second is quickly verified using the fact that
Y\gtrsim 1
(as this implies that
(x,y)
is bounded away from a tangency location of
\mathfrak{A}_{q}
), and the third holds by (
C.102
) with the definition of
\breve{X}
. To see this, observe using (
C.102
) that

(C.103)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} -\xi(\breve{X},Y)&=\big(X^{\prime}(B^{\prime}+C^{\prime})-Y^{\prime}(A^{\prime}+C^{\prime})-A^{\prime}B^{\prime}+(\mathfrak{q}^{-1}-1)A^{\prime}B^{\prime}Y^{\prime}\big)^{2}\\
&\qquad-4A^{\prime}B^{\prime}Y^{\prime}\big(A^{\prime}+C^{\prime}-X^{\prime}+(1-\mathfrak{q})(A^{\prime}+C^{\prime})Y^{\prime}\big)\\
&-\big(\breve{X}(B+C)-Y(A+C)-AB+(\mathfrak{q}^{-1}-1)ABY\big)^{2}\\
&\qquad+4ABY\big(A+C-\breve{X}+(1-\mathfrak{q})(A+C)Y\big)\\
&=\Delta(2X^{\prime 2}Y^{\prime}+2X^{\prime}Y^{\prime 2}-4X^{\prime 3}-4Y^{\prime 3}+2X^{\prime 2}+2Y^{\prime 2}+4X^{\prime}+4Y^{\prime}-2)+O(\varepsilon\Delta).\end{aligned}

Again since
\mathfrak{A}_{q}
is smooth in
q
, the distance between
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
to a corresponding point
(\mathfrak{r}_{0}(Y^{\prime}),Y^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{A}
(that is, at
q=0
) is
O(|q|)=O(\varepsilon)
. Since (by (
B.12
)) we have
\mathfrak{A}=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:4x^{2}-4xy+4y^{2}-4x-4y+1=0\}
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}_{0}(Y^{\prime})=\displaystyle\frac{Y^{\prime}+1+(6Y^{\prime}-3Y^{\prime 2})^{1/2}}{2}.

Inserting this, with the bound
X^{\prime}=\mathfrak{r}_{0}(Y^{\prime})+O(\varepsilon)
, into (
C.103
) yields

\displaystyle-\xi(\breve{X},Y)=\displaystyle\frac{\Delta}{2}\cdot(1-2Y^{\prime})(3-2Y^{\prime})(6Y^{\prime}-3Y^{\prime 2})^{1/2}+O(\varepsilon\Delta)\gtrsim\Delta,

where the last inequality follows from the fact that
1/2-Y^{\prime}\gtrsim 1
(as
1/2-y\gtrsim 1
, since
\ell_{0}(x,y)
is the south edge of
\mathfrak{X}
). As mentioned above, this yields the lemma.
∎

Proof of
3.19
.

This follows from
C.25
, since
x=\mathfrak{r}_{q}(y)
and
x^{\prime}=\mathfrak{r}_{q^{\prime}}(y)
.
∎

Lemma C.26
.

There exists
C>1
such that the following holds whenever
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small. For any
q,q^{\prime}\in[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]
, we have for any
e\in[0,\mathfrak{r}_{q}(y)]
that

\displaystyle|\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{r}_{q}(y)-e,y)-\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{r}_{q^{\prime}}(y)-e,y)|\leq C|q-q^{\prime}|y^{1/4}e^{1/2}.

Proof.

Denote
x_{1}=\mathfrak{r}_{q}(y)
and
x=x_{1}-e
, and let
z=(x,y)
; observe that
(\mathfrak{d}_{z},\mathfrak{e}_{z;q})=(y^{1/2},e)
. Also set
X=-[-x]_{q}
and
Y=-[-y]_{q}
. Further let
\xi_{q}
and
\xi_{q^{\prime}}
denote the function defined by (
B.13
), with the parameter equal to
q
and
q^{\prime}
, respectively (including in the definitions of
(A,B,C)
through
(a,b,c)
).

First observe that

(C.104)

\displaystyle\zeta(X,Y)\geq\displaystyle\frac{1}{2},

since

\displaystyle\zeta(X,Y)=2(A+C-X+(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-1})(A+C)Y)\geq 2(2-x)+\mathcal{O}(q)\geq\displaystyle\frac{1}{2},

for
q
sufficiently small. Further observe that

\displaystyle\Imaginary f(z)\asymp(y^{1/2}e)^{1/2};\qquad\Real f(z)\lesssim(y^{-1/2}e)^{1/2}.

Indeed, if
\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}\leq\omega
for some sufficiently small constant
\omega>0
, then this follows (using the fact that
e\lesssim y^{1/2}
) from either
C.13
or
C.15
, according to whether
\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}\leq(1/{\bf K})y^{1/2}=(1/{\bf K})\mathfrak{d}_{z}
or not, with
\bf K
a large constant. Otherwise, it follows from (
B.16
). Together with (
B.9
), this implies that
\Imaginary v\asymp(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}
, which by (
C.104
) and (
B.14
) yields

(C.105)

\displaystyle\xi(X,Y)\asymp-y^{1/2}e;\qquad\eta(X,Y)\lesssim(y^{-1/2}e)^{1/2}.

Further observe that

(C.106)

\displaystyle|\partial_{q}\xi(X,Y)|+|\partial_{q}\eta(X,Y)|+|\partial_{q}\zeta(X,Y)|\lesssim 1,

by (
B.8
) and (
B.13
), with the fact that
\partial_{q}[R]\lesssim 1
for
R\lesssim 1
.

By (
C.105
), (
C.104
), (
C.106
), and the second statement in (
B.5
), it suffices to show that
|\xi_{q}(X,Y)^{1/2}-\xi_{q^{\prime}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})^{1/2}|\lesssim|q-q^{\prime}|y^{1/4}e^{1/2}
. Therefore, we must show that

(C.107)

\displaystyle|\partial_{q}(\xi_{q}(X,Y)^{1/2})|\lesssim y^{1/4}e^{1/2}.

To that end, we may assume that
e=\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}
(corresponding to
z
is closer in the
\ell_{z}
direction to the right side of
\mathfrak{A}_{q}
than to its left side), as the proof in the alternative case is entirely analogous. Since (
C.105
) implies that
-\xi_{q}(X,Y)\asymp y^{1/2}e
, to verify (
C.107
), we must show

(C.108)

\displaystyle|\partial_{q}\xi_{q}(X,Y)|\lesssim y^{1/2}e.

Recalling that
x_{1}=\mathfrak{r}_{q}(y)
, set
X_{1}=-[-x_{1}]_{q}
. Then
\xi_{q}(X_{1},Y)=0
by (
B.12
), so

\displaystyle\xi_{q}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\xi_{q}(X,Y)-\xi_{q}(X_{1},Y)

\displaystyle=(B+C)(X-X_{1})\big((B+C)(X+X_{1})-2Y(A+C)-2AB+2(1-\mathfrak{q}^{-1})ABY\big)

\displaystyle\qquad+4ABY(X-X_{1}).

Using the fact that for any uniformly bounded
r,r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
we have
|[r]-[r^{\prime}]|\lesssim|r-r^{\prime}|
and
|\partial_{q}[r]-\partial_{q}[r^{\prime}]|\lesssim|r-r^{\prime}|
, with the facts that
|x-x_{1}|=e
, it follows that

(C.109)

\displaystyle|\partial_{q}\xi_{q}(X,Y)|\lesssim ey+e\cdot|(B+C)(X+X_{1})-2AB|.

Denoting
X_{SW}=-[-x^{SW}_{q}]_{q}
, we also have that

\displaystyle|(B+C)(X+X_{1})-2AB|\lesssim|X+X_{1}-2X_{SW}|\lesssim|x-x_{q}^{SW}|+|x_{1}-x^{SW}_{q}|\lesssim y^{1/2},

where the first statement follows from
B.7
, the second from the fact that
|[r]-[r^{\prime}]|\lesssim|r-r^{\prime}|
for any uniformly bounded
r,r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
; and the third from the uniform convexity of
\mathfrak{A}_{q}
from
3.10
. Inserting this into (
C.109
) yields (
C.108
) and thus the lemma.
∎

Proof of
3.21
.

Since
\mathfrak{A}_{q}
varies smoothly in
q
, we have
|\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}-\mathfrak{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}|\lesssim q-q^{\prime}
. If either
z\notin\mathfrak{L}_{q}
or
z\notin\mathfrak{L}_{q^{\prime}}
, we have that
\max\{\mathfrak{e}_{z;q},\mathfrak{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}\}\leq q-q^{\prime}
, and so the result then follows from the third part of
3.18
(if
z=(x,y)
with
\ell_{z}
being the south edge of
\mathfrak{X}
and
x\geq x_{q}^{SW}
; the other cases are entirely analogous by symmetry). So, we may assume in what follows that
z\in\mathfrak{L}_{q}\cap\mathfrak{L}_{q^{\prime}}
.

Then, again since
|\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}-\mathfrak{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}|\lesssim q-q^{\prime}
, it suffices to show that

(C.110)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)-\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)\asymp(q-q^{\prime})\mathfrak{d}_{z}^{1/2}\cdot\max\{\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}^{1/2},\mathfrak{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}^{1/2}\}.

Let us first reduce to the case when
\dist(z,\mathfrak{A}_{q})\leq C^{-1}
for a sufficiently large constant
C>1
. Indeed, if (
C.110
) holds under this situation, then we have
\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)-\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)\gtrsim q-q^{\prime}
for
z\in\mathfrak{X}
satisfying
\dist(z,\mathfrak{A}_{q})=C^{-1}
; hence,
C.24
yields
\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)-\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)\gtrsim q-q^{\prime}
for all
z\in\mathfrak{X}
satisfying
\dist(z,\mathfrak{A}_{q})\geq C^{-1}
, which shows the lower bound in (
C.110
). To show the upper bound, observe from the content following (
B.16
) that
v(z)
is uniformly smooth in
q
for
z\in\mathfrak{X}
satisfying
\dist(z,\mathfrak{A}_{q})\geq C^{-1}
. We also have by (
B.16
) that
\Imaginary v(z)\gtrsim 1
there, which implies by (
B.5
) that
\nabla\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)
is uniformly smooth in
q
for
z\in\mathfrak{X}
satisfying
\dist(z,\mathfrak{A}_{q})\geq C^{-1}
. Together with the fact that
|\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)-\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)|\lesssim q-q^{\prime}
for
z\in\mathfrak{X}
satisfying
\dist(z,\mathfrak{A}_{q})\geq C^{-1}
, this implies that
|\mathcal{H}_{q}(z)-\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)|\lesssim q-q^{\prime}
for all
z\in\mathfrak{X}
satisfying
\dist(z,\mathfrak{A}_{q})\geq C^{-1}
; this confirms the upper bound in (
C.110
).

Thus, it suffices to show (
C.110
) when
\dist(z,\mathfrak{A}_{q})\leq C^{-1}
for sufficiently large
C>1
. Denote
z=(x,y)
. Recall from
C.1
that we are assuming by symmetry that
\ell_{z}
is the south edge of
\mathfrak{X}
, that
y\leq 49/100
and
x>x^{SW}_{q}
. Therefore,
\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}=\mathfrak{r}_{q}(y)-x
; this in particular implies that
\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}\geq\mathfrak{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}
. We then have

(C.111)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{H}_{q}(z)-\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(z)&=\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}}\big(\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{r}_{q}(y)-e,y)-\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{r}_{q^{\prime}}(y)-e,y)\big)de\\
&\qquad-\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}}^{\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}}\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{r}_{q^{\prime}}(y)-e,y)de\\
&=\displaystyle-\int_{\mathfrak{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}}^{\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}}\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{r}_{q^{\prime}}(y)-e,y)dy+O((q-q^{\prime})\mathfrak{d}_{z}^{1/2}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}^{3/2}),\end{aligned}

where the first statement holds since
\mathcal{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{r}_{q}(y),y)=0=\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{r}_{q^{\prime}}(y),y)
, and the second holds by
C.26
. Next, we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\int_{\mathfrak{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}}^{\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}}\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}_{q^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{r}_{q^{\prime}}-e,y)de\asymp\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{e}_{z;q^{\prime}}}^{\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}}e^{1/2}\mathfrak{d}_{z}^{1/2}de\asymp(q-q^{\prime})\mathfrak{d}_{z}^{1/2}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}^{1/2},

where in the first statement we used the first statement in
3.18
and in the second we performed the integration. Inserting this into (
C.111
) and taking
\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}
sufficiently small (by taking
\dist(z,\mathfrak{A}_{q})
sufficiently small) yields (
C.110
) and thus the lemma.
∎

C.3.
Level lines close to the arctic boundary

In this section, we prove
3.26
.
We let

(C.112)

\displaystyle z_{s}:=(x_{s},y_{s})=(s+\mathfrak{l}_{q;a,b,c}(h_{0}),\mathcal{U}^{h_{0}}_{q;a,b,c}(s+\mathfrak{l}_{q;a,b,c}(h_{0}))).

Note that the condition on

s_{-},s_{+},s_{0}
in the statement of the proposition ensures that
if
s\in[s_{-},s_{+}]
, then
z_{s}

(with
a=b=c=1
)
is inside the liquid region
\mathfrak{L}_{q}
and if
s=s_{0}
, then
z_{s_{0}}
is to the right of
p^{SW}_{q}
and bounded away from
p^{SE}_{q}
.
Note also that, since
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(z_{s})=h_{0}
(for any
a,b,c
),

(C.113)

\displaystyle\partial_{s}\mathcal{U}^{h_{0}}_{q;a,b,c}(s+\mathfrak{l}_{q;a,b,c}(h_{0}))=-\frac{\partial_{x}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(z_{s})}{\partial_{y}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(z_{s})}.

Since
h_{0}
is smaller than
\varepsilon
, if
\varepsilon

is small
z_{s}
is close to the arctic boundary for every

s\in[s_{-},s_{+}]
and we can assume that either

\mathfrak{e}_{z_{s};q}\leq(1/{\bf K})\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}}
with
{\bf K}
a large constant,
or
\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}}\leq(1/{\bf K})\wedge({\bf K}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{s};q})
. We will
actually distinguish three situations:

(I)

\mathfrak{e}_{z_{s};q}\leq(1/{\bf K})\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}}
and
z_{s}
is to the right of
p_{q}^{SW}
.

(II)

\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}}\leq(1/{\bf K})\wedge({\bf K}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{s};q})
.

(III)

\mathfrak{e}_{z_{s};q}\leq(1/{\bf K})\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}}
and
z_{s}
is to the left of
p_{q}^{SW}
. Note that as
s\to 0
,
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{s};q}\sim s
while
\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}}
tends to
\sqrt{h_{0}}
.
As in
C.17
, write

\mathcal{H}_{q}(x_{s},y_{s})=y_{s}-\mathfrak{d}_{s}^{1/2}\mathfrak{e}_{s}^{3/2}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x}_{s},\hat{y}_{s})
, with
(\mathfrak{e}_{s},\mathfrak{d}_{s})=(\mathfrak{e}_{z_{s};q},\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}})
. We can
approximate the derivatives of

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x}_{s},\hat{y}_{s})
as in
C.20
.

Note that in cases (I) and (II), we have
\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}}\asymp s
.
Since
s_{0}
satisfies (
3.15
), then
z_{s_{0}}
falls into either case (I) or (II) (with
a=b=c=1
).

We will first prove (in Sections
C.3.1
to
C.3.4
) the statements of
3.26
under the assumption that
s_{-}\geq{\bf K}h_{0}^{1/2}
, with
{\bf K}
a large constant. This means that
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{s};q}\leq(1/{\bf K})\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}}
for all
s\in[s_{-},s_{+}]
, possibly with a different (but still large) constant
{\bf K}
. Note that in this case,
Item

2
of
3.26
is void, since the interval
[s_{-},C^{-1}h_{0}^{1/2}]
is empty for
C>1
. Afterwards, in
Section

C.3.5
we briefly outline the changes needed in the case where
s_{-}\leq{\bf K}h_{0}^{1/2}
.

For the proof of
3.26
, we need to approximate precisely the level lines
\mathcal{U}^{h_{0}}
of the height function.
The following statement will be of use in the case where
z_{s}
falls into case (I) above:

Lemma C.27
.

If
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{s};q}\leq(1/{\bf K})\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}}
and
z_{s}
is to the right of
p_{q}^{SW}
, we have

(C.114)

\displaystyle\partial_{s}\mathcal{U}^{h_{0}}_{q;a,b,c}(s+\mathfrak{l}_{q;a,b,c}(h_{0}))=\gamma^{\prime}_{s}(1+\hat{\mathcal{O}}((\mathfrak{e}_{z_{s};q}/\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}})^{1/2})+\mathcal{O}((\mathfrak{e}_{z_{s};q}\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}})^{1/2}))

and

(C.115)

\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{s}\mathcal{U}^{h_{0}}_{q;a,b,c}(s+\mathfrak{l}_{q;a,b,c}(h_{0}))=\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{s}+\hat{\mathcal{O}}((\mathfrak{e}_{z_{s};q}/\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}})^{1/2})+\mathcal{O}((\mathfrak{e}_{z_{s};q}\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}})^{1/2}),

where
\gamma^{\prime}_{s},\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{s}
are the first and second derivative of the arctic curve computed at the point
\bar{z}_{s}\in\mathfrak{A}_{q;a,b,c}
closest to
z_{s}
.
The error terms are as in
C.20
.

Proof of
C.27
.

Given
h>0
let
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{q;a,b,c}^{h}
denote the
\mathfrak{d}^{-1/2}\mathfrak{e}^{-3/2}h
-th level line of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{q;a,b,c}
adopting the notations of

C.17
where

(C.116)

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}:=\gamma^{\prime}_{s},\gamma^{\prime\prime}:=\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{s},\mathfrak{d}:=\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}},\mathfrak{e}:=\mathfrak{e}_{z_{s};q},

namely,

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{q;a,b,c}(\hat{s},\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{q;a,b,c}^{h}(\hat{s}))=\mathfrak{d}^{-1/2}\mathfrak{e}^{-3/2}h
. Recall that we abbreviate
(q;a,b,c)=q
if
(a,b,c)=(1,1,1)
and
(q;a,b,c)=(a,b,c)
if
q=0
, and that
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{q;a,b,c}^{h}
similarly denotes the
h
-th level line of
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}
. Let us first evaluate
\partial_{s}\mathcal{U},\partial_{s}^{2}\mathcal{U}
in terms of
\partial_{\hat{s}}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}},\partial_{\hat{s}}^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}
.

Recall the change of coordinates in (
C.34
), where we will eventually take
(x,y)
to be
z_{s}
, so that
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=\bar{z}_{s}
. Since the level lines of
\mathcal{H}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
coincide, we find that

(C.117)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(x^{\prime}+(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}\hat{s}-\gamma^{\prime}\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(\hat{s}))=y^{\prime}+\hat{s}\gamma^{\prime}(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}+\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(\hat{s}).

Differentiating w.r.t.
\hat{s}
we get

(C.118)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(x)=\frac{\gamma^{\prime}+\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}(\hat{s})}{1-\gamma^{\prime}\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}(\hat{s})},\quad\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}(x)=\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime\prime}(\hat{s})\frac{1+\gamma^{\prime 2}}{(1-\gamma^{\prime}\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}(\hat{s}))^{3}}

where we have denoted

(C.119)

\displaystyle x=x^{\prime}+(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}\hat{s}-\gamma^{\prime}\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(\hat{s}).

Next, note that

(C.120)

\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}(\hat{s})=-\left.\frac{\partial_{\hat{x}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})}{\partial_{\hat{y}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})}\right|_{\hat{x}=\hat{s},\hat{y}=\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(\hat{s})},\quad\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime\prime}(\hat{s})=-\left.\frac{(A,D^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})A)}{\partial_{\hat{y}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})}\right|_{\hat{x}=\hat{s},\hat{y}=\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(\hat{s})},A=(1,\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}(\hat{s})).

By the first of (
C.120
), together with (
C.69
) and the bounds
\gamma^{\prime}\asymp\mathfrak{d},\mathfrak{e}\lesssim\mathfrak{d}
, we set
x
equal to
s+\mathfrak{l}_{q;a,b,c}(h_{0})=x_{s}
(recall (
C.112
)) and we deduce (
C.114
). To prove (
C.115
), note first of all that

\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}(x)=\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime\prime}(\hat{s})(1+\gamma^{\prime 2})(1+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{3/2}\mathfrak{e}^{1/2}))
.
Then, (
C.115
) follows from (
C.120
) and (
C.69
).
∎

C.3.1.
Proof of

Item

1
of
3.26

Recall that we are assuming that
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{s};q}\leq(1/{\bf K})\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}}
for all
s\in[s_{-},s_{+}]
, in particular for
s=s_{0}
.

We wish to fix
a,b,c=3-a-b
to meet the requirements of
3.26
. We first note that the map
(a,b,x)\mapsto(\gamma^{\prime}_{x},\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{x},\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}_{x})
is a local bijection with bounded Jacobian. Since the arctic boundary is smooth in
\mathfrak{q},a,b,c
, it is enough to prove this for
a=b=\mathfrak{q}=1
. Recall from
Section

C.1.2
that the map
x\mapsto\xi_{x}
is a local bijection. Next, we claim that the map
(a,b,\xi_{x})\mapsto(\gamma^{\prime}_{x},\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{x},\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}_{x})
is a local bijection with bounded Jacobian, at
a=b=\mathfrak{q}=1
. For this, one uses the explicit formulas (
C.17
), (
C.20
) to find that the determinant of the Jacobian matrix is

(C.121)

\displaystyle\frac{((-2+2\xi_{x}-2\xi_{x}^{2})^{6}(-8+16\xi_{x}-24\xi_{x}^{2}+16\xi_{x}^{3}-8\xi_{x}^{4})}{24(1-2\xi_{x})^{9}}.

As discussed just after (
C.67
), in the range of parameters we are interested in,
\xi_{x}
ranges from
\frac{1}{2}(1-\sqrt{3})+O(\varepsilon)
and
O(\varepsilon)
(recall that
q,|a-1|,|b-1|=O(\varepsilon)
), and it is easily checked that in this range the ratio (
C.121
) is bounded away from zero and infinity.

Consequently, we can fix the parameters
x,a,b
(recall that
c
is fixed to
c=3-a-b
) in order to tune the first three derivatives of the arctic boundary
\mathfrak{A}_{a,b,c}
. In particular (using also
C.27
to relate the derivative of the level lines to those of the arctic boundary) we can fix

a-1,b-1=O(q)
and
\mathfrak{r}
(the latter is equivalent to fixing the coordinate
x
) in such a way that (
3.17
) holds, and at the same time (letting
z_{s_{0}}:=(s_{0}+\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0}),\mathcal{U}^{h_{0}}_{q}(s_{0}+\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})))
,
z^{\prime}_{s_{0}}:=(s_{0}+\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+\mathfrak{r},\mathcal{U}^{h_{0}}_{a,b,c}(s_{0}+\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+\mathfrak{r}))
)

(C.122)

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime\prime}(q,1,1,1)-\gamma^{\prime\prime}(0,a,b,c)=3C^{-1}q{s_{0}},\qquad\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}(q,1,1,1)=\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}(a,b,c)

where
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(q,1,1,1),\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}(q,1,1,1)
denote the derivatives of the arctic boundary
\mathfrak{A}_{q}
at the point closest to
z_{s_{0}}
, and
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(0,a,b,c),\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}(0,a,b,c)
denote the derivatives of the arctic boundary
\mathfrak{A}_{a,b,c}
at the point closest to
z^{\prime}_{s_{0}}
. Here,
C
is the positive constant (independent of

{\bf K}
) that appears in the statement of
3.26
.

For later convenience, we make the following observation.

Remark C.28
.

The values
a,b,c
satisfy
(a-1),(b-1),(c-1)=O(q)
. Moreover, as in
C.21
, the rescaled coordinates
(\hat{x}_{0},\hat{y}_{0}),(\hat{x}_{1},\hat{y}_{1})
of the points
z_{s_{0}},z^{\prime}_{s_{0}}
satisfy
|\hat{x}_{0}-\hat{x}_{1}|,|\hat{y}_{0}-\hat{y}_{1}|=O(\mathfrak{d}q)=O(s_{0}q)
. Finally, since
z_{s_{0}},z^{\prime}_{s_{0}}
are on two level lines of the limit shapes, corresponding to different parameters
(q,1,1,1)
and
(0,a,b,c)
, but to the same height value
h_{0}
, from (
C.69
) we have that

(C.123)

\displaystyle\frac{2(\gamma^{\prime 2}+1)\hat{y}_{0}-\gamma^{\prime\prime}(q,1,1,1)\hat{x}^{2}_{0}}{2(\gamma^{\prime 2}+1)\hat{y}_{1}-\gamma^{\prime\prime}(0,a,b,c)\hat{x}^{2}_{1}}=\frac{\Gamma_{1}^{1/3}}{\Gamma_{0}^{1/3}}+\mathcal{O}({\bf K}^{-1/2}s_{0}),

where (cf. (
C.36
))
\Gamma_{0}=\gamma^{\prime\prime}(q,1,1,1)-\gamma^{\prime}(1-\gamma^{\prime})q
,
\Gamma_{1}=\gamma^{\prime\prime}(0,a,b,c)
.

Next, we claim that with this choice of
a,b,c
,

(C.124)

\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{s}\mathcal{U}^{h_{0}}_{q}(s_{0}+\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0}))-\partial^{2}_{s}\mathcal{U}^{h_{0}}_{a,b,c}(s_{0}+\mathfrak{r}+\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0}))\geq 2C^{-1}qs_{0}.

This follows from (
C.122
) and
(
C.115
), provided that
{\bf K}
is large
enough. Indeed, on one
hand, the error term
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}})
in (
C.115
) is smooth with
respect to
a,b,c,q
, so that it contributes order

\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}}O(\max(q,|a-1|,|b-1|,|c-1|)=O(\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}}q)=O(s_{0}q/{\bf K}^{1/2})\leq(10C)^{-1}q{s_{0}}
to the l.h.s. of (
C.124
), provided that
\bf K
is large enough. On the other hand, also the error term

\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\mathfrak{e}/\mathfrak{d}})
contributes order

O(s_{0}q/{\bf K}^{1/2})
. In fact, by
C.20
it is smooth in
a,b,c
(which change by
order
\mathfrak{d}q_{0}\asymp qs_{0}
) and it depends on
q
only through
\Gamma
which (as one sees from its definition
(
C.36
)) changes by order
\gamma^{\prime}q\asymp qs_{0}
when
\log\mathfrak{q}
changes from
0
to
q
.

We have proven (
C.124
) at
s=s_{0}
and now we extend the proof (with
2C^{-1}
replaced by
C^{-1}
) to all
s\in[s_{-},s_{+}]
. Thus,
Item

1
then follows by integration with respect to
s
. Recall that we are working under the assumption
s_{-}\geq{\bf K}h_{0}^{1/2}
, which implies that
z_{s}
falls into case (I) described at the beginning of
Section

C.3
.
Since the arctic curve is smooth (in
q,a,b,c
), it follows from
C.27
, (
C.122
) and (
C.124
) that

(C.125)

\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{s}\mathcal{U}^{h_{0}}_{q}(s+\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0}))-\partial^{2}_{s}\mathcal{U}^{h_{0}}_{a,b,c}(s+\mathfrak{r}+\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0}))\geq 2C^{-1}qs_{0}-O(q(s_{+}-s_{-})^{2})\geq C^{-1}qs_{0}

for all
s\in[s_{-},s_{+}]
, where the last bound comes from the assumption that

s_{+}-s_{-}\leq C^{-1}s_{0}^{1/2}
, taking
C
large enough.

Item

1
of
3.26
is proven, under the assumption that
s_{-}\geq{\bf K}h_{0}^{1/2}
.

C.3.2.
Proof of

Item

3
of
3.26

Recall that we are assuming that
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{s};q}\leq(1/{\bf K})\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}}
for all
s\in[s_{-},s_{+}]
.
Note first of all that

(C.126)

\displaystyle\partial_{h}\mathcal{U}^{h}_{q;a,b,c}(s+\mathfrak{l}_{q;a,b,c}(h))=\Big(\partial_{y}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(\mathfrak{l}_{q;a,b,c}(h)+s,\mathcal{U}^{h}_{q;a,b,c}(\mathfrak{l}_{q;a,b,c}(h)+s))\Big)^{-1},

since
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(\mathfrak{l}_{q;a,b,c}(h)+s,\mathcal{U}^{h}_{q;a,b,c}(\mathfrak{l}_{q;a,b,c}(h)+s))=h
. Therefore, (
3.21
) follows if we prove

(C.127)

\partial_{y}\mathcal{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s_{0},\mathcal{U}^{h}_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s_{0}))\\
\leq\partial_{y}\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s_{0}+\mathfrak{r},\mathcal{U}^{h}_{a,b,c}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s_{0}+\mathfrak{r})).

Observe from (
C.34
) that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{q;a,b,c}\bigg(\displaystyle\frac{\overline{x}+\gamma^{\prime}\overline{y}^{h}}{(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}(\gamma^{\prime 2}+1)},\displaystyle\frac{\overline{y}^{h}-\gamma^{\prime}\overline{x}}{\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}(\gamma^{\prime 2}+1)}\bigg)=\displaystyle\frac{h}{\mathfrak{d}^{1/2}\mathfrak{e}^{3/2}},

where

\displaystyle\overline{x}=s-x^{\prime};\qquad\overline{y}^{h}=\mathcal{U}_{q;a,b,c}^{h}(s)-y^{\prime}.

Differentiating with respect to
h
yields

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{h}\mathcal{U}_{q;a,b,c}^{h}}{\gamma^{\prime 2}+1}\cdot\Bigg(

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{-1/2}\gamma^{\prime}\partial_{\hat{x}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{q;a,b,c}\bigg(\displaystyle\frac{\overline{x}+\gamma^{\prime}\overline{y}^{h}}{(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}(\gamma^{\prime 2}+1)},\displaystyle\frac{\overline{y}^{h}-\gamma^{\prime}\overline{x}}{\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}(\gamma^{\prime 2}+1)}\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad+(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{-1}\partial_{\hat{y}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{q;a,b,c}\bigg(\displaystyle\frac{\overline{x}+\gamma^{\prime}\overline{y}^{h}}{(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}(\gamma^{\prime 2}+1)},\displaystyle\frac{\overline{y}^{h}-\gamma^{\prime}\overline{x}}{\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}(\gamma^{\prime 2}+1)}\bigg)\Bigg)=\mathfrak{d}^{-1/2}\mathfrak{e}^{-3/2}.

Thus,

(C.128)

\displaystyle\partial_{h}\mathcal{U}_{q;a,b,c}^{h}=\mathfrak{d}^{1/2}\mathfrak{e}^{-1/2}(\gamma^{\prime 2}+1)\cdot\big((\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}\gamma^{\prime}\partial_{\hat{x}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{q;a,b,c}(\hat{x},\hat{y})+\partial_{\hat{y}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{q;a,b,c}(\hat{x},\hat{y})\big)^{-1}.

Now we specialize to
s=s_{0}
. To prove
Item

3
of
3.26
, it is enough to show that, when changing parameters from
(0;a,b,c)
to
(q,1,1,1)
(with
a,b,c
the values chosen in the proof of
Item

1
), the denominator of (
C.128
) decreases. The derivative
\partial_{\hat{y}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
is related to
\hat{f}
via (
C.49
), where the ratio
\mathfrak{d}/|\gamma^{\prime}|\asymp 1
, and (
C.37
) gives an approximation for
\hat{f}
. Recalling
C.28
and in particular the fact that the parameter

\Gamma
in (
C.36
) increases by
\Gamma_{1}-\Gamma_{0}\asymp\gamma^{\prime}q\asymp\mathfrak{d}q\asymp qs_{0}
, we see that
\partial_{\hat{y}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
decreases by order
qs_{0}
. The derivative
\partial_{\hat{x}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
also changes by order
qs_{0}
but, since it is multiplied by
(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}\gamma^{\prime}\lesssim{\bf K}^{-1/2}
in (
C.128
), we do not need to determine the sign of this change. Altogether the denominator of (
C.128
) decreases, and (
3.21
) follows, under the assumption that
s_{-}\geq{\bf K}h_{0}^{1/2}
.

C.3.3.
Proof of

Item

4
of
3.26

Recall that we are assuming that
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{s};q}\leq(1/{\bf K})\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}}
for all
s\in[s_{-},s_{+}]
.
The denominator on the right side of (
C.128
) is bounded below and changes by
O(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}}q)
upon changing
(q;a,b,c)
from
(q;1,1,1)
to
(0;a,b,c)
. Therefore, it follows that

(C.129)

\displaystyle|\partial_{h}\mathcal{U}_{q}^{h}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s)-\partial_{h}\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{h}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s+\mathfrak{r})|\lesssim\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}}^{3/2}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{s};q}^{-1/2}q\asymp q\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}}^{5/3}h^{-1/3}\lesssim qs_{+}^{5/3}h^{-1/3},

where in the second step we use the third part of
3.18
. The bound (
3.22
) then follows by integrating with respect to
h
.

C.3.4.
Proof of
Item

5
of
3.26

By symmetry, we may show (
3.23
) when
x=(x,y)
is such that
x\geq x_{q}^{SW}
, so that
s\asymp\mathfrak{d}_{z}
. Let
z_{0}=(x_{0},y_{0})
with
x_{0}=\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s
and
y_{0}=\mathcal{U}_{q}^{h_{0}}(x_{0})
.
By (
3.17
), (
3.19
), and (
3.20
), we have that
\mathcal{U}_{q}^{h_{0}}(x_{0})\geq\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{h_{0}}(x_{0}+\mathfrak{r})+\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
. In particular, the left-hand side of (
3.23
) is non-negative at
z_{0}
. Hence, it is enough to prove the claim at
z=(x_{0},y)
with
y<y_{0}
. Let
x_{0}\geq x^{SW}_{q}
and
y,y^{\prime}\leq y_{0}
be such that
\mathcal{H}_{q}(x_{0},y^{\prime})=\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(x_{0}+\mathfrak{r},y+\mathfrak{r}^{\prime})=h\in[0,h_{0}]
. For any
h\in[0,h_{0}]
, we have

\displaystyle y^{\prime}-y=\mathcal{U}_{q}^{h}(x_{0})-(\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{h}(x_{0}+\mathfrak{r})+\mathfrak{r}^{\prime})\gtrsim-qs^{5/3}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{h_{0}}v^{-1/3}dv\asymp-q\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}^{5/3}h_{0}^{2/3},

where the first inequality follows from integrating (
C.129
) (with
\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}}
not yet replaced by
s_{+}
) (and using the bound
\mathcal{U}_{q}^{h_{0}}(x_{0})\geq\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{h_{0}}(x_{0}+\mathfrak{r})+\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
), and the second follows from the fact that
s\asymp\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}
.
Write

(C.130)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(x_{0}+\mathfrak{r},y+\mathfrak{r}^{\prime})=\mathcal{H}_{q}(x_{0},y^{\prime})=\mathcal{H}_{q}(x_{0},y)+\int_{y}^{y^{\prime}}\partial_{u}\mathcal{H}_{q}(x_{0},u)du

and recall (
3.10
). We have

\mathcal{H}_{q}(w)^{1/3}\mathfrak{d}_{w}^{-2/3}\asymp(\mathfrak{d}_{w}^{-1}\mathfrak{e}_{w;q})^{1/2}
(by the third item of
3.18
) and we prove below that

(C.131)

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{d}_{z}^{-1}\mathfrak{e}_{z;q})^{1/2}\lesssim(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q})^{1/2}.

for
z=(x_{0},y),y\leq y_{0}
. Then, we deduce

(C.132)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{a,b,c}(x_{0}+\mathfrak{r},y+\mathfrak{r}^{\prime})-\mathcal{H}_{q}(x_{0},y)\gtrsim-q\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}^{5/3}h_{0}^{2/3}(\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}^{-1}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{0};q})^{1/2}\asymp-q\mathfrak{d}_{z_{0}}h_{0}

which confirms (
3.23
). It remains to prove
(
C.131
). To that purpose, first observe that since
\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}\lesssim\mathfrak{d}_{z}
for all
z\in\mathfrak{L}_{q}
, we can assume that the r.h.s. of (
C.131
) is smaller than some small constant
\epsilon
. Next, note that the slope
\rho_{y}
of the arctic boundary with vertical coordinate
y
satisfies
\rho_{y}\leq Cy^{1/2}
for some constant
C
(as the arctic boundary has uniformly positive and bounded curvature). Increasing
y=\mathfrak{d}_{w}^{2}
by
\delta

increases
\mathfrak{e}_{z;q}
by
\delta\rho_{y}^{-1}(1+o(\delta))
. Hence,

\partial_{y}(\mathfrak{e}_{w;q}/\mathfrak{d}_{w})=(\frac{1}{\rho_{y}\mathfrak{d}_{w;q}}-\frac{\mathfrak{e}_{w;q}}{2\mathfrak{d}_{w}^{3}})\geq\frac{1}{\mathfrak{d}_{w}^{2}}(\frac{1}{C}-\frac{\mathfrak{e}_{w;q}}{2\mathfrak{d}_{w}})
. For
w=z_{0}
, the quantity in parenthesis is larger than
1/C-\epsilon^{2}/2>0
, where positivity holds choosing
\epsilon
small enough. Therefore, the ratio
\mathfrak{e}_{w;q}/\mathfrak{d}_{w}
decreases when moving
w
from
z_{0}
vertically downward. As a consequence, the quantity in parenthesis is positive for all
w
between
z
and
z_{0}
, and (
C.131
) follows.

C.3.5.
Proof of
3.26
in the case
s_{-}\lesssim h_{0}^{1/2}

The proof of
3.26
requires some additional arguments in the case
s_{-}\lesssim h_{0}^{1/2}
. The reason is that, in that case, the interval
[s_{-},s_{+}]
contains points for which
\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}}
is of the same order as
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{s};q}
. In this regime, the limit shape and its level lines cannot be approximated via
C.20
and we will rely on
C.18
instead.
In the present section, we will show how the proof of
Item

1
is modified with respect to the case
s_{-}\geq{\bf K}h_{0}^{1/2}
(similar considerations hold also for
Item

4
–
Item

5
, but we will skip details there) and we will prove
Item

2
(recall that in the case
s_{-}\geq{\bf K}h_{0}^{1/2}
this statement is void) as well as
Item

3
.
Let us additionally assume that
\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s_{0}}}\leq(1/{\bf K})\wedge({\bf K}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{s_{0}};q})
, since in the case
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{s_{0}};q}\leq(1/{\bf K})\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s_{0}}}
the proof is closer to that in
Section

C.3.1
.

We need to prove that we can find parameters
a,b,c\in[1-C\varepsilon,1+C\varepsilon]
, as well as
\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
, such that (
3.19
), (
3.20
) and (
3.21
) hold, as well as (
3.17
) and (
3.18
). The choice of
\mathfrak{r}
will correspond to the choice of
\hat{x}_{1}
below, while
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}
is defined by (
3.18
) itself.
We start with (
3.19
) and we restrict our attention to the case where
z_{s}
is either to the left of
p^{SW}_{q}
, or it is to the right and
\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}}\leq(1/{\bf K})\wedge({\bf K}\mathfrak{e}_{z_{s};q})
, since in the opposite case the proof is similar to that in
Section

C.3.1
. We use the notation of
C.18
, and write

(C.133)

\displaystyle x_{s}=x_{q}^{SW}+\mathfrak{d}\hat{x}_{s},y_{s}=\mathfrak{d}^{2}\hat{y}_{s},\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{x}_{s},\hat{y}_{s})=\mathfrak{d}^{-2}\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}(z_{s}),\qquad\mathfrak{d}=\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s_{0}}}\asymp s_{0}\asymp h_{0}^{1/2}.

As shown in
Section

C.1.4
, the map
(a,b)\mapsto(\gamma^{\prime\prime}(q,a,b,c),\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}(q,a,b,c))
(with
c
fixed to
3-a-b
and
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(q,a,b,c),\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}(q,a,b,c)
denoting the second and third derivatives of the arctic boundary at
p^{SW}_{q;a,b,c}
) is a local bijection with uniformly bounded Jacobian.
We then choose
a,b,c
such that
a-1,b-1,c-1=O(q)
and

(C.134)

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime\prime}(q,1,1,1)-\gamma^{\prime\prime}(0,a,b,c)=-q\mathfrak{d}(2\hat{x}_{s_{0}}+\eta)

(C.135)

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}(q,1,1,1)-\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}(0,a,b,c)=3q\gamma^{\prime\prime}(q,1,1,1)

for some small
\eta>0
, and we wish to show that with this choice (
3.19
) holds.
Thanks to
C.18
, we can replace the level line
\mathcal{U}^{h_{0}}_{q;a,b,c}
(or equivalently, the corresponding level line of the rescaled height function
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
) with the
h:=h_{0}/\mathfrak{d}^{2}\asymp 1
level line of
\mathfrak{H}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}(q,a,b,c),\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}(q,a,b,c)}
. In fact, when comparing the second derivatives of the level lines with parameters
(q,1,1,1)
and
(0,a,b,c)
, the error term
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{2})
in
C.18
translates into an error term of order
q\mathfrak{d}^{2}
, where the factor
q
comes from
a-1,b-1,c-1=O(q)
. Note that
q\mathfrak{d}^{2}\asymp qs_{0}\mathfrak{d}
is by an order
\mathfrak{d}\leq(1/\bf K)
smaller than the gain in curvature of order
qs_{0}
that we are after in (
3.19
), and therefore we can safely neglect it.

To proceed, we need to state a few facts about the explicit functions
\mathfrak{H}_{q,\gamma^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}}
and
\mathtt{H}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}
defined in
C.18
. The proofs are based on elementary calculus, and can be found in
Section

C.3.6
. We begin with the following:

Lemma C.29
.

Fix
\gamma^{\prime\prime}>0,\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathfrak{H}_{q,\gamma^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}}
be as in (
C.61
) and let

(C.136)

\displaystyle X=\hat{x}+\frac{\mathfrak{d}q}{2\gamma^{\prime\prime}}(\mu\hat{x}+\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}^{2});\qquad Y={\hat{y}}.

Then,

(C.137)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{H}_{q,\gamma^{\prime\prime}+\mu q\mathfrak{d},\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}+3\gamma^{\prime\prime}q}(\hat{x},{\hat{y}})=\mathfrak{H}_{0,\gamma^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}}(X,Y)+\mathcal{O}(q\mathfrak{d}^{2}+q^{2}\mathfrak{d}).

Definition C.30
.

With the notations of
C.29
, set
Q=\mathfrak{d}q/2
and
M=\mu/\gamma^{\prime\prime}
. Define

(C.138)

\Phi_{M,Q}({\hat{x}}):={\hat{x}}+Q(M{\hat{x}}+{\hat{x}}^{2}),

so that the change of variables (
C.136
) from
(X,Y)
to
({\hat{x}},{\hat{y}})
is
(X,Y)=(\Phi_{M,Q}({\hat{x}}),{\hat{y}})
. Define
G_{M,Q}({\hat{x}},{\hat{y}}):=\mathtt{H}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\bigl(\Phi_{M,Q}({\hat{x}}),{\hat{y}}\bigr).
Given
h>0
and
{\hat{x}}
such that
{\hat{x}},\Phi_{M,Q}({\hat{x}})>-\sqrt{2h/\gamma^{\prime\prime}}
, let
U_{h}({\hat{x}})
and
U_{h;M,Q}({\hat{x}})
denote the unique values such that

(C.139)

\mathtt{H}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}({\hat{x}},U_{h}({\hat{x}}))=G_{M,Q}({\hat{x}},U_{h;M,Q}({\hat{x}}))\Bigl(=\mathtt{H}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}(\Phi_{M,Q}({\hat{x}}),U_{h;M,Q}({\hat{x}}))\Bigr)=h.

Remark C.31
.

From
C.29
it follows that if we wish to estimate (neglecting error terms
\mathcal{O}(q^{2}\mathfrak{d}+q\mathfrak{d}^{2})
) the difference between the derivatives of the level lines at level
h
of
\mathfrak{H}_{q,\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{0},\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}+3\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{0}q}
and of
\mathfrak{H}_{0,\gamma^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}}
with
\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{0}-\gamma^{\prime\prime}=\mu q\mathfrak{d}
, it is enough to compare the corresponding level lines of
\mathtt{H}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}(\Phi_{M,Q}({\hat{x}}),{\hat{y}})
and of
\mathtt{H}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}({\hat{x}},{\hat{y}})
, that is,
U_{h;M,Q}
and
U_{h;0,0}
.

The next lemma compares the derivatives with respect to
h
of
U_{h;M,Q}
and
U_{h;0,0}
at points where the slopes coincide.

Lemma C.32
.

Fix
h>0
and a base point
{\hat{x}}_{0}\geq 0
. Let
{\hat{x}}_{1}={\hat{x}}_{1}^{[M]}
be chosen so that

\displaystyle U_{h;M,Q}^{\prime}({\hat{x}}_{0})=U_{h;0,0}^{\prime}({\hat{x}}_{1})

(suppressing from the notation the dependence of
\hat{x}_{1}
on
\hat{x}_{0}
and
Q
.)
The choice
M=-2{\hat{x}}_{0}-\eta
, for sufficiently small
\eta>0
, has the following property: for each fixed
h^{\prime}\in(0,h]
, there is a constant

c=c(h^{\prime},h,{\hat{x}}_{0})>0
, such that

(C.140)

\displaystyle\partial_{h}U_{h^{\prime};M,Q}({\hat{x}}_{0})-\partial_{h}U_{h^{\prime};0,0}({\hat{x}}_{1}^{[-2{\hat{x}}_{0}-\eta]})\geq cQ\eta.

For
0<\varepsilon<h
one has
\inf\{c(h^{\prime},h,{\hat{x}}_{0}):h^{\prime}\in[\varepsilon,h]\}>0
.

Finally, the next lemma allows to compare the second derivatives with respect to
{\hat{x}}
of
U_{h;M,Q}
and
U_{h;0,0}
in the neighborhood of points where the slopes coincide.

Lemma C.33
.

Let
h>0,\delta>0,\eta>0,{\hat{x}}_{0}\geq 0,w\in\mathbb{R}
, write
{\hat{x}}^{\prime}={\hat{x}}_{0}+w
, and choose

M=-2{\hat{x}}_{0}-\eta
.
There exists a constant
c>0
such that
for fixed
h
,
{\hat{x}}_{0}
, and
w
satisfying
w+{\hat{x}}_{0}>\delta-\sqrt{2h/\gamma^{\prime\prime}}
, it holds for
\eta
small enough

(C.141)

\displaystyle\bigl(U_{h;M,Q}({\hat{x}}^{\prime})-U_{h;M,Q}({\hat{x}}_{0})\bigr)-\bigl(U_{h;0,0}({\hat{x}}_{1}+w)-U_{h;0,0}({\hat{x}}_{1})\bigr)\geq c\delta^{1/2}Qw^{2}+\mathcal{O}(Q^{2}),

as
Q\to 0
,
where
{\hat{x}}_{1}={\hat{x}}_{1}^{[-2{\hat{x}}_{0}-\eta]}
.

We go back to the proof of (
3.19
).
Thanks to
C.31
, within the level of approximation we seek in
3.26
, we can replace the
h
-level lines of
\mathfrak{H}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}(q,1,1,1),\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}(q,1,1,1)}
and
\mathfrak{H}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}(0,a,b,c),\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}(0,a,b,c)}

with
U_{h;M,Q}
and
U_{h;0;0}
respectively, defined in
Definition

C.30
. Then, recalling the rescaling of variables (
C.133
), (
3.19
) follows directly from
C.33
, where the assumption
w+\hat{x}_{0}>\delta-\sqrt{2h/\gamma^{\prime\prime}}
there (with
\delta\asymp C^{-1}
) is guaranteed by the assumption
s\geq C^{-1}h_{0}^{1/2}
in
Item

1
(indeed, recall from (
C.133
) that the variable
\hat{x}
is rescaled by a factor
\mathfrak{d}\asymp h_{0}^{1/2}
with respect to
s
).

Next, we prove

Item

3
. Here we only discuss the case when

h\geq\mathbf{K}^{-1}h_{0}
, as the case
h<\mathbf{K}^{-1}h_{0}

can be addressed very similarly to as in
Section

C.3.2
(as
then we are in the regime where

\mathfrak{e}\leq\mathbf{K}^{-1}\mathfrak{d}
, where the
approximations used there apply). In the case
h\geq\mathbf{K}^{-1}h_{0}
,
Item

3

follows directly from
C.32

(keeping
C.31
in mind). Note that, with respect to
(
3.21
),
(
C.140
) gives a
quantitative lower bound of order
Q\asymp q\mathfrak{d}
on the
difference of
h
-derivatives of the
(q,1,1,1)
and
(0,a,b,c)

level lines. This positive margin allows to neglect the errors
(that are of higher order in
q
and/or
\mathfrak{d}
, thanks to

C.18
and
C.31
) involved in replacing this
difference of level lines of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
with the difference
of the lines
U_{h;M,Q},U_{h;0;0}
defined in

Definition

C.30
.

Finally, we prove
Item

2
.
Let
s\in[s_{-},C^{-1}h_{0}^{1/2}]
, that is,

z_{s}
satisfies condition (III) described at the beginning of
Section

C.3
:
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{s};q}\lesssim\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}}/C
for some large
C>1
and
z_{s}
is to the left of
p^{SW}_{q}
(for the rest of this proof, we will write
\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{d}
instead of
\mathfrak{e}_{z_{s};q},\mathfrak{d}_{z_{s}}
). In this case, the level lines of
\mathcal{H}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
no longer coincide, since we are in the case
\gamma^{\prime}<0
of (
C.47
). In fact, the
h
-level line
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}^{h}_{q;a,b,c}
of
\mathcal{H}_{q;a,b,c}
coincides with the
\mathfrak{d}^{-1/2}\mathfrak{e}^{-3/2}h
level line
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{q;a,b,c}^{h}
of

(C.142)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}^{-1/2}\mathfrak{e}^{-3/2}y-\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{q;a,b,c}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\mathfrak{d}^{-1/2}\mathfrak{e}^{-3/2}(y^{\prime}+(\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e})^{1/2}\gamma^{\prime}\hat{x}+\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}\hat{y})-\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{q;a,b,c}(\hat{x},\hat{y}).

Formulas (
C.117
) to (
C.119
) hold unchanged.

To determine
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}
, we set
\hat{y}=\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(\hat{x})
in (
C.142
), differentiate with respect to
\hat{x}
and impose the derivative to equal zero. Using
(
C.69
) to approximate the derivatives of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
with those of
\mathfrak{G}
, we get immediately
(letting
\Delta:=2(\gamma^{\prime 2}+1)\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(\hat{s})-\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{s}
)

(C.143)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}(\hat{s})=-\frac{\frac{\gamma^{\prime}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{e}}}+3\Xi\Delta^{1/2}\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{s}\sqrt{\frac{\mathfrak{e}}{\mathfrak{d}}}+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{e})+\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{e}/\mathfrak{d})}{1-3\Xi\Delta^{1/2}(1+\gamma^{\prime 2})\sqrt{\frac{\mathfrak{e}}{\mathfrak{d}}}}.

Plugging this into the first of (
C.118
), we deduce

(C.144)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(x)=3\Xi\Delta^{1/2}\frac{|\gamma^{\prime}|\sqrt{\frac{\mathfrak{e}}{\mathfrak{d}}}-\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}{(1+\gamma^{\prime 2})}\hat{s}\mathfrak{e}}{1-3\Xi\Delta^{1/2}\sqrt{\frac{\mathfrak{e}}{\mathfrak{d}}}}\Big(1+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\mathfrak{e}\mathfrak{d}})+\hat{\mathcal{O}}((\mathfrak{e}\mathfrak{d})^{1/2})\Big).

This holds for general
(q,a,b,c)
. Now we compare the cases
(q,a,b,c)=(q,1,1,1)
and
(q,a,b,c)=(0,a,b,c)
,
where the values
a,b,c
are those fixed so that (
C.134
) holds.

Taking
x=s+\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})
, we claim that

(C.145)

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{s}\mathcal{U}_{q}^{h_{0}}(s+\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0}))-\partial_{s}\mathcal{U}_{a,b,c}^{h_{0}}(s+\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+\mathfrak{r})\right|\lesssim\sqrt{\mathfrak{e}\mathfrak{d}}qs_{0}\lesssim C^{-1/2}qs_{0}^{2}

for all
s\leq s_{1}:=C^{-1}h_{0}^{1/2}
, where the second bound follows from (
C.133
) and
\mathfrak{e}\lesssim\mathfrak{d}/C
. The first estimate in (
C.145
) follows quickly from (
C.144
) together with the facts that
|\gamma^{\prime}|\asymp\mathfrak{d}
, and that
\gamma^{\prime}
and
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
change by
\mathcal{O}(q\mathfrak{d}^{2})
and
\mathcal{O}(q\mathfrak{d})
, respectively, from passing from
\mathfrak{A}_{q}
to
\mathfrak{A}_{a,b,c}
(where that
\gamma^{\prime}
changes by
\mathcal{O}(q\mathfrak{d}^{2})
follows from the facts that
\gamma^{\prime}=0
in both
\mathfrak{A}_{q}
and
\mathfrak{A}_{a,b,c}
at their SW tangency locations, and that
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
changes by
\mathcal{O}(q\mathfrak{d})
when passing from
\mathfrak{A}_{q}
to
\mathfrak{A}_{a,b,c}
).

We have then (
C.145
) for
s\leq s_{1}
and, on the other hand, for
s=s_{1}
we deduce from (
C.141
)
(where in our case
\delta\asymp C^{-1}
, as indicated above) that

(C.146)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{h_{0}}_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s_{1})-(\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}+\mathcal{U}^{h_{0}}_{a,b,c}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s_{1}+\mathfrak{r}))\gtrsim C^{-1/2}qs_{0}^{3},

where we used the fact that
s_{0}-s_{1}\gtrsim s_{0}
, that follows from the assumptions of
3.26
, as well as
s\leq C^{-1}h_{0}^{1/2}
.
Integrating (
C.145
) from
s
to
s_{1}
, noting that
s_{1}-s\lesssim C^{-1}s_{0}
and using (
C.146
) we deduce

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{h_{0}}_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s)-(\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}+\mathcal{U}^{h_{0}}_{a,b,c}(\mathfrak{l}_{q}(h_{0})+s+\mathfrak{r}))\gtrsim C^{-1/2}qs_{0}^{3}-qs^{3}_{0}C^{-3/2}\gtrsim C^{-1/2}qs_{0}^{3}>0,

where in the last bound we assumed that
C
is large enough.
Item

2
is proven.

C.3.6.
Properties of
\mathfrak{H}_{q,\gamma^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}}
,
\mathtt{H}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}
and their level lines

In this section, we prove the statements of
C.29
,
C.32
and
C.33
about the function
\mathfrak{H}
and its level lines.

Proof of
C.29
.

Write for lightness of notation
\psi:=\gamma^{\prime\prime}+\mu q\mathfrak{d}
and
S(\hat{x},{\hat{y}}):=\sqrt{2{\hat{y}}-\gamma^{\prime\prime}\hat{x}^{2}}
.
For
\mathtt{H}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}
one computes

(C.147)

\displaystyle\hat{x}\partial_{x}\mathtt{H}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}({\hat{x}},{\hat{y}})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\hat{x}\sqrt{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}}{\pi}S({\hat{x}},{\hat{y}})=2\gamma^{\prime\prime}\partial_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\mathtt{H}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}({\hat{x}},{\hat{y}});\quad\partial_{y}\mathtt{H}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}({\hat{x}},{\hat{y}})=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\pi}\arctan(\frac{\sqrt{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}{\hat{x}}}{S({\hat{x}},{\hat{y}})}).

A Taylor expansion gives

\displaystyle\mathtt{H}_{\psi}({\hat{x}},{\hat{y}})

\displaystyle=\mathtt{H}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}(X,Y)-\frac{\mathfrak{d}q}{2\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\Bigl(\mu X+\gamma^{\prime\prime}X^{2}\Bigr)\,\partial_{x}\mathtt{H}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}(X,Y)+\mu q\mathfrak{d}\,\partial_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\mathtt{H}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}(X,Y)+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{2}q^{2})

\displaystyle=\mathtt{H}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}(X,Y)+\frac{\mathfrak{d}q}{\pi\sqrt{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime}X^{2}}{2}S(X,Y)+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{2}q^{2}).

Also, in the term in
\mathfrak{H}_{q,\psi,\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}+3\gamma^{\prime\prime}q}
proportional to
\mathfrak{d}
, we may replace

{\hat{x}},{\hat{y}},\gamma^{\prime\prime}+\mu q\mathfrak{d}
there by
X,Y,\gamma^{\prime\prime}
up to an error
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{2}q)
, obtaining

\frac{\mathfrak{d}}{\pi\sqrt{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}}\left[\left(\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}{3}+\frac{2\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}}{9\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\right)S(X,Y)^{3}-\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}}{3\gamma^{\prime\prime}}YS(X,Y)\right]\\
+\frac{\mathfrak{d}q}{\pi\sqrt{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}}\left[\frac{1}{2}S(X,Y)^{3}-YS(X,Y)\right]+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{2}q).

Using
S(X,Y)^{3}=(2Y-\gamma^{\prime\prime}X^{2})S(X,Y)
, the part proportional to
\mathfrak{d}q
becomes

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathfrak{d}q}{\pi\sqrt{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}}\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime}X^{2}}{2}S(X,Y),

which cancels exactly with the
\mathfrak{d}q
contribution from
\mathtt{H}_{\psi}({\hat{x}},{\hat{y}})
. Therefore, the claim of the lemma follows. ∎

Proof of
C.32
.

For notational convenience, write
U_{h^{\prime}}:=U_{h^{\prime};0,0}
and

\widetilde{U}_{h^{\prime}}:=U_{h^{\prime};M,Q}
. By (
C.139
),

(C.148)

\displaystyle\widetilde{U}_{h^{\prime}}(t)=U_{h^{\prime}}\bigl(\Phi_{M,Q}(t)\bigr).

Take first
\eta=0
.
Evaluating at the fixed base point
{\hat{x}}_{0}
and using
M=-2{\hat{x}}_{0}
gives for every
h^{\prime}\in(0,h]

(C.149)

\displaystyle\widetilde{U}_{h^{\prime}}({\hat{x}}_{0})=U_{h^{\prime}}\bigl({\hat{x}}_{0}+Q(M{\hat{x}}_{0}+{\hat{x}}_{0}^{2})\bigr)=U_{h^{\prime}}({\hat{x}}_{0}-Q{\hat{x}}_{0}^{2}).

Differentiating (
C.148
) with respect to
t
and then
evaluating at
t={\hat{x}}_{0},h^{\prime}=h
gives

(C.150)

\displaystyle\widetilde{U}_{h}^{\prime}({\hat{x}}_{0})

\displaystyle=U_{h}^{\prime}({\hat{x}}_{0}-Q{\hat{x}}_{0}^{2})\bigl(1+Q(M+2{\hat{x}}_{0})\bigr)=U_{h}^{\prime}({\hat{x}}_{0}-Q{\hat{x}}_{0}^{2}),

because
M+2{\hat{x}}_{0}=0
. By the definition of
{\hat{x}}_{1}
, this shows that

(C.151)

\displaystyle{\hat{x}}_{1}^{[-2{\hat{x}}_{0}]}={\hat{x}}_{0}-Q{\hat{x}}_{0}^{2}.

Substituting (
C.151
) into
(
C.149
) yields
\widetilde{U}_{h^{\prime}}({\hat{x}}_{0})=U_{h^{\prime}}({\hat{x}}_{1}^{[-2{\hat{x}}_{0}]})
.
Differentiating this identity with respect to the level parameter
h^{\prime}
gives

(C.152)

\displaystyle\partial_{h}U_{h^{\prime};M,Q}({\hat{x}}_{0})-\partial_{h}U_{h^{\prime};0,0}({\hat{x}}_{1}^{[-2{\hat{x}}_{0}]})=0.

For the strict comparison, assume
Q>0
and choose
M=-2{\hat{x}}_{0}-\eta
. Put

\displaystyle\Phi:=\Phi_{M,Q}({\hat{x}}_{0})={\hat{x}}_{0}+Q(M{\hat{x}}_{0}+{\hat{x}}_{0}^{2})={\hat{x}}_{0}-Q{\hat{x}}_{0}^{2}-Q\eta{\hat{x}}_{0}.

Then for every
h^{\prime}\in(0,h]
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{U}_{h^{\prime}}({\hat{x}}_{0})=U_{h^{\prime}}(\Phi),

so it remains to compare
\partial_{h}U_{h^{\prime}}(\Phi)
with
\partial_{h}U_{h^{\prime}}({\hat{x}}_{1})
, with
{\hat{x}}_{1}={\hat{x}}_{1}^{[-2{\hat{x}}_{0}-\eta]}
.

We first record two monotonicity facts.
Let

(C.153)
